TL;DR: PATE-Forensics introduces Perception-as-Tool, decoupling detection/localization from explanation; uses a DINOv3-based tool for multi-granularity detection and cue-guided localization; outputs provide structured context for a general LLM to generate explanations without task-specific fine-tuning; achieves best score on DDL-X Track 3 (0.89).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of task-specific explainable deepfake forensics that tightly couple detection, localization, and explanations and to enable scalable, natural-language explanations via a general-purpose LLM by providing structured forensic context.

Method: Architectural decoupling via Perception-as-Tool; DINOv3-based tool with multi-granularity detection (global, patch-level, segment-level) integrated with cue-guided localization; spatialization of evidence into forgery score maps guiding dense mask prediction; generation of explanations using a general-purpose LLM with prompt constraints, no task-specific fine-tuning.

Result: Achieves best official score 0.89 on DDL-X Track 3, outperforming second place by 0.19 points; code released.

Conclusion: The paradigm effectively unifies detection, localization, and explanation under a tool-assisted framework, yielding strong performance and enabling LLM-based explanations without fine-tuning; further work could explore robustness across datasets and manipulations.

Abstract: Existing explainable deepfake forensic methods typically rely on task-adapted MLLM to jointly address detection, localization, and explanation. Inspired by agent-style tool use, we instead introduce a Perception-as-Tool paradigm and instantiate it as PATE-Forensics, which architecturally decouples detection and localization from explanation generation while coupling detection and localization as tightly as possible within a forensic perception tool. The DINOv3-based tool couples a multi-granularity detection module that integrates global, patch-level, and segment-level evidence with a cue-guided localization module by spatializing the patch-level and segment-level evidence into forgery score maps that guide dense mask prediction. The original image and forensic perception outputs produced by the tool form structured forensic context for a general-purpose MLLM, which is guided by prompt constraints to generate explanations without task-specific fine-tuning. On DDL-X Track 3, PATE-Forensics achieves the best official score of 0.89, outperforming the second-ranked team by 0.19 points. Our code is available at https://github.com/yqli00000/PATE-Forensics.

</details>


### [27] [MR-IQA-2: Faithful Image Quality Reflection via Fine-Grained Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18579)
*Yuan li,Youyuan Lin,Chenhui Chu,Shin'ya Nishida*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: MR-IQA-2 introduces an actor-editor-judge framework for blind image quality assessment that decouples reasoning from rating, provides verifiable supervision via a judge that compares original and edited images, and uses fine-grained credit assignment with masked-token updates to align reasoning with ratings. It achieves competitive rating alignment and offers richer, faithful visual reflection for downstream tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve faithfulness and reliability of multimodal large language model (MLLM) based blind IQA by ensuring that the reasoning underpinning quality scores is veridical and separable from the final rating, addressing issues where correct ratings could arise from chance or shared rewards.

Method: An actor generates quality-related reasoning for an input image; an editor revises the image according to identified quality factors; a frozen judge compares the original and edited images to provide reflective supervision for the actor's reasoning; fine-grained credit assignment decouples reasoning and rating supervision; judge feedback supervises reasoning while human ratings supervise the predicted rating; masked token-specific updates preserve causal relations from reasoning to rating.

Result: MR-IQA-2 achieves competitive alignment with human ratings on IQA benchmarks; the visual reflection produced by the framework enables richer, more faithful visual understanding beyond mere rating, potentially benefiting image-quality optimization and downstream tasks.

Conclusion: The MR-IQA-2 framework advances blind IQA by decoupling credit assignment for reasoning and rating, providing verifiable supervision, and enabling faithful, analyzable reasoning through actor–editor–judge dynamics, with demonstrated competitive performance and additional interpretability through visual reflection.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have shown strong potential for image quality assessment (IQA) by improving consistency between quality ratings and their underlying reasoning. However, most approaches supervise reasoning through human-provided ratings and rarely examine whether it faithfully reflects image quality. Rating accuracy alone does not ensure faithful reasoning; a shared reward also obscures supervision sources and may reinforce unfaithful reasoning when a correct rating occurs by chance. To improve the faithfulness and reliability of blind IQA, we aim to (1) decouple credit assignment for reasoning and rating and (2) provide verifiable supervision for faithful reasoning. We introduce MR-IQA-2, an actor-editor-judge framework that operationalizes reasoning-editing-reflection. The actor generates quality reasoning for an input image, and the editor revises the image according to the identified quality factors. A frozen judge compares the original and edited images and provides reflective supervision for the actor's reasoning. MR-IQA-2 further uses fine-grained credit assignment to decouple reasoning and rating supervision. Judge feedback supervises reasoning, whereas human ratings supervise the predicted rating. Masked token-specific updates distinguish these signals while preserving the causal relation from reasoning to rating. Across IQA benchmarks, MR-IQA-2 achieves competitive rating alignment with humans. Visual reflection also enables richer and more faithful visual understanding beyond rating, which may inform image-quality optimization and related downstream tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/RobinY99/MR-IQA-2.

</details>


### [28] [SPARC: Subspace Position-Aware Robust Few-Shot Calibration for Distribution-Shifted Industrial Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18585)
*Seokhee Han,Seungjun Chu,Mateusz Nowak,Peter Chin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SPARC is a gradient-free, few-shot calibration method for patch-based vision anomaly detectors. It removes deployment-time nuisance from encoder patch features via per-cell subspace projection, using k≤8 verified-normal images and no weight updates, and improves shift-prone detectors across seven detectors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-based industrial anomaly detectors trained on one distribution degrade under deployment shifts (illumination, fixture, sensor changes). Labeled anomalies are scarce, so calibration must be achieved with very few verified-normal images.

Method: Interposes on patch features between the encoder and detector and applies a closed-form, per-cell subspace projection to remove a spatially indexed nuisance. Uses saturation rank r = k−1 on the encoder’s native patch grid, requiring no gradient updates and being compatible with memory-bank, density, prototype, and mutual detectors.

Result: On shift-prone benchmarks, SPARC improves pooled Image AUROC and AU-PRO@0.3 by +13.8 and +3.5 percentage points across seven detectors. On benchmarks without engineered shifts, gains are small and mixed. Ablations confirm the saturation-rank choice and indicate robustness to backbone and calibration conditions; controls show gains arise from the per-cell subspace structure rather than calibration images alone.

Conclusion: SPARC provides a simple, gradient-free, few-shot calibration mechanism that effectively removes deployment-time nuisances from patch features, is widely compatible with existing detectors, and yields substantial gains under distribution shifts with minimal labeled data.

Abstract: Vision-based industrial anomaly detectors are calibrated on one distribution but may be deployed on another that differs in illumination, fixture placement, or sensor characteristics, sharply degrading an otherwise accurate detector. Adapting to the incoming lot is a natural response, but labeled anomalies are scarce. We therefore consider calibration using only a handful of verified-normal images available before scoring the rest of the lot. Existing fixes require backpropagation, detector-specific tuning, or choices about feature directions that few calibration samples cannot justify. We present SPARC, a few-shot calibration method that intercepts patch features between encoder and detector and removes a closed-form, spatially indexed estimate of deployment-time nuisance through per-cell subspace projection. It needs only $k \le 8$ verified-normal images and uses the algebraic saturation rank $r{=}k{-}1$ on the encoder's native patch grid. The correction requires no gradient or weight updates and works with memory-bank, density, prototype, and mutual detectors. On the shift-prone benchmarks, SPARC improves pooled Image AUROC and AU-PRO$_{0.3}$ for all seven detectors whose image scores depend on corrected patch features by $+13.8$ and $+3.5$ percentage points (pp), respectively; on benchmarks without engineered shift, the changes are small and mixed. Controls that give competing corrections the same calibration images attribute these gains to the per-cell subspace structure rather than the images alone. Further ablations support the saturation-rank choice and characterize sensitivity to backbone and calibration conditions.

</details>


### [29] [OmniHandwritingOCR: A Diagnostic Benchmark for Evaluating Multimodal LLMs in Handwritten OCR Scenarios](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18586)
*Zinuo Guo,Min Zhang,Bo Jiang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A comprehensive diagnostic benchmark (OmniHandwritingOCR) for handwritten OCR tailored to MLLMs, with 77.57k labeled images across six subtasks/more, including multi-line math, and evaluation of 13 systems across five metrics; demonstrates current models struggle with complex handwriting and multi-line formulas, with hallucinations observed in generative models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing OCR benchmarks focus on printed text or clean inputs; need to evaluate MLLMs' ability to faithfully read diverse handwritten content, multilingual text, error-prone handwriting, and complex mathematical expressions; identify failure modes (language, content, structure, visual grounding).

Method: Assemble OmniHandwritingOCR: six subtasks and twelve subsets; 77.57K labeled images from public datasets + newly collected student writings; introduce difficulty-stratified multi-line formula corpus; evaluate thirteen open- and closed-source systems using five complementary metrics under a unified protocol.

Result: Current systems underperform on faithful transcription; large performance drop on complex multi-line formulas; model rankings vary by language and formula setting; some generative models hallucinate plausible but unsupported corrections.

Conclusion: OmniHandwritingOCR is a challenging testbed to diagnose language, content, structural, and visual-grounding failure modes in handwritten OCR for MLLMs, enabling targeted improvements.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) are increasingly used as OCR systems in document and knowledge-processing pipelines, but their ability to faithfully read real handwriting remains underexplored. Existing OCR benchmarks focus largely on printed text or clean single-line inputs, leaving limited coverage of realistic handwritten OCR scenarios such as multilingual handwriting, writer errors, and structurally complex mathematical expressions. We introduce OmniHandwritingOCR, a diagnostic benchmark for evaluating MLLMs and OCR systems on handwritten OCR. It covers handwritten text recognition and handwritten mathematical expression recognition across six subtasks and twelve subsets, totaling 77.57K labeled images from public datasets and newly collected student writings. A key component is a difficulty-stratified multi-line formula corpus designed to test robustness under increasing structural complexity. We evaluate thirteen open- and closed-source systems with five complementary metrics under a unified protocol. Results show that current systems remain far from faithful transcription: performance drops sharply on complex multi-line formulas, model rankings vary across language and formula settings, and several generative models hallucinate plausible but visually unsupported corrections. OmniHandwritingOCR provides a challenging testbed for diagnosing language, content, structural, and visual-grounding failure modes of multimodal models in handwritten OCR scenarios.

</details>


### [30] [FD-CanKD: Frequency-Decoupled Cross-Attention Distillation as a Refinement Prior for Compact Object Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18590)
*YoungJae Cheong,Jhonghyun An*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FD-CanKD is a detector-oriented, three-level knowledge distillation framework for compact detectors that combines head-level prediction supervision, relation-level cross-attention-based non-local context transfer, and frequency-level component-selective alignment. In a controlled COCO YOLOv12 teacher-student setup, it achieves competitive results after distillation and further refinement, with a light 19.7M-parameter student, where distillation modules are removed at deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compact detectors suffer from limited representation capacity and accuracy gaps compared to large models. Traditional distillation focuses on single-target supervision. FD-CanKD aims to close the gap by transferring teacher knowledge across multiple complementary levels, including spatial context and frequency-domain cues.

Method: Three-tier distillation: (1) head-level prediction supervision; (2) relation-level non-local context transfer via cross-attention from teacher to student; (3) frequency-level component-selective alignment to preserve structural and detail-sensitive cues. Training uses a fixed 50-epoch from-scratch distillation on COCO with YOLOv12 in a teacher-student setting, followed by 20-epoch fine-tuning. Distillation modules are removed after training, producing a deployment-ready student of 19.7M parameters.

Result: Competitive with representative detector knowledge distillation baselines in controlled COCO experiments. Post-distillation fine-tuning yields stronger refinement-ready student. Final metrics after 20 extra epochs: mAP50:95 = 48.87, mAP50 = 65.84, mAP75 = 53.40. The deployed model remains at 19.7M parameters.

Conclusion: FD-CanKD demonstrates effective, multi-level knowledge transfer tailored for compact detectors, achieving strong performance while maintaining deployment efficiency. The approach is validated in a representative YOLOv12 teacher-student setup on COCO, with all distillation modules removed during deployment.

Abstract: Compact object detectors are suitable for resource-constrained visual perception, but their limited representation capacity creates an accuracy gap relative to large models. Conventional detector distillation often relies on prediction-level supervision or a single feature-alignment target, such as response, distribution, correlation, or frequency-domain matching. Frequency-Decoupled Cross-Attention Knowledge Distillation (FD-CanKD) is presented as a detector-oriented framework that transfers teacher knowledge at three complementary levels: head-level prediction supervision, relation-level non-local context transfer, and frequency-level component-selective alignment. Student features first aggregate teacher-side spatial context through cross-attention-based relation transfer, after which frequency-aware alignment preserves complementary structural and detail-sensitive cues. Under controlled Microsoft Common Objects in Context (COCO) experiments, fixed 50-epoch from-scratch comparisons show that FD-CanKD remains competitive with representative detector knowledge distillation baselines. Post-distillation continued fine-tuning further produces a stronger refinement-ready student than detector-only fine-tuning, reaching 48.87 mean average precision (mAP) at intersection-over-union thresholds from 0.50 to 0.95 (mAP50:95), 65.84 mAP50, and 53.40 mAP75 after 20 additional epochs. All distillation modules are removed after training, leaving the deployed student unchanged at 19.7M parameters. The framework is instantiated and evaluated in a controlled YOLOv12 teacher-student setting as a representative compact-detector case study.

</details>


### [31] [ReX-Shot: Single-Image Rephotography via Geometry- and Camera-Grounded Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18593)
*Ruiqi Zhang,Hao Zhu,Wenhao Zhang,Qi Zhang,Junqi Shi,Ming Lu,Xun Cao,Zhan Ma*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: ReX-Shot is a geometry- and camera-grounded generative framework that jointly controls viewpoint, focal length, and photographic effects from a single image for high-quality single-image rephotography, outperforming baselines with near-real-time interactivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods treat viewpoint, focal length, and appearance effects separately, leading to geometric distortions, aliasing, and limited 2D-only editing when attempting joint control. Imperfect single-image 3D reconstruction and sampling limits of focal-length enlargement hinder reliable cross-view synthesis and detail restoration.

Method: Propose ReX-Shot: (1) use implicitly transformed foundation-model features for robust target-view guidance to reduce projection bias; (2) formulate focal-length enlargement as a geometry-guided super-resolution problem; (3) apply generative detail priors to recover details lost during sparse 3D resampling; (4) build a 3D-aware generative backbone and extend photographic-effect control to 3D-aware appearance editing to maintain content consistency across viewpoints and focal lengths.

Result: Empirical results show ReX-Shot outperforms representative baselines across viewpoint, focal length, and photographic-effect controls, and enables near-real-time interactive rephotography.

Conclusion: ReX-Shot is the first unified framework to jointly control viewpoint, focal length, and parameterized photographic effects from a single image, enabling consistent 3D-aware rephotography with improved detail preservation and control.

Abstract: Single-image rephotography aims to synthesize new shots of a scene from a single reference image with specified viewpoints, focal lengths, and photographic effects, which are intrinsically coupled in imaging. Existing methods typically treat these factors separately and struggle under joint control: novel-view synthesis may introduce geometric distortions under focal-length changes, while super-resolution and instruction-guided editing remain confined to 2D and cannot reliably extend detail restoration or appearance control to novel viewpoints. We attribute these limitations to imperfect single-image 3D reconstruction and the sampling limit of continuous focal-length enlargement. To reduce projection bias from geometric errors, we use implicitly transformed foundation-model features for robust target-view guidance. We further formulate focal-length enlargement as a geometry-guided super-resolution problem and exploit generative detail priors to recover details lost during sparse 3D resampling. Built on this 3D-aware generative backbone, we lift photographic-effect control from 2D filtering to 3D-aware appearance editing, preserving content consistency across viewpoints and focal lengths. These components form ReX-Shot, a geometry- and camera-grounded generative framework for single-image rephotography. To our knowledge, ReX-Shot is the first unified framework to jointly control viewpoint, focal length, and parameterized photographic effects from a single image. Experiments show that ReX-Shot outperforms representative baselines across all three controls while enabling near-real-time interactive rephotography.

</details>


### [32] [Teach a Molmo2Fish: Towards interactive fish tracking with natural language guidance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18602)
*Kai Van Brunt,Justin Kay,Sara Beery*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A multimodal LLM-based interactive workflow (Molmo2Fish) for correcting imperfect tracking predictions in ecological sonar datasets. Demonstrates high performance on fish tracking and track correction, with variability in leveraging natural language guidance; code and data released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To convert imperfect tracking outputs into usable results in ecology by integrating human-in-the-loop corrections via a conversation with a multimodal LLM, addressing the challenges of multi-object tracking in large ecological datasets.

Method: An interactive prediction correction workflow where an LLM guides and corrects tracking predictions, tailored to a sonar fishDataset. The model corrects both its own predictions and external tracks, evaluated on guided and unguided tasks; results and code/data are made publicly available.

Result: Molmo2Fish achieves high performance on fish tracking and track correction tasks, but performance is not yet at the level to fully capitalize on natural language guidance; room for improvement in natural language interaction.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of LLM-guided correction for ecological tracking and provides open-source resources; suggests future work to better integrate natural language guidance and enhance practical usability.

Abstract: Computer vision is increasingly used to automate recognition tasks in large ecological datasets, but more complex tasks such as multi-object tracking continue to pose challenges. As researchers seek to incorporate vision models in ecology workflows, various lines of research have explored how to make imperfect predictions useful through human-in-the-loop processes. We propose a new approach to working with imperfect tracking predictions through an interactive prediction correction workflow taking place as a conversation with a multimodal large language model, which we tailor to a sonar fish tracking dataset as an initial proof of concept. We investigate the performance of the tool, Molmo2Fish, across guided and unguided tasks, correcting its own predicted tracks and external tracks. We find that Molmo2Fish achieves high performance on fish tracking and track correction tasks, but there is still much room to improve on incorporating natural language guidance. The code and data are publicly available at https://github.com/tidalove/molmo2fish.

</details>


### [33] [Dynamic SpectraFormer for Ultra-High-Definition Underwater Image Enhancement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18662)
*Zhiqiang Hu,Tao Yu,Shouren Huang,Masatoshi Ishikawa*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Dynamic SpectraFormer uses a frequency-domain transformer with an ultra-high-resolution sparse spectrum attention module and a dynamic spectrum weight generation layer to rectify both low- and high-frequency distortions in underwater images, showing improved quality on benchmarks with code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underwater images suffer color distortion, haze, and low visibility due to light absorption and refraction in water; distortions occupy different frequency bands. Traditional spatial-domain methods struggle with mixed distortions and may fail to capture long-range dependencies.

Method: Propose Dynamic SpectraFormer: a frequency-domain transformer with an ultra-high-resolution sparse spectrum attention module to capture long-range dependencies without sacrificing universal approximation power, and a dynamic spectrum weight generation layer that adaptively selects/weights spectrum bands to emphasize informative frequencies and suppress less relevant ones.

Result: Significant improvements in underwater image quality on multiple benchmarks, supported by extensive ablation studies; source code released at the provided GitHub URL.

Conclusion: A frequency-domain transformer with adaptive spectrum weighting effectively mitigates both low- and high-frequency distortions in underwater images, enhancing quality and robustness; the approach is validated by ablations and cross-benchmark results.

Abstract: Underwater images suffer from color distortion, haze, and poor visibility due to light refraction and absorption in water. These challenges significantly impact the utilization of Autonomous Underwater Vehicles (AUVs) or marine robots. Typically, color and brightness distortions manifest at lower frequencies, while edge and texture distortions are prevalent at higher frequencies. Traditional methods struggle to concurrently rectify these mixed distortions as they primarily concentrate on the spatial domain. To address these issues, we introduce the Dynamic SpectraFormer, which enhances underwater images through a frequency domain transformer. The Dynamic SpectraFormer introduces an ultra-high-resolution sparse spectrum attention module, which could capture the long-term dependency without losing the universal approximating power. Additionally, we have developed a dynamic spectrum weight generation layer that serves as an adaptive spectrum band selector, accentuating critical frequency bands and suppressing less relevant ones. Consequently, this method significantly improves underwater image quality by addressing both high- and low-frequency distortions. Our extensive ablation studies and comparative evaluations consolidate the Dynamic SpectraFormer's efficacy across multiple underwater image enhancement benchmarks. The source code is available at https://github.com/arifence2024/DynamicSpectraFormer.git.

</details>


### [34] [VA-Judger: Reward Modeling from Human Preference Feedback for Joint Video-Audio Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18607)
*Yinming Huang,Shuyuan Tu,Xi Yan,Zihan Yang,Jianhua Han,Xu Hang,Yu-Gang Jiang,Zuxuan Wu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces VA-Judger, a chain-of-thought omni-reward model for joint video-audio generation, and VAPref-10K/VA-Judger-Bench datasets to align post-training rewards with human preferences, addressing reward hacking and coherence across text prompts, video, and audio.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metric-based rewards for joint video-audio generation fail to capture overall semantic and temporal coherence with human preferences, leading to reward hacking and incoherence.

Method: 1) Create VAPref-10K: a large-scale human-preference dataset (9K prompts, 10.3K fine-grained paired comparisons) for joint video-audio generation. 2) Propose VA-Judger-Bench to evaluate alignment of reward models with human preferences (in-domain and out-of-domain). 3) Propose VA-Judger: a chain-of-thought omni-reward model that (a) learns from easy pairs to shape output and coarse preferences, (b) distills explanations for harder near-quality pairs via rejection sampling with human verification, (c) performs dimension-wise RL decomposing feedback into individual quality dimensions for dense rewards.

Result: VA-Judger outperforms metric baselines in predicting human preferences on in-domain and out-of-domain data; using its rewards improves post-training generation quality for audio-video models.

Conclusion: Human-aligned, dimension-wise reward modeling via VA-Judger yields better alignment with human preferences and improves joint video-audio generation quality, mitigating reward hacking and promoting coherence.

Abstract: Using reinforcement learning to post-train joint video-audio generation models requires a reward signal. Existing methods construct this reward by combining metrics for individual quality dimensions, including audio quality, visual fidelity, and synchronization. However, these metrics evaluate perceptual dimensions separately and fail to capture the overall semantic and temporal coherence among the text prompt, video, and audio that shapes human preferences. Optimizing models against these metrics encourages reward hacking, generating video-audio content that achieves high scores on these metrics yet appears incoherent or unfaithful to human viewers. To address this problem, we first construct a large-scale human-preference dataset VAPref-10K for joint video-audio generation, comprising 9K prompts and 10.3K fine-grained paired comparisons from open-source generation models. We also introduce the VA-Judger-Bench benchmark with both in-domain and out-of-domain model comparisons to evaluate whether reward models truly align with human preferences. We further propose VA-Judger, a chain-of-thought omni-reward model for joint video-audio generation. In particular, VA-Judger first learns from pairs with clear quality gaps to establish structured output and coarse preference discrimination, then distills reliable preference explanations for harder near-quality comparisons via rejection sampling verified against human annotations, and finally performs dimension-wise reinforcement learning that decomposes human feedback into individual quality dimensions for denser reward signals than a single binary preference label. Experiments show that VA-Judger outperforms metric baselines in predicting human preferences on both in-domain and out-of-domain evaluations. Using its human-aligned rewards for post-training audio-video generation model also yields significant improvements in generation quality.

</details>


### [35] [CDGP: Contrastive Dual Gaussian Processes for Weakly Supervised Anomaly Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18614)
*Seungjun Chu,Seokhee Han,Mateusz Nowak,Peter Chin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CDGP introduces a weakly supervised, contrastive Gaussian-process framework for industrial anomaly segmentation that jointly models normal- and anomaly-inducing variables over dense tokens; uses a posterior-dominance statistic to produce spatial evidence and image-level confidence, achieving state-of-the-art localization on several benchmarks without pixel-level annotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Pixel-level defect masks are costly to collect at scale, and unusual-but-normal regions can look defective under simple deviation-based scoring. There is a need for uncertainty-aware, weakly supervised localization that can differentiate true defects from normal variations.

Method: Propose Contrastive Dual Gaussian Processes (CDGP). Model predictive distributions over dense tokens for normal and anomaly-inducing variables using Gaussian processes with a linear-kernel component. Compute a posterior-dominance statistic that standardizes the predictive-mean difference by joint predictive uncertainty, yielding spatial evidence and image-level confidence. Combine this evidence with hierarchical normal-reconstruction residuals for precise localization. Calibration relies solely on training data, with no pixel annotations or test-time fitting. Ablate with factorized and matched linear-head controls to assess the role of the GP formulation.

Result: CDGP ranks first among evaluated methods on all MVTec AD 2 localization metrics and is first-place or competitive on KSDD2 and VisA, demonstrating strong localization performance under weak supervision. The ablations quantify the contribution of the linear-kernel GP component.

Conclusion: CDGP offers a principled, uncertainty-aware, weakly supervised approach to dense anomaly localization that avoids test-time fitting and achieves strong cross-dataset performance. The linear-kernel GP formulation and posterior-dominance statistics are key drivers of performance, with calibration relying entirely on training data. Future work could explore richer kernels, scalability improvements, and extensions to other domains.

Abstract: Industrial visual inspection must both decide whether a product is defective and localize the defect, yet pixel-level masks are costly to collect at scale. Most anomaly-segmentation methods learn only from defect-free images and score deviations from normality. A true defect and an unusual-but-normal region, however, can both deviate substantially and receive similarly high scores. We propose Contrastive Dual Gaussian Processes (CDGP), a weakly supervised framework that models normal and anomaly inducing-variable predictive distributions over dense tokens. Its posterior-dominance statistic standardizes their predictive-mean difference by the joint predictive uncertainty, providing both spatial evidence and image-level confidence. This evidence complements hierarchical normal-reconstruction residuals for fine localization. All calibration uses training data only, without human pixel annotations or test-time fitting. Across MVTec AD~2, KSDD2, and VisA, CDGP ranks first among the evaluated methods on all MVTec AD~2 localization metrics and is first-place or competitive on KSDD2 and VisA. Factorized and matched linear-head controls delimit the contribution and scope of the linear-kernel Gaussian process (GP) formulation.

</details>


### [36] [SPARC: Slice-to-volume Pipeline for Automated Reconstruction of gated 3D+time fetal Cardiac MRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18616)
*Arnaud Boutillon,Naomi Clarke,Tomas Woodgate,Daniel West,Alina Schneider,Rachael Franklin,Anthony Price,Jo Hajnal,Kuberan Pushparajah,David Lloyd,Maria Deprez*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SPARC is a physics-informed slice-to-volume reconstruction pipeline for 3D+time fetal cardiac MRI from 2D+time slices. It fuses Doppler ultrasound gated stacks with deep learning-based thoracic segmentation and anatomical reorientation, delivering ~10x faster reconstructions and robust automatic processing suitable for clinical use; available as a Docker container.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconstructing 3D+time fetal cardiac volumes is hampered by unpredictable fetal motion and lack of robust, automated clinical-grade tools. A fast, reliable, end-to-end solution is needed to enable widespread clinical deployment.

Method: Physics-informed slice-to-volume reconstruction (SVR) of Doppler ultrasound–gated stacks, aided by deep learning: thoracic segmentation via ensemble DL and anatomical reorientation. The pipeline, SPARC, is fully automated and deployed as a Docker container; tested on a large clinical cohort.

Result: SVR reduced reconstruction time to 4.8±1.0 min vs 49.0±14.1 min (p<0.0001). Thoracic segmentation Dice 84.7±3.9% (ensemble) vs 81.4±7.7% inter-rater (p<0.05). Anatomical reorientation success 90.1%. On n=121 held-out cases, fully automatic processing achieved 82.6% with mean runtime 7.1±1.3 min. The SPARC pipeline is publicly available via Docker and deployed clinically as a research tool.

Conclusion: SPARC provides a fast, automatic, clinically viable solution for fCMR reconstruction, combining physics-based SVR with DL-based segmentation and orientation, and is openly available for deployment and further validation.

Abstract: Fetal cardiac MRI (fCMR) provides valuable diagnostic information complementary to echocardiography, particularly for complex congenital heart disease (CHD). Dynamic cine imaging captures cardiac motion essential for assessment of cardiac function; however, the reconstruction of 3D+time cine volumes from 2D+time acquired slices remains challenging due to unpredictable fetal motion and the absence of automated and robust processing tools suitable for clinical deployment. We present the SPARC pipeline (Slice-to-volume Pipeline for Automated Reconstruction of gated 3D+time fetal Cardiac MRI) which combines physics-informed slice-to-volume reconstruction (SVR) of Doppler ultrasound (DUS) gated stacks of slices, assisted by deep learning (DL) models for thoracic segmentation and anatomical reorientation. The proposed SVR algorithm achieves a tenfold reduction in reconstruction time relative to existing frame-wise approaches ($4.8 \pm 1.0$ vs $49.0 \pm 14.1$ min, $p < 0.0001$) while improving the reconstruction quality. Thoracic segmentation performance using ensemble aggregation exceeded inter-rater agreement (Dice $84.7 \pm 3.9\%$ vs $81.4 \pm 7.7\%$, $p<0.05$), while anatomical reorientation achieved a success rate of $90.1\%$. End-to-end evaluation on a large held-out clinical cohort ($n = 121$) demonstrated fully automatic processing in $82.6\%$ of cases with a mean runtime of $7.1 \pm 1.3$ min, compatible with clinical deployment. The complete SPARC pipeline is publicly available as a Docker container https://hub.docker.com/r/aboutill/sparc and is currently deployed at our institution as a clinical research tool.

</details>


### [37] [PALATE: Personalized Aesthetic Learning through Adaptive Taste Evolution for Multi-User Portrait Retouching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18622)
*Jingxuan Wang,Yifan Mei,Yuxia Niu,Chaowan Jiao,Qijin Shen*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: PALATE introduces a personalized reward-evolution framework for portrait retouching that personalizes among retouched candidates without retraining editors, achieving strong accuracy and low per-user cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Aesthetics are subjective; population-wide standards fail to capture individual taste, and per-user retraining is costly.

Method: A fixed image editor with a three-level personalization: a global backbone, category-level residuals for similar users, and a lightweight user adapter. Anti-collapse regularizers maintain complementarity. A cyclic dual-level distillation distills user preferences into category rewards and then consolidates category knowledge into the global backbone across evolution rounds, enabling unseen users to calibrate from few rankings. Each new user uses ~512 bytes of parameters with millisecond scoring.

Result: On expert-retouched candidates from PPR10K with held-out users/images, PALATE achieves 72.83% pairwise preference-prediction accuracy, surpassing all baselines (strongest PickScore at 58.06%). Efficiency: 512 bytes per user, millisecond-level scoring.

Conclusion: Personalized reward evolution enables scalable, data-efficient customization of portrait editing, improving cross-user calibration and outpacing prior reward/aesthetic baselines; unseen users can be calibrated quickly with minimal data and computation.

Abstract: Automatic portrait retouching has advanced rapidly, yet its objective is inherently subjective: the same portrait admits multiple professionally valid results, and users disagree about which one is best. Most existing methods optimize a population-level aesthetic standard and therefore cannot capture individual taste, while fine-tuning a separate editing model for every user incurs prohibitive training, storage, and data costs. We propose PALATE, a shared reward-evolution framework that keeps the image editor fixed and instead personalizes the selection among retouched candidates of the same source portrait. PALATE decomposes the reward for each user into a global backbone shared by all users, category-level residuals shared by aesthetically similar users, and a lightweight user adapter, with anti-collapse regularizers keeping the three levels complementary.A cyclic dual-level distillation scheme first distills user-specific preferences into category rewards and then consolidates the resulting category-level knowledge into the global backbone, which is redistributed to initialize the next evolution round. In this way, the shared initialization improves progressively across rounds, enabling unseen users to be calibrated from only a few rankings. On expert-retouched candidates from PPR10K with held-out users and held-out images, PALATE attains 72.83% pairwise preference-prediction accuracy, surpassing all reward, aesthetic, and image-quality baselines, of which the strongest, PickScore, reaches 58.06%. Each new user costs only 512 bytes of user-specific parameters and millisecond-level scoring.

</details>


### [38] [Evaluation of Image Matching Methods for Visual Odometry on UAVs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18624)
*Gašper Spagnolo,Luka Čehovin Zajc,Matej Dobrevski*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Downward-facing VO for UAVs was evaluated with recent image-matching methods; RoMa yields best results, but SIFT still outperforms some SOTA methods in this setting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNSS outages motivate relying on vision-based navigation for UAVs. Assess how modern image-matching methods perform within a VO pipeline on UAV data, highlighting potential gaps between perception advances and VO accuracy.

Method: Experimental evaluation comparing recent deep-learning image matchers (e.g., RoMa) against SIFT within a UAV VO setup using a downward-facing camera on a synthetic dataset; performance measured for UAV position tracking.

Result: RoMa provides the best matching performance among the evaluated methods, but traditional SIFT remains competitive and can outperform some newer methods in certain scenarios.

Conclusion: Vision-based VO for UAVs is viable, with RoMa showing promise; however, traditional features like SIFT can still outperform some DL-based matchers. Further work should test on real data, assess robustness, and explore integration into full VO pipelines.

Abstract: Unmanned aerial vehicles (UAVs) are becoming a powerful tool for many environmental monitoring and transport applications. Yet, their reliance on Global Navigation Satellite System (GNSS) technology for navigation makes them susceptible to catastrophic failures in scenarios where the positioning signal is unavailable or disrupted. This work explores Visual Odometry (VO) as a crucial navigation component. Recently, numerous deep-learning-based methods for image matching have been proposed that are yet to be implemented in a fully-fledged VO system. In this paper, we evaluate recent state-of-the-art image matching methods for the task of VO for UAV position tracking, with a downwards-facing camera, on our synthetic dataset, and find that while the best results are generated by the recent RoMa matcher, SIFT features can outperform some recent state-of-the-art.

</details>


### [39] [PCQA-R1: Advancing Generalized 3D Point Cloud Quality Assessment with Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18627)
*Kangning Ye,Yunhao Li,Sijing Wu,Yucheng Zhu,Guangtao Zhai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RL-based LMM PCQA-R1 improves cross-dataset PCQA by modeling quality understanding and scoring with chain-of-thought prompts and Gaussian calibration, using GRPO and a cold-start PCQA-CoT dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No-reference PCQA is essential for assessing point-cloud quality, yet large multimodal models (LMMs) have not been well explored in this domain. Existing LMM approaches rely on supervised fine-tuning to predict absolute MOS, which struggles to generalize across datasets with heterogeneous MOS scales and limited annotations. Relative quality ranking is more stable under distribution shifts, but prior work lacks a framework that learns quality understanding and scoring with cross-dataset robustness. The paper addresses this gap by developing an RL-based LMM that emphasizes ranking and calibrated scoring across datasets.

Method: Proposes PCQA-R1, a reinforcement-learning LMM built on group relative policy optimization (GRPO). It creates a chain-of-thought dataset (PCQA-CoT) via reverse reasoning to bootstrap cold-start training of the LMM’s reasoning process. Introduces a Gaussian proximity reward to anchor score predictions within the source MOS range, mitigating calibration drift. Training leverages ranking signals and reasoning traces to learn both quality understanding and scoring.

Result: PCQA-R1 achieves state-of-the-art cross-dataset generalization across five benchmarks and maintains competitive in-domain accuracy. Ablation studies demonstrate the effectiveness of ranking, the Gaussian proximity reward, and cold-start traces in boosting performance.

Conclusion: The combination of ranking-focused objectives, reasoning traces, and calibration-friendly rewards enables a robust RL-based LMM for PCQA, yielding strong cross-dataset generalization. The approach highlights the value of chain-of-thought data and score anchoring for no-reference PCQA and suggests further exploration of cross-domain LMM calibration and richer relative-judgment supervision.

Abstract: No-reference point cloud quality assessment (PCQA) has been an active topic in recent years and is used to measure and optimize the visual experience of point clouds. However, large multimodal models (LMMs) have rarely been explored in this area. Previous LMM-based methods mainly rely on supervised fine-tuning to directly predict numerical quality scores, lacking the ability to generalize across datasets with heterogeneous MOS scales and limited annotations. A key difficulty is that absolute MOS regression can be brittle across datasets with different score scales and distortion distributions, whereas relative quality ranking is more stable under such shifts. In this paper, we present PCQA-R1, the first reinforcement learning LMM for 3D point cloud quality assessment to simultaneously model quality understanding and scoring. Built upon the group relative policy optimization (GRPO) strategy, PCQA-R1 first constructs a chain-of-thought dataset, PCQA-CoT, which serves as cold-start training data through a reverse reasoning strategy that teaches the LMM to generate its reasoning process. We further introduce a Gaussian proximity reward that prevents calibration drift by anchoring score predictions to the source MOS range. Experimental results demonstrate that PCQA-R1 achieves state-of-the-art cross-dataset generalization across five benchmarks and competitive in-domain accuracy. Ablation studies support the role of ranking, Gaussian reward, and cold-start traces.

</details>


### [40] [When Safety Overrides Vision: Exploring Dynamics between Vision Influence and Safety Alignment in Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18628)
*Mehak Gupta,Tanmoy Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Safety alignment doesn't erase grounding; abstention arises from late-stage dynamics; targeted suppression of refusal can restore grounded answering.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand whether safety constraints suppress perceptual grounding or simply redirect generation; characterize internal decoding and interventions across architectures.

Method: Cross-architecture empirical study on multimodal benchmarks; decoding dynamics analysis; activation-level interventions to suppress refusal representations without retraining or changing inputs.

Result: Visual evidence continues to influence decoding even when refusals occur; grounding largely preserved; refusal representations differ across architectures; safety shifts late-stage hidden states toward refusal; suppression of refusal representations restores grounded answers.

Conclusion: Safety alignment can override grounded expression despite preserved perceptual evidence; reveals a failure mode in aligned VLMs; suggests mitigation via activation-level interventions; potentially transferable across models.

Abstract: Aligned vision-language models (VLMs) are designed to balance grounded visual reasoning with safe generation behavior. However, we observe a striking phenomenon: under safety-constrained instruction, models frequently abstain from answering questions that remain correctly answerable under default instruction despite receiving identical image-question inputs. This raises a fundamental question: does safety alignment suppress perceptual grounding itself, or does visual evidence remain internally available while generation is redirected toward abstention? In this work, we investigate the internal decoding dynamics underlying safety-induced abstention in aligned VLMs. Across multiple architectures and multimodal benchmarks, we show that abstained generations remain consistently influenced by visual evidence throughout decoding, indicating that perceptual grounding is largely preserved despite refusal behavior. We further demonstrate that, although the representational organization of refusal differs substantially across architectures, safety-constrained instruction consistently alters late-stage hidden-state dynamics toward refusal-oriented decoding. Finally, through targeted activation-level interventions, we show that suppressing refusal-related representations reliably restores grounded answering behavior across models without retraining or modifying visual inputs. Together, these findings reveal a previously underexplored failure mode in aligned VLMs: safety alignment can override grounded visual expression even when perceptual evidence remains internally preserved.

</details>


### [41] [SAM2Dual: Training-Free, Dual Memory for Long-Term Video Object Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18640)
*JeongRae Kim,Changwon Lim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes SAM2Dual, a training-free inference-time enhancement for long-term video object segmentation, using Dual Memory and Text-Aware Memory to boost stability without fine-tuning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-term VOS suffers error accumulation due to extended occlusions, re-appearance, and scene changes; SAM2's zero-shot performance can drift over long horizons because streaming memory accumulates unreliable predictions.

Method: Dual Memory: explicitly separates short-term memory for rapid local adaptation and long-term memory built via interval-based sampling to preserve global identity cues, fused through a gated mechanism. Text-Aware Memory (TAM): extracts compact word-level cues from early frames and uses text embeddings to reweight memory contributions based on semantic compatibility, aiding identity preservation when visual cues are weak.

Result: On MOSEv2, J&F improves from 49.33 to 50.65; LVOSv2 shows consistent gains, demonstrating improved stability on long videos without model fine-tuning.

Conclusion: SAM2Dual delivers training-free, plug-and-play robustness enhancements for long-video VOS by combining a dual-memory architecture with semantic reweighting, yielding tangible gains in long-horizon scenarios.

Abstract: Long-term video object segmentation (VOS) remains challenging due to error accumulation under extended occlusions, re-appearance, and scene changes. Although SAM2 provides strong zero-shot performance, its streaming memory can amplify drift over long horizons when recent, unreliable predictions dominate the memory state. We propose SAM2Dual, a training-free, plug-and-play inference-time enhancement that improves long-video robustness without updating model weights. SAM2Dual introduces a Dual Memory design that explicitly separates (i) short-term memory for rapid local adaptation and (ii) long-term memory built via interval-based sampling to preserve global identity cues, combined through a gated fusion strategy. In addition, we present Text-Aware Memory (TAM), which extracts a compact word-level cue from early frames and uses text embeddings to reweight memory contributions based on semantic compatibility, supporting identity preservation when visual evidence becomes weak or ambiguous. Across long-term benchmarks, SAM2Dual consistently improves stability on long videos, raising J&F from 49.33 to 50.65 on MOSEv2 and achieving consistent gains on LVOSv2.

</details>


### [42] [Clinically Structured Surrogate Rewards for Post-SFT Medical Image Captioning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18654)
*Hyun Jun Kim,Heeseung Shin,Changwon Lim*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Structured surrogate rewards for post-SFT medical image captioning using distributional image-neighborhood alignment and clinical graph consistency, combined with semantic/lexical fidelity, yielding improvements in Overall, Relevance, and Factuality across backbones and tracks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical image captions must be factually correct and clinically consistent; traditional rewards based on surface similarity or generic alignment fail to capture clinical claim structure and local visual neighborhoods. A structured reward framework targets both local lexical fidelity and high-level clinical entities and relations.

Method: Four independently normalized rewards are proposed: semantic/lexical fidelity, distributional image-neighborhood alignment (matching the distributions induced by reference and generated captions on an image bank), and clinical graph consistency (maximum-weight one-to-one matching among entities, assertion states, and typed relations). These are combined with fixed relative weights and optimized via GDPO (gradient-based policy optimization). Evaluations span Standard and Synthetical ImageCLEFmedical Caption tracks across three vision-language backbones (six backbone-track combos).

Result: The method achieves improvements in Overall, Relevance, and Factuality over matched SFT baselines across all six backbone-track combinations, with average relative gains of 3.4%, 2.1%, and 5.8%, respectively. Ablation studies show the structured rewards provide complementary signals, reducing image-neighborhood divergence and improving entity-assertion-relation consistency.

Conclusion: Structured rewards that encode image-neighborhood distribution and clinical graph consistency, alongside semantic/lexical fidelity, yield consistent gains in factuality and relevance for medical image captioning across diverse backbones and evaluation tracks, validating the benefit of a clinically structured surrogate reward framework.

Abstract: Medical image captioning requires translating heterogeneous visual evidence into concise clinical descriptions, where errors in findings, assertion states, or anatomical relations can alter clinical meaning despite surface-level fluency. Sequence-level policy optimization can directly optimize complete captions, but common rewards rely on global text similarity, direct image-caption compatibility, or unordered concept overlap, leaving visual neighborhoods and clinical-claim structure implicit. We propose a clinically structured surrogate reward framework for post-SFT medical image captioning. The framework combines biomedical semantic and short-range lexical fidelity with two structured rewards: distributional image-neighborhood alignment, which matches the medical-image-bank distributions induced by reference and generated captions, and clinical graph consistency, which applies maximum-weight one-to-one matching to entities, assertion states, and typed relations. The four rewards are independently normalized within each rollout group, combined with fixed relative weights, and optimized with GDPO. Across organizer-evaluated hidden test sets for the Standard and Synthetical ImageCLEFmedical Caption tracks and three vision-language backbones, the method improves Overall, Relevance, and Factuality over matched SFT baselines in all six backbone-track combinations, with average relative gains of 3.4%, 2.1%, and 5.8%, respectively. Ablations and paired diagnostics indicate that the structured rewards provide complementary signals, reducing image-neighborhood divergence and improving entity-assertion-relation consistency.

</details>


### [43] [Teeth2Point: A Two-Stage Dental CBCT ROI-to-Point Segmentation Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18667)
*Qi Ma,Shipra Jain,Niko Benjamin Huber,Ender Konukoglu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Teeth2Point introduces a point-based transformer for dental CBCT semantic segmentation to overcome patch-based attention trade-offs by localizing tooth ROIs, converting them into point tokens via adaptive sampling, and pretraining with domain-tailored self-supervised learning (DINO-like) before supervised fine-tuning, achieving notable gains over strong baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense patch-based self-attention on high-resolution CBCT is constrained by computational limits, forcing a trade-off between global context and fine-grained detail. Accurate tooth labeling, even with missing/malpositioned teeth, requires both global anatomical context and high-resolution local information.

Method: Localize volumetric ROIs around teeth with a CNN; convert ROIs into point tokens using adaptive sampling; process tokens with a transformer to produce segmentations; initialize with SSL pretraining (DINO-style) using domain-specific augmentations, then fine-tune supervised on labeled data.

Result: Outperforms the strongest two-stage baseline by 1.44 DSC points on average across four datasets and surpasses the first-stage nnU-Net by 1.9 points in DSC.

Conclusion: A sparse, ROI-focused, point-based transformer with SSL pretraining can capture global context without sacrificing resolution in CBCT tooth segmentation, offering improved robustness to anatomical variation and abnormal cases.

Abstract: Modern deep learning architectures have demonstrated strong performance in dental CBCT segmentation. One remaining crucial challenge is accurate tooth labeling in cases with missing or malpositioned teeth, which are highly relevant for dental practice. Transformer-based architectures should in theory be able to resolve such ambiguities using global anatomical context. However, due to the high resolution of CBCT volumes and the wide spatial distribution of teeth within volumes, dense patch-based volumetric processing faces an inherent trade-off. Computational costs limit the number of patches that can be used in self-attention and thus, one can either increase the extent of the context captured in self-attention or capture fine-grained structural details by using small patches, but not both. In this work, we present Teeth2Point, an efficient point-based transformer framework for dental CBCT semantic segmentation that can avoid this trade-off. Teeth2Point first localizes volumetric regions of interest (ROIs) surrounding teeth using a convolutional model, then converts ROIs into point tokens using adaptive sampling. A transformer model predicts accurate segmentations using the point tokens, which allow capturing global context while retaining high resolution. The transformer is first pretrained using self-supervised learning (SSL), in the style of DINO but using domain-specific augmentation strategies, followed by supervised finetuning. The SSL pretraining, which includes random token masking, provides robustness to complex anatomical variations. Compared with the strongest two-stage baseline, Teeth2Point improves abnormal-case performance by 1.44 DSC points on average across four datasets; relative to the first-stage nnU-Net, the gain is 1.9 points.

</details>


### [44] [Vision-Language Models for Egocentric Video: From Hand-Object Interaction to Embodied AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18671)
*Mohammad Zamani,Fatemeh Ziaeetabar*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Survey of vision-language models (VLMs) for egocentric video understanding; highlights progress, gaps, and priorities for embodied AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address egocentric vision challenges (ego-motion, occlusion, long-range temporal dependencies) by leveraging multimodal foundation models; bridge first-person perception with actionable, embodied intelligence.

Method: Critical review organizing literature by tasks, datasets, hand-object interaction understanding, temporal reasoning, frame/clip selection, multimodal representation learning, prompting, semantic alignment, and model adaptation; emphasis on graph-based and object-centric reasoning for modeling relations over time; discussion of applications to wearable assistance, robot skill learning, and H-to-R transfer; synthesize limitations and priorities.

Result: Identifies that current models better recognize visible objects than evolving interactions and user intent across long activities; summarizes trends toward multimodal and foundation models; highlights gaps in temporal grounding, interaction-aware supervision, long-video efficiency, graph-enhanced representations, cross-domain generalization, privacy, and evaluation; proposes key research priorities for deployable embodied intelligence.

Conclusion: VLMs are promising foundations for egocentric understanding, but substantial challenges remain, requiring temporally grounded, interaction-aware, and privacy-conscious approaches; future work should focus on robust long-video perception, relational reasoning, and trustworthy evaluation to enable embodied AI applications.

Abstract: Egocentric video captures activities from the wearer's perspective, providing a direct view of human attention, hand--object interaction, and goal-directed behavior. This perspective is increasingly important for wearable intelligence, assistive systems, human--robot interaction, and embodied AI, yet it introduces challenges including ego-motion, occlusion, small active objects, viewpoint-dependent appearance, and long-range temporal dependencies. Vision--language models (VLMs) offer a promising foundation for addressing these challenges by linking visual observations with semantic knowledge and natural-language supervision. This survey presents a critical review of VLMs for egocentric video understanding, tracing the progression from conventional recognition architectures to multimodal foundation models and embodied systems. We organize the literature around tasks, datasets, hand--object interaction understanding, temporal reasoning, frame and clip selection, multimodal representation learning, prompting, semantic alignment, and model adaptation. Particular attention is given to graph-based and object-centric reasoning as mechanisms for modeling relations among hands, objects, actions, and scene context over time. We further examine how first-person perception and multimodal foundation models support wearable assistance, robot skill learning, human-to-robot transfer, and embodied decision making. Across the reviewed literature, a consistent limitation emerges: current models recognize visible objects more reliably than evolving interactions, actions, and user intent, especially over long activities. We therefore identify temporally grounded reasoning, interaction-aware supervision, efficient long-video processing, multimodal fusion, graph-enhanced representations, cross-domain generalization, privacy, and trustworthy evaluation as key priorities for deployable embodied intelligence.

</details>


### [45] [DynCur-Geo: Dynamic Curiosity Reward Shaping for Multimodal Active Geo-Localization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18673)
*Yiming Sun,Yang Zhang,Pengfei Zhu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DynCur-Geo introduces a distance-aware dynamic curiosity mechanism for active geo-localization in UAVs, adjusting intrinsic rewards by remaining target distance, with a distance gate and potential-based shaping to balance exploration and convergence, yielding improved performance across varied settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In active geo-localization, balancing exploration with target convergence is hard due to multimodal cues, restricted views, and sparse feedback; fixed intrinsic rewards can keep rewarding novelty after nearing the target, causing detours.

Method: Dynamic intrinsic reward as function of remaining distance; distance-aware gate to encourage early exploration and shift to goal-directed near target; potential-based reward shaping for dense progress guidance.

Result: Consistent gains over baselines across multimodal, cross-scene, disaster-affected, and long-range settings in active geo-localization tasks.

Conclusion: A distance-aware, dynamic curiosity framework can improve search efficiency and target convergence in UAV geo-localization by modulating exploration with proximity to the goal; suggests broader applicability to goal-directed exploration problems.

Abstract: Active geo-localization enables low-altitude UAVs to search for specified targets from limited local aerial observations, supporting time-sensitive applications such as search and rescue and emergency inspection. However, multimodal target cues, restricted views, and sparse feedback make it difficult to balance exploration with target convergence. Existing curiosity-driven methods assign a fixed intrinsic-reward weight throughout search, which can continue rewarding novelty after the agent nears the target and induce detours. We propose DynCur-Geo, a dynamic curiosity framework that adjusts prediction-error intrinsic reward according to remaining target distance. A distance-aware gate encourages early exploration and shifts the policy toward goal-directed behavior near the target, while potential-based reward shaping supplies dense progress guidance. Experiments across multimodal, cross-scene, disaster-affected, and long-range settings show consistent gains over active geo-localization baselines.

</details>


### [46] [FRAGMENT: Factorized Graph Representations for Document Generation and Editing via Entity-Aware Transformations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18679)
*Ayoub El Bouchtili,Guilhaume Leroy-Meline*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: FRAGMENT is a two-stage factorized graph generative model for documents that jointly models structure and content, enabling tractable likelihoods for forgery detection and prompt-guided editing; evaluated on DocLayNet, FUNSD, SROIE against autoregressive, layout-only, and graph baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Structured documents exhibit intertwined spatial layout, text, and logical structure. Pixel/token-level generative models struggle to capture these dependencies and provide limited anomaly signals. A factorized graph representation aims for explicit modeling of relations and content to improve generation, detection, and editing.

Method: Stage Architect: a causally masked Transformer conditioned on document category; autoregressively generates the graph topology and typed spatial relations. Stage Builder: a GATv2-based graph network that enriches the graph with normalized bounding boxes, text, and visual/style attributes. Both stages define explicit likelihoods, yielding a tractable document-level likelihood for forgery detection. For editing, a prompt-conditioned extension injects instruction embeddings into the Builder via cross-attention to enable semantic and entity-aware modifications. Training on DocLayNet; fine-tuning on FUNSD and SROIE. Evaluation on DocLayNet, FUNSD, SROIE against autoregressive, layout-only, and graph-based baselines, with analysis of trade-offs.

Result: Demonstrates a factorized graph generation framework with tractable likelihoods and editing capabilities; shows empirical performance trends and trade-offs across datasets and baselines, highlighting when the factorized approach provides advantages in modeling structure-content interactions and in forgery detection.

Conclusion: FRAGMENT provides a modular, likelihood-based approach to document generation that captures the coupling of structure and content, enabling forgery detection and controlled editing. It offers advantages over single-stage autoregressive or layout-only methods, at the cost of increased model and training complexity, with demonstrated effectiveness on standard document datasets.

Abstract: Structured documents such as invoices, forms, reports, and scientific articles derive meaning from the interplay between spatial layout, textual content, and logical structure. Generative models operating at the pixel or token level often struggle to capture these dependencies effectively. We explore FRAGMENT, a generative framework that represents a document as a typed relational graph and factorizes its distribution as p(structure, content) = p(structure) * p(content | structure). The framework consists of two stages. The first stage, the Architect, is a causally masked Transformer conditioned on document category that autoregressively generates the graph topology and typed spatial relations. The second stage, the Builder, is a GATv2-based graph attention network that enriches the graph with normalized bounding boxes, text, and visual style attributes. Both stages define explicit likelihood models, yielding a tractable document-level likelihood that serves as an anomaly score for forgery detection. For controlled editing, a prompt-conditioned extension injects instruction embeddings into the Builder through cross-attention, enabling semantic and entity-aware modifications. We describe training on DocLayNet and fine-tuning on FUNSD and SROIE. Experiments on DocLayNet, FUNSD, and SROIE evaluate FRAGMENT alongside representative autoregressive, layout-only, and graph-based baselines, providing an empirical analysis of the characteristics and trade-offs of the proposed factorized graph generation framework.

</details>


### [47] [DocClaw: A Unified Agentic System for Intelligent Document Processing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18685)
*Siqi Xiang,Zhipeng Xu,Yufei Liu,Junhao Ji,Qing Liu,Zulong Chen,Zhibo Yang,Chunyan Miao,Shijian Lu*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: DocClaw proposes a unified agentic framework for diverse intelligent document processing tasks (OCR, DocQA, KIE) that uses an agent–document interaction loop, a shared document state, and task-specific skills to achieve competitive results across benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address fragmentation and inefficiencies of task-specific IDP pipelines by introducing a single, reusable framework that can handle multiple tasks through shared interactions, tools, and document state, enabling progressive refinement of information.

Method: Define a shared interaction loop where an agent, given a document and a task query, selects an appropriate document skill, iteratively identifies required information, invokes relevant tools, and integrates observations into the final output. A structured document state stores reusable knowledge and task context, allowing accumulation and revision of information. Task requirements are expressed via the agent’s interpretation of the query and chosen skill; the loop, tools, and state are shared across tasks.

Result: Empirical evaluation across multiple IDP benchmarks shows DocClaw can handle diverse tasks within one framework and achieves competitive performance compared with both general-purpose vision-language models and task-specific methods.

Conclusion: A unified agentic framework can effectively unify IDP tasks under a single system, leveraging shared interaction loops, tools, and state to deliver strong performance and flexibility across OCR, DocQA, and KIE tasks.

Abstract: Intelligent document processing (IDP) encompasses a broad range of tasks, including optical character recognition (OCR), document question answering (DocQA), and key information extraction (KIE). Despite their distinct objectives, these tasks share a common need to perceive document content, acquire task-relevant information, and progressively refine intermediate results. However, they are typically formulated as separate prediction problems and addressed by task-specific models or processing pipelines. We introduce DocClaw, a unified agentic system that formulates diverse intelligent document processing tasks as a shared process of interaction between an agent and a document. Given a document and a task-specific query, DocClaw follows an appropriate document skill to iteratively identify the information required, invoke relevant tools, and integrate the resulting observations into the desired output. Throughout this process, a structured document state organizes reusable document knowledge and task-specific interaction context, allowing the agent to accumulate, revisit, and progressively refine information as the interaction proceeds. Under this formulation, task-specific requirements are captured by the agent's interpretation of the query objective and the corresponding document skill, while the underlying interaction loop, tool space, and document state are shared across tasks. Extensive experiments across multiple intelligent document processing benchmarks demonstrate that DocClaw effectively handles diverse tasks within a single agentic framework and achieves competitive performance compared with both general-purpose VLMs and task-specific methods.

</details>


### [48] [Composed Historical Image Retrieval by Modeling Temporal Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18694)
*Adrià Molina Rodríguez,Oriol Ramos Terrades,Josep Lladós Canet*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TDIR learns temporally decomposable embeddings by separating date and content into orthogonal subspaces; proves when decomposition is possible, quantifies error if not, and reveals emergent orthogonality; enables transitive temporal manipulation and achieves competitive results on historical image retrieval tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explicitly modeling temporal structure in image embeddings without sacrificing retrieval performance; historical archives require combining content with time cues, yet linear time complicates embeddings.

Method: Formulate a joint optimization that factorizes embeddings into orthogonal date/content subspaces; derive conditions for exact decomposition; bound errors under partial satisfaction; show orthogonality emerges; implement transitive operations to transfer temporal info; validate on Composed Image Retrieval with date labels or examples.

Result: Theoretical conditions for decomposition; error analysis; emergent orthogonality from optimization; operational transference of temporal information; empirical validation shows competitive date estimation and object retrieval in a real-world historical photo setting.

Conclusion: TDIR provides interpretable, temporally structured embeddings that support temporal manipulation while maintaining retrieval performance; offers a principled foundation for temporally aware visual search in archives.

Abstract: While time evolves linearly, the geometry of neural embedding spaces is inherently multi-dimensional, often chaotic, and difficult to interpret. In principle, one could constrain an embedding space to a single temporal dimension; however, such a reduction would sacrifice performance on downstream tasks, as one-dimensional embeddings cannot retain sufficient expressive capacity. This paper asks whether it is possible to learn representations that preserve temporal structure while remaining effective for image and object retrieval, and answers this question by building the mathematical foundations of such a system. We propose Temporally Decomposable Image Representations (TDIR), a representation learning algorithm that decomposes historical photographs into separate date and content components through orthogonal subspaces. We define and prove the conditions under which such a decomposition is achievable, characterize the error incurred when those conditions are only partially met, and show that orthogonality between temporal and categorical subspaces emerges naturally from the joint optimization, without requiring it to be imposed explicitly. Beyond its geometric properties, TDIR enables a class of transitive operations on embedding spaces: the temporal information of one image can be extracted and injected into the representation of another, with no label supervision required. All theoretical properties are grounded and validated in the real-world problem of Composed Image Retrieval on historical photographs, where a query simultaneously specifies object content and a target time period, either through labels or through example images. This in-the-wild setting serves as a concrete backing for the propositions we derive, offering an intuitive and interpretable way to navigate photographic archives while maintaining competitive performance in both date estimation and object retrieval.

</details>


### [49] [Impact of Iterative Fine-Tuning on Transcription Accuracy in Complex Historical Sanskrit Manuscripts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18696)
*Kartik Chincholikar,Kaushik Gopalan,Mihir Hasabnis*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A traditional OCR pipeline for handwritten historical manuscripts is iteratively fine-tuned at layout- and appearance-level to adapt to a target manuscript, reducing annotation effort and improving predictions on subsequent pages. The work introduces a dataset with granular layout-level (PAGE-XML) annotations for three Sanskrit manuscripts, benchmarks Multimodal LLMs on the dataset, and releases code and data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Handwritten historical manuscripts exhibit complex, heterogeneous layouts and non-standard appearance due to aging, writing styles, textures, and noise, making OCR difficult. Annotation is expensive and requires domain expertise; there is a need for domain-adaptive, data-efficient OCR pipelines and corresponding datasets to facilitate scholarly access.

Method: A local traditional OCR pipeline that can be iteratively fine-tuned on the target manuscript distribution at both layout-level and appearance-level. The pipeline adapts to the manuscript distribution to improve predictions on subsequent pages, reducing human annotation effort. The authors digitize three complex Sanskrit manuscripts and provide granular layout-level annotations in PAGE-XML with Unicode. They also benchmark leading Multimodal Large Language Models on the introduced dataset and release code and data.

Result: The approach yields quantitative gains with iterative fine-tuning, improving OCR predictions on later pages. A new dataset with granular layout annotations is introduced, and the performance of leading Multimodal LLMs on this dataset is benchmarked. Code and dataset are released.

Conclusion: Iterative adaptation of a traditional OCR pipeline to a manuscript’s layout and appearance distributions can improve OCR accuracy and reduce annotation effort for historical documents. The created dataset offers a resource for future research and serves as a benchmark for integrating traditional OCR with multimodal LLMs.

Abstract: Digitizing the text from handwritten historical manuscripts is required to make them easily accessible, preservable, and to enable historical scholars to study them in new ways. Historical manuscripts, however, often exhibit complex heterogeneous layouts and non-standard appearance due to period-specific writing styles, page textures, camera noise, and other nuisance factors, making them difficult to perform OCR on. To tackle this challenge, we introduce a local traditional OCR pipeline, which can be iteratively fine-tuned on the target manuscript at the layout-level and the appearance-level. By adapting to the target manuscript distribution, the proposed Traditional OCR pipeline makes better predictions on subsequent pages, causing iterative reduction in human annotation effort, which is expensive and time-consuming as it requires historical domain expertise. Using this pipeline, we digitize text from three complex historical Sanskrit manuscripts and introduce a dataset with granular layout-level annotations, along with Unicode annotations in the standard PAGE-XML format.
