We demonstrate quantitative gains due to iterative fine-tuning of the proposed traditional OCR pipeline, and also benchmark the performance of leading Multi-Modal Large Language Models on the introduced Dataset. Code and dataset are available at: https://github.com/flame-cai/gnn-synthetic-layout-historical/.

</details>


### [50] [A Critical Synthesis of Uncertainty Quantification and Foundation Models for Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18709)
*Steven Landgraf,Joceline Hinz,Markus Ulrich*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: This paper systematically evaluates uncertainty quantification (UQ) methods for a semantic-segmentation foundation model, fine-tuning a lightweight DPT decoder on SAM2 and benchmarking Monte Carlo Dropout, Deep Sub-Ensemble, Test-Time Augmentation, and Evidential Deep Learning on Cityscapes, NYUv2, and out-of-domain data. It reveals trade-offs between segmentation accuracy, calibration, uncertainty quality, and inference time, indicating promise and current limitations for robust, efficient deployment of uncertainty-aware foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of interpretability and reliability of foundation models under real-world domain shifts by integrating principled UQ into segmentation models, enhancing safety and trust in critical applications.

Method: Fine-tune a lightweight DPT decoder atop a pretrained SAM2 encoder to create a competitive baseline; evaluate four UQ approaches (Monte Carlo Dropout, Deep Sub-Ensemble, Test-Time Augmentation, Evidential Deep Learning) across Cityscapes, NYUv2, and out-of-domain settings; assess segmentation accuracy, calibration, uncertainty quality, and inference time; analyze cross-domain robustness and efficiency.

Result: The study finds clear trade-offs among predictive performance, reliability (calibration/uncertainty quality), and computational cost; no single method uniformly dominates across metrics; cross-domain results underscore ongoing robustness challenges; overall, uncertainty-aware foundation models show promise but current limitations necessitate further work.

Conclusion: Uncertainty-aware segmentation foundation models are promising for safer deployment, but future work must jointly optimize accuracy, calibration/uncertainty quality, and efficiency, particularly under domain shifts.

Abstract: Foundation models are increasingly breaking what seemed to be impossible not long ago by enabling unprecedented accuracy and cross-domain generalization. Yet their lack of interpretability, tendency to be overconfident, and sensitivity to real-world domain shifts pose critical challenges for safety- and mission-critical applications. Uncertainty quantification (UQ) offers a principled way to address these issues, but its integration into segmentation foundation models has yet to be explored. In this paper we present the first systematic evaluation of UQ methods applied to a foundation model for semantic segmentation. We fine-tune a lightweight DPT decoder on top of the pretrained SAM2 encoder to establish a simple yet competitive baseline and benchmark four representative UQ approaches - Monte Carlo Dropout, Deep Sub-Ensemble, Test-Time Augmentation, and Evidential Deep Learning - across Cityscapes, NYUv2, and two challenging out-of-domain settings. Our analysis compares segmentation accuracy, calibration, uncertainty quality, and inference time, revealing clear trade-offs between predictive performance, reliability, and computational cost. These results highlight both the promise and the current limitations of uncertainty-aware foundation models, pointing to the need for future work that jointly optimizes accuracy, robustness, and efficiency for real-world deployment.

</details>


### [51] [CamWorldQA: Perceptual Quality Assessment of Camera-Controlled World Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18710)
*Yunhe Li,Likun Wu,Sijing Wu,Xinyu Tian,Huiyu Duan,Yixuan Gao,Yunhao Li,Guangtao Zhai*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes CamWorldQA, a perceptual QA benchmark for camera-controlled world video generation, and CWQA, a no-reference model with three branches (spatial, temporal motion, optical flow) to predict quality. Dataset includes 720 videos from 6 methods, 20 sources, 6 trajectories with human scores; CWQA outperforms baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing VQA methods target natural videos and fail to capture camera-controlled generation's unique perceptual cues such as viewpoint consistency, motion coherence, and content preservation.

Method: 1) Build CamWorldQA dataset of 720 generated videos across 6 methods, 20 source videos, and 6 camera trajectories with subjective scores. 2) Propose CWQA, a no-reference quality assessment network with three complementary branches for spatial features, temporal motion features, and optical flow features to predict quality scores.

Result: CWQA achieves superior performance compared to existing quality assessment methods on the CamWorldQA dataset.

Conclusion: The work fills a gap in perceptual VQA for camera-controlled world video generation by providing a dedicated benchmark and a state-of-the-art no-reference QA model, enabling more robust evaluation and future research.

Abstract: Recent advances in generative video models have enabled camera-controlled world video generation, allowing models to synthesize videos under user-defined camera trajectories. However, existing video quality assessment (VQA) methods are mainly developed for natural videos and fail to capture the unique perceptual characteristics of camera-controlled generation, such as viewpoint consistency, motion coherence, and content preservation. In this work, we introduce CamWorldQA, the first benchmark for perceptual quality assessment of camera-controlled world video generation. CamWorldQA contains 720 generated videos produced by 6 representative generation methods from 20 diverse source videos under 6 camera trajectories, where each video is annotated with a human-rated perceptual quality score through subjective experiments. Furthermore, we propose CWQA, a no-reference quality assessment network with three complementary branches that extract spatial features, temporal motion features and optical flow features to jointly predict quality scores. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CWQA achieves superior performance over existing quality assessment methods on the CamWorldQA dataset.

</details>


### [52] [EgoHRV: Continuous Heart Rate Variability Estimation from Egocentric Systems for Autonomic Response and Skill Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18711)
*Berken Utku Demirel,Christian Holz*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces EgoHRV, a method to estimate HR and HRV from egocentric gaze video using a 3D backbone and a low-high frequency decomposition to extract BVP signals, with cross-domain pretraining; achieves state-of-the-art accuracy and improves downstream tasks, code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Egocentric vision systems miss autonomic indicators such as stress and arousal; HRV is a key noninvasive marker of autonomic regulation that is difficult to recover from gaze video due to motion and noise; enabling HRV estimation from egocentric data expands perceptual capabilities.

Method: A 3D backbone network paired with a novel low-high decomposition module to extract blood volume pulse (BVP) signals from gaze video. Cross-domain pretraining aligns frequency-domain representations between contact-based (physiological sensors) and camera-derived signals, providing the temporal precision needed to recover HRV from gaze data.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art accuracy for HR and HRV estimation from egocentric video. An uncertainty-aware design improves downstream behavioral modeling. Integrating HRV estimates into EgoExo4D’s proficiency estimator increases accuracy by 17.8%.

Conclusion: Continuous HRV estimation from egocentric systems enables stress- and arousal-aware tasks, broadening the scope of affective and behavioral analysis in wearable-camera platforms. Code is publicly available.

Abstract: Egocentric vision systems capture human behavior from visible cues, but overlook physiological indicators of autonomic states such as stress, engagement, and attention. Heart rate variability (HRV) is a widely used noninvasive marker of autonomic regulation under stress. HRV reflects small timing differences between successive heartbeats and has so far been out of reach for egocentric platforms, where motion and noise in gaze video mask exactly this fine-grained timing. We propose EgoHRV, a method that estimates HRV as well as heart rate (HR) from the gaze cameras that are already integrated into egocentric headsets. Our pipeline combines a 3D backbone with a novel low--high decomposition module that extracts the blood volume pulse (BVP) signal from gaze video. Our cross-domain pretraining aligns the frequency-domain representations of contact-based and camera-derived signals. This alignment gives EgoHRV the temporal precision to recover HRV from the subtle fluctuations in gaze video. EgoHRV achieves state-of-the-art accuracy for HR and HRV estimation from egocentric video, and its uncertainty-aware design improves downstream behavioral modeling. Integrating our HRV estimates and confidence measures into EgoExo4D's proficiency estimator raises accuracy by 17.8%. Beyond skill, continuous HRV estimation also opens egocentric systems to stress- and arousal-aware estimation tasks. Code: https://github.com/eth-siplab/EgoHRV

</details>


### [53] [The Impact of CutMix on Reliability and Robustness in Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18715)
*Steven Landgraf,Markus Ulrich*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: CutMix barely affects segmentation accuracy but consistently improves reliability and calibration, especially under distribution shifts, across CNN-based and transformer-based segmentation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how CutMix, a common data augmentation, influences reliability, calibration, and uncertainty in dense semantic segmentation tasks, which is critical for safety-critical deployments.

Method: Empirical evaluation using two architectures (CNN-based DeepLabV3+ and transformer-based SegFormer) on in-domain and out-of-domain data, assessing segmentation accuracy, calibration, and uncertainty quality with and without CutMix.

Result: CutMix yields only minor changes in segmentation accuracy but substantially improves reliability-related metrics, notably calibration and uncertainty quality, particularly under distribution shifts.

Conclusion: CutMix primarily enhances the trustworthiness of a model's calibrated confidence and uncertainty estimates rather than its raw segmentation performance; this makes it valuable for safety-critical deployments where reliable confidence estimates are essential.

Abstract: Ensuring not only high accuracy but also reliable and robust predictions is critical for the deployment of semantic segmentation models in safety-critical applications such as autonomous driving. Despite the widespread use of CutMix - a simple yet powerful data augmentation strategy - its effect on the reliability and robustness in dense predictions tasks remains unexplored. Motivated by recent findings that semi-supervised segmentation methods, where CutMix is a core component, can severely degrade reliability, this study isolates and systematically analyzes the influence of CutMix on segmentation accuracy, calibration, and uncertainty quality. We evaluate two representative architectures, the CNN-based DeepLabV3+ and the transformer-based SegFormer, across both in-domain and out-of-domain scenarios. Our results show that CutMix has only a minor impact on segmentation accuracy but consistently improves the reliability, particularly under distribution shifts. These improvements indicate that CutMix primarily enhances the trustworthiness of the model's calibration and uncertainty rather than the raw segmentation prediction itself. This distinction is crucial for safety-critical deployment, where reliable confidence estimates are as important as raw performance.

</details>


### [54] [A Few Cases Are All You Need: An Empirical Study of Annotation-Efficient LoRA Fine-Tuning of MedSAM3](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18731)
*Sachin Dudda Nagaraju,Bendik Skarre Abrahamsen,Ashkan Moradi,Mattijs Elschot*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LoRA-adapted MedSAM3 achieves clinically useful multi-organ segmentation from as few as 10 annotated cases, outperforming data-hungry specialist tools on several metrics and generalizing to cardiac segmentation, with fast per-organ training on a single GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce annotation burden and training time for medical segmentation by leveraging large foundation models via parameter-efficient adaptation; address how many expert annotations are needed to reach clinical usefulness.

Method: Fine-tune MedSAM3 with LoRA for five abdominal organs (liver, kidneys, spleen, gallbladder, pancreas) on CT and MRI using 1, 2, 5, 10 labeled cases; evaluate on AMOS22; compare to TotalSegmentator/MRSegmentator; external validation on Whole Heart Segmentation for LV; measure Dice; training time 3–5 hours per organ on a single GPU.

Result: With 10 annotated cases, performance approaches specialist systems trained on far more data; gallbladder segmentation becomes reliable (Dice 0.68 CT, 0.59 MRI; baseline 0.0004); liver/kidneys/spleen within 5–10% of MRSegmentator using >100x more annotations; external validation shows competitive LV performance; training per organ 3–5 hours on one GPU, ~2–3x faster than nnU-Net.

Conclusion: Ten annotated cases suffice for clinically useful segmentation, demonstrating the viability of LoRA-based adaptation of medical foundation models to substantially reduce annotation and training bottlenecks, with demonstrated generalization to cardiac segmentation.

Abstract: Medical image segmentation is essential for clinical workflows such as treatment planning and disease assessment. While specialist tools like TotalSegmentator and MRSegmentator achieve strong performance, they require large annotated datasets for training. Medical foundation models offer a promising alternative through large-scale pretraining that reduces the annotation burden for new tasks, but zero-shot performance remains limited. Parameter-efficient adaptation via Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) enables efficient specialization with few trainable parameters, but a key question remains: how many expert-annotated cases are needed to achieve clinically useful segmentation performance? We address this by adapting MedSAM3 with LoRA for five abdominal organs (liver, kidneys, spleen, gallbladder, and pancreas) in CT and MRI using only 1, 2, 5, and 10 annotated cases, evaluating on AMOS22 dataset. With just 10 cases, models achieve performance competitive with specialist systems trained on orders of magnitude more data. Notably, this includes reliable gallbladder segmentation (Dice 0.68 CT, 0.59 MRI) where existing tools fail almost completely (Dice 0.0004), while remaining within 5--10% of MRSegmentator for liver, kidneys, and spleen using over 100 times fewer annotations. Furthermore, external validation on the Whole Heart Segmentation dataset shows that the approach extends to cardiac segmentation, a use case beyond the scope of TotalSegmentator (MRI) and MRSegmentator, achieving competitive left ventricle (LV) performance with only 10 annotated cases. Training requires only3--5,hours per organ on a single GPU, approximately 2--3 times faster than nnU-Net. These findings suggest that ten annotated cases are sufficient for clinically useful segmentation, effectively reducing bottlenecks for both image annotation and training time.

</details>


### [55] [CL4D: Contrastive Language-4D Pretraining for Vision-Language Reasoning in Dynamic Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18734)
*Kumal Hewagamage,Isuranga Senavirathne,Sasika Amarasinghe,Hasitha Gallella,Dulanga Weerakoon,Vigneshwaran Subbaraju,Ranga Rodrigo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes CL4D, a 4D vision encoder for dynamic point clouds trained with contrastive learning to align 4D representations with language, enabling zero-shot motion-to-text and text-to-motion retrieval; builds 4DVLM to generate language from 4D dynamics; trained on DynAction4D; achieves state-of-the-art results and surpasses frontline video VLMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current encoders either handle static 2D images, static 3D point clouds, or 2D videos, but fail to jointly model spatial structure and temporal dynamics with accurate geometry. A unified 4D representation is needed for robust dynamics understanding and cross-modal alignment.

Method: Train CL4D with a contrastive objective to learn a shared embedding space between text and 4D scene dynamics (dynamic point clouds). Use CL4D as a backbone to build 4DVLM, a vision-language model that conditions language generation on dynamic 4D geometry. Introduce a new dataset DynAction4D capturing diverse human motions and interactions for training. Evaluate on multiple 4D human action benchmarks.

Result: CL4D achieves state-of-the-art performance, with improvements of ~16.75% over prior methods. 4DVLM outperforms frontline video VLMs such as Gemini and GPT-5 even when those models receive RGB videos corresponding to the same scenes expressed as 4D point clouds for 4DVLM.

Conclusion: First foundational 4D vision encoder operating directly on 4D point clouds; enables zero-shot motion-to-text and text-to-motion retrieval and serves as a robust foundation for 4D vision-language tasks. The 4DVLM demonstrates strong cross-modal generation capabilities and surpasses existing video-based VLMs on 4D data.

Abstract: 4D understanding and reasoning is a fundamental capability for embodied AI agents operating in dynamic physical environments. However, existing vision encoders are largely limited to static 2D images or 3D point clouds without temporal modeling, or to 2D videos that lack accurate geometric depth reasoning. Consequently, current approaches fail to jointly capture spatial structure and motion evolution in dynamic scenes. We present CL4D, the first foundational 4D vision encoder that directly operates on dynamic point clouds, trained with a contrastive learning objective to align spatio-temporal geometric representations with natural language descriptions. By learning a shared embedding space between text and 4D scene dynamics, CL4D enables zero-shot motion-to-text and text-to-motion retrieval in dynamic environments and serves as a foundational 4D vision encoder for downstream 4D vision-language tasks. Building on this encoder, we introduce 4DVLM, a 4D vision-language model that conditions language generation on dynamic geometric representations. 4DVLM is the first VLM designed to operate directly on 4D point clouds without relying on 2D images, 2D videos, or static 3D point clouds. We train CL4D and subsequently 4DVLM on a newly constructed dataset termed DynAction4D capturing diverse human motions across varying object interactions and scene environments. Extensive experiments across multiple 4D human action benchmarks demonstrate that CL4D achieves state-of-the-art performance, with improvements of approximately ~16.75% over prior methods. Furthermore, 4DVLM outperforms frontier video VLMs such as Gemini and GPT-5 even when these models are provided with RGB video sequences corresponding to the same scenes represented as 4D point clouds for 4DVLM.

</details>


### [56] [SED-FOD: Scattering-Aware Expert Decomposition for Few-Shot Cross-Sensor SAR Object Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18755)
*Shu Yang,Zhen Chen,Zhiyu Jiang,Yanlei Li,Xingdong Liang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Proposes a scattering-aware shared-specific feature decomposition for few-shot SAR domain adaptation object detection, combining a transferable shared path with soft-gated scattering-specific experts, guided by routing-domain auxiliary and expert-balancing losses to handle sensor heterogeneity while enabling asymmetric domain alignment; demonstrates improvement on bidirectional SAR domain tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain shifts across SAR frequency bands, resolutions, backgrounds, and sensor responses degrade detectors; few-shot target-domain data limits adaptation; existing methods favor domain-invariant features at the cost of useful sensor-specific scattering cues.

Method: Decompose detection features into a shared path for transferable structure and multiple soft-gated scattering-specific expert paths. The shared path enables asymmetric domain alignment; scattering-specific experts adapt to sensor responses. Introduce routing-domain auxiliary loss to encourage experts to capture sensor routing preferences and an expert balancing loss to prevent routing collapse.

Result: Extensive experiments on four bidirectional SAR adaptation tasks (FARAD-X/FARAD-Ka and MiniSAR) under various few-shot settings show the proposed method achieves superior performance in both forward and reverse adaptation directions compared with baselines.

Conclusion: A scattering-aware decomposition effectively preserves sensor-relevant cues while enabling transferable object structure, enhancing few-shot SAR domain adaptation for object detection.

Abstract: Synthetic aperture radar (SAR) object detection is an important part of remote sensing interpretation. However, because of variations in frequency band, resolution, background clutter, and target scattering responses, the performance of existing detectors often degrades when training and testing data are acquired from different SAR domains. Although domain adaptation methods offer a promising paradigm for solving this problem, most of them mainly pursue domain-invariant feature alignment and suppress sensor-dependent scattering characteristics that are useful for object detection. This problem becomes more challenging in few-shot scenarios, where only a few fully annotated target-domain SAR images are available. To address this issue, we propose a scattering-aware shared-specific feature decomposition framework for few-shot SAR domain adaptation object detection. We decompose detection features into a shared path and several soft-gated scattering-specific expert paths. The shared path learns transferable object structural information and is used for asymmetric domain alignment, while the scattering-specific experts adaptively compensate heterogeneous SAR responses. In addition, routing-domain auxiliary loss is introduced to encourage specific experts to capture sensor-dependent routing preferences, and an expert balancing loss is used to prevent routing collapse. Extensive experiments on four bidirectional heterogeneous SAR detection tasks between FARAD-X/FARAD-Ka and MiniSAR under different few-shot settings have been conducted and experimental results demonstrate that the proposed method achieves superior performance in both forward and reverse adaptation directions.

</details>


### [57] [MIFR: A Modality-Invariant and Fair Representation Framework for Skin Disease Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18774)
*Asonyu Senge Njih,Yvan Guifo Fodjo,Vianney Kengne Tchendji,Jerry Lacmou Zeutouo,Kerol Djoumessi*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A modality-invariant framework (MIFR) uses ViT encoders to fuse clinical photos and dermoscopic images for skin disease classification, incorporating fairness losses across skin types and modality alignment via a five-component multi-objective loss; evaluated on HIBA+Derm7pt, PAD-UFES-20, ISIC 2019 with competitive accuracy and fairness; t-SNE shows aligned multimodal embeddings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome reliance on a single modality and performance disparities across skin tones in skin disease diagnosis, enabling robust, fair multi-modal classification.

Method: Two ViT-based encoders for clinical and dermoscopic inputs, modality-specific projection heads mapping to shared embedding space; five-component loss: (1) weighted cross-entropy; (2) confusion loss; (3) skin-type classification loss; (4) per-modality supervised contrastive loss; (5) modality-invariance loss; training on paired datasets; evaluation on HIBA+Derm7pt, PAD-UFES-20, ISIC 2019; visualization via t-SNE.

Result: Competitive predictive performance versus baselines; competitive fairness on internal dataset; t-SNE demonstrated aligned embeddings for the same disease across modalities; generalization on external datasets.

Conclusion: Modality-invariant representation learning with joint objectives yields accurate and fair skin disease classification across modalities, supporting multi-modal integration and robustness to skin tone variation.

Abstract: Skin diseases represent a major global public health burden, yet machine learning tools developed to assist in their diagnosis suffer from two critical limitations: reliance on only one modality for diagnosis and systematic performance disparities across skin tones. While existing approaches address each challenge separately, this work proposes a modality-invariant framework with fair representation (MIFR) for skin disease classification. The architecture pairs clinical photographs with dermoscopic images using ViT-based encoders, projecting each input into a high-dimensional embedding space via modality-specific projection heads. The resulting model is trained with a five-component multi-objective loss including weighted cross-entropy for classification, confusion and skin-type classification losses for fairness, per-modality supervised contrastive loss for class alignment, and a modality-invariance loss for clinical and dermoscopic modality alignment. Experiments on the HIBA+Derm7pt paired dataset and the external PAD-UFES-20 and ISIC 2019 datasets showed that modality-invariant representation learning provides competitive predictive performance compare to relevant baseline models and competitive fairness on the internal dataset. t-SNE visualizations confirmed that clinical and dermoscopic embeddings of the same disease are geometrically aligned, validating the joint objectives.

</details>


### [58] [EfficientSync: Real-Time Lip Synchronization via Deformation-Based Reference Texture Mixing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18832)
*Fa-Ting Hong,Runzhen Liu,Luchuan Song,Hongmin Cai,Chuhua Xian*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EfficientSync is a real-time deformation-based framework for audio-driven lip synchronization that preserves authentic textures by transferring textures from reference frames rather than re-synthesizing them. It uses a multi-reference texture fusion, a masking strategy to prevent lower-face leakage, and a fast pre-processing step to select diverse references, achieving state-of-the-art quality at 166 FPS on a single GPU.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors identify that preserving identity textures is hindered not by a lack of reference frames but by the absence of a mechanism to faithfully transfer genuine textures. They aim to avoid resynthesizing textures and reduce hallucinations in intra-oral details.

Method: Three main components: (1) Dynamic Texture Mixer uses channel-wise fusion of multiple spatially aligned references through global-context evaluation and channel-wise weighted summation to preserve texture; (2) Spatio-Temporal Shifted Adaptive Masking decomposes the source frame into lip-generation conditions and a background prior to suppress leakage and blend the mouth with the background; (3) STAR Sampling is a zero-overhead pre-processing step that selects the sharpest and most topologically diverse reference frames; overall a real-time deformation-based pipeline.

Result: On HDTF and VFHQ datasets, EfficientSync delivers state-of-the-art visual quality and identity preservation at 166 frames per second on a single GPU, with video demos available online.

Conclusion: EfficientSync demonstrates that texture transfer from reference frames within a deformation-based pipeline can achieve real-time, high-fidelity lip-sync with preserved identity, minimizing intra-oral artifacts without heavy resynthesis.

Abstract: Audio-driven lip synchronization manipulates the mouth region of a talking-face video to match the driving audio while preserving head pose, identity, and background. Although the task is inherently local editing, prevailing approaches reconstruct the entire lower face with heavy GAN- or diffusion-based decoders, incurring substantial latency and, more critically, hallucinating intra-oral details such as teeth and lip wrinkles instead of preserving authentic textures. We contend that the bottleneck in identity preservation is not the scarcity of reference frames, but the lack of a mechanism that faithfully transfers the genuine textures they already contain. We therefore present EfficientSync, a real-time deformation-based framework that retains reference textures rather than resynthesizing them. First, the Dynamic Texture Mixer reformulates multi-reference fusion as channel-wise selection, evaluating each spatially aligned reference in a global context and aggregating them by channel-wise weighted summation, preserving textural integrity at low cost. Second, Spatio-Temporal Shifted Adaptive Masking decomposes the source frame into lip-generation conditions and an independent background prior, suppressing lower-face leakage while blending the synthesized mouth seamlessly into the background. Third, STAR Sampling, a zero-overhead pre-processing step, retrieves the sharpest and most topologically diverse reference frames. Experiments on HDTF and VFHQ show state-of-the-art visual quality and identity preservation at 166 FPS on a single GPU. Video demos: https://alunaticat.github.io/EfficientSync/index.html.

</details>


### [59] [EVADE: Evidence-Verified Agentic Diagnosis with Escape](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18833)
*Mohaimenul Azam Khan Raiaan,Nur Mohammad Fahad*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: EVADE is an inference-time mechanism for a single frozen medical VLM that enforces reliability by cross-checking full-image and zoomed-view responses, localizing diagnostically relevant regions, and abstaining when the two views disagree; this yields better calibration and selective risk without sacrificing accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address widespread overconfidence, poor calibration, and lack of robust test-time reasoning in medical vision-language models, enabling safer deployment without retraining.

Method: Compute and compare responses from two views of the input (full image and zoomed region); localize the diagnostically relevant region; re-answer using the zoomed view; only commit when both views agree; abstain otherwise; verify gate consistency across views rather than re-reading model text; evaluate on VQA-RAD, SLAKE, PathVQA with Qwen2.5-VL-7B.

Result: EVADE reduces expected calibration error (ECE) by up to 45% versus zero-shot, while improving calibration and selective risk and maintaining accuracy; chain-of-thought, self-consistency, and self-verification methods fail on at least one axis; a grounding analysis shows self-proposed regions localize diagnostic structures better, but 7B VLM cannot use this localization to revise answers; reliability gains stem from the consistency gate and calibrated abstention.

Conclusion: A single frozen VLM can achieve improved reliability through a consistency-based gating strategy and calibrated abstention, with localised evidence used to guide re-evaluation; the approach outperforms other reasoning strategies on key reliability axes and highlights the importance of cross-view verification over self-critique.

Abstract: Medical vision-language models (VLMs) can achieve high accuracy but remain unreliable: they are systematically overconfident, benefit little from test-time reasoning, and lack the ability to reliably calibrate trust in their own responses. We introduce EVADE (Evidence-Verified Agentic Diagnosis with Escape), an inferential, non-training method that enhances the safety of deploying a single frozen VLM. EVADE responds and, when uncertain, localises the region most diagnostically relevant, re-answers on a zoomed view, and commits only when both the entire image and the zoomed view responses agree; otherwise, it abstains. To directly address verification hallucination in single-model self-checking, our main idea is to verify gate consistency across different image views rather than re-reading the model's own text. Experimental evaluation on VQA-RAD, SLAKE, and PathVQA using Qwen2.5-VL-7B reports that EVADE is the only method that simultaneously improves both calibration and selective risk while maintaining accuracy, reducing expected calibration error (ECE) by up to 45% compared to zero-shot. Chain-of-thought, self-consistency, and self-verification all fail at least one axis. A grounding analysis reports that self-proposed regions perform better at diagnostic structure localisation than centres or random crops. However, a 7B VLM cannot use this localisation to revise answers. Therefore, reliability gains come from the consistency gate and calibrated abstention.

</details>


### [60] [RVLoss: Runoff Vote Loss for Self-Supervised LiDAR Scene Flow Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18864)
*Shiming Wang,Liangliang Nan,Julian Kooij,Holger Caesar,Yancong Lin*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RVLoss introduces a self-supervised loss for LiDAR scene flow that enforces motion rigidity with a two-stage runoff vote, producing pseudo-labels for rigid and free-form flows. It improves self-supervised performance on Argoverse2 2026 Challenge by ~20% over baselines and generalizes across four additional datasets; code to be released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nearest-neighbor-based Chamfer losses for LiDAR scene flow can yield non-rigid, inconsistent flows within object instances. Regularization improves this but is often insufficient for large objects. There is a need for a loss design that inherently enforces motion rigidity without requiring heavy supervision.

Method: Introduce RVLoss that computes dominant flow candidates via top-k voting from the initial NN flow. When source points are compensated by these candidates, the component giving the best representation of rigid motion yields the highest consensus in a second top-1 vote. This runoff voting produces cluster-wise rigid flows and free-form flows as pseudo-labels for self-supervised learning, integrable into standard feedforward architectures.

Result: On Argoverse2 2026 Challenge, RVLoss-trained models achieve state-of-the-art performance among self-supervised methods, outperforming baselines by about 20%. Cross-dataset evaluation across four additional datasets shows consistent improvements.

Conclusion: Two-stage runoff voting effectively imposes motion rigidity in self-supervised LiDAR scene flow, yielding improved flow consistency, better generalization, and compatibility with existing architectures; code release planned.

Abstract: LiDAR scene flow estimates point-wise motion between two consecutive scans, referred to as the source and target. Leading self-supervised methods typically minimize the Chamfer loss, the nearest neighbor distance between the flow-compensated source and the target. However, nearest-neighbor search does not enforce motion rigidity, often leading to inconsistent flows within object instances. Existing approaches address this issue with additional regularization terms, but flow consistency among points remains limited, especially for large objects. We propose RVLoss, a self-supervised loss that incorporates motion rigidity by design through a runoff vote mechanism. Our key observation is that the point-wise motion, calculated from nearest neighbor search, can often be grouped into a small set of dominant flow candidates by voting (top-k voting). Furthermore, when compensating the source by these candidates, the flow that best represents the underlying rigid motion often yields the highest consensus after a second voting (top-1 voting). Based on this insight, we incorporate the two-stage runoff vote into loss design and create cluster-wise rigid flows and free-form flows as pseudo-labels for self-supervised learning. RVLoss can be seamlessly integrated into existing feedforward architectures. Experiments on the Argoverse2 2026 Challenge show that models trained with RVLoss achieve state-of-the-art performance among self-supervised approaches, outperforming baseline models trained with alternative loss designs by 20%. Moreover, cross-dataset evaluations demonstrate consistent performance improvements across four additional datasets. Code will be released upon acceptance.

</details>


### [61] [Falcon Perception-HD: High Density Perception via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18881)
*Sofian Chaybouti,Yasser Dahou,Ngoc Dung Huynh,Reda Alami,Hilde Kuehne*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: RL-based post-training to align open-vocabulary autoregressive perception with evaluation metrics (GRPO) on Falcon Perception; improves performance in very dense scenes, reduces reliance on NMS and coordinate deduplication, and introduces self-annotation pipelines; model weights released; datasets forthcoming.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard supervised fine-tuning with per-token cross-entropy optimizes a proxy objective that poorly aligns with perception metrics (precision/recall), especially in dense scenes with many entities. There is a need to align training with evaluation for set-structured outputs and reduce reliance on deduplication heuristics.

Method: Post-training reinforcement learning using GRPO built on Falcon Perception. Key innovations include reward design for set-structured outputs and multi-head sampling control; two hybrid self-annotation pipelines tailored for difficult referring expressions and very dense scenes; penalty for false positives/negatives to preserve object existence knowledge without negative samples; removal of NMS and coordinate deduplication through learned policy.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance in very dense scenes (up to 500 objects per image); mitigates mask repetitions and largely eliminates the need for NMS and coordinate deduplication; improvements across referring expression segmentation on PBench and SACO-Gold; preserves MCC-based existence knowledge without negative samples using a simple reward; two effective self-annotation pipelines improve RL training.

Conclusion: Reinforcement learning post-training can effectively align autoregressive perception models with evaluation metrics, enabling robust performance in dense scenes, reducing reliance on heuristic deduplication, and enabling simpler reward structures. The work provides a practical pathway for metric-aligned perception and releases model weights (Falcon Perception revision) with datasets to follow.

Abstract: Autoregressive perception models trained to localize visual entities under the open-vocabulary setting are mostly trained using Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) with maximum likelihood, yet it optimizes a proxy objective (per-token cross-entropy) that is fundamentally misaligned with perception metrics such as precision and recall. In this paper, we explore post-training reinforcement learning (RL), specifically GRPO, to directly align these models with their evaluation metrics. Building up on the recently introduced Falcon Perception, we design an RL framework that addresses perception-specific challenges: reward design for set-structured outputs and multi-head sampling control. We discover multiple benefits from RL for perception: first, RL unlocks state-of-the-art performance in very dense scenes (up to 500 objects per scene), a regime where most existing systems degrade sharply or collapse; furthermore it fixes common issues in autoregressive perception models like mask repetitions and removes almost entirely the need for NMS and coordinate deduplication, which improve both performance and efficiency and remove the need for hyperparameters tuning; overall, we notice improvements on all levels of difficulties in referring expression segmentation (on PBench and SACO-Gold), and we find an elegant way to preserve the knowledge of whether an object exists or not (as evaluated by MCC) without training on negative samples. We show that a simple reward that penalizes false negatives and positives is sufficient. We develop two hybrid self-annotation pipelines, respectively tailored for difficult referring expressions and very dense scenes, and show their benefits on RL-training. Model weights are released as a Falcon Perception revision~\footnote{https://huggingface.co/tiiuae/Falcon-Perception}. Datasets will be published.

</details>


### [62] [Learning-State-Aware Dynamic Generative Data Augmentation on Small-Scale Datasets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18907)
*Ting Xiang,Chenxi Deng,Jinhui Zhao,Bingting Jiang,Ke Zhang,Changjian Chen,Zhuo Tang*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: LSADA: learning-state-aware dynamic GDA that maps per-sample learning state to augmentation strength and uses decoupled augmentation-diffusion fusion to emphasize class-relevant regions while diversifying class-irrelevant regions; achieves state-of-the-art gains across 9 public datasets (4.5% avg on 6 natural, 2.5% on 3 medical).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Small-scale image classification is data-limited. Existing GDA methods are often task-agnostic and fail to tailor augmentation to downstream model needs. Dynamic GDA with model feedback improves this but struggles with per-sample augmentation strength and region-aware adaptation, risking a trade-off between image diversity and class semantics.

Method: Construct a learning state per sample using current loss and loss-decrease rate, map it to a per-sample augmentation strength. Employ a decoupled augmentation-diffusion fusion: apply strength-controlled transformations to class-relevant regions and generate diverse class-irrelevant regions, then progressively fuse these regions to boost diversity while preserving class semantics.

Result:  LSADA outperforms the prior SOTA dynamic GDA method by an average of 4.5% on six natural image datasets and 2.5% on three medical image datasets.

Conclusion: By aligning augmentation strength with learning state and decoupling region-focused augmentation from broader diffusion-based diversification, LSADA provides more effective data augmentation for small-scale classification and demonstrates robust gains across diverse datasets.

Abstract: Small-scale image classification is often limited by the scarcity of training data. Generative data augmentation (GDA) based on pretrained generative models has emerged as an effective solution. However, existing methods rely on task-agnostic augmentation strategies that overlook downstream model needs. Although recent dynamic GDA methods incorporate model feedback to guide augmentation, they still struggle to reliably determine sample-specific augmentation strengths and adapt augmentation strategies to different image regions while balancing image diversity and class semantics.
  To address these issues, we propose learning-state-aware dynamic generative data augmentation (LSADA). Specifically, LSADA constructs a learning state for each sample based on its current loss and loss-decrease rate, which is then mapped to a sample-specific augmentation strength. Furthermore, LSADA introduces a decoupled data augmentation and diffusion fusion strategy that applies strength-controlled transformations to class-relevant regions and generates diverse class-irrelevant regions, progressively fusing them to improve image diversity while preserving class semantics. Experiments on nine public datasets show that LSADA outperforms the existing SOTA dynamic GDA method by an average of 4.5% on six natural image datasets and 2.5% on three medical image datasets.

</details>


### [63] [Simple, Safe, and Overlooked: Reclaiming Sustainable Domain Generalization with Statistical Color Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18915)
*Sebastian Doerrich,Francesco Di Salvo,Shyam Nandan Rai,Marco Lents,Christian Ledig*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Colorist: a training-free color-matching augmentation that applies global mean-std normalization in RGB space to generate domain variations for medical image classifiers. It preserves structure, is interpretable and computationally efficient, and outperforms deep generative and baseline augmentation across multiple medical imaging domains (histopathology, blood smears, dermatology, retina).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Domain shifts between development and deployment (hardware shifts, color variations, patient characteristics) degrade medical image classifiers. Standard color jittering lacks diversity; deep generative style transfer can hallucinate features and is resource-intensive. A safe, interpretable, efficient augmentation is needed to improve robustness.

Method: Data augmentation that performs training-free global mean-std matching in RGB space (colorist). No neural networks; applies color statistics matching to images within the dataloader, preserving anatomical structures while introducing domain-like color variations.

Result: Across histopathology, peripheral blood, dermatology, and retinal datasets, Colorist yields up to +9% better balanced accuracy than state-of-the-art domain generalization regularizers and up to +13% over baselines without augmentation.

Conclusion: Statistical color matching provides a safe, interpretable, and efficient alternative to deep generative methods for improving clinical robustness without compromising structure or increasing computational cost; source code is publicly available.

Abstract: Hardware shifts, color variations, and changing patient characteristics between development and deployment routinely break trained medical image classifiers. Existing remedies fall short: standard color jittering provides insufficient diversity, while deep generative style transfer algorithms hallucinate features, destroy clinically relevant structures, and waste massive compute resources. To address this, we revisit classical statistical color matching and repurpose it as Colorist, a highly efficient data augmentation strategy that applies global mean-standard deviation matching directly in the RGB color space. We demonstrate that this training-free, fully interpretable approach safely generates structurally intact domain variations, outperforming deep generative models in structural fidelity and color alignment. Across out-of-distribution histopathology, peripheral blood, dermatology, and retinal datasets, it improves balanced accuracy by up to +9% over state-of-the-art domain generalization regularizers and by +13% over an unaugmented baseline. Moreover, by avoiding neural networks in the augmentation loop, Colorist preserves anatomical structure, minimizes carbon footprint, and integrates seamlessly into standard dataloaders. Together, these findings establish statistical matching as a safe, interpretable, yet overlooked alternative to deep architectures for clinical robustness. Source code is available at https://github.com/sdoerrich97/colorist.

</details>


### [64] [Institutional Books - Visual Elements: An open-source pipeline for extracting, classifying, deduplicating, and captioning visual elements from digital book collections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18957)
*Jimmy Mendez,Matteo Cargnelutti,David Lowry-Duda,Catherine Brobston,Salwa Ismail,Greg Leppert,Amanda Watson,Jonathan Zittrain*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: An open-source end-to-end pipeline to detect, classify, deduplicate, and caption visual elements in historical books, and a large initial dataset (22.6M visual elements from 983,004 Harvard Library volumes) released to enable AI training and digital humanities research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Visual content in historical book collections is under-explored in large-scale digitization. OCR captures text but misses illustrations, photographs, engravings, and decorative art, limiting computational access to these visuals for analysis and model training.

Method: Development and release of Institutional Books - Visual Elements, an open-source end-to-end pipeline for detecting, classifying, deduplicating, and captioning visual elements. The authors also release an initial dataset comprising 22.6 million extracted visual elements from 983,004 scanned volumes of the Harvard Library Institutional Books collection.

Result: Provision of a working pipeline and a large-scale visual-element dataset, enabling new use cases for digitized libraries, including AI model training and digital humanities research.

Conclusion: The work advances community-wide access to digitized book visual content, promoting computational analysis and new research directions in digitized library collections.

Abstract: Historical book collections contain rich visual elements - such as illustrations, photographs, engravings, and decorative art - that are frequently under-explored in large-scale digitization projects. While Optical Character Recognition (OCR) has standardized the extraction of textual content, these visual components offer a layer of nuance and context that remains largely untapped by automated text extraction workflows. This technical report introduces Institutional Books - Visual Elements, an open-source end-to-end pipeline for detecting, classifying, deduplicating, and captioning visual elements from historical book collections. Alongside this pipeline, we release an initial dataset of 22.6 million visual elements extracted from the 983,004 scanned volumes that comprise the Institutional Books: Harvard Library dataset. This work contributes to ongoing, community-wide efforts to enable new use cases for digitized library collections through computational access, from artificial intelligence model training to digital humanities research.

</details>


### [65] [Frozen DINO Localizes Image Edits Without a Localizer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18968)
*Zane Kumar,Vishal Jain,Bernhard Kainz*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: TRAIL: Training-free localization of AI-edits using drift in frozen DINO patch-tokens. A global Haar perturbation induces patch-token cosine drift that serves as a localization map for edits. Achieves competitive AUROC and Dice vs a supervised baseline on CocoGlide; generalizes to Poisson interpolation without a generator. Best performance from late DINO layers; global context improves detection. Code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Image edits can alter meaning; forensic localization of edits is needed without requiring labeled training data or generator-specific detectors.

Method: Apply one global Haar perturbation to input; compute cosine drift between corresponding patch tokens in frozen DINO encoders; produce localization map from drift; evaluate patch-level AUROC and Dice against ground truth masks; compare to Detective SAM; analyze effect of thresholding (oracle) and perturbation type; test across multiple DINO encoders and perturbation placements (global vs local); test transfer to Poisson interpolation.

Result: TRAIL achieves patch AUROC 0.903 on CocoGlide vs 0.912 for Detective SAM; fixed-threshold Dice 0.619 vs 0.709; oracle threshold improves TRAIL to 0.790; Poisson interpolation: AUROC 0.855 vs 0.864; best block depth around 0.80–0.94; global context enhances performance; perturbation type and context affect visibility; signals persist without a generator.

Conclusion: Frozen DINO patch tokens encode a strong late-layer localization cue for AI edits; the cue is robust across edit types and datasets but its visibility depends on perturbation strategy and preserved context. TRAIL is training-free and generator-agnostic, offering a practical forensic tool; code available.

Abstract: Localized image edits can change a photograph's meaning while leaving most of it authentic, so forensic analysis must identify where an edit occurred. We show that patch-level perturbation responses from frozen DINO encoders are themselves localization maps. Training-free Localization of AI-image Edits from patch-token Drift (TRAIL) applies one global Haar perturbation and maps cosine drift between corresponding patch tokens. On 80 source-disjoint CocoGlide test images, TRAIL reaches .903 patch AUROC versus .912 for the mask-supervised Detective SAM; fixed-threshold Dice is .619 versus .709, while an oracle threshold raises TRAIL to .790. Transferred unchanged to Poisson image interpolation, TRAIL reaches .855 AUROC versus .864, showing that the cue persists without a generator. Across sixteen DINO encoders, the best block lies at normalized depth .80-.94. Global context matters: AUROC falls from .903 globally to .857 for local-in-canvas perturbations and .735 for independently encoded crops. Frozen DINO patch tokens therefore contain a strong late-layer localization signal whose visibility depends on the perturbation and preserved context. Code: https://github.com/VishalJ99/trail-image-edit-localization.

</details>


### [66] [When Simplicity Wins: Bottleneck-Aware Context Modeling for Lightweight Semantic Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18979)
*Mian Muhammad Naeem Abid,Nancy Mehta,Zongwei Wu,Radu Timofte*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: SiConMo is a lightweight semantic segmentation framework that emphasizes bottleneck-stage design to fuse local and global context under low compute, with RGB-only and GME-enhanced variants, achieving strong accuracy-efficiency trade-offs on standard datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The bottleneck stage has been under-explored for contextual aggregation in high-resolution semantic segmentation. CNNs excel at local patterns but miss long-range dependencies; Transformers capture global context but at high cost. The goal is to exploit the bottleneck to efficiently integrate local and global information under resource constraints.

Method: Propose three components: Token Pyramid Extraction Module for multi-scale representation; Transformer-Branched Depthwise Convolution block for bottleneck-aware context modeling; and a Feature Merging Module to preserve spatial structure while boosting semantic consistency. Presents two variants: RGB-only SiConMo and GME-enhanced SiConMo†.

Result: Demonstrates state-of-the-art accuracy–efficiency trade-off among lightweight semantic segmentation models across ADE20K, PASCAL Context, Cityscapes, and COCO-Stuff, validating the framework’s efficiency and accuracy gains on multiple benchmarks.

Conclusion: A simplicity-driven bottleneck design yields strong performance; integrating multi-scale tokens, transformer-inspired bottleneck convolutions, and feature merging in the bottleneck effectively balances local and global context under tight compute budgets.

Abstract: Semantic segmentation demands a careful balance between accuracy, efficiency, and scalability, which remains difficult to achieve for high-resolution imagery. Convolutional networks effectively model local patterns but struggle with long-range dependencies, whereas Vision Transformers capture global context at a high computational cost. While recent work largely focuses on encoder design, the bottleneck stage, central to contextual aggregation and information flow, has been relatively overlooked. We propose SiConMo, a lightweight yet effective framework, implemented in two variants: an RGB-only model (SiConMo) and a GME-enhanced variant (SiConMo$_\dagger$). We show that simplicity arises from a key design principle: at very low computational budgets, the bottleneck is the most efficient stage to integrate local and global context. SiConMo integrates three complementary components: a Token Pyramid Extraction Module for hierarchical multi-scale representation, a Transformer-Branched Depthwise Convolution block for bottleneck-aware context modeling, and a Feature Merging Module that preserves spatial structure while enhancing semantic consistency. Extensive experiments on ADE20K, PASCAL Context, Cityscapes, and COCO-Stuff demonstrate that SiConMo achieves a state-of-the-art accuracy-efficiency trade-off among lightweight semantic segmentation models, highlighting simplicity as a powerful design principle.

</details>


### [67] [Uncertainty-Aware Art-Historical Dating with Vision-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18984)
*Stefanie Schneider,Peter Bell*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Temporal entanglement in pretrained image representations: dating artworks is feasible via uncertainty-aware regression on frozen embeddings, with Vision-Language Models outperforming purely visual self-supervised baselines; the temporal signal reflects collecting/preservation biases rather than true production dates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenging the assumption that pretrained visual models encode genuine artistic production time; museums and archives constrain data visibility, creating institutional temporal biases that can masquerade as historical time.

Method: Uncertainty-aware regression on frozen image embeddings to date artworks; evaluation across several pretrained vision models on a temporally controlled Wikidata corpus; comparison of Vision-Language Models (VLMs) with self-supervised baselines; qualitative analysis of biases in temporal knowledge.

Result: Models contain usable temporal information; VLMs outperform purely visual self-supervised baselines; qualitative analysis reveals that the learned temporal knowledge is shaped by institutional biases and data curation processes.

Conclusion: Temporal signals in pretrained representations reflect archival/curatorial histories (temporal entanglement) rather than pure production time; researchers should account for biases when interpreting dating signals from embeddings and consider the role of data provenance in model behavior.

Abstract: Museum and archival datasets do not mirror historical artistic production, but materialize the contingent histories of collecting, preservation, cataloging, and digitization. This has direct consequences for interpreting pretrained image representations: they may appear to encode historical time while actually encoding the institutional conditions under which objects become visible as data. We describe this phenomenon as temporal entanglement and investigate it by formulating artwork dating as an uncertainty-aware regression task over frozen image embeddings. We evaluate several pretrained vision models on a temporally controlled Wikidata corpus of artworks. Our results show that these models contain usable temporal information, with Vision-Language Models (VLMs) outperforming purely visual self-supervised baselines. However, a qualitative analysis indicates that this temporal knowledge is shaped by various biases.

</details>


### [68] [X-LMC: Cross-View Spatiotemporal Collateral Circulation Scoring from DSA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18986)
*Maedeh Hafezi Moghadas,Hakim Baazaoui,Lukas Bastian Otto,Susanne Wegener,Björn Menze,Ezequiel De la Rosa*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Introduces X-LMC, a spatiotemporal deep-learning framework for automated ASITN/SIR collateral grading from time-resolved DSA; uses a DINOv2 backbone, cross-view token fusion, and a recurrent module to model contrast dynamics; shows improved agreement metrics over baselines on a multicenter M1 occlusion dataset and aligns with human inter-rater agreement; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Automate and standardize leptomeningeal collateral (LMC) assessment to reduce manual variability, enable objective hemodynamic phenotyping, and support prognosis and treatment planning in stroke care.

Method: A multi-view spatiotemporal architecture: spatial frames encoded with a DINOv2 backbone; orthogonal view fusion via token-level cross-view attention; temporal dynamics captured by a recurrent network processing the contrast bolus trajectory. Evaluated with 5-fold cross-validation on 134 patients with M1 occlusions from multiple centers.

Result: X-LMC achieved QWK 0.398 (baseline 0.322) and macro-F1 0.711 (baseline 0.663), outperforming static and spatiotemporal baselines. Inter-rater QWK observed clinically was 0.314, indicating competitive agreement. First study to automate LMC scoring from DSA; demonstrates that multi-view temporal DL captures collateral kinetics.

Conclusion: The work establishes a reproducible baseline for automated ASITN/SIR grading, enabling objective hemodynamic phenotyping in stroke cohorts and setting performance boundaries for automated collateral scoring; code availability enhances reproducibility and benchmarking.

Abstract: Digital subtraction angiography (DSA) is the reference standard for leptomeningeal collateral (LMC) assessment, providing critical prognostic insights to guide secondary treatment strategies, neurorehabilitation planning, and retrospective stroke research. However, clinical LMC grading via the ASITN/SIR scale relies on manual, highly variable visual inspection. We introduce X-LMC, a spatiotemporal framework for automated collateral scoring from time-resolved biplane DSA. The proposed architecture encodes spatial frame representations through a DINOv2 backbone, fuses orthogonal projections via a token-level cross-view attention module, and models representations of contrast bolus dynamics using a recurrent network architecture. We evaluate our framework on a multicenter dataset of 134 patients with M1-segment occlusions. In a 5-fold cross-validation setting, X-LMC yields higher point estimates than static architectures and spatiotemporal baselines adapted from related angiographic tasks, achieving a Quadratic Weighted Kappa (QWK) of 0.398 (vs. 0.322) and a dichotomized macro-F1 score of 0.711 (vs. 0.663) against the best-performing baseline. X-LMC performance also aligns with the observed clinical inter-rater agreement (QWK: 0.314). As the first DSA study attempting to automate LMC scoring, we demonstrate that multi-view temporal deep learning can capture collateral-specific contrast kinetics. Ultimately, these benchmarks delineate the clinical ambiguities and achievable performance boundaries of automated ASITN/SIR grading, establishing a reproducible foundation for objective hemodynamic phenotyping in stroke cohorts. Code is available at https://github.com/maedehafezi/X-LMC.

</details>


### [69] [ForeSightGuide: An Anticipatory Framework toward Accurate and Low-Redundancy Guidance for the Visually Impaired](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18993)
*Zhiyuan Wang,Xu Li,Shikang Guo,Wei Meng,Quan Liu,Jie Zuo*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Anticipatory, VLM-based guidance for visually impaired navigation that filters non-threatening objects, reducing alerts and preserving safety; validated on a dynamic traffic dataset with state-of-the-art results (0.299 redundant alerts per guidance, 0.112 missed hazards).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-Language Models deliver rich scene understanding but generate many false positives in dynamic environments, increasing cognitive load for assistive systems; there is a need for anticipatory reasoning to prune non-threatening objects and provide concise guidance.

Method: ForeSightGuide combines semantic scene understanding with predictive hazard assessment, leveraging VLM reasoning to anticipate obstacle motion and filter non-threatening objects to produce concise, actionable guidance. A novel dataset capturing complex, dynamic real-world traffic scenes was created to benchmark predictive capabilities. Evaluations on public benchmarks and the proposed dataset demonstrate strong performance.

Result: ForeSightGuide achieves state-of-the-art performance, notably reducing redundant alerts to 0.299 per guidance output while maintaining a low missed-hazard rate of 0.112.

Conclusion: The framework effectively reduces information overload without compromising safety in walking assistance; the accompanying dataset enables robust evaluation of anticipatory guidance in dynamic scenes and supports future improvements in VLM-based assistive systems.

Abstract: Electronic travel aids are pivotal for the independent mobility of the visually impaired. While Vision-Language Models (VLMs) offer rich environmental understanding, they often suffer from excessive false positives in dynamic scenarios, leading to cognitive overload. To address this, we present ForeSightGuide, an anticipatory assistive guidance framework that couples semantic scene understanding with predictive hazard assessment. Unlike reactive systems, ForeSightGuide leverages the reasoning capabilities of VLMs to anticipate obstacle motion, effectively filtering out non-threatening objects to provide concise, actionable guidance. To validate our approach, we introduce a novel dataset captured in complex, dynamic real-world traffic scenes, designed to benchmark predictive capabilities. Extensive experiments on both public benchmarks and our proposed dataset demonstrate that ForeSightGuide achieves state-of-the-art performance. Notably, it significantly mitigates information overload by reducing redundant alerts to 0.299 per guidance output while maintaining a low missed-hazard rate of 0.112, proving its efficacy for safe walking assistance.

</details>


### [70] [GrabVG: Graph-Attentive Binding for Visual Grounding in UAV Imagery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18996)
*Chaowei Wang,Yan Di,Jingjun Sun,Baozhe Liu,Jiaxu Tian,Yuheng Li,Guangqian Guo,Shan Gao*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: GrabVG is a two-stage visual grounding framework for UAV imagery that reduces visual clutter via distillation-guided proposal induction and text-aware filtering, then uses a sparse graph with graph attention to bind intra- and inter-instance cues, achieving favorable accuracy-speed in crowded scenes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High visual redundancy and topological ambiguity in dense UAV scenes make grounding hard; existing methods rely on visual-language alignment or dense context but struggle to distinguish subtle inter-instance differences and to exploit spatial topology.

Method: Two-stage design: 1) preattentive hypothesis search with distillation-guided proposal induction and text-aware hypothesis filtering to produce reliable object hypotheses; 2) graph-attentive feature binding where hypotheses form a sparse graph; language-guided intra-instance cues and inter-instance topology are bound and propagated via graph attention for spatial reasoning.

Result: On AerialVG and AerialSense datasets, GrabVG achieves Acc@0.5 of 67.31% and 80.34% respectively, outperforming baselines by 10.55 and 8.76 percentage points; favorable accuracy–speed trade-off.

Conclusion: GrabVG effectively reduces distractions, leverages topological relationships, and achieves accurate grounding in crowded UAV imagery through a two-stage, graph-attentive framework.

Abstract: Visual grounding in Unmanned Aerial Vehicle (UAV) imagery aims to localize a target object in complex bird's-eye-view scenes according to a natural language description. However, the abundance of small, densely distributed, and visually similar objects creates high visual redundancy, while repetitive local configurations give rise to strong topological ambiguity. Existing approaches mainly focus on visual--language feature alignment or dense contextual interaction, yet they struggle to distinguish subtle inter-instance differences and effectively exploit spatial topological structures, leading to inaccurate grounding in highly crowded scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose $\textbf{GrabVG}$, a novel visual grounding framework inspired by human visual search. GrabVG explicitly decomposes grounding into two sequential stages: $\textit{preattentive hypothesis search}$ and $\textit{graph-attentive feature binding}$. Specifically, we first generate a compact set of reliable object hypotheses through distillation-guided proposal induction and text-aware hypothesis filtering, substantially reducing background distractions and semantic mismatches. These hypotheses are then organized into a sparse graph, where language-guided intra-instance visual cues and inter-instance topological relationships are jointly bound and propagated via graph attention, enabling efficient spatial reasoning and accurate target localization. Extensive experiments on AerialVG and AerialSense show that GrabVG achieves a favorable accuracy--speed trade-off, reaching 67.31$\%$ and 80.34$\%$ Acc@0.5 and outperforming the corresponding baselines by 10.55 and 8.76 percentage points, respectively.

</details>


### [71] [Mise-en-Scène: Implicit Layout Emergence in Diffusion Transformers for Human-AI Design Co-Creation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19000)
*Zipeng Xu,Ryan Murdock,Umberto Michieli*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Mise-en-Scène integrates layout emergence into a pretrained diffusion transformer and uses a deterministic match-and-place step, yielding editable, high-fidelity multi-element designs that surpass prior layout-planning methods on PrismLayersPlus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing pipelines separate spatial layout from visual synthesis by predicting explicit bounding boxes, which produces rigid, mis-scaled compositions and hurts asset fidelity.

Method: Two-stage framework. Stage 1: a diffusion transformer adapted with small, knockout-selected LoRA drafts a complete design with element arrangement emerging jointly with the canvas. Stage 2: a deterministic match-and-place aligns original high-res assets to the drafted positions, preserving exact assets and enabling layered editing. Requires only minimal adaptation, no heavy specialized conditioning.

Result: On PrismLayersPlus, Mise-en-Scène yields designs closest to ground truth in perceived quality among all methods, outperforming both a language-model-based layout planner and a dedicated layout transformer by a wide margin; the match-and-place stage bridges the remaining fidelity gap.

Conclusion: Emergent layout within a diffusion model plus deterministic asset placement provides high-quality, editable, layer-friendly compositions with strong asset fidelity, suggesting efficiency with minimal architectural changes.

Abstract: Automating graphic design synthesis from user-provided elements requires both a coherent overall composition and the exact preservation of each asset. Existing methods predict a layout as explicit bounding-box coordinates with a language model and then paste the assets into it, which separates spatial planning from visual synthesis and tends to produce rigid, mis-scaled compositions. We instead ask whether the layout can emerge implicitly inside a pretrained image-editing diffusion transformer. We present Mise-en-Scène, a two-stage framework. In the first stage, a diffusion transformer adapted with a small, knockout-selected LoRA drafts a complete design in which the arrangement of the elements emerges jointly with the rendered canvas. In the second stage, a deterministic match-and-place step moves the original high-resolution assets to the drafted positions, which guarantees exact asset fidelity and yields an editable, layered design that a designer can keep refining rather than a flat image. Notably, a minimal adaptation of the pretrained transformer already suffices, without the specialized conditioning machinery commonly introduced for multi-element generation. On the large-scale PrismLayersPlus benchmark, the designs produced by Mise-en-Scène are the closest to the ground truth in perceived quality among all compared methods, by a wide margin over both an LLM layout planner and a specialized layout transformer, while our match-and-place stage bridges the remaining fidelity gap to the ground-truth composites.

</details>


### [72] [One-Stage Object Detectors in Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19014)
*Jonel Roman,Ryan Sirjue,Peter Nguyen,Daniel Krutky,Juan Jesus,Sudip Dhakal*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Survey of one-stage object detectors for autonomous driving, comparing YOLO family (including YOLOv1–v10), SSD, RetinaNet, EfficientDet, and anchor-free detectors like FCOS and CenterNet; analyzes design choices, feature fusion, losses, deployment trade-offs, and benchmark performance; covers datasets, metrics, challenges, and future directions; emphasizes speed–accuracy–robustness trade-offs and the gap to real-world autonomous driving performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Autonomous driving requires fast, reliable perception. A systematic understanding of one-stage detectors can guide architecture choices, emphasize deployment realities, and identify gaps between benchmarks and real-world robustness.

Method: Literature survey and comparative analysis of major one-stage detectors, tracing architectural evolution, analyzing design choices (backbones, necks, heads), feature fusion strategies, loss formulations, and deployment considerations; synthesis across datasets and metrics; discussion of open challenges and future directions.

Result: Compiles a comparative landscape of one-stage detectors, highlighting how anchor-based and anchor-free designs, backbones, and detection heads affect speed and accuracy; notes trends toward efficient models (e.g., EfficientDet, compact YOLO variants) and robustness challenges; provides guidance on evaluation datasets/metrics and practical deployment considerations; identifies research gaps in real-world robustness and domain adaptation.

Conclusion: One-stage detectors hold strong potential for real-time autonomous driving perception, but gaps remain in robustness and reliability under diverse operational conditions. Progress requires stronger evaluation protocols, realistic datasets, and methods that bridge the gap between benchmark performance and dependable real-world deployment.

Abstract: Autonomous vehicles depend on fast and reliable perception systems to detect surrounding vehicles, pedestrians, cyclists, traffic signs, and other road objects in real time. This paper presents a comprehensive survey and analysis of one-stage object detectors for autonomous driving rather than an implementation of a new detection system. The survey reviews the evolution of major one-stage detectors, including YOLOv1, SSD, RetinaNet, EfficientDet, anchor-free detectors such as FCOS and CenterNet, and recent real-time models such as YOLOv10. It compares these architectures through their design choices, feature-fusion strategies, loss functions, deployment trade-offs, and reported benchmark performance. The paper also summarizes commonly used autonomous-driving datasets, evaluation metrics, open challenges, and future research directions. Overall, this survey highlights how one-stage detectors balance speed, accuracy, efficiency, and robustness, while also emphasizing the remaining gap between benchmark results and dependable real-world autonomous-driving performance.

</details>


### [73] [Orthogonal Polynomial Approximation for Matrix Log Normalization in Global Covariance Pooling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19021)
*Md Rifat Ur Rahman,Md Raihan Khan,Md Sakib Hossain Shovon,Pietro Liò,Mohammad Ali Moni*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: Polynomial, decomposition-free approximation of matrix logarithm for SPD covariance pooling (MLN-COV) yields stable, fast gradient and improved accuracy over spectral log and matrix square root; degree-8 Chebyshev expansion with mean-eigenvalue pre-normalization and closed-form singular log compensation, evaluated via GEMMs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable stable, efficient backpropagation for Global Covariance Pooling on SPD manifolds by bypassing eigen-decompositions while preserving the Riemannian log map.

Method: Pre-normalize covariance spectrum near 1, apply a finite polynomial (degree-8 Chebyshev) approximation to log(A), use three-term recurrence for forward/backward passes; include post-compensation to recover singular part, compare against other bases (Legendre, Laguerre, Taylor, Pade) and against sqrt-based methods; ensures all ops are GEMMs.

Result: Decomposition-free log via Chebyshev polynomial outperforms spectral log and sqrt-based methods on three fine-grained benchmarks and ImageNet-1k in accuracy and speed; log-target beats sqrt-target given equal basis and degree.

Conclusion: Faithful Riemannian mapping via a decomposition-free log is superior for GCP; the improvement stems from preserving the manifold geometry rather than using a particular polynomial family.

Abstract: Global Covariance Pooling (GCP) improves deep networks by capturing second-order feature statistics, and is especially effective for fine-grained recognition. Because covariance matrices live on the Symmetric Positive Definite (SPD) manifold, a normalization step is required before the Euclidean classifier. The faithful choice is the matrix logarithm (MLN-COV), which maps the SPD manifold to its tangent space; in practice it was abandoned in favour of the matrix square root because its eigendecomposition-based gradient is numerically unstable. We show that this instability is an artifact of computing the logarithm spectrally, not of the logarithm itself. Approximating the logarithm with finite polynomials in the covariance matrix removes the eigendecomposition from both passes: every operation becomes a General Matrix Multiplication (GEMM), the gradient stays bounded on the spectral support of the pre-normalized covariance, and the unstable 1/(lambda_i-lambda_j) term never appears. The key ingredient is a mean-eigenvalue pre-normalization that centres the spectrum near 1, away from the singularity of log, with a scalar post-compensation that returns the singular part of log(A) in closed form. Our recommended normalizer is a degree-8 Chebyshev expansion evaluated by a three-term matrix recurrence, with a matching reverse recurrence for the backward pass; Legendre, Laguerre, Taylor and Pade expansions are studied as controls that isolate the roles of the basis and of the target function. On three fine-grained benchmarks and ImageNet-1k the decomposition-free logarithm is both faster and more accurate than the spectral logarithm and than the square-root approximations it replaces, and at matched basis and degree the log target beats the square-root target, confirming that the gain comes from the faithful Riemannian map rather than from a better polynomial family.

</details>


### [74] [Counterfactual Contrastive Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19032)
*Yunlong He,Pietro Gori*

Main category: cs.CV

TL;DR: A classifier-free VCE method using contrastive analysis to generate high-fidelity, model-agnostic counterfactuals by swapping salient factors between two datasets (e.g., healthy vs. diseased) within StyleGAN2's latent feature space, showing improved quality on medical imaging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses classifier-dependent VCEs and susceptibility to biases and shortcut features by creating model-agnostic explanations that do not rely on decision boundaries.
