To bridge this gap, we introduce LabDex, a large-scale real-world dataset and benchmark for dexterous manipulation in chemistry laboratories, organized around a hierarchical task taxonomy spanning atomic skills, compositional tasks, and long-horizon experiments. First, LabDex is cross-platform and, for the first time, unifies real-world and simulation platforms under a common framework, providing standardized task definitions, demonstrations, and evaluation protocols. Second, LabDex is large-scale and systematically organizes chemistry laboratory operations into three interconnected levels: Atomic Skills, which characterize fundamental dexterous manipulation capabilities; Compositional Skills; and Long-Horizon Laboratory Workflows. This hierarchical design not only supports the evaluation of end-task performance, but also enables the analysis of how fundamental dexterous skills compose and influence more complex laboratory operations. We conduct cross-level evaluations of representative robot learning methods in both real-world and simulation environments. The experimental results validate the effectiveness of the LabDex task design and demonstration data, and show that the benchmark supports the training and systematic evaluation of existing robotic policies across laboratory dexterous manipulation tasks at different levels, providing a foundation for further research and development of autonomous laboratory robots.

</details>


### [100] [Evaluation of Monocular SLAM Systems on High-Altitude Nadir UAV Footage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18632)
*Gašper Spagnolo,Matej Dobrevski,Danijel Skočaj*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Aerial nadir monocular SLAM is highly challenging; benchmarking shows limited reliability with notable variability by environment; DROID-SLAM is best overall but vertical accuracy and global trajectory integrity remain poor.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate robustness of monocular SLAM for aerial nadir video without inertial or GNSS aiding to identify limits, dataset effects, and trajectory-scale dependencies.

Method: Benchmark five monocular SLAM systems on three datasets (local UAV flights, synthetic city-scale imagery, long-range aerial sequences) with no inertial/GNSS input. Assess horizontal path-length error (as % of reference) and trajectory integrity, including vertical accuracy and loop closure ability.

Result: MASt3R-SLAM achieves the lowest mean horizontal MAE on five DJI flights (≈0.53% of reference path length). DROID-SLAM performs best overall, averaging ≈2.88% of reference path length across completed runs. None consistently preserves global trajectory shape on long GES/ALTO sequences; vertical position remains poor and large-area trajectories are highly distorted despite loop closure.

Conclusion: Monocular SLAM in the aerial nadir regime is insufficient for reliable visual-only navigation; environment, trajectory scale, and perceptual aliasing strongly limit performance, indicating a need for additional sensing or robust fusion.

Abstract: Aerial nadir video combines weak geometric constraints with severe perceptual aliasing, making it a difficult regime for monocular SLAM. We benchmark five monocular SLAM systems on local UAV flights, synthetic city-scale imagery, and long-range aerial sequences. To isolate visual performance, we provide no inertial or GNSS aiding. Performance varies strongly with environment and trajectory scale: MASt3R-SLAM achieves the lowest mean horizontal MAE on the five DJI flights (0.53\% of reference path length), whereas no system consistently preserves global trajectory shape on the long GES and ALTO sequences. Overall, DROID-SLAM performs best, averaging 2.88\% of reference path length across completed runs. Vertical position remains poor, and large-area trajectories remain highly distorted despite loop-closure capability. Current monocular SLAM methods are by themselves therefore insufficient for reliable visual-only aerial navigation.

</details>


### [101] [Progressive Experience Fusion for Multi-Task World Model Control in Endovascular Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18647)
*Harry Robertshaw,Maxence Boels,Nikola Fischer,Sebastien Ourselin,Christos Bergeles,Alejandro Granados,Thomas C Booth*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PEF with adaptive horizon planning improves autonomous endovascular navigation across multiple vasculatures; demonstrates transfer to unseen in vitro vasculature; requires further clinical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable autonomous delivery of mechanical thrombectomy across diverse vascular anatomies and enable patient-specific adaptation to reduce inter-hospital transfer times.

Method: Train a multi-task TD-MPC2 controller using Progressive Experience Fusion (PEF) across five subtasks in ten training anatomies with held-out targets; evaluate a heuristic that adjusts Model Predictive Path Integral horizon via residual action-sequence dispersion; perform fine-tuning in a patient-specific simulation; train with 30 vasculatures for adaptive-horizon planning; test transfer to unseen in vitro vasculature; evaluate path ratios and success rates with statistical tests.

Result: PEF achieved 74% mean success vs 37% Soft Actor-Critic and 65% base TD-MPC2; p<0.001; adaptive-horizon PEF achieved 90% success in held-out vasculatures; transfer to unseen in vitro vasculature improved path ratio from 63% to 80% with fine-tuning (p<0.001) after ~40k steps (~107 minutes of inter-hospital transfer time).

Conclusion: This work demonstrates proof of concept for multi-vasculature training and patient-specific adaptation; clinical deployment requires further validation.

Abstract: Autonomous endovascular navigation could support the delivery of mechanical thrombectomy to underserved areas, but controllers must navigate long, multi-stage paths across varying vascular anatomies. This study investigates Progressive Experience Fusion (PEF) to train a multi-task TD-MPC2 controller. We additionally evaluate a heuristic that changes the Model Predictive Path Integral planning horizon using residual action-sequence dispersion, and fine-tuning in a patient-specific simulation. Across five subtasks in ten known training anatomies with held-out targets, PEF achieved a mean success rate of 74%, compared with 37% for Soft Actor-Critic (p < 0.001) and 65% for base TD-MPC2 (p = 0.053). A PEF controller with adaptive-horizon planning trained on 30 vasculatures achieved a mean success rate of 90% in ten held-out vasculatures. The PEF agent successfully transferred to an unseen in vitro stroke patient vasculature under fluoroscopy, achieving a mean path ratio improvement from 63% to 80% with fine-tuning (p < 0.001), following 40x103 fine-tuning steps (corresponding to approximately 107 min of clinical inter-hospital transfer time). This work represents a proof of concept for multi-vasculature training and patient-specific adaptation, while further validation is required before clinical deployment.

</details>


### [102] [Orienteering Problem with Uncertain Time-Varying Rewards: Framework and Benchmark for Everyday Service Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18672)
*Masafumi Endo,Kohei Honda,Yuu Jinnai,Ryo Yonetani*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces OP-UTVR, a time-varying uncertain reward variant of OP; develops three planners with varying horizons/adaptivity; provides theoretical bounds; benchmarks a mobile service robot; experiments show long-horizon planning with online adaptation improves performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-world OP with uncertain rewards caused by dynamic demand; existing OP assumes fixed rewards; need predictive estimates and online adaptation to maintain good routing decisions.

Method: Formulation of OP-UTVR; design of three planners with different planning horizons and adaptivity levels; derivation of performance bounds under reward stochasticity; creation of a mobile service robot indoor benchmark with pedestrians; experimental evaluation comparing planners.

Result: Theoretical performance bounds established; empirical results reveal trade-offs between horizon length and adaptivity; long-horizon planning with online adaptation is effective; the benchmark validates applicability.

Conclusion: OP-UTVR provides a framework for uncertain, time-varying rewards; planning horizon and online adaptation critically affect performance; future work includes improving reward forecasting, robustness, and scaling; potential extensions to other domains.

Abstract: We present the orienteering problem with uncertain time-varying rewards (OP-UTVR), a novel variant of the orienteering problem (OP). While most existing OP formulations assume rewards to be known in advance, practical applications involve uncertain and time-varying rewards, as with shifting customer demand for delivery agents. OP-UTVR relaxes this assumption by allowing agents to estimate reward dynamics from observations and forecast future rewards. This enables informed routing decisions despite stochastic reward changes and inevitable prediction errors. We address this problem using three planners that differ in planning horizon and online adaptivity, and derive theoretical bounds on their performance under reward stochasticity. We further introduce a mobile service robot benchmark for OP-UTVR, where a robot navigates among pedestrians in indoor environments. Experiments reveal trade-offs between planning horizon and adaptivity, and demonstrate the effectiveness of long-horizon planning with online adaptation.

</details>


### [103] [SoftVTBench: A Deformation-Aware Visuo-Tactile Dataset and Benchmark for Deformable-Object Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18701)
*Bowen Jing,Mingxin Wang,Ruiyang Hao,Chenchen Ge,Hanwen Shen,Junjie He,Yang Cui,Yiming Hou,Weitao Zhou,Jiawei Wang,Minglei Li,Dandan Zhang,Ding Zhao,Houde Liu,Xiaofan Li,Si Liu,Ping Luo,Haibao Yu*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SoftVTBench: a visuo-tactile dataset and benchmark for deformation-aware manipulation; 4,000 expert demonstrations, 50+ assets, introduces Deformation-aware Success Rate (DSR); under distribution shift, visuo-tactile data improves success/DSR in several configurations, but touch alone does not guarantee better multimodal fusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the lack of visuo-tactile datasets that pair policy-visible contact observations with independent physical ground truth over complete tasks, enabling evaluation of how touch affects interaction quality with deformable objects.

Method: Assembles SoftVTBench with 4,000 expert demonstrations and 50+ assets (deformable objects and rigid twins). Records at 20 Hz with multi-view RGB, dual-finger tactile RGB, marker motion, proprioception, language, and gripper actions, plus evaluator FEM states. Defines Deformation-aware Success Rate (DSR) using fixed object-specific calibration. Evaluates three policies (Diffusion Policy, π0.5, FastWAM) across 12 in-distribution configurations; tests distribution shift comparing visuo-tactile vs baseline variants.

Result: Across in-distribution configurations, 0.7–24% of successes violate the deformation tolerance. Under distribution shift, visuo-tactile variants achieve higher task success in all six policy–suite comparisons and higher DSR in five, with mixed benefits in-distribution. Concludes that simply providing touch does not guarantee improved multimodal fusion; SoftVTBench offers a common resource to study the interaction with deformable objects and when touch helps.

Conclusion: SoftVTBench provides a standard visuo-tactile resource to analyze not only success, but the nature of physical interaction with deformable objects and the role of touch in improving interaction, under both nominal and shifted distributions.

Abstract: Physical interaction quality is central to deformable-object manipulation, yet most benchmarks evaluate task success alone. A policy may complete the task while allowing slip or causing excessive compression. A primary bottleneck is the absence of visuo-tactile datasets that pair policy-visible contact observations with independent physical ground truth over complete tasks. We introduce SoftVTBench, a visuo-tactile dataset for physical-interaction-aware deformable-object manipulation. It contains 4,000 expert demonstrations and more than 50 assets, including volumetric deformable objects and visually matched rigid twins. At 20 Hz, each episode synchronizes multi-view RGB, dual-finger tactile RGB and marker motion, proprioception, language, and binary and continuous gripper actions, alongside evaluator-only finite-element (FEM) states. Building upon this dataset, we establish a closed-loop benchmark that uses fixed object-specific calibration to define the Deformation-aware Success Rate (DSR), which counts a rollout as successful only when it completes the task and keeps peak normalized deformation within tolerance. Across Diffusion Policy, $π_{0.5}$, and FastWAM, all 12 in-distribution configurations contain successful rollouts that violate the deformation tolerance, accounting for 0.7--24% of each configuration's successes. Under distribution shift, visuo-tactile variants achieve higher task success in all six policy--suite comparisons and higher DSR in five, whereas their in-distribution benefits are mixed. These results show that making touch available does not by itself ensure effective multimodal fusion. SoftVTBench therefore provides a common visuo-tactile resource for studying not only whether a policy succeeds, but how it physically interacts with deformable objects and when touch improves that interaction.

</details>


### [104] [Dream2Reward: Transition-Alignment Reward Models from Positive Demonstrations for Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18787)
*Haoyu Zhang,Zecui Zeng,Bin Wang,Lusong Li,Liang Lin,Long Cheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Dream2Reward learns a language-conditioned latent transition field from positive demonstrations to produce dense rewards by comparing observed motion with an predicted successful change, improving success–failure separation and reducing reward hacking without failure annotations; it benefits online/offline policy learning and even real-robot manipulation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense, informative rewards are needed for robot policy learning when demonstrations may degrade; progress-based rewards can misguide when an incorrect transition occurs. A reward signal grounded in the predicted successful transition from positive demonstrations can provide better guidance without failure labels.

Method: Train a language-conditioned latent transition field using positive demonstrations. Given the visual history at a transition start, predict the latent displacement for successful execution and score the observed displacement with signed directional and symmetric magnitude agreement. Penalize wrong direction, overshoot, and stagnation. No failure annotations or synthetic negatives required; yields a dense, causal reward.

Result: The approach yields stronger separation between success and failure across mechanism diagnostics and shared-trajectory evaluations, and provides more informative feedback on low-quality behavior than progress-based methods. In online and offline policy learning, using the frozen reward model reduces reward hacking and improves downstream performance, including in real-robot manipulation.

Conclusion: Comparing realized motion with predicted successful change is an effective strategy to convert positive demonstrations into dense rewards for robot learning and can enhance both learning efficiency and real-world performance.

Abstract: Learning robotic policies requires dense rewards that remain informative when behavior departs from successful demonstrations. Progress-based rewards estimate how far an observation has advanced along a nominal successful trajectory, but may remain high after an incorrect transition. We introduce Dream2Reward, which learns a language-conditioned successful latent transition field from positive demonstrations. Given the visual history up to a transition start, the model predicts the latent displacement associated with successful execution and scores the observed displacement through signed directional and symmetric magnitude agreement. This transition-level comparison penalizes wrong-direction, overshooting, and stagnant motion even when the resulting observation appears to show progress. Dream2Reward requires no failure annotations, progress labels, or synthetic negatives, and produces a dense causal reward. Across mechanism diagnostics and shared-trajectory evaluations, it provides stronger success-failure separation and more informative feedback on low-quality behavior than progress-based alternatives. Across online and offline policy learning, the same frozen reward model reduces reward hacking and supports stronger downstream performance, including in real-robot manipulation. These results show that comparing realized motion with predicted successful change provides an effective way to convert positive demonstrations into dense rewards for robot learning.

</details>


### [105] [Beyond Placement and Articulation: Usage-Driven Code Scenes for Embodied Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18840)
*Zijian Xiao,Zipeng Ye,Jinkun Hao,Xiong Yang,Yuchen Xie,Ran Yi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A framework (RoomWright) to synthesize 3D scenes as executable code, guided by usage and task-driven object reasoning with trigger-based interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond visual-only scene generation toward functional environments for embodied AI and policy learning by encoding object affordances, causal dependencies, and orientation guidance.

Method: Anchor-based task-centers for usage; incorporate task-required objects and their affordances; a code agent compiles interactions into trigger-condition-effect rules that update structured object states; annotation-informed usage-guided orientation; scenes are executable, editable, and simulation-ready.

Result: Extensive experiments show the method is effective; generated scenes are executable, editable, and ready for simulation in embodied AI and policy-learning contexts.

Conclusion: RoomWright provides a usage-driven pipeline to synthesize interactive, executable 3D scenes from code, linking object states and interactions to support embodied agents and learning protocols.

Abstract: Indoor scene synthesis provides essential environments for embodied AI, robotic manipulation, and simulation-based policy learning. Recent code-based scene generation methods produce editable and extensible environments, yet they remain focused on visual construction and object-level articulation, leaving the functional usage of scenes largely unmodeled. To address this problem, we present RoomWright, an agentic usage-driven framework for generating 3D scenes represented entirely as code for embodied interaction. RoomWright performs usage-driven object reasoning, which treats each anchor as a task centre and admits task-required objects and their affordances. A code agent further enables multi-part interaction by compiling each interaction into a trigger, condition, effect rule that updates structured object states, capturing causal dependencies across objects. Moreover, since manipuland orientation is ambiguous and hard to recover from pixels, RoomWright alleviates this via annotation-informed usage-guided orientation. Extensive experiments demonstrate the effectiveness of our method. The resulting scenes are executable, editable, and simulation-ready, providing interactive environments for embodied AI and policy learning.

</details>


### [106] [RoboEdit: Turning Human Manipulation Videos into Scalable Robot Experience](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18948)
*Yaowei Guo,Zeng Tao,Yuxin Jiang,Yunuo Chen,Zhiyang Dou,Yuxiang Ma,Yin Yang,Demetri Terzopoulos,Ying Jiang,Chenfanfu Jiang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: RoboEdit is a video-to-robot editing pipeline that converts human manipulation videos into aligned 3D robot hand states, enabling scalable supervision via a large multi-embodiment dataset (RoboEdit-14M).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data scarcity in robot hand-object interaction labels; abundant unlabeled human videos are not directly usable for robot learning. The work proposes cross-embodiment video editing and 3D retargeting to bridge this gap.

Method: RoboEdit suite including RoboEdit-ADC automatic pipeline for reconstructing/retargeting 3D interactions from RGB videos; RoboEdit-Trans editing engine with cross-embodiment adaptation; 3D Robot-State Decoder; RoboEdit-14M dataset (174K aligned video pairs, 14M frames, 7 robot embodiments, diverse scenes/interactions).

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art editing quality; improves downstream robot control policies in real-world manipulation tasks; demonstrates scalable, high-fidelity visual and 3D motion supervision for generalizable robot learning.

Conclusion: Unlocks the potential of unlabeled human videos for scalable, cross-embodiment robot learning by producing aligned video and 3D motion data for supervision.

Abstract: Collecting robot hand-object interaction data is costly and embodiment-specific, yet abundant human-object videos remain unusable for robot training. We present RoboEdit, a human-to-robot video editing suite that transforms human manipulation videos into action-consistent, physically plausible robot videos with aligned 3D hand states. To enable scalable supervision, we introduce RoboEdit-ADC, an automatic pipeline that reconstructs and retargets 3D interactions from RGB videos across embodiments. This pipeline generates RoboEdit-14M, a large-scale dataset of 174K aligned video pairs (14M frames) spanning seven robot embodiments, diverse scenes, and interaction types. The core editing engine, RoboEdit-Trans, employs cross-embodiment adaptation modules to preserve temporal coherence while adapting appearance and motion. It further integrates a 3D Robot-State Decoder to recover per-frame hand states for structured motion supervision. Experiments show that RoboEdit achieves state-of-the-art editing quality and supports downstream robot control policies in real-world manipulation tasks. Ultimately, the RoboEdit suite unlocks the vast potential of unlabeled human videos, providing scalable, high-fidelity visual and 3D motion supervision for generalizable robot learning.

</details>


### [107] [Autonomous Agricultural Tractor: Integrated Weed Detection and LiDAR Navigation for Precision Paddy Farming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19004)
*Benjamin Merryman-Smith,Tony Nguyen,Bilal Dogutas,Krish Shah,Anthony Raphael,Sudip Dhakal*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: AgriNav integrates ROS-based perception and fusion to enable robust site-specific weed management in paddy farming by addressing GNSS outages, weed-rice discrimination, and misclassification costs with a multi-module architecture and LiDAR-camera fusion; simulation shows strong row navigation, good weed detection, and ROI pruning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Significant herbicide reductions require robust field autonomy under canopy where GNSS is unreliable, plus real-time discriminative weed detection and minimizing costly rice misclassification.

Method: Four ROS modules: (1) PyTorch-reimplemented WeedDet for rice weed detection; (2) lightweight CNN-FPN with asymmetric class weighting; (3) inverted-logic veto to protect rice via confidence gate; (4) 6-state CV EKF fusing GNSS, IMU, wheel odometry; plus a LiDAR-camera fusion bridge for ROI constraint, world projection, ground-plane filtering, and bidirectional confidence fusion, built without extra hardware; simulation-based evaluation with GNSS outages and cross-domain imagery.

Result: Demonstrated continuous positioning during 20-second GNSS outage; crop-row detection confidence >0.9; rice detection confidence 0.32–0.95 across domains; LiDAR ROI reduces inference region by 30–50%.

Conclusion: Integrated system offers robust navigation and selective weeding with reduced computational load; requires real-world validation; ROI fusion helps efficiency; asymmetric priority of protecting rice improves safety against irreversible misclassification.

Abstract: Site-specific weed management in paddy farming offers substantial reductions in herbicide use over conventional broadcast spraying, but field deployment has been limited by three persistent challenges: robust crop-row navigation under canopy where GNSS degrades, real-time visual discrimination between rice and morphologically diverse weeds, and the asymmetric cost of misclassifying rice as weed, which is irreversible.
  This paper presents AgriNav, an integrated autonomous tractor system built around four ROS-coupled modules: a custom PyTorch reimplementation of WeedDet for rice detection, a parallel lightweight 1.68M-parameter CNN-FPN variant with asymmetric class weighting, an inverted-logic discrimination module that protects the rice class through a hardcoded confidence-gate veto, and a 6-state constant-velocity-turn-rate Extended Kalman Filter fusing GNSS, IMU, and wheel odometry with three-level outage bridging. Our primary system-level contribution is a four-mechanism LiDAR-camera fusion bridge that uses the navigation LiDAR for region-of-interest constraint, world-coordinate projection, ground-plane filtering, and bidirectional confidence fusion at zero additional hardware cost.
  Simulation experiments demonstrate continuous position tracking through a 20-second GNSS outage, crop row detection confidence above 0.9 throughout operation, and rice-detection confidences from 0.32 to 0.95 across paddy, aerial, and post-flood imagery. The LiDAR ROI constraint reduces detection inference region by an estimated 30 to 50 percent.

</details>


### [108] [LT-Mem: Volatility-Aware Spatio-Temporal Memory for Lifelong Scene Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19059)
*Yumin Lee,Hyoseok Ju,Giseop Kim*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: LT-Mem introduces a volatility-aware memory evolution framework for lifelong, object-centric perception across sessions, supported by a Tri-Memory structure (Live, Delta, Meta) and a multi-session SLAM backbone; accompanied by LT-VQA dataset. It achieves persistent object identity with lower token usage and strong ablations, outperforming baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome temporal amnesia in evolving environments where object history across sessions is lost when maps are overwritten or when semantic snapshots lack cross-session identity.

Method: A multi-session SLAM backbone aligns per-object observations across sessions. A reasoning layer uses deterministic evidence scoring to preserve cross-session identity and a volatility-aware policy to decide overwrite/hold/multi-hypothesis actions. Memory is stored in Tri-Memory (Live: current, Delta: recent changes, Meta: history/events). Introduces LT-VQA dataset with multi-session recordings and temporal QA tasks.

Result: LT-Mem consistently outperforms baselines across metrics; uses order of magnitude fewer tokens; ablations show gains from memory architecture rather than LLM size.

Conclusion: A structured memory architecture enables robust longitudinal object-centric reasoning in changing environments; LT-VQA provides a benchmark for evaluation; the framework reduces token usage while maintaining persistent object identity.

Abstract: Long-term robot operation in evolving environments requires object-level understanding that persists across repeated revisits. Existing systems either overwrite history to maintain an up-to-date map or store semantic snapshots without consistent cross-session object identity, resulting in temporal amnesia: the systematic loss of object history that prevents answering queries such as "Where has the green chair been across all sessions?" We propose LT-Mem, a volatility-aware memory evolution framework that unifies spatially aligned instance-level 3D perception with volatility-conditioned temporal reasoning. First, a multi-session SLAM backbone provides spatially aligned per-object observations across sessions. Second, a reasoning layer governs how object memory evolves: deterministic evidence scoring preserves cross-session identity, and a volatility-aware policy selects among overwrite, hold, and multi-hypothesis actions based on each object's dynamics. Third, the resulting Tri-Memory structure (Live, Delta, Meta) preserves both current states and event histories, enabling longitudinal object-centric reasoning. We further introduce LT-VQA, a dataset and evaluation suite comprising multi-session recordings, persistent identity annotations, and temporal QA pairs. Experiments show that LT-Mem consistently outperforms baselines across all metrics while consuming an order of magnitude fewer tokens, and ablations confirm that gains are driven by the structured memory architecture rather than LLM capacity.

</details>


### [109] [DA-WAM: Decision-Aligned Future Latents for Driving World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19085)
*Ruiguo Zhong,Benshan Ma,Xiaolong Chen,Lang Zhang,Mingyue Feng,Yaonong Wang,Pei Liu,Jun Ma*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: DA-WAM unifies predictive world-model learning with planning by producing action-conditioned future latents per trajectory and scoring them with a dedicated future-aware objective, aided by an online encoder and momentum target to keep representations aligned during planning. Achieves state-of-the-art on NAVSIM benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap where predictive future representations are learned separately from planning optimization or shared across trajectories, leading to action-agnostic consequences. The goal is action-conditioned futures that directly inform trajectory selection and safety-conscious decision-making.

Method: Introduce an end-to-end framework that maintains predictive supervision during planner optimization via an online encoder and a stable momentum target. An action-conditioned predictor outputs a distinct future latent for each trajectory candidate. A future-latent-conditioned, factorized scorer evaluates these latents. The expert-matched trajectory supervision aligns the predicted future latent with the observed future representation, while hard negatives near planning boundaries provide additional safety supervision.

Result: Experiments on NAVSIM-v1 and NAVSIM-v2 show state-of-the-art performance. Ablations and diagnostics validate the contributions of online predictive supervision, action-conditioned futures, and the specialized scorer.

Conclusion: DA-WAM effectively aligns predictive world-model learning with decision-making, enabling action-specific future representations to influence trajectory selection in autonomous driving. The framework offers insights via ablations and confirms the value of maintaining predictive supervision during planning.

Abstract: Anticipating how scenes evolve under ego actions is fundamental to safe autonomous driving, yet the full potential of world models for decision-making remains unrealized. The critical challenge lies in ensuring that future modeling is not merely predictive, but decision-informative: the predicted future must directly shape which trajectory is selected. Existing approaches decouple future representation learning from planning optimization, or share predicted states across trajectory candidates, thereby diluting the action-specific consequences that ought to guide selection. To bridge this gap, we propose DA-WAM, a framework that unifies predictive representation learning, action-conditioned future modeling, and trajectory scoring under a single decision-making objective. DA-WAM maintains predictive supervision throughout planner optimization via an online encoder and a stable momentum target, allowing future representations to co-evolve with the driving task. An action-conditioned predictor generates a distinct future latent state per trajectory candidate, which is then evaluated by a future-latent-conditioned factorized scorer. For the expert-matched trajectory, the predicted future latent is supervised by the observed future representation, while safety-critical hard negatives provide additional supervision near planning boundaries. Extensive experiments on NAVSIM-v1 and NAVSIM-v2 demonstrate state-of-the-art performance, while ablations and diagnostic analyses validate the key components.

</details>


### [110] [ADEPT: Accelerating Dexterity via Pre-Training and Post-Training using Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19182)
*Jayjun Lee,Jessica Yin,Asif Rana,Nicholas Blauch,Sam Mady,Mohak Bhardwaj,Nima Fazeli,Nathan Ratliff,Karl Van Wyk,Ankur Handa*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: ADEPT is a pretraining-and-distillation framework enabling sim-to-real transfer for high-DoF dexterous manipulation. It pretrains on a generic object reposing task, then post-trains downstream tasks with the pretrained policy as a prior, using a stable recipe (behavior-cloning distillation, critic warm-up, conservative on-policy updates) and a joint-space Geometric Fabric to couple RL with the robot. Post-training distills into perceptive students that achieve zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on two 23/29-DoF embodiments, solving long-horizon tasks from challenging initial states with human-level dexterity speed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Learning dexterous manipulation with many DoFs and visuo-tactile input is sample-inefficient and brittle to sim-to-real gaps. Reusing a pretrained dexterous behavior across downstream tasks can improve data efficiency and enable zero-shot transfer if supported by stable adaptation and robust perception.

Method: Pretrain a dexterous policy on a generic object reposing task. Post-train downstream tasks using this pretrained behavior as a prior, employing a stable recipe: behavior-cloning distillation, critic warm-up, and conservative on-policy updates. Introduce a joint-space Geometric Fabric to mediate between the RL policy and the robot. Distill post-trained teachers into perceptive students for zero-shot sim-to-real transfer. Validate on two embodiments: 23-DoF Kuka-Allegro with two RGB cameras; 29-DoF Flexiv-Sharpa with two RGB cameras and five vision-based tactile sensors.

Result: The approach enables zero-shot sim-to-real transfer for the downstream tasks on both embodiments and solves long-horizon tasks from challenging initial states with dexterity at human-level speed.

Conclusion: ADEPT demonstrates that pretraining plus stable post-training and distillation can unlock cross-robot dexterity transfer for complex robotic hands, reducing task-specific relearning and enabling practical sim-to-real performance with high DoF systems.

Abstract: We introduce Accelerating Dexterity via Pre-Training (ADEPT), a large-scale reinforcement learning (RL) framework for learning sim-to-real transferable dexterity across high degree-of-freedom (DoF) robot embodiments that can solve long-horizon tasks directly from raw visuo-tactile perception. ADEPT pretrains a dexterous policy on a generic object reposing task, then post-trains downstream policies with this pretrained behavior as a prior. ADEPT enables learning new behaviors that are otherwise difficult to discover from scratch on multi-fingered robots and avoids learning the same set of skills over again for every new downstream task. The pretrained policy zero-shots the reposing phase of downstream tasks, but naïve RL fine-tuning rapidly degrades this capability during transfer. We address this with a stable post-training recipe combining behavior-cloning distillation, critic warm-up, and conservative on-policy updates. To safely exploit the full kinematic dexterity, we introduce a joint-space Geometric Fabric that mediates between the RL policy and the robot. We distill post-trained teachers into perceptive students that zero-shot sim-to-real transfer on two embodiments: a 23 DoF Kuka-Allegro with two RGB cameras, and a 29 DoF Flexiv-Sharpa with two RGB cameras and five vision-based tactile sensors, and can solve long-horizon tasks from challenging initial states with dexterity at human-level speed.

</details>


### [111] [PartialBiGrasp: Inferring Hidden Local Geometry for Bimanual Grasping from Partial Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19188)
*Ayush Kaura,Vignesh Vembar,Md Faizal Karim,Keshab Patra,K Madhava Krishna*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: PartialBiGrasp: dual-arm grasp generation from partial point clouds using convolutional occupancy networks to reason about graspability and collision-free regions, followed by sampling-based refinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Large, heavy, geometrically complex objects often have sparse graspable regions and full-point-cloud data may be unavailable in real scenarios; existing bimanual methods rely on complete geometry, limiting applicability under partial observations.

Method: Learn implicit geometric features from partial observations with convolutional occupancy networks to enable local reasoning about graspability, collision-free contact regions, and object thickness; generate force-closure compliant dual-arm grasp pairs; refine via sampling-based optimization to resolve ambiguity due to incomplete geometry.

Result: Analytical force-closure metrics, large-scale simulations, and real-world experiments on noisy partial point clouds show robust and physically stable dual-arm grasp generation.

Conclusion: PartialBiGrasp enables robust dual-arm grasp planning under partial observations by combining occupancy-based geometric reasoning with optimization-based refinement.

Abstract: Dual-arm robotic grasping is essential for manipulating large, heavy, and geometrically complex objects that cannot be reliably handled using a single manipulator. These large objects often contain only sparse graspable regions determined by local geometric properties such as thickness, edge structure, and gripper clearance. Prior bimanual grasping methods assume access to a full point cloud of the object which inherently contains this geometric information, but may not be accessible in real scenarios. This work proposes PartialBiGrasp, a dual-arm grasp generation framework that operates directly on partial point cloud observations. Our model learns geometric features implicitly through convolutional occupancy networks, enabling local reasoning about graspability, collision-free contact regions, and object thickness. We leverage this understanding to generate force-closure compliant grasp pairs, which are further refined using a sampling-based optimization to correct for ambiguity caused by incomplete geometry. We evaluate our approach using analytical force-closure metrics, large-scale simulation experiments, and real-world robot evaluations on noisy partial point clouds of novel objects, demonstrating robust and physically stable dual-arm grasp generation.

</details>


<div id='cs.CG'></div>

# cs.CG [[Back]](#toc)

### [112] [Computing All Optimal Partial $p$-Wasserstein Matchings on the Line](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18875)
*Sebastian Angrick,Jacobus Conradi,Mónika Csikós,Niko Hastrich,Danny Mittal,André Nusser,Krzystof Onak,Sharath Raghvendra*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Proposes an FFT-based data structure to compute all optimal partial transports on the line for any finite p ≥ 1 in O(p n log^2 n) time, plus a conditional subquadratic lower bound for p = ∞ under the (min,+)-Convolution Hypothesis, and provides an open-source implementation; shows a complexity separation from full OT (O(n log n)).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Partial and unbalanced transport on the real line is less understood for p > 1, with existing algorithms being quadratic in n. The paper extends the efficiently solvable p = 1 case to all finite p and seeks a faster, unified approach.

Method: Develops an FFT-based data structure for balanced-interval transport queries and reduces all-optimal-partial-transport on the line to these queries, yielding an O(p n log^2 n) algorithm for finite p ≥ 1; provides an open-source C++ implementation.

Result: Algorithm computes all optimal partial transports on the line for every finite p ≥ 1 in O(p n log^2 n) time, outperforming previous quadratic baselines; empirical results on synthetic data; implementation available.

Conclusion: This work bridges the gap between p = 1 and general p for partial transport on the line, establishing a near-quadratic improvement and a conditional lower bound for p = ∞, while highlighting a clear separation from full OT which remains O(n log n).

Abstract: For $p \ge 1$, the $p$-Wasserstein distance measures the minimum cost of transporting probability mass between distributions, where moving unit mass between two points costs the $p$th power of their distance. For discrete distributions in one dimension, full transport is especially simple: after sorting, mass is matched in order along the line. By contrast, partial and unbalanced transport on the line remains much less understood. Recently, Chapel and Tavenard [ICLR'25] showed that, for $p=1$, all optimal partial transport plans between distributions supported on $n$ points, with uniform mass at each point, can be computed in $O(n\log n)$ time by exploiting the metric structure of the cost. For $p>1$, this structure no longer applies, and existing approaches require $Ω(n^2)$ time. Our main contribution is an FFT-based data structure for balanced-interval transport queries, which bypasses this quadratic bottleneck and yields an $O(p\,n\log^2 n)$-time algorithm for computing all optimal partial transports on the line for every finite $p\ge 1$. We also provide an open-source C++ implementation that outperforms the state-of-the-art baseline on a range of synthetic instances. Finally, we establish a conditional lower bound for $p=\infty$: any subquadratic-time algorithm for computing all optimal partial transport plan costs on the line would violate the $(\min,+)$-Convolution Hypothesis. This separates the problem from full optimal transport, which is solvable in $O(n\log n)$.

</details>


<div id='cs.LG'></div>

# cs.LG [[Back]](#toc)

### [113] [Entropy-Constrained Adaptive Stochastic Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18147)
*Ran Ben Basat,Yaniv Ben-Itzhak,Michael Mitzenmacher,Shay Vargaftik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Entropy-constrained adaptive stochastic quantization (ECASQ) jointly optimizes quantization values under an entropy budget and unbiasedness, offering exact and GPU-friendly approximate dynamic programs with guaranteed MSE bounds, plus an iterative refinement to boost practical performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing unbiased quantization (e.g., ASQ) ignores the entropy-encoding stage, leaving potential accuracy gains on the table. ECASQ aims to minimize MSE end-to-end by incorporating the entropy budget from subsequent lossless encoding, improving compression performance for model/gradient/KV-cache data and related workloads.

Method: Formulate ECASQ as a constrained optimization: minimize MSE subject to an entropy budget and an unbiasedness constraint. Provide an exact dynamic program with O(s d^2) time and O(d^2) space for a vector of length d with at most s quantization values, and a GPU-friendly approximate dynamic program with O(s d^2) time and O(d) space. The approximate solution has a provable guarantee: its MSE is no larger than the optimal solution that uses one fewer bit of entropy per entry. Also present an iterative refinement procedure to improve the approximate solution.

Result: An optimal DP solver (O(s d^2) time, O(d^2) space) and a GPU-efficient approximate DP solver (O(s d^2) time, O(d) space) for ECASQ, with an MSE guarantee relative to a one-bit-reduced-entropy baseline. An iterative refinement method further delivers near-optimal results with substantial speed advantages over the exact solver.

Conclusion: ECASQ provides a principled, end-to-end quantization strategy that jointly accounts for the subsequent entropy encoding, enabling better trade-offs between MSE and entropy. It offers practical solvers with formal guarantees and an efficient refinement path for large-scale applications.

Abstract: Adaptive stochastic quantization (ASQ) is a recently introduced quantization approach that optimizes the Mean Squared Error (MSE) for a given input while preserving unbiasedness. It is designed to alleviate the communication and memory bottlenecks of modern data and machine learning workloads, including model, gradient, and KV-cache compression and nearest-neighbor search. Further, practical systems can then compress quantized data with a lossless entropy encoder. However, existing unbiased methods, including ASQ, choose their quantization values without considering this later encoding stage, leaving accuracy on the table.
  We formulate the Entropy Constrained Adaptive Stochastic Quantization (ECASQ) problem, which jointly selects adaptive quantization values to minimize MSE under an entropy budget and an unbiasedness constraint. We give an optimal dynamic program with $O(sd^2)$ time and $O(d^2)$ space for a length-d vector and at most s quantization values, and a GPU-friendly approximate dynamic program with $O(sd^2)$ time and $O(d)$ space. The approximation guarantees that the solution has an MSE no larger than the optimal solution that uses one fewer bit of entropy per entry. We also provide an iterative refinement procedure for the approximation solution that, in our experiments, yields near-optimal results while retaining a substantial speed advantage over our solver for the optimal solution.

</details>


### [114] [Towards Reversible Forgetting: Managing Obsolete Knowledge in Continual Enterprise AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18177)
*Nilutpaul Sarker Yash,Tirtho Roy,Ushashi Bhattacharjee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes reversible forgetting in continual learning for enterprise AI via a three-state memory (active, dormant, retired) with a reactivation pathway, instantiated as a Hysteretic Reversible Memory Controller to balance retention and adaptability in non-stationary environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-stationary enterprise contexts (customers, policies, tools, regulations, market regimes) can render previously useful knowledge harmful as environments evolve. Indiscriminate retention risks negative transfer and operational risk; need to separate temporary suppression from permanent erasure and allow knowledge to re-enter when relevant.

Method: Introduce a memory controller with three states (active, dormant, retired) and a reactivation transition; accumulate relevance evidence; apply asymmetric thresholds to prevent state oscillations; test reactivation in shadow mode; gate retirement via policy; instantiate as a Hysteretic Reversible Memory Controller; use finance as an illustrative domain.

Result: Conceptual framework with mechanisms to reduce influence of obsolete information; demonstrates reactivation in shadow mode; shows knowledge can regain relevance when conditions recur; finance illustrates the regime-dependent nature of usefulness.

Conclusion: Reversible forgetting extends continual learning to non-stationary enterprise AI, providing a practical mechanism to suppress obsolete knowledge while preserving potential for future relevance, thereby reducing negative transfer without erasing history.

Abstract: Continual learning has traditionally treated forgetting as a failure, emphasizing preservation of previously acquired knowledge as environments evolve. We argue that this objective is incomplete for enterprise AI agents operating in non-stationary environments, where customers, policies, tools, workflows, regulations, and market conditions change over time. Indiscriminate retention can allow obsolete knowledge to influence decisions, creating negative transfer and operational risk. We therefore propose reversible forgetting: a conceptual framework with three operational memory states: active, dormant, and retired, and a reactivation transition that can restore dormant knowledge when its relevance returns. We instantiate the framework as a Hysteretic Reversible Memory Controller that accumulates relevance evidence, uses asymmetric thresholds to prevent state oscillation, tests reactivation in shadow mode, and gates retirement through policy. The framework reduces the influence of obsolete information without conflating temporary suppression with permanent erasure. Finance illustrates the idea: knowledge useful under one market regime may become harmful under another yet regain relevance when similar conditions recur.

</details>


### [115] [Accelerating Visual On-Policy Distillation with Batched Speculative Jacobi Rollouts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18183)
*Bingqi Shan,Zhehao Yu,Kenhong Lin,Baoquan Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HB-SJD is a batched speculative Jacobi decoding rollout backend for visual on-policy distillation (OPD) that parallelizes token processing across images, reducing rollout and training time while maintaining generation quality; it dynamically switches between Full and Compact execution as images finish.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the high cost of online, token-by-token rollout in visual OPD by enabling parallel decoding without needing an auxiliary draft model.

Method: Introduce HB-SJD: a batched SJD rollout backend where each image progresses independently according to its own decoding progress, while sequence-position checks are performed in batched model forwards; as images complete, switch between Full and Compact execution to cut rollout cost; only replaces the student rollout backend; teacher, distillation objective, and optimization procedure remain unchanged.

Result: HB-SJD substantially reduces rollout and end-to-end training time in experiments with LlamaGen, while preserving the generation quality of the distilled student.

Conclusion: HB-SJD effectively accelerates visual OPD training by enabling batched, parallel decoding without altering the distillation framework, delivering significant efficiency gains with preserved model quality.

Abstract: Visual on-policy distillation (OPD) improves the training of compact visual autoregressive models by learning from trajectories generated by the current student. However, these online rollouts are still produced token by token with autoregressive decoding, which adds substantial cost to every on-policy training step. Speculative Jacobi Decoding (SJD) provides an alternative because it can process multiple tokens in parallel without an auxiliary draft model, but the original method is designed for single-sequence inference. We introduce HB-SJD, a batched SJD rollout backend for visual OPD. HB-SJD allows each image to advance independently according to its own decoding progress, while images at different sequence positions are still verified in batched model forwards. As images finish, HB-SJD switches between Full and Compact execution to reduce the cost of later rollout rounds. HB-SJD only replaces the student rollout backend and leaves the teacher, distillation objective, and optimization procedure unchanged. Experiments with LlamaGen show that HB-SJD substantially reduces rollout and end-to-end training time while preserving the generation quality of the distilled student.

</details>


### [116] [H$^2$EDL: Hyper Evidential Deep Learning for Hierarchical Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18185)
*Yuanye Liu,Xiahai Zhuang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes H^2EDL, a tree-structured hyper-opinion model for fine-grained hierarchical recognition that unifies uncertainty about coarse concepts and fine-grained descendants, using Dirichlet opinions at each branching node to assign mass in closed form.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the gap in uncertainty representation for fine-grained, hierarchically structured labels. Flat evidential classifiers capture total ignorance at leaves, while hierarchical classifiers miss explicit evidence semantics. A principled, scalable hyper-opinion framework is needed that respects taxonomy structure and provides consistent predictions across hierarchy levels.

Method: Introduce H^2EDL by leveraging the taxonomy as the hyperdomain. Each branching node gets a local Dirichlet opinion; this yields a linear-size focal family and closes-form composite masses. The model supports two interpretations: (i) a hierarchical classifier ensuring consistency across levels, (ii) a tree-structured hyper-opinion where node mass reflects belief to refrain from further specialization. Training leverages the taxonomy-derived composites without requiring explicit composite labels; masses are induced analytically from the local Dirichlet opinions.

Result: On FGVC-Aircraft and DERM12345, H^2EDL reduces calibration error by about 50% compared with cross-entropy baselines, with larger gains for deeper hierarchy levels and bigger training budgets.

Conclusion: H^2EDL provides a principled, scalable integration of evidence and hierarchy for fine-grained recognition. It yields calibrated, consistent predictions and offers dual interpretations (predictive classifier and probabilistic hyper-opinion) grounded in the taxonomy structure.

Abstract: Fine-grained recognition often involves hierarchical label spaces, where a model may be confident about a coarse semantic concept while remaining uncertain among its descendant classes. Such structured ambiguity requires uncertainty representations that capture both fine-grained classes and intermediate concepts. However, existing tools each capture only half of it: flat evidential classifiers quantify total ignorance with a single vacuity on the leaf frame, and hierarchical classifiers propagate point probabilities with no notion of evidence. Hyper-opinions would unify the two, but their general form is exponential in the label count, and existing hyper-evidential networks either require composite labels to be supplied in the training data or read them off an unstructured weight pattern, with no principled notion of which composites deserve mass. We observe that the taxonomy itself is the missing hyperdomain. Its subtrees and leaf singletons form a linear-size focal family, and one local Dirichlet opinion per branching node induces every composite mass in closed form. The resulting model, H$^2$EDL, can be interpreted in two complementary ways using the same set of parameters. From a prediction perspective, it functions as a hierarchical classifier that preserves consistency across different levels of the label tree. From a probabilistic perspective, it defines a valid tree-structured hyper-opinion, where the mass assigned to each node represents the belief that reaches that node but does not provide sufficient confidence to further specialize into its descendants. On FGVC-Aircraft and DERM12345, H$^2$EDL reduces calibration error by approximately half compared with cross-entropy baselines, with the improvement becoming more pronounced at deeper hierarchy levels and under larger training budgets.

</details>


### [117] [What Can Artificial Intelligence Learn from Medicine? Generative Analogies and Reliable Machine Learning Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18186)
*Emanuele Ratti,Lena Zuchowski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper uses Hesse’s analysis of clinical translation to build an analogue for ML in medicine, proposing a reliabilist framework (ML reliabilism) that transfers warrants from clinical translation to ML, yielding a distinct yet compatible theory of ML reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address epistemic/methodological uncertainties of ML in medicine and provide a principled foundation by mapping clinical translation warrants onto ML using a reliabilist lens.

Method: Conceptual analysis drawing on Hesse’s work to identify warrants of clinical translation and examine their applicability to ML through reliabilist interpretation, culminating in a proposed ML reliabilism.

Result: Clarifies which clinical-translation warrants are invoked in analogies, demonstrates their valid applicability to ML, and yields a new form of ML reliabilism that is distinct from but compatible with existing reliabilist accounts in AI philosophy.

Conclusion: The study offers a principled grounding for evaluating ML in clinical contexts by translating clinical translation standards into ML reliabilism, potentially enhancing trustworthiness and acceptance of ML in medicine.

Abstract: In the past few years, machine learning (ML) has been widely (and to an extent, successfully) implemented in medicine. However, uncertainties surrounding ML have made it difficult to establish the bases of its epistemic and methodological warrants. In the literature, a parallel has been drawn between medicine and ML, suggesting that we should model epistemic and methodological standards for ML on the standards of clinical translation. By developing tools from Hesse work, we characterise the nature of this parallel as a generative analogy between the process of clinical translation and the process of building ML systems. We identify more precisely the epistemic and methodological warrants of clinical translation that are typically only mentioned when appealing to the analogy, and we show in which sense such warrants apply analogically to the context of ML. In particular, we interpret warrants of clinical translation in reliabilist terms, and we show how this can inform a new form of ML reliabilism, which is distinct from (though compatible with) existing reliabilist accounts in philosophy of AI.

</details>


### [118] [A systematic review of machine learning techniques to address diagnosis and treatment of autism: challenges and opportunities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18188)
*Rafael Muñoz-Terol,Jesús Peral,Sandra Amador,David Gil*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A 2017-2023 systematic review of ML in ASD analyzing 55 studies; supervised learning prevalent; DL rising with data; future interest in hybrid/unsupervised/fuzzy; emphasis on multimodal data integration and collaboration; wearable data for continuous monitoring; need larger ASD-specific datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ASD etiology unclear; early diagnosis crucial; ML can reveal features and correlations across heterogeneous data; aim to synthesize current trends, datasets, methods, and challenges.

Method: Systematic review of 55 studies (2017-2023), qualitative synthesis of ML techniques, datasets, modalities; identification of trends and gaps.

Result: Supervised learning dominates; DL expanding with data; hybrid/unsupervised/fuzzy methods potential; emphasis on multimodal data (genetic, clinical, wearable); wearable sensors enable continuous monitoring; datasets tailored to ASD needed; interdisciplinary collaboration.

Conclusion: Future ML models will rely on broader multimodal data integration, combining genetic, clinical, and sensor data; expansion of ASD-specific datasets; cross-disciplinary work to improve diagnostic accuracy and treatment outcomes.

Abstract: Autism spectrum disorder (ASD) is a developmental disability characterized by challenges in social interaction and communication. As the causes of ASD remain unclear, identifying relevant features and hidden correlations is crucial for early diagnosis. This systematic review evaluates 55 studies from 2017 to 2023 on the application of machine learning (ML) techniques to ASD. The primary objective is to examine recent ML applications in ASD research, identifying trends, techniques, and datasets that enhance diagnosis and treatment. Supervised learning methods dominate, as they align well with ASD diagnostic needs; however, the role of deep learning is expanding with greater data availability. Emerging techniques based on hybrid methods, where unsupervised, deep learning, and fuzzy logic could be included, will be interesting to observe in the future. The review highlights key challenges and opportunities, particularly the need for models that can integrate complex data -such as genetic and clinical information- to improve diagnostic accuracy and treatment outcomes. Additionally, incorporating innovative data sources, like wearable devices and biometric sensors, could enable continuous and non-intrusive monitoring, providing a more holistic understanding of ASD. Findings emphasize that addressing current challenges requires interdisciplinary collaboration and expanded datasets tailored to ASD. Future ML models will benefit from broader multimodal data integration, enabling researchers to more comprehensively address the complexities of ASD.

</details>


### [119] [Safe Domain Adaptation for Physics: Overcoming Nuisances, Label Shifts, and Simulation Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18190)
*Ivan Kharuk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Adaptive domain adaptation via reweighting to isolate genuine physical mismatch in domain transfer, plus a label-free model selection rule; avoids naive spectrum alignment when target quantity distributions differ.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In physics, domain shifts arise not only from nuisance effects but also from incorrect physics in simulations and from differences in the target quantity's distribution (e.g., energy spectra). Standard adversarial domain adaptation can misalign spectra and anchor results to the simulation prior, compromising physical realism.

Method: Introduce adaptive domain adaptation that reweights simulated events to focus the domain adaptation on the true physical mismatch, mitigating the impact of spectrum differences; pairs a reweighting mechanism with a label-free model selection rule to pick a near-best operation point.

Result: In a toy air-shower benchmark with controllable detector nuisance, physical simulation shift, and energy-spectrum shift, the method reduces bias from spectrum misalignment and concentrates adaptation on physical mismatch; standard adversarial methods fail by aligning spectra; the proposed approach yields more faithful transfer.

Conclusion: Adaptive domain adaptation provides a practical solution for mismatched domain distributions in physics applications, preserving physics-driven differences while avoiding spurious alignment; the label-free model selection rule enables robust hyperparameter tuning without labeled data.

Abstract: Domain adaptation is widely used to make neural networks trained on simulations applicable to experimental data. Its premise is that the two domains differ only in nuisances, and that the quantity of interest is distributed identically in both. In physics neither assumption holds: simulations can be wrong about the physics, and the distribution of the target quantity - an energy spectrum, a redshift distribution - is often the measurement itself. We study the consequences of such mismatches on a toy air-shower benchmark in which a detector-response nuisance, a physical simulation shift, and an energy-spectrum shift can be switched on separately or together. Standard adversarial adaptation handles the conditional shifts, but once the two spectra differ it aligns them, replacing an uncontrolled bias by one anchored on the simulation prior. We present adaptive domain adaptation, which reweights the simulated events so as to focus domain adaptation on the genuine physical mismatch alone. Since the predicted spectrum depends on model training configuration, we provide a label-free model selection rule for selecting the near-the-best operation point.

</details>


### [120] [ChiroEcho: extending automated bat vocalisation classification beyond the learned taxonomy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18191)
*Burooj Ghani,Welmoed Eversteijn,Milan van Hirtum,Juan Sebastián Cañas,Vincent J. Kalkman,Dan Stowell,A. Leonie Baier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A deep learning framework jointly predicts bat species and genus from echolocation calls, augmented by geographic distribution to resolve unseen species, expanding operational coverage across Europe.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bats are key ecosystem indicators and protected; passive acoustic monitoring is essential but species-level identification is challenging due to call variation, behavioural/environmental effects, and overlap. Geographic information is proposed as external constraints to extend taxonomy.

Method: Train a deep model to predict both species and genus from audio; at inference combine genus predictions with geographic species distributions. When a predicted genus contains only one species regionally, resolve to that species. Evaluate on 35 European bat species; analyze rare-species performance; conduct a held-out proof-of-principle experiment.

Result: Closed-set performance assessed; rare-species performance instability observed with limited data; genus+location can recover species labels unavailable to the species head. Geographic resolution raises operational coverage from 35/48 native species (73%) to 41/48 (85%). This is the broadest reported automatic European bat classification to date.

Conclusion: Geographic information effectively extends, rather than constrains, fine-grained taxonomy, enabling resolution of unseen classes; the framework offers a proof-of-principle for combining coarse predictions with external constraints to expand monitoring coverage.

Abstract: Bats are key indicators of ecosystem health and are protected throughout Europe, making reliable population monitoring a conservation priority. Their cryptic nocturnal lifestyle makes passive acoustic monitoring essential, yet automated identification remains difficult as echolocation calls vary with behaviour and environment and overlap among species. We present a deep learning framework that jointly predicts species and genus and combines genus predictions with geographic species distributions at inference. When only one species of a predicted genus occurs in a region, the framework can resolve species absent from the learned taxonomy. This reframes geographic information as a means of extending, rather than constraining, a classifier's effective taxonomy. Using recordings spanning 35 European bat species, we evaluate closed-set classification, examine the instability of performance estimates for sparsely represented species, and conduct a controlled held-out proof-of-principle experiment. The rare-species analysis shows how limited evaluation data can obscure species-level performance, while the held-out experiment shows that genus predictions and location can recover labels unavailable to the species head. Geographic resolution extends operational coverage from 35 to 41 of the 48 native European bat species, increasing coverage from 73% to 85%. To our knowledge, this is the broadest operational coverage reported for automated European bat classification. More broadly, the bat framework provides proof of principle for resolving unseen fine-grained classes by combining coarse predictions with transparent external constraints.

</details>


### [121] [Think Shallow, Solve Deep: Controlling Recurrent Dynamics for Reliable Test-Time Depth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18222)
*Ivan Viakhirev,Kirill Borodin,Amirah Almutairi,Serguei Barannikov,Maxim Abramov,Grach Mkrtchian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: The paper links the effect of extra test-time iterations in recurrent-depth reasoners to a measurable finite-time dynamical regime (settling, marginal, drifting): settling predicts depth-safety where further iterations cannot change the answer once per-step changes are small relative to the decoder margin. Empirically, settling operators generally maintain or improve accuracy with increased depth on several tasks, while non-settling (drifting) models can degrade. A single terminal fixed-point objective shifts the regime toward depth-safety and enables depth-safe carry propagation; removing it induces drift. Four operational criteria are proposed to assess test-time depth, enabling failure-mode cataloging, with Huggingface’s Huginn-3.5B used as a consistency check, showing non-settling behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand when additional test-time iterations of a recurrent update help or hurt answers, and to identify practical criteria and training objectives that yield depth-safe extrapolation in iterative neural systems.

Method: Introduce a measurable finite-time dynamical regime based on per-step displacement vs. decoder margin; derive a sufficient condition for depth-safety; perform empirical studies on algorithmic tasks trained with limited data, assess depth behavior across difficulty tiers, analyze the impact of a terminal fixed-point objective, and apply the same diagnostics to Huginn-3.5B to classify its regime.

Result: Settling operators tend not to degrade with added depth and can improve accuracy on harder unseen instances (e.g., Sudoku tasks improve from 0.19 to 0.34 past training horizon). A terminal fixed-point objective shifts regimes toward depth-safety; removing it causes drift and loss of gains; simple recurrence with such an objective yields depth-safe carry propagation. Four operational criteria help diagnose useful test-time depth, and the authors catalogue failure modes; Huginn-3.5B falls into the non-settling family.

Conclusion: The finite-time dynamical regime is a practical predictor of how test-time depth will affect outputs. Proper training objectives (e.g., a terminal fixed-point) and dynamical properties enable depth-safe extrapolation, while certain models exhibit drift. The proposed criteria provide actionable diagnostics for evaluating and guiding test-time depth across models.

Abstract: Recurrent-depth reasoners aim to solve harder problems by iterating their update longer at test time, but additional iterations can improve, preserve, or degrade an answer. We show that a measurable property of the trained operator, its finite-time dynamical regime (estimated as settling, marginal, or drifting), indicates which of these occurs. We give a sufficient condition for depth-safety: once an operator's per-step displacement is small relative to the decoder margin, the decoded answer cannot change under further iterations. Empirically, on algorithmic tasks trained from $800$ unaugmented examples per difficulty tier, settling operators do not degrade with added depth, and on some tasks convert it into higher accuracy on harder unseen instances (Sudoku, $0.19$ to $0.34$ past the training horizon). A single terminal fixed-point objective moves the regime and the depth behavior together: removing it induces drift and removes the gains, and adding it to a generic recurrence yields depth-safe extrapolation on carry propagation. We give four operational criteria for useful test-time depth, use them to catalogue failure modes, and, as a consistency check, apply the same measurements to Huginn-3.5B, which falls in the non-settling family.

</details>


### [122] [Allocating Recurrent Compute in Looped Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18230)
*Ruhai Lin,Yiyang Guo,Rui-Jie Zhu,Hao Ye,Jason K. Eshraghian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MixerLoop shows that selective loop of mixer blocks with a single FFN pass can outperform full recurrence and scale with reduced FLOPs; introduces ITR and marginal ITR to quantify cross-token influence trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate what to loop in recurrent LMs to gain benefits while reducing cost; define metrics to characterize influence propagation across layers and time.

Method: Propose Iterative Transport Rank (ITR) and marginal ITR; implement MixerLoop by looping each Gated DeltaNet mixer and applying FFN once; compare with no recurrence and FullLoop at 15M and 110M params; use finite context-off intervention to assess readout influence.

Result: MixerLoop beats FullLoop on aggregate CORE at 15M; at 110M retains 41.5% of CORE improvement; reduces recurrent-backbone FLOPs by 45.9%.

Conclusion: Recurrent depth benefits can be retained without re-running dense FFN, enabling cost-efficient recurrence; the concept of nonredundant influence through readout supports selective looping.

Abstract: Looped language models improve reasoning and knowledge manipulation by applying shared computation repeatedly. Existing systems usually repeat an entire layer stack, although a mixer and a dense feed-forward network (FFN) perform different operations and have different costs. We ask a narrower question: what should loop? We view recurrence as repeated composition of a state update and argue that an application is valuable when it exposes a new cross-position influence direction that remains observable at the task readout. Iterative Transport Rank (ITR) describes the cumulative influence trajectory; marginal ITR describes the nonredundant influence contributed by successive applications. This view motivates MixerLoop, which repeats each Gated DeltaNet mixer while applying its dense FFN once. We compare MixerLoop with no recurrence and full-block recurrence at 15M and 110M parameters under the same data, initialization, and architecture. A finite context-off intervention tests whether later mixer applications produce distinct, non-negligible, and beneficial changes at the final language-model readout. MixerLoop surpasses FullLoop on aggregate CORE at 15M and retains 41.5% of its CORE improvement at 110M while reducing recurrent-backbone projection FLOPs by 45.9%. These results show that the benefits of recurrent depth can be retained without repeatedly executing the dense FFN.

</details>


### [123] [Classifying Directional Trajectories Near Criticality in the Three-State Majority-Vote Model with Deep Belief Networks and Bidirectional GRUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18235)
*Mauricio A. Valle,Gonzalo A. Ruz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A DBN latent space partially separates four MV3 trajectory types; a Bi-GRU on DBN encodings yields near-perfect discrimination and enables real-time regime sensing, forming a hierarchical architecture for detecting critical transitions in agent-based opinion dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether static, unsupervised latent representations and subsequent temporal models can distinguish four dynamically distinct trajectory types in MV3, and whether such models can operate in real time to detect regime shifts.

Method: Pretrain a Gaussian-Bernoulli RBM-based DBN (784→4096→225→81) on static equilibrium samples to encode lattice snapshots into 81-d latent vectors; assess latent separation via t-SNE. Train a two-layer Bi-GRU on sequences of length T=50 of these encodings; evaluate classification via hidden-state space (with t-SNE visualization) on train and test. Apply the trained Bi-GRU in a sliding window to continuous MV3 dynamics for real-time regime sensing.

Result: DBN latent space shows partial separation of the four trajectory types; the Bi-GRU classifier achieves near-perfect separation in its hidden state space on both training and test sets; t-SNE confirms separation; sliding-window deployment demonstrates real-time detection of the current dynamical regime; overall establishes a hierarchical approach for detecting and classifying critical transitions in agent-based opinion dynamics models.

Conclusion: Static representations alone cannot fully resolve directional temporal structure, but combining them with a temporal model (Bi-GRU) yields effective discrimination of dynamical regimes and enables real-time monitoring of critical transitions in MV3.

Abstract: In this work, we investigate whether the latent representations learned by a Deep Belief Network (DBN) and a Bidirectional Gated Recurrent Unit (Bi-GRU) can discriminate among four dynamically distinct trajectory types in the three-state majority vote model (MV3): approach from disorder, approach from order, departure to disorder, and departure to order. The DBN, pre-trained in an unsupervised manner on static equilibrium samples via a Gaussian-Bernoulli Restricted Boltzmann Machine input layer and architecture $784 \to 4096 \to 225 \to 81$, encodes each lattice snapshot into an 81-dimensional latent vector. A t-SNE analysis of the DBN latent space reveals only partial separation of the four trajectory types, reflecting the fact that a model trained on static configurations cannot fully resolve directional temporal structure. A two-layer Bi-GRU classifier, trained on sequences of DBN-encoded snapshots of length $T = 50$, achieves near-perfect separation of all four trajectory types in its hidden state space, as confirmed by t-SNE visualization on both training and test sets. Furthermore, a sliding-window application of the trained Bi-GRU to continuous MV3 dynamics demonstrates its ability to sense the system's current dynamical regime in real-time. These results establish a principled hierarchical architecture for detecting and classifying critical transitions in agent-based opinion dynamics models.

</details>


### [124] [ClosureBench: A Constructive Benchmark for Compositional Graph Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18242)
*Stefano Goria*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ClosureBench is a programmatically generated, ground-truth verified benchmark for compositional graph-relational reasoning, enabling fresh-instance evaluation; key findings include memorization leakage on fixed test sets, a graph-reading/reasoning bottleneck as size/depth grow, and strong performance from models that synthesize executable programs (Ein or Python+NetworkX) with ~94% held-out accuracy on frontier scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome data contamination in fixed-test-set benchmarks and systematically study compositional graph-relational reasoning with verifiable ground truth and controllable difficulty.

Method: Generate on-demand tasks via Ein tensor-logic programs; 26 task categories across three compositional levels (L1-L3); vary difficulty along graph size, edge density, and query depth; evaluate models from 1.5B to frontier systems; measure memorization, reasoning robustness, and program-synthesis-based solution targets (Ein and Python+NetworkX).

Result: Three main findings: (1) memorization leakage on fixed test sets: fine-tuned models show a 19.3 percentage-point gap between accuracy on seen vs. fresh instances; (2) accuracy declines with larger graphs and deeper queries, with misreading NL graph descriptions causing reasoning on the wrong graph; this bottleneck persists across input formats (text, JSON edge list, adjacency matrix); (3) a 4B model trained to emit executable programs (targeting Ein or Python+NetworkX) maintains near-flat performance across compositional levels and reaches 94.3% held-out accuracy, indicating the value of verified program synthesis.

Conclusion: On-demand, program-verified generation makes the benchmark resistant to data contamination and highlights a reasoning bottleneck tied to graph comprehension; program-synthesis targets offer strong performance with lower token cost, suggesting a productive direction for scalable, verifiable reasoning benchmarks and models.
