Abstract: We introduce ClosureBench, a constructive benchmark for compositional graph-relational reasoning with programmatically verified ground truth. Unlike fixed-test-set benchmarks vulnerable to data contamination, ClosureBench generates instances on demand: each task's reference answer is computed by executing a program in the Ein tensor-logic language, ensuring machine-verified correctness. The benchmark spans 26 task categories at three compositional levels (L1-L3), with difficulty controlled along three independent axes: graph size, edge density, and query depth.
  We evaluate models from 1.5B open weights to frontier systems (o3, GPT-4.1, Gemini 2.5, Claude Sonnet 4) and report three findings. First, because the benchmark can always supply fresh instances, it measures memorisation directly: a model fine-tuned on a fixed test set shows a 19.3 percentage-point gap between its accuracy on seen and on fresh instances, which a static test set cannot reveal. We scope this to supervised fine-tuning on answer pairs, not pretraining contamination. Second, accuracy falls as graph size and query depth increase, and the two interact: models misread the graph from its natural-language description and then reason correctly over the wrong graph, so even the strongest frontier model degrades from atomic to compositional queries. This bottleneck is a property of the reasoning rather than the input format: it persists when the graph is given as a JSON edge list or an adjacency matrix instead of prose. Third, a 4B model fine-tuned to emit executable programs rather than answers stays nearly flat across compositional levels and approaches frontier accuracy (94.3% on held-out instances) at a fraction of the token cost. This holds for two program targets, Ein and Python+NetworkX, so it is a property of verified program synthesis rather than of one language.

</details>


### [125] [Bidirectional representational alignment between biological and artificial neural networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18244)
*Samuel Kostousov,Abhinn Kaushik,Brokoslaw Laschowski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A spectral-regularization framework can steer the geometry of learned representations to modulate bidirectional alignment between biological and artificial neural networks, yielding large gains in reverse predictivity and near-symmetric forward/reverse predictivity at mid-range spectral exponents.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address asymmetry in forward vs. reverse predictivity between model representations and neural responses by testing whether controlled manipulation of representational geometry can influence bidirectional alignment.

Method: Introduce spectral regularization into self-supervised vision models and assess bidirectional predictivity with neural data, examining effective dimensionality and shared representational subspace across varying spectral exponents.

Result: Steering spectral geometry substantially increases reverse predictivity with modest losses in forward predictivity, achieving a 55% relative improvement in bidirectional predictivity; effective dimensionality decreases and the shared subspace reorganizes so forward and reverse predictivity become near-symmetric at intermediate spectral exponents.

Conclusion: Representational geometry can be systematically steered to modulate bidirectional alignment between biological and artificial networks, offering a controllable mechanism to balance forward/reverse predictivity through spectral properties.

Abstract: Recent work has shown that representational alignment between biological and artificial neural networks is asymmetric: model representations predict neural responses much better than neural responses predict model representations. This asymmetry raises the question of whether representational geometry contributes to bidirectional representational alignment. We hypothesized that steering representational geometry during training can systematically influence bidirectional alignment. To test this hypothesis, we developed a computational framework that integrates spectral regularization with bidirectional predictivity analyses. As an initial demonstration, we evaluated our framework using self-supervised contrastive vision models. Steering the spectral geometry of the learned representations substantially increased reverse predictivity with modest reductions in forward predictivity, yielding a 55% relative improvement in bidirectional predictivity. These improvements were accompanied by reduced effective dimensionality and a reorganization of the shared representational subspace, within which forward and reverse predictivity became approximately symmetric at intermediate spectral exponents. Overall, these findings demonstrate that representational geometry can be systematically steered to modulate bidirectional representational alignment between biological and artificial neural networks.

</details>


### [126] [SIGMA: Symmetry-aware, Intelligent, Geometric, Multi-objective Adaptive Control for Robust, Dependable Traffic Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18263)
*Pratham Payra,Jagadish B,Tanmay Sen,Tanujit Chakraborty*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SIGMA is a symmetry-aware, language-guided, multi-objective traffic control framework that augments RL with an LLM to adapt objectives and uses orientation-invariant learning via rotational augmentation, plus offline-to-online training and reliability guarantees. It achieves better average and emergency queueing metrics and throughput in SUMO across Kolkata intersections, with robustness to LLM failures and geometric rotations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL-based traffic control methods fix objectives, ignore dynamic priority changes (e.g., emergencies), and struggle to generalize across geometrically similar intersections. There is a need for adaptive objective tuning, geometry-agnostic learning, and reliability guarantees to ensure safe, robust performance in real-world settings.

Method: SIGMA employs a multi-objective actor-critic controller whose objective weights are dynamically tuned by a large language model that translates natural-language emergency commands into priority vectors. It uses rotational augmentation to achieve orientation-invariant transferability across four-way intersections and an offline-to-online learning strategy for stable warm-start and gradual adaptation. Reliability properties (emergency service levels, graceful degradation under LLM failures, and demand sensitivity) are defined and assessed via bootstrap statistics.

Result: In SUMO, SIGMA outperforms fixed-time, actuated, and DQN baselines on four Kolkata intersections by reducing average and emergency waiting times, lowering queue lengths, and increasing throughput. Ablation studies corroborate the framework’s robustness to component failures and rotations, demonstrating improved transferability and reliability.

Conclusion: SIGMA delivers a reliable, language-guided, multi-objective traffic control system with statistical reliability assurance and better generalization across geometric configurations, addressing key RL limitations in real-time traffic management.

Abstract: Traffic signal control is a complex sequential decision-making problem requiring real-time adaptation and trade-offs among throughput, delay fairness, signal stability, and emergency vehicle priority. Existing RL methods often fix objectives, ignore dynamic priority changes, and fail to generalize across geometrically similar intersections.We propose SIGMA (Symmetry-aware, Intelligent, Geometric, Multi-objective Adaptive traffic control), an RL framework enhanced with a large language model (LLM) for adaptive objective tuning and orientation-invariant learning. SIGMA converts natural-language emergency commands into priority vectors for a multi-objective actor-critic controller, avoiding manual reward engineering. Rotational augmentation improves transferability across four-way intersections, while offline-to-online learning ensures stable initialization and gradual adaptation to changing traffic.We define reliability properties covering emergency service levels, graceful degradation under LLM failures, and demand sensitivity, validated via bootstrap statistics. Evaluated in SUMO on four Kolkata-based urban intersections against fixed-time, actuated, and DQN controllers, SIGMA reduces average/emergency waiting times and queue lengths, and boosts throughput. Ablation studies confirm robustness to component failures and geometric rotations. Overall, SIGMA offers a reliable, language-guided, multi-objective traffic control system with statistical reliability assurance.

</details>


### [127] [Rethinking Privileged Information in On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18271)
*Samyak Shrestha,Alexander Tessier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: OPSD gains do not reliably stem from privileged reference information; students often align with the base model's reasoning and can improve without the correct reference. A correct reference is not consistently beneficial across settings, and stronger reference alignment does not predict larger gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle whether on-policy self-distillation gains come from leveraging privileged reference information or from recovering existing reasoning behavior in the base model, and to understand how alignment signals relate to actual performance.

Method: Experiments on science and mathematics tasks using Qwen3 models (1.7B–8B). The study separates supervision induced by a correct privileged reference from supervision without the reference, then measures how each alignment correlates with changes in the student’s predictions. It varies teacher generation modes, model sizes, and training datasets, and uses controls from other problems to decompose and compare alignments.

Result: No consistent performance benefit from the correct reference across settings. Students can improve without the correct reference, and a solution from a different problem can outperform the correct solution on several math benchmarks. Student predictions align more with the base model's thinking than with reference supervision; controls reproduce much of both alignments. Stronger alignment with the correct reference does not reliably correlate with greater performance gains. Thus, performance gains and distributional alignment alone cannot determine the role of privileged reference information in OPSD.

Conclusion: OPSD performance and alignment metrics are insufficient to infer how privileged reference information contributes to student learning; alternative evaluation frameworks are needed to distinguish true utilization of reference signals from recovery of existing reasoning.

Abstract: On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) trains a student on its own responses using token-level supervision from the same model conditioned on privileged reference information. We investigate whether performance gains from OPSD show that the student learned the information in the reference or instead reflect recovery of reasoning behavior already present in the base model. We perform OPSD experiments on science and mathematics datasets using Qwen3 models ranging from 1.7B to 8B. Our analysis framework separates the supervision induced by the reference from the supervision provided by the teacher without the reference and measures how each aligns with changes in the student's predictions. The correct reference does not provide a consistent performance benefit across teacher generation modes, model sizes, and training datasets. Students can improve without the correct reference, and a solution from another problem can outperform the correct solution on several mathematical reasoning benchmarks. The student's predictions align more strongly with the base model's thinking behavior than with the supervision induced by the reference, but controls constructed from other problems reproduce much of both alignments. Moreover, stronger alignment attributable to the correct reference does not reliably coincide with a greater performance benefit from the reference. Performance gains and distributional alignment alone therefore cannot determine how privileged reference information contributes to student learning in OPSD.

</details>


### [128] [SingularClip: Preventing Spectral Collapse to Maintain Plasticity in Continual and Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18319)
*Tyler Kastner,Nimrod De La Vega,Amir-massoud Farahmand*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Loss of plasticity arises from increasing anisotropy in weight matrices' singular values during training. Introduces SingularClip to periodically clip singular values, mitigating plasticity loss and improving performance in continual supervised learning and deep RL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Neural networks trained on nonstationary tasks often fail to fit new targets; the growth of weight singular value anisotropy during training is identified as a novel source of plasticity loss.

Method: Propose SingularClip, a procedure that periodically clips the singular values of all weight matrices. Provide empirical and theoretical analysis of the phenomenon and evaluate across continual supervised learning and deep reinforcement learning tasks, comparing against baselines.

Result: SingularClip performs strongly against baselines across the tested tasks, demonstrating improved adaptation to new targets and mitigation of plasticity loss in both continual learning and deep RL settings.

Conclusion: Clamping the spectrum of weight matrices via SingularClip is an effective, generalizable approach to mitigate plasticity loss due to weight anisotropy growth, with applicability across domain tasks.

Abstract: Neural networks trained on nonstationary tasks frequently lose the ability to fit new targets, a phenomenon referred to as loss of plasticity. We identify a novel source of plasticity loss due to the growing anisotropy of weight matrices' singular values during training, and analyze this phenomenon both empirically and theoretically. To mitigate this issue, we introduce SingularClip, a procedure that periodically clips the singular values of all weight matrices. We show that SingularClip performs strongly against baselines across a range of tasks in both continual supervised learning and deep reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [129] [When Does Dynamic Ensembling Pay Off? Diagnosing Regionwise Gains in Regression under Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18330)
*Tianxin Zhou,Ruixi Lin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A probe-based cross-fitted estimator (D_CF5) predicts when regional dynamic ensembling beats static blends under distribution shift; strong empirical validation across shifts; introduces OpenRegShift for reproducible evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To decide when shifting trust across regions of the input space (dynamic ensembling) yields gains over the best static blend, using a small labeled target-domain probe.

Method: Define D_CF5 to estimate the cross-fitted gain of regionwise convex combination over the best static convex blend via a probe on labeled target data; evaluate across 12 dataset-shift pairs (and 16-pair sensitivity analysis), compare against alternative probe diagnostics, and deploy a Probe-Validated Ensemble Selector with gating against a static affine floor; release OpenRegShift.

Result: D_CF5 correlates with realized regionwise gains at dataset level Spearman +0.98 (CI [+0.83, +1.00], p=5e-5); in 16-pair sensitivity, Spearman +0.83; alternative probes reach at most +0.66; regionwise gain correlates strongly (0.98) while smooth covariate-dependent stacking shows near-zero correlation (0.01); dynamic gains arise with shift heterogeneity and local competence; 128-256 probe labels suffice; gating improves risk by providing 11% and 16% reductions in test risk in prospective deployment; OpenRegShift released.

Conclusion: A probe-based, cross-fitted estimator effectively predicts when regionwise dynamic ensembling is beneficial under distribution shift and supports a gating deployment strategy; the work provides a reproducible benchmark (OpenRegShift) for regression ensembles under shift.

Abstract: Whether input-dependent ("dynamic") combination of a regression model pool beats the best static blend depends on the shift and is rarely known before deployment. Can a small labeled target-domain probe tell us when reallocating trust across regions of the input space will pay off? We answer this with $\widehat{D}_{\mathrm{CF5}}$, which estimates from the probe the cross-fitted gain of the regionwise convex combination over the best static convex blend: the realizable value of deciding, region by region, whom to trust. Across a frozen suite of 12 dataset-shift pairs (spatial, temporal, domain, feature-cluster), $\widehat{D}_{\mathrm{CF5}}$ predicts realized regionwise test gains with dataset-level Spearman $+0.98$ (95% CI $[+0.83, +1.00]$; $p=5\times10^{-5}$), including two cases overturning preregistered expectations. The relationship holds in a 16-pair sensitivity analysis (Spearman $+0.83$), whereas alternative probe diagnostics reach at most $+0.66$. This contrast isolates regional trust reallocation: correlation is $+0.98$ for regionwise-convex gain, but $+0.01$ for smooth covariate-dependent stacking after affine correction. A controlled generator shows dynamic gains arise from the interaction of shift heterogeneity and local competence, increase with shift severity, and become realizable between 128 and 256 probe labels in the tested grid. The Probe-Validated Ensemble Selector chooses among a static affine stacker and dynamic realizers, deploying a candidate only when a held-out lower confidence bound clears the static-convex floor. In a preregistered prospective batch, it matched or improved the floor in all 12 runs; two deployments reduced test risk by 11% and 16%, while the gate rejected a candidate whose un-gated deployment incurred $>30\times$ the static loss. We release OpenRegShift, a reproducible evaluation harness for regression ensembles under distribution shift.

</details>


### [130] [Selection, Recombination, or a Fresh Solve? A Candidate-Free Control for Single-Pass Test-Time Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18379)
*Guiv Farmanfarmaian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Candidate-free control reveals mixed effects: conditioning on multiple correct candidates improves accuracy, while all-wrong candidate pools hurt accuracy; exactly-one-correct results are unclear. Effects survive conservative correction; counterfactual analysis reverses all-wrong interpretation at scale. Mechanisms remain unresolved; explicit answer-field interventions show steering but generic masking has no effect; findings are limited to a single LLM family and two math benchmarks with specific prompting settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate whether including candidate context in multi-answer generation provides value beyond an extra generation pass, and to understand how candidate pool composition (c0, c1, c2+) affects test-time reasoning performance across math benchmarks.

Method: Compare candidate-conditioned prompting to a candidate-free control under identical token budgets. Stratify results by the number of correct candidates (c0, c1, c2+). Use AIME-2025 and HMMT-2025 benchmarks with Qwen3-4B. Apply adaptive two-benchmark corrections. Also perform a separate structured intervention where explicit answer fields steer outputs; examine masking vs original-format results.

Result: Δ_cand(c2+)=+0.290; Δ_cand(c0)=-0.123; Δ_cand for c1 unresolved. c2+ and c0 conclusions survive the adaptive correction. Counterfactual analysis reverses all-wrong recovery interpretation at this scale: conditioning on an all-wrong pool lowers accuracy vs fresh solve. Original-format matching and placebo results describe failures but mechanism remains unresolved. Explicit answer fields causally steer outputs; masking yields no measurable improvement; equivalence with original format not established. Evidence limited to one Qwen3-4B family, two mathematics benchmarks, first-answer-truncated candidate fragments, single-pass prompted aggregation.

Conclusion: Candidate conditioning yields mixed, regime-dependent effects on test-time reasoning accuracy. The beneficial effect occurs when multiple candidates are correct; detrimental effect occurs when all candidates are wrong. The all-wrong-case reversal under counterfactual suggests that at scale, candidate pooling can harm rather than help. Mechanisms are not yet resolved, and generalizability remains limited to the specified setup; further causal and cross-model investigations are needed.

Abstract: When every candidate is wrong, correct-candidate selection is unavailable, yet the aggregation call can still solve the problem afresh. A correct aggregate answer may therefore reflect recombination, fresh solving, or both. For efficient test-time reasoning, the relevant question is whether candidate context adds value beyond the additional generation pass. We introduce the missing candidate-free control under the same maximum output-token allowance and stratify by the number of correct candidates. Across AIME-2025 and HMMT-2025 with Qwen3-4B, candidate conditioning improves accuracy when multiple candidates are correct ($Δ_{\mathrm{cand}}$(c2+) = +0.290), lowers accuracy when every candidate is wrong ($Δ_{\mathrm{cand}}$(c0) = -0.123), and remains unresolved in the one-correct regime. The c2+ and c0 conclusions survive a conservative correction for the adaptive two-benchmark procedure. Under this counterfactual, the interpretation of all-wrong recovery reverses at this scale: conditioning on an all-wrong candidate pool lowers accuracy relative to a fresh solve. Original-format matching and placebo results characterize the failures descriptively but leave their mechanism unresolved. Within a separate structured intervention, explicit answer fields causally steer outputs toward their values; masking yields no measurable accuracy improvement, and equivalence with the original format was not established. The evidence is limited to one Qwen3-4B family, two mathematics benchmarks, first-answer-truncated candidate fragments, and single-pass prompted aggregation.

</details>


### [131] [Vector Symbolic Policy Gradient](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18404)
*Ryozo Masukawa,Sanggeon Yun,SungHeon Jeong,Hyunwoo Oh,Raheeb Hassan,Pietro Mercati,Nathaniel D. Bastian,Mahdi Imani,Mohsen Imani*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: VSPG uses unit-norm action hypervectors with state-similarity scoring; update equals advantage-weighted hypervector bundling and normalization; hypervectors serve as fixed-size kernel memories; greedy choices on bipolar memories are robust to bit flips; links VSA memories, log-linear gradients, and kernel policy search with robustness guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to achieve sample-efficient learning in discrete-action RL by encoding action memories and evidential support as compact hypervectors.

Method: represent actions as unit-norm hypervectors; score actions by encoder-state similarity; under softmax policy gradient, prove update equals advantage-weighted hypervector bundling followed by normalization; trained action hypervectors form fixed-size kernel memories storing an advantage-weighted kernel expansion over visited states; for bipolar action memories, analyze stability of greedy selection under random bit flips.

Result: theoretical proof of update decomposition and compatibility with standard advantage estimators; demonstration that action memories act as compressed kernel memories; robustness guarantee: greedy action selection remains stable under random bit flips with failure probability decaying exponentially in hypervector dimension.

Conclusion: VSPG unifies VSA action memories, log-linear policy gradients, and kernel policy search, providing a quantitative robustness guarantee and enabling sample-efficient learning without increasing inference-time memory.

Abstract: We answer this question with Vector-Symbolic Policy Gradient (VSPG), a discrete-action actor that represents each action by a unit-norm hypervector and scores it by similarity to the encoded state. Under the standard softmax policy-gradient surrogate, we prove that its update is exactly advantage-weighted hypervector bundling followed by normalization, and therefore supports standard advantage estimators. We further show that each trained action hypervector is a fixed-size compressed kernel memory, storing an advantage-weighted kernel expansion over visited states and transferring evidence according to the encoder-induced similarity. This provides a concrete mechanism that can support sample-efficient learning without increasing inference-time memory. Finally, for bipolar action memories, we prove that greedy action selection is stable under random bit flips, with failure probability decaying exponentially in the hypervector dimension. VSPG thus connects VSA action memories, log-linear policy gradients, and kernel policy search while providing a quantitative robustness guarantee.

</details>


### [132] [Role-Conditioned Sub-Token Routing for Efficient Vision-Language-Action Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18410)
*Wei Jiang,Wei Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A novel sub-token routing method (RoleSub) compresses retained token representations to improve efficiency in vision-language-action models, complementing token pruning and enabling strong performance under aggressive compression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce compute and memory in long multimodal token sequences for VLA models while preserving performance, addressing the limitations of token pruning that discards entire token representations.

Method: Introduce Role-Conditioned Sub-Token Routing (RoleSub). After visual token pruning, split retained value representations into orthogonal groups and use a lightweight router to decide which groups to keep. Routing is conditioned on the token, a learned latent role representation, and language context. The same mechanism can be applied to language values to compress both modalities without removing additional tokens.

Result: Evaluated on OpenVLA-OFT-7B across four LIBERO suites. RoleSub outperforms a trained token-only baseline in 33/36 settings at matched visual-KV budgets, especially under aggressive compression. Combined visual and language compression achieves only 9.2–11.3% of the original KV while maintaining strong control performance on most tasks.

Conclusion: Sub-token compression via RoleSub provides a strong complementary approach to token pruning for efficient VLA inference, enabling substantial KV reduction with minimal performance loss.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models process long multimodal token sequences, making inference expensive in both memory and computation. Existing efficiency methods mainly reduce visual tokens, but aggressive token pruning becomes fragile because removing a token discards its entire representation. Sub-token compression provides a complementary alternative by retaining more tokens while reducing their value width. However, directly applying sub-token compression to VLA policies is less effective because information important for perception, language understanding, and control is distributed differently across the multimodal representation.
  We introduce Role-Conditioned Sub-Token Routing (RoleSub), which learns how to compress the value representations of retained tokens. After visual token reduction, RoleSub partitions each retained value representation into groups in an orthogonal space and uses a lightweight router to determine which groups should be preserved. The routing decision is conditioned on the token representation, a learned latent role representation, and language context. The same mechanism can also be applied to language values, allowing visual and language representations to be compressed without removing additional tokens.
  We evaluate RoleSub on OpenVLA-OFT-7B across the four LIBERO suites. At matched visual-KV budgets, RoleSub outperforms a trained token-only control in 33 of 36 settings, with the largest gains under aggressive compression. Combining visual and language compression reduces total KV to 9.2--11.3% of the original while retaining strong control performance on most tasks. These results show that reducing the representation within retained tokens provides an effective complement to token pruning for aggressive VLA compression.

</details>


### [133] [The Road Taken: The Role of Optimizers at the Edge of Stability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18415)
*Jaerin Lee,Kyoung Mu Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A realized edge of stability based on directional Hessian and gradient-alignment to the actual optimizer update, removing optimizer-dependent offsets and enabling new diagnostics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classical stability bounds based on the maximum Hessian eigenvalue and learning rate poorly predict observed behavior, showing large, optimizer-dependent deviations that can be as large as 21.1x; a formulation tied to the actual update is needed.

Method: Derive a stability threshold from the directional Hessian along the update direction and the gradient-alignment score with respect to the performed update; formulate the realized edge of stability and develop diagnostics to reveal the optimizer's role in balancing temporal and spatial budgets.

Result: The realized stability threshold eliminates optimizer-dependent offsets and provides more consistent predictions across optimizers; introduces diagnostics that illuminate how optimizers balance temporal and spatial resources during first-order optimization.

Conclusion: Reframes the edge of stability as an update-aligned, optimizer-dependent phenomenon; offers practical tools for diagnosing and understanding optimization dynamics across first-order methods.

Abstract: The edge of stability refers to a phenomenon in deep learning with gradient-based optimizers where the Hessian eigenvalues of the loss remain stable above a threshold that the classical descent lemma predicts to be unstable. Previous works formulate the edge of stability with respect to the maximum Hessian eigenvalue and the learning rate. However, we observe that many first-order methods, including gradient descent, significantly violate the stability bound predicted by these theories by a factor as large as $\times 21.1$. Moreover, this deviation turns out to be systematic and highly dependent on the underlying optimizer, which is not captured by previous formulations. This calls for a new formulation of the stability threshold, which we derive from the directional Hessian and the gradient-alignment score with respect to the actual update taken by the optimizer, rather than the maximum curvature mode. Our new formulation of the realized edge of stability not only removes optimizer-dependent offsets and provides more consistent predictions of the stability threshold, but also introduces new diagnostic tools that reveal the unique role of the optimizer in actively balancing between the temporal and spatial budgets in first-order optimization.

</details>


### [134] [Mechanistic Interpretability of Structure-Aware Numerical Reasoning in LLaMA 3.1 8B](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18419)
*Rahul Chowdhury,Timothy A Rupprecht,Senhao Cao,Jiahao Liu,Octavia Camps,David Bau,Pu Zhao,Yanzhi Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLama 3.1-8B demonstrates implicit learning of sequence structure via first-difference representations; mechanistic probing reveals an induction-like circuit that retrieves and adds the difference to the current value.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to uncover the mechanisms behind LLM time-series prediction and assess whether they explicitly encode structural information like first differences, using mechanistic interpretability.

Method: design a sequence task requiring structural cues (repeat with offset); evaluate Llama 3.1-8B; use probing to detect internal first-difference representations; use activation patching counterfactuals to identify an induction-like mechanism that adds the difference to the current value.

Result: model shows strong performance; probes indicate internal first-difference representations; patching reveals retrieval of difference and its use in updating the sequence; constitutes a first demonstration of concept induction in LLMs.

Conclusion: provides evidence that LLMs can internalize and manipulate structural numerical features; advances mechanistic interpretability and understanding of time-series reasoning in LLMs.

Abstract: Recent work has shown that large language models (LLMs) exhibit strong numerical sequence modeling capabilities and show promise in time-series prediction. While LLMs display in-context learning capabilities, the mechanisms with which they accomplish time-series prediction remain unclear. Specifically, whether they truly understand the underlying structure, which at a minimum requires reasoning over first differences in the sequence of numbers. To study this, we investigate Llama 3.1-8B from a mechanistic interpretability point of view. Mechanistic interpretability is an emerging field concerned with the reverse engineering of the algorithms learned by neural networks such as LLMs. To assess Llamas' numerical sequence modeling capabilities and to facilitate our mechanistic interpretability analysis, we create a sequence modeling task that cannot be solved without picking up structural cues. Specifically, we sample n random numbers and repeat them with an offset. We find that Llama displays strong performance on our tasks suggesting that it can pick up on the underlying structure. To understand the mechanisms that allow it to do so, we perform probing experiments and activation patching based counterfactual analysis. Probing reveals that the model computes and stores first differences in its internal representations without explicit supervision, indicating that it tracks structural information about the sequence. Activation patching reveals that Llama retrieves the relevant first-difference with a mechanism similar to an induction circuit and subsequently adds it to the current value. Notably, our work represents one of the first studies to identify this form of concept induction in LLMs.

</details>


### [135] [Adaptive Multi-Agent Feature Selection for Personalized Fall Risk Prevention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18450)
*Chang Liu,Ladda Thiamwong,Yanjie Fu,Rui Xie*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Reinforcement-learning-based adaptive feature selection (PAFIR) for fall risk using longitudinal multimodal data to enable personalized, time-adaptive prevention.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Falls in older adults are a major public health issue with sparse outcomes and heterogeneous, time-varying data. Static models inadequately capture evolving risk factors across modalities and time.

Method: PAFIR treats adaptive feature selection as a reinforcement learning problem over longitudinal multimodal health data. It models dependencies among correlated assessment variables and temporal dynamics in wearable activity data, learning subject-specific feature-selection policies across study visits with rewards derived from sparse fall events. Applied to the PEER trial.

Result: PAFIR more effectively captures longitudinal and structural patterns of feature relevance than state-of-the-art baselines, enabling dynamic, subject-specific feature selection and timely, personalized fall prevention strategies.

Conclusion: Adaptive RL-based feature selection over longitudinal multimodal data can improve fall risk identification and prevention by tailoring features to individuals over time.

Abstract: Falls among older adults represent a major public health challenge driven by complex, time-varying interactions across multiple risk domains. Effective fall risk factor identification requires learning from heterogeneous longitudinal data while accounting for sparse and delayed fall-related outcome events. However, existing approaches are largely static and fail to adaptively model evolving, individualized risk factors across modalities and time. We propose PAFIR, a Personalized and Adaptive Feature selection framework for fall risk Identification and pRevention, which formulates adaptive feature selection as a reinforcement learning problem over longitudinal multimodal health data. PAFIR jointly models structural dependencies among correlated assessment variables and temporal dynamics in wearable-derived physical activity data, and learns adaptive selection policies across repeated study visits using reward signals derived from sparse fall incidence outcomes. We apply PAFIR to data from the Physio fEedback Exercise pRogram (PEER) cluster-randomized trial. Experimental results demonstrate that PAFIR more effectively captures longitudinal and structural patterns of feature relevance than state-of-the-art baselines, and enables dynamic, subject-specific feature selection. By adapting selected features over time, PAFIR supports more timely and personalized fall prevention strategies.

</details>


### [136] [Atrial Fibrillation Detection with Arbitrary Leads via a Codebook-Based Reconstruction-Classification Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18451)
*Hongtao Li,Jia Wei,Guoyao Li,Yuchen Lei,Guangnian Ma,Jia Xiao,Yuanjun Lai,Shuzhen Lv,Xueqiang Ouyang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A dual-codebook graph collaborative network (DCGCNet) for robust AF detection from ECG, using a vector-quantized variational autoencoder with a Local-Global Contrastive Module and an Adaptive Codebook Vector Quantizer, achieving state-of-the-art intra-dataset performance and strong cross-dataset generalization (AUC > 0.98) under noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: AF detection from ECG in real-world clinical settings is hindered by variable leads, cross-dataset domain shifts, and artifacts. There is a need for a robust, generalizable model that can operate across datasets and under realistic noise.

Method: End-to-end vector-quantized VAE that jointly performs AF classification and ECG reconstruction. Key components: (1) Local-Global Contrastive Module to learn noise-invariant representations; (2) Adaptive Codebook Vector Quantizer dynamically refines codebook prototypes to align with input distributions and prevent codebook collapse, improving generalization.

Result: DCGCNet achieves state-of-the-art performance in standard intra-dataset 12-lead evaluation and demonstrates exceptional cross-dataset generalization across seven diverse settings, consistently attaining AUC > 0.98 in all cases. It also maintains high diagnostic accuracy under realistic noisy conditions (baseline wander, powerline interference, EMG artifacts).

Conclusion: DCGCNet sets a new benchmark for robust, generalizable, and noise-resilient AF detection, with strong potential for deployment in real-world clinical environments.

Abstract: \textbf{Background and Objective}: Reliable atrial fibrillation (AF) detection from electrocardiogram (ECG) signals remains challenging in real-world clinical settings due to variable lead configurations, cross-dataset domain shifts, and pervasive physiological and technical artifacts. So we develop a robust and generalizable deep learning model for accurate AF detection.\\ \textbf{Methods}: We propose the Dual-Codebook Graph Collaborative Network (DCGCNet), a novel end-to-end vector-quantized variational autoencoder that jointly performs AF classification and ECG reconstruction. DCGCNet introduces two key components: (1) a Local-Global Contrastive Module for learning noise-invariant representations, and (2) an Adaptive Codebook Vector Quantizer that dynamically refines codebook prototypes to better align with input data distributions, thereby preventing codebook collapse and enhancing generalization.\\ \textbf{Results}: DCGCNet achieves state-of-the-art performance in standard intra-dataset 12-lead evaluation and demonstrates exceptional cross-dataset generalization across seven diverse settings, consistently attaining AUC > 0.98 in all cases. Furthermore, it maintains high diagnostic accuracy under realistic noisy conditions, including baseline wander, powerline interference, and EMG artifacts.\\ \textbf{Conclusions}: DCGCNet establishes a new benchmark for robust, generalizable, and noise-resilient AF detection, showing strong potential for deployment in real-world clinical environments.

</details>


### [137] [ERASE: EaRly bAckpropagation SchEdule for Faster Training of Modern Recommendation Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18469)
*Ergan Shang,Flavio Sales Truzzi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ERASE overlaps detached backward passes with forward passes using separate CUDA streams to improve throughput for lightweight proxy models; achieves up to 9.51% speedup on a large CTR model, with concurrency limited by kernel saturation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Underutilization of accelerators due to disjoint forward/backward passes in small-kernel models; seeking to exploit overlapping computation by reinterpreting Forward-Forward detachment as a scheduling primitive.

Method: Detach subgraphs via Forward-Forward; execute each detached subgraph's backward pass on its own CUDA stream and overlap it with subsequent forward work; validate with a lightweight transformer to study concurrency limits and with a large-scale CTR model using six dense subarchitectures.

Result: Shows feasible overlap; kernel saturation limits concurrency; reported training throughput gains up to 9.51% while maintaining normalized entropy close to baseline.

Conclusion: Detaching subgraphs serves as a viable scheduling primitive to boost training throughput on accelerators; effectiveness hinges on hardware concurrency and kernel saturation, with notable gains in larger models.

Abstract: Lightweight proxy models enable rapid experimentation without repeatedly training frontier-scale systems, but their small kernels often leave modern accelerators underutilized. Conventional training compounds this inefficiency by scheduling the forward and backward passes as disjoint phases, so spare capacity in one cannot be filled by work from the other. We reinterpret the detachment mechanism of Forward-Forward (FF) as a scheduling primitive: given a local objective, detaching a block's output removes downstream gradient dependencies, making its backward pass ready when its forward pass finishes. ERASE launches each detached subgraph's backward pass early on a separate CUDA stream, overlapping it with subsequent forward work. Execution trace on a lightweight transformer demonstrates this overlap and its limit: a kernel that saturates the device leaves no capacity for concurrency. On a large-scale click-through-rate model, detaching six dense subarchitectures improves training throughput by up to $9.51\%$ while keeping normalized entropy close to the baseline.

</details>


### [138] [Physics-Unrolled Neural Operator for Wireless Field Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18495)
*Rafid Umayer Murshed,Saif Ur Rahman,Mingyue Tang,Elahe Soltanaghai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A three-stage physics-informed neural operator (PU-HNO) learns high-fidelity indoor radio maps from cheap low-fidelity simulations by unrolling physics of reflection, diffraction, and scattering, achieving higher accuracy than baselines while handling noisy labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate radio maps are costly to simulate; cheap labels from finite-ray simulations include Monte Carlo noise. Need a learning approach that leverages physically informed structure to denoise and generalize across layouts.

Method: Three-stage cascade neural operator that progressively captures propagation effects, uses low-fidelity ray-tracing outputs and scene priors; physics unrolled hybrid neural operator; theory: under conditionally unbiased label noise, can learn stable propagation and even outperform training labels.

Result: Empirical results on diverse floorplans show outperformance of image-to-image baselines, wireless learning models, and monolithic neural operators in image-quality and deployment metrics.

Conclusion: PU-HNO provides a principled, effective approach to high-fidelity radio-map prediction with noise-resilient learning and superior performance over baselines; it highlights the benefit of physics-unrolled architectures for wireless propagation tasks.

Abstract: Radio maps are essential for wireless decision-making tasks such as access-point placement, coverage planning, and localization, but their fine spatial details are governed by complex propagation effects and are costly to simulate accurately. Machine learning offers a path to high-fidelity radio-map prediction without running expensive high-fidelity simulations for every scene. However, generating high-quality training labels at scale is also difficult: the affordable labels come from finite-ray simulations, which are richer than low-fidelity inputs but carry residual Monte Carlo noise. We address this challenge with Physics-Unrolled Hybrid Neural Operator (PU-HNO), a three-stage cascade that predicts high-fidelity indoor radio maps from low-fidelity ray-tracing outputs and scene priors by progressively capturing reflection, diffraction, and scattering effects, rather than treating radio maps as generic images. We prove that, under conditionally unbiased label noise, the model can learn stable propagation structure and outperform its own training labels. Experiments across diverse floorplans show that PU-HNO outperforms image-to-image baselines, wireless learning models, and monolithic neural operators across both image-quality and wireless deployment metrics.

</details>


### [139] [Tianmu-TC: Physics-constraints Generative Artificial Intelligence for Global Tropical Cyclone Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18500)
*Shiqi Zhang,Pan Mu,Cheng Huang,Hanting Yan,Yuchao Zhu,Jinglin Zhang,Shengyong Chen,Shoujuan Shu,Cong Bai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A physics-constrained generative AI model (Tianmu-TC) for global tropical cyclone forecasting improves forecast reliability and reduces computational cost, outperforming both deterministic/ensemble ML and authoritative NWP like ECMWF in Western North Pacific data, and remains robust under data sparsity, anomaly tracks, rapid intensification and weakening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Forecasting tropical cyclones is hampered by chaotic dynamics and rapid error growth. Conventional NWP and deep learning models are computationally expensive and can fail in complex scenarios. There is a need for reliable, efficient, global TC forecasting that can handle data sparsity and extreme events.

Method: Proposes Tianmu-TC, a physics-constraints generative framework trained on Western North Pacific data. The model enforces physics-based constraints to generate controllable outputs with reduced uncertainty, balancing accuracy with computational efficiency.

Result: Empirical results show Tianmu-TC outperforms deterministic and ensemble meteorological AI models and ECMWF in global ocean basins, with significantly lower computational cost. The approach also performs well under challenging conditions such as data sparsity, anomaly tracks, rapid intensification and weakening.

Conclusion: Physics-constraints generative AI is a promising avenue for reliable, efficient global tropical cyclone forecasting, offering improved reliability with lower computational demands.

Abstract: Tropical cyclones (TCs) pose severe risks from strong winds and heavy rainfall. However, forecasting their track and intensity remains challenging due to chaotic atmosphere and the rapid amplification of initial condition errors, leading to growing forecast uncertainty. While numerical weather prediction (NWP) and deep learning models have made progress, they remain computationally demanding and often fail under complex meteorological scenarios. Here, we present Tianmu-TC, a physics-constraints generative framework for global TC forecasting. Trained on Western North Pacific data, Tianmu-TC leverages physics-constraints to generate controllable outputs with reduced uncertainty thus improving forecast reliability. Experiments show Tianmu-TC outperforms deterministic and ensemble meteorological artificial intelligence models and authoritative NWP systems such as ECMWF in global ocean basins, with significantly lower computational cost. We further show Tianmu-TC performs well in challenging scenarios such as data sparsity, anomaly tracks, rapid intensification and weakening. These findings suggest physics-constraints generative AI offers a promising approach for reliable, efficient global TC forecasting.

</details>


### [140] [LLM-Powered Predictive Decision-Making for Sustainable Data Center Operations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18503)
*Hanzhao Wang,Jingxuan Wu,Yumeng Li,Yu Pan,Guanting Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LLM-driven predictive scheduling reduces energy use and latency in data centers, achieving 32% energy savings and 30% lower queueing time.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rising energy demand and environmental impact of AI workloads in data centers necessitate proactive, accurate resource management to improve efficiency and sustainability.

Method: Develop an LLM-based predictor that estimates execution time and energy from source code, optionally extending to other sustainability metrics; pair with a real-time GPU scheduling algorithm to optimize energy usage and queuing delays, leveraging fast inference and low training data.

Result: Achieved 32% reduction in energy consumption and 30% reduction in waiting time through collaboration with a data center; demonstrated generalization across task types and potential applicability to broader sustainability metrics.

Conclusion: The framework offers a practical, scalable approach to sustainable AI infrastructure, with strong potential for broader environmental impact once broader metric tracking is available.

Abstract: The growing demand for AI-driven workloads, particularly from Large Language Models (LLMs), has raised concerns about the significant energy and resource consumption in data centers. This work introduces a novel LLM-based predictive scheduling system designed to enhance operational efficiency while reducing the environmental impact of data centers. Our system utilizes an LLM to predict key metrics such as execution time and energy consumption from source code, and it has the potential to extend to other sustainability-focused metrics like water usage for cooling and carbon emissions, provided the data center can track such data. The predictive model is followed by a real-time scheduling algorithm that allocates GPU resources, aiming to improve sustainability by optimizing both energy consumption and queuing delays. With fast inference times, the ability to generalize across diverse task types, and minimal data requirements for training, our approach offers a practical solution for data center scheduling. This framework demonstrates strong potential for advancing sustainability objectives in AI-driven infrastructure. Through our collaboration with a data center, we achieved a 32% reduction in energy consumption and a 30% decrease in waiting time.

</details>


### [141] [Evaluating and Explaining Prompt Sensitivity of LLMs Using Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18539)
*Ruiyang Qin,Qingzhuo Wang,Tian Wang,Zhihua Wei,Wen Shen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Interaction-based Prompt Sensitivity (IPS) to diagnose LLM prompt sensitivity by decomposing the output score into input interactions, revealing instability in low-order interactions despite unchanged outputs. Applies IPS to 50 open-source LLMs, identifying four factors—supervised fine-tuning, larger model size, dense architectures, and few-shot learning—that reduce prompt sensitivity, primarily by dampening low-order interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing prompt-sensitivity metrics rely on final outputs and fail to explain internal mechanisms. A fine-grained, interaction-level analysis can reveal how small prompt changes propagate through the model to affect internally defined interactions, offering a causal or explanatory account of robustness versus instability.

Method: Decompose the LLM output score into a set of nonlinear interactions among input variables. Define the Interaction-based Prompt Sensitivity (IPS) metric as the change in these interactions caused by subtle prompt perturbations. Apply IPS to 50 open-source LLMs to identify factors that mitigate sensitivity, and analyze how different factors affect low-order versus high-order interactions.

Result: Subtle prompt changes can trigger severe instability in the interactions even when the LLM outputs remain the same. Four factors reduce prompt sensitivity: supervised fine-tuning, larger model scales, dense architectures, and few-shot learning. The common mechanism is that these factors tend to dampen the sensitivity of low-order interactions (involving few input variables).

Conclusion: IPS provides a fine-grained diagnostic tool for prompt sensitivity and suggests practical design guidance: to improve robustness, favor supervision, scale, dense architectures, and few-shot strategies that suppress low-order interaction sensitivity, thereby reducing overall prompt sensitivity.

Abstract: The remarkable capabilities of large language models (LLMs) are often undermined by their instability. Even subtle and semantically irrelevant changes in prompts can cause dramatic fluctuations in performance, a phenomenon known as prompt sensitivity. Previous studies typically evaluate prompt sensitivity by comparing the LLM's final outputs when prompts change. However, such coarse-grained metrics fail to explain the internal reasons for prompt sensitivity. In this paper, we introduce interactions as a fine-grained tool to analyze prompt sensitivity of LLMs. Specifically, we decompose the output score of the LLM into a set of interactions. Each interaction represents a nonlinear relationship involving a set of input variables. We discover that subtle changes to prompts can trigger severe instability in interactions, even when the outputs of the LLM remain the same. To this end, we propose an Interaction-based Prompt Sensitivity (IPS) metric by quantifying changes in interactions when we introduce subtle changes to prompts. We apply the IPS metric to 50 open-source LLMs and uncover four factors that reduce the prompt sensitivity of LLMs, including supervised fine-tuning, increased model scales, dense architectures, and few-shot learning. More crucially, we discover a common mechanism by which these four factors reduce prompt sensitivity: all four factors tend to reduce the prompt sensitivity of low-order interactions (i.e., interactions involving few input variables).

</details>


### [142] [MARCUS: Missing-Aware Region Representation with Contextual Urban Signals for Rent Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18546)
*Chenya Huang,Bin Liang,Zhidong Li,Yuxi Lu,Kunqi Li,Justin Wang,Fang Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MARCUS treats missingness as a contextual signal for multimodal urban region representation, achieving substantial MAE improvements in rent prediction (Sydney: -51.35%, New York: -12.62% vs baselines) via intra/inter/fusion stages and extensive ablations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imputation-based handling of missing data discards potential semantic information contained in missingness. Exploit missing patterns as urban context to improve region representations.

Method: Three-stage architecture: (1) Intra Learning—jointly encode observed features and missing patterns, (2) Inter Learning—estimate modality reliability to guide cross-modal interaction, (3) Fusion—apply missing-aware and time-aware gating to produce the final region embedding. Applied to rent prediction with real-world Sydney and New York datasets; includes imputation-based ablation and randomized missingness analysis.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance with large MAE reductions (Sydney: 51.35%, New York: 12.62%); ablation and randomized missingness experiments validate effectiveness of missing-aware approach.

Conclusion: Missingness is a valuable contextual signal for urban multimodal learning. MARCUS effectively leverages it to improve region representations and prediction robustness, with strong empirical support and potential for broader urban analytics tasks.

Abstract: Multimodal urban data has expanded the applications of urban region representation learning, such as functional zone identification and real estate appraisal, but also introduces challenges caused by data incompleteness. Existing studies usually handle missing data through imputation, treating missingness as noise while ignoring its potential semantic value. To address this issue, we propose MARCUS, a missing-aware region representation model that treats missingness as a contextual urban signal. MARCUS models missingness in three stages: Intra Learning jointly encodes observed features and missing patterns, Inter Learning estimates modality reliability to guide cross-modal interaction, and Fusion uses missing-aware and time-aware gating to generate the final region embedding. We apply MARCUS to rent prediction, a task with long-term trends and seasonal fluctuations, using real-world datasets from Sydney and New York. Experimental results show that MARCUS achieves state-of-the-art performance, reducing MAE by 51.35% on Sydney and 12.62% on New York compared with the best baselines. Additional experiments, including an imputation-based ablation study and randomized additional-missingness analysis, further demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

</details>


### [143] [Performance Drift Detection in Machine Learning as a Service (MLaaS) for IoT Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18555)
*Deepak Kanneganti,Sajib Mistry,Sheik Mohammad Mostakim Fattah,Erik Elmroth,Aneesh Krishna,Monowar Bhuyan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes MPDD with Adaptive-Temporal Drift Detection (APDDM) for MLaaS in IoT to detect performance drift under data distribution changes and updates; shows notable accuracy gains and reduced miss detection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: IoT environments exhibit non-stationary data distributions; MLaaS users are black-box, making drift detection challenging; periodic updates further cause performance drift.

Method: An MLaaS extraction model learns service behavior from input-output pairs to identify prediction-influencing features; MPDD jointly captures data and MLaaS behavior drift; APDDM adaptively tunes monitoring frequency based on observed variations.

Result: Real-world experiments show MPDD improves drift-detection accuracy by 22-25% over baselines; APDDM provides ~4% average accuracy gain and ~9% reduction in miss-detection compared to fixed-interval monitoring.

Conclusion: The framework enables timely drift detection and effective MLaaS management in dynamic IoT settings, with demonstrated practical improvements.

Abstract: Machine Learning as a Service (MLaaS) is a powerful cloud paradigm enabling data-driven intelligent applications in Internet of Things (IoT) environments, widely adopted across healthcare, smart homes, and industry due to its cost-effectiveness. However, the dynamic nature of IoT frequently alters data distributions, affecting MLaaS stability, while periodic MLaaS updates further introduce performance drift. Unlike traditional ML systems, MLaaS clients operate as black-box users without access to internal data or parameters, making drift detection particularly challenging. To address this, we propose a novel MLaaS Performance Drift Detection framework for IoT environments. The framework first employs an MLaaS extraction model that learns service behavior from input-output pairs and identifies prediction-influenced features. Building on this, the proposed MLaaS Performance Drift Detection (MPDD) model jointly captures variations in input data and MLaaS behavior. We further design an Adaptive-Temporal Performance Drift Detection Mechanism (APDDM) that dynamically adjusts monitoring frequency based on behavioral and data variations, enabling timely drift detection for effective service management. Extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that MPDD achieves up to 22-25% accuracy improvement over baseline drift detection methods. APDDM provides an average accuracy gain of approximately 4% and reduces the miss detection rate by around 9% compared to fixed-interval monitoring.

</details>


### [144] [MorphoGP: A Nonparametric Framework for Predicting Equilibrium Beach Profiles Under Tidal Influence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18558)
*Xi Wu,Yanqing Wei,Hang Yin,Pengze Li,Hongshuai Qi,Xi Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MorphoGP uses contour-based morphology clustering and category-specific Gaussian process experts with a gating network to predict equilibrium beach profiles under tidal influence, achieving significant RMSE improvement over baselines on 180 Chinese coast profiles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate prediction of EBPs in tidal contexts is challenged by nonlinear wave-tide-sediment interactions and limited generalization of existing models; a morphology-aware data-driven framework could improve predictive performance and coastal management.

Method: ContourCluster (contrastive-learning based morphology classifier) partitions tide-influenced beaches into morphology categories; within each, a Gaussian process expert models environmental-to-profile mappings (waves, tides, sediments); a Gating Net probabilistically weights expert outputs to yield the EBP prediction.

Result: On 180 tide-influenced beach profiles along the Chinese coast, MorphoGP reduces test RMSE by ~59.3% compared with the best baseline, achieving RMSE = 0.297 m, outperforming conventional and deep-learning models.

Conclusion: MorphoGP offers a physically-informed, data-driven approach to predict EBPs under tidal influence; emphasizes the importance of morphology-specific modeling; future work should strengthen process-level physical coupling and broader validation.

Abstract: The prediction of equilibrium beach profiles under tidal influence is of fundamental importance for sustainable coastal development, informing shoreline protection strategies and managing coastal ecosystems under changing environmental conditions. However, it remains challenging due to the highly nonlinear interactions among wave, tide, and sedimentary processes. Traditional empirical and numerical models often exhibit limited adaptability across diverse coastal environments, with especially pronounced limitations in beach systems where tidal processes are important . To improve data-driven prediction under these conditions, this study proposes MorphoGP, a unified category-specific Gaussian process framework for predicting equilibrium beach profiles (EBPs) under tidal influence. The framework first introduces a ContourCluster model based on contrastive learning to classify tide-influenced beach morphologies automatically. Within each morphological category, a specialized Gaussian process expert learns statistical associations between environmental descriptors including waves, tides, and sediments and the beach profile's shape. A Gating Net then integrates the outputs of all experts through a probabilistic weighting mechanism to produce the final prediction. Evaluated on data from over 180 beach profiles from tide-influenced coasts along the Chinese coast, MorphoGP achieves improved predictive performance compared with conventional and deep learning models, reducing the test RMSE by about 59.3\% compared with the best baseline and achieving a final RMSE of 0.297 m. The proposed framework provides a physically informed, data-driven tool for equilibrium beach-profile prediction under tidal influence and coastal management, while stronger process-level physical coupling remains an important direction for future development.

</details>


### [145] [To Go Far, Go Together: Diverse Preferences Induce a Curriculum for Reward Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18770)
*Taehyung Kim,Jongeun Choi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: CurriPO introduces a tree-structured curriculum to personalize reward-model optimization for diverse users, enabling a single traversal to cover population objectives. It outperforms strong baselines in population satisfaction and reduces training time by leveraging multi-user structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Per-user reward-models and policy optimization improve alignment, but some users are harder to optimize at the policy level. With a diverse user population, a curriculum that interleaves easy- and hard-to-optimize reward models can better allocate training resources and reduce underserved groups.

Method: Automatic construction of a tree-structured curriculum over heterogeneous user reward models (CurriPO). The curriculum can branch and reuse previously learned reward models, allowing efficient coverage of diverse user objectives. Evaluation on personalized continuous control in simulation compares population satisfaction and training efficiency against baselines.

Result: CurriPO achieves 1.2×–2.1× population satisfaction of the strongest baseline and significantly reduces training time. Analyses indicate the gains stem largely from addressing users underserved by conventional optimization.

Conclusion: This work is the first to explicitly exploit multi-user structure to address optimization in AI alignment. A curriculum-based, tree-structured approach can efficiently cover a heterogeneous user population and improve fairness in outcome by reducing neglect of hard-to-optimize users.

Abstract: Learning a reward model from human feedback and optimizing a policy against it is one approach to aligning AI systems with individual users. From a fairness perspective, existing work improves such alignment by developing data-efficient and accurate reward models that capture minority preferences despite scarce data. We push this line of inquiry one step further and argue that data-efficient and accurate per-user reward models are not sufficient: users whose reward models are difficult to \textit{optimize} at the policy level can become a new underserved group. We start from the observation that one user's reward model can be easy to optimize from the initial policy while another's is not. We argue that, given a sufficiently diverse user population, a curriculum naturally emerges between easy- and hard-to-optimize reward models. Building on this insight, we propose CurriPO, which grows a tree-structured curriculum to accommodate diverse user-specific objectives, covering the population in a single traversal. Specifically, CurriPO automatically constructs a curriculum over diverse user reward models, allowing it to branch from the existing curriculum and reuse reward models previously incorporated into the curriculum. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first work to explicitly exploit multi-user structure to address optimization in AI alignment. Extensive experiments on personalized continuous control in a simulated environment show that CurriPO achieves $1.2$--$2.1\times$ the population satisfaction of the strongest baseline while substantially reducing training time. Additional analysis attributes much of this improvement to the users left underserved by conventional optimization.

</details>


### [146] [NanoSleep: A Parameter-Efficient Hybrid Temporal Convolutional Network for Single-Channel Sleep Stage Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18571)
*S M Asif Hossain,Shruti Kshirsagar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NanoSleep is a compact hybrid temporal convolutional network for sleep staging on single-channel EEG, integrating a learnable Sinc-conv front end, dual-branch multi-scale temporal/spectral features, gated dilated TCN with channel recalibration, and CRF decoding, trained with weighted calibrated focal loss; it achieves state-of-the-art accuracy with compact size on Sleep-EDF datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable accurate sleep stage classification on resource-constrained wearables and home-based monitoring by reducing model size while preserving performance.

Method: Front end: learnable Sinc-convolution; dual-branch feature extractor for multi-scale temporal and spectral representations; backbone: gated dilated temporal conv net with channel recalibration; decoder: conditional random field for sequence labeling; loss: weighted calibrated focal loss to address class imbalance; evaluation on Sleep-EDF and Sleep-EDF-Expanded with subject-wise CV; ablation study.

Result: NanoSleep consistently outperforms six baselines; ablation confirms each component's contribution; demonstrates favorable accuracy-efficiency balance for wearables and clinical settings.

Conclusion: NanoSleep offers an effective, compact solution for sleep staging suitable for wearables and resource-constrained clinical applications.

Abstract: Sleep stage classification from single-channel electroencephalography (EEG) is essential for wearable and home-based sleep monitoring. However, many deep learning models achieve high accuracy at the cost of large model sizes, which limits their deployment on resource-constrained devices. In this work, we present NanoSleep, a compact hybrid temporal convolutional network for automatic sleep stage classification. NanoSleep combines a learnable Sinc-convolutional front end, a dual-branch feature extractor that fuses multi-scale temporal and spectral representations, a gated dilated temporal convolutional backbone with channel recalibration, and a conditional random field for sequence-level decoding. We further employ a weighted calibrated focal loss to address class imbalance. We evaluate NanoSleep on the Sleep-EDF and Sleep-EDF-Expanded datasets using subject-wise cross-validation. The proposed model consistently outperforms six representative baseline methods, and an ablation study confirms the contribution of each major component. These results demonstrate that NanoSleep provides an effective balance between accuracy and efficiency, making it well suited for wearable devices, home-based sleep monitoring, and resource-constrained clinical applications.

</details>


### [147] [Continual Reasoning Gym: Diagnosing and Harnessing Shared Reasoning in Continual RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18574)
*Lirui Luo,Guoxi Zhang,Hongming Xu,Rongqing Li,Cong Fang,Lifeng Fan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Continual RLVR with CPR can approach joint MTRL performance, but sequential continual updates still lag; reusable reasoning helps but does not fully close the gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand whether incremental continual-RLVR updates can match jointly trained MTRL in terms final performance, and identify mechanisms (e.g., shared reasoning) that support transfer across tasks.

Method: Introduce Continual Reasoning Gym (CRG), a continual-RLVR environment with five task sequences of text/visual reasoning tasks. Analyze forgetting vs final gap and propose Continual Prompt Replay (CPR) that replays previous-task prompts and regenerates their responses with the current policy to exploit shared reasoning.

Result: Sequential RLVR shows modest forgetting; final performance remains below MTRL. Forgetting explains only part of the gap. Shared transferable reasoning accounts for some improvement. CPR leverages this and, on average, reaches MTRL-level performance.

Conclusion: Shared reasoning enables partial transfer in continual RLVR; CPR demonstrates that prompt replay with updated policy can recover MTRL-level performance, suggesting future work on stronger transfer mechanisms and more efficient replay strategies.

Abstract: Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) commonly post-trains reasoning models on multiple tasks, while rerunning multitask RLVR (MTRL) as new tasks are added makes capability expansion costly. We therefore study continual RLVR, which updates the existing model as each task arrives. The central question is whether a model updated this way can perform as well as a jointly trained model. To answer this question, we introduce Continual Reasoning Gym, a continual-RLVR environment that organizes text and visual reasoning tasks into five task sequences. In this setting, we identify two key observations: Sequential RLVR exhibits modest forgetting, yet its final performance remains below that of MTRL. To understand the latter, we decompose final performance and show that forgetting accounts for only part of the gap. To explain the former, we identify shared reasoning: transferable reasoning structure allows training on one task to support others on average. We therefore introduce Continual Prompt Replay (CPR), which harnesses shared reasoning to improve learning on the arriving and future tasks by replaying previous-task prompts and regenerating their responses with the current policy. On average, only CPR reaches MTRL-level performance.

</details>


### [148] [Beyond receptive fields: sequence-pooled normalization can supply most of a sequence labeler's context](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18576)
*Qing Tian*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Normalization-based global statistics in sequence models can create a sequence-spanning path that bypasses the convolutional receptive field, challenging standard receptive-field interpretations and attribution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Challenge the assumption that a model's usable context equals its convolutional receptive field; show that normalization layers can provide global context, altering performance and attribution.

Method: Theoretical derivation from the Jacobian of the normalization layer to identify a closed-form sequence-spanning path; synthetic labeling with computable optima to quantify global summary; experiments on simulated genomes and real 1000 Genomes haplotypes; ablation studies on receptive-field-enlarging blocks; compare per-sequence vs per-position statistics.

Result: Global summaries encoded by normalization can match much of the gain from enlarging the receptive field in long-label regimes; a network reaching 9 positions approximates the whole-sequence optimum (error ~0.009) while reach otherwise near-chance; ablations overstate receptive-field contributions by 8.3–16.1x relative to retraining from scratch; using per-position stats multiplies the value of enlarging receptive fields by up to an order of magnitude; the effect fades as labels switch more often.

Conclusion: Normalization-induced sequence-spanning paths confound receptive-field-based interpretations and attribution; practitioners should account for this path when evaluating context reliance and performing attribution analyses; longer label runs increase reliance on the normalization path, but both receptive-field and normalization components can contribute and be misattributed.

Abstract: A convolutional sequence labeler's receptive field is routinely treated as the extent of the model's usable context: it sets dilation schedules, bounds streaming horizons, and underwrites locality claims. However, we show that this can be false: when a normalization layer computes statistics from the current input along the sequence at inference, those statistics open a sequence-spanning path that bypasses the convolutional receptive field to provide global context. We derive this from the layer's Jacobian (the criterion needs no experiment), and what the path carries has a closed form. On a synthetic labeling process with computable optima, the global summary that a sequence-spanning normalization encodes already supplies almost all of what a larger receptive field would buy where labels come in long runs: a network reaching 9 positions comes within 0.009 of the whole-sequence optimum, against a near-chance bound for its reach. Closing the path, by taking the same statistics per position, multiplies what enlarging the receptive field is worth by up to an order of magnitude on simulated genomes at every difficulty level tested and on real 1000 Genomes haplotypes. The same path also confounds attribution: ablating a trained network's receptive-field-enlarging blocks severs part of the path, overstating their contribution 8.3-16.1-fold relative to retraining from scratch. The substitution of normalization for receptive field fades as labels switch more often. Where labels run long, neither the receptive-field justification nor the ablation is wrong about its numbers, but both credit the wrong component.

</details>


### [149] [Infrared Universality of Collective Dynamics across Transformer and State-Space Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18592)
*Byung Gyu Chae*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Across Mamba and Transformer architectures, full-block spectra reveal near-marginal slow-mode dynamics with a power-law TDOS ρ(λ) ∼ λ^β; exponents differ by architecture (β_M ≈ -0.17, β_Tr ≈ -0.1); selective dynamics reshape microscopic relaxation but the emergent infrared organization is robust and architecture-dependent in detail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test whether distinct sequence architectures support common collective slow dynamics, and separate explicit memory from collective infrared structure, testing predictions of Cognitive Field Theory.

Method: Compute intrinsic generator spectrum, input-conditioned selective rescaling, and full-block TDOS from the block Jacobian; extract ρ(λ) and β; derive memory kernel K(t) ∼ t^{-(1+β)}; compare Mamba and Transformer across sequence lengths.

Result: Full-block dynamics yield a reproducible slow-mode continuum; infrared sector becomes better resolved with longer sequences; β_M ≈ -0.17, yielding K(t) ∼ t^{-0.83}; Transformer full-block β_Tr ≈ -0.1, yielding K(t) ∼ t^{-0.9}. Selective dynamics reorganize microscopic hierarchy but do not erase the slow-mode organization.

Conclusion: Demonstrates that near-marginal slow-mode dynamics and infrared organization arise across different architectures, supporting a robust dynamical structure described by Cognitive Field Theory and extending it beyond Transformers.

Abstract: Whether distinct neural architectures develop common collective dynamics remains an open question. Recent analysis of Transformer language models revealed a nearly flat, weakly infrared-enhanced time-scale density of states (TDOS) associated with near-marginal long-memory dynamics. Here we test whether a closely related organization emerges in Mamba, whose selective state-space dynamics provides a fundamentally different microscopic mechanism. Mamba allows relaxation dynamics to be resolved at three levels: the intrinsic spectrum of the learned state-space generator, its input-conditioned selective rescaling, and the collective TDOS of the complete block measured from its Jacobian.
