These spectra are not identical: selective dynamics and the remaining block transformations substantially reorganize the microscopic relaxation hierarchy. Nevertheless, the full block develops a reproducible slow-mode continuum whose infrared sector becomes progressively better resolved with increasing sequence length. Cumulative analysis yields $ρ(λ)\simλ^β$, with the long-sequence Mamba exponent stabilizing near $β_{\rm M}\simeq-0.17$. The corresponding memory dynamics follows $K(t)\sim t^{-(1+β)}$, close to the marginal $1/t$ regime. Despite fundamentally different microscopic dynamics, Transformer full-block spectra exhibit closely related infrared organization, with representative exponents of order $β_{\rm Tr}\sim-0.1$. These results separate explicit state-space memory from collective infrared organization and show that distinct sequence architectures can develop closely related near-marginal slow-mode dynamics. They extend infrared collective organization beyond Transformers and provide an independent test of the dynamical structure described by Cognitive Field Theory.

</details>


### [150] [Off-Manifold Collapse in Guided Protein Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18597)
*Shuibai Zhang,Xinchi Liu,Fred Zhangzhi Peng,Zhihan Yang,Shutong Wu,Yingzi Ma,Jiawei Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Mahalanobis-filtering to detect and discard off-manifold activation collapse in guided protein language models, improving both property alignment and structural plausibility without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Guided PLMs optimize a target property at inference, risking off-manifold representations and degenerate, low-complexity sequences that still score well on the oracle. A cheap, training-free mechanism is needed to preserve natural activation statistics.

Method: Introduce a density prior over natural activations using a Mahalanobis distance. Apply a post-hoc filter (Mahalanobis filtering) to keep candidates whose activations stay typical. Generator remains unchanged and method-agnostic, applicable across guidance approaches.

Result: Under matched guidance, the filtering improves both the property score and the structural plausibility of kept sequences at negligible cost, and the approach transfers across different guidance methods.

Conclusion: Activation-based density filtering is an effective, training-free post-hoc tool to mitigate off-manifold collapse in guided PLMs for protein design, preserving quality without altering generation.

Abstract: Protein language models are widely used priors for protein sequence design, and a growing body of work controls them at inference time as an alternative to fine-tuning. Such guidance faces a dilemma: mild enough to preserve natural activation statistics, it barely moves the property; strong enough to move it, the generations become progressively harder to fold. We show the failure has a specific and cheaply detectable signature, an off-manifold collapse of the model's own representations. Guided activations fall toward a region statistically indistinguishable from random amino-acid input, and the sequences degenerate to low complexity, yet the property oracle being optimized can still score these generations as a success. The optimized oracle can therefore fail to witness the collapse and, for solubility, can actively reward it, whereas structure and composition expose the failure. Because the failure is already visible in a finished candidate, we detect it at the output rather than modify the generator. We introduce a cheap density prior over natural protein activations and keep only the candidates that remain typical under it, a training-free post-hoc step we call Mahalanobis filtering. At matched guidance settings it improves both the property score and the structural plausibility of the sequences it keeps at negligible cost, without touching the generator, and transfers across different guidance methods. We release the activation statistic at https://huggingface.co/Shuibai12138/off-manifold-collapse-plm

</details>


### [151] [Denoising-Aware Inversion: Revealing Privacy Risks in Noise-Protected Text Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18610)
*Yubo Wang,Shujie Cui,James Bailey,Hongzhi Yin,Wenyu Liang,Min Tang,Shiyue Qin,Weiqing Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DAEI enables high-quality inversion of Gaussian-noise-perturbed text embeddings by denoising and inverting embeddings, outperforming prior baselines in BLEU, F1, and ROUGE-L.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Dense text embeddings protect privacy when perturbed with Gaussian noise, but adaptive attackers observing only noisy embeddings may still recover sensitive information; existing generative inversion methods fail under this noise trap. Need a robust inversion approach and to reassess defense effectiveness.

Method: DAEI combines a residual denoising autoencoder with generative text inversion. The denoiser is trained unsupervised via Stein's unbiased risk estimate (SURE) to denoise from noisy observations alone, enabling a denoising-aware inversion pipeline.

Result: Experiments report approximately 154% relative BLEU improvement over existing generative inversion baselines, with 32–60% gains in token-level F1 and ROUGE-L, indicating strong information leakage from noise-protected embeddings.

Conclusion: Gaussian perturbation may not suffice to prevent sensitive information leakage from embeddings; the proposed DAEI undermines a common defense and highlights a need for stronger privacy protections or different defense strategies.

Abstract: Dense text embeddings are widely used in data mining, retrieval, and downstream machine learning systems due to their compact and semantically rich representations, but recent embedding inversion attacks have shown that they can expose substantial information about the original text, leading to serious privacy leakage risks. A common defense is to release perturbed embeddings by adding Gaussian noise, which is simple yet effective against standard inversion attacks and does not significantly degrade embedding utility for downstream tasks. However, it remains unclear whether such noise-protected embeddings are sufficiently safe against adaptive attackers that explicitly account for the perturbation process. In this paper, we study text embedding inversion in a noise-protected setting, where the attacker can observe only noisy embeddings and has no access to clean embedding targets. We first analyze why existing generative inversion methods fail under this setting and identify a "Double Noise Trap", which fundamentally prevents standard generative inversion models from achieving high-quality reconstruction. To address this challenge, we propose DAEI, a denoising-aware embedding inversion pipeline that combines a residual denoising autoencoder with generative text inversion where the denoiser is trained in an unsupervised manner using Stein's unbiased risk estimate to enable denoising from noisy observations alone. Extensive experiments show that DAEI achieves approximately 154\% relative improvement in BLEU over the existing generative inversion baseline, while also improving token-level F1 and ROUGE-L by 32--60\%. The promising inversion performance of DAEI challenges the prevailing assumption that simple Gaussian perturbation is sufficient to prevent sensitive information leakage from embedding representations.

</details>


### [152] [Scalable Geospatial Machine Learning for Power-Line Asset Risk: Integrating Remote Sensing for Lightning and Vegetation Risk Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18611)
*Artur Sokolovsky,Bhavik Merai,Moe Jafari,Muen Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A modular, scalable PoF (probability-of-failure) framework for utility assets that enables asset-level risk estimation using a geospatial ML pipeline, demonstrated for vegetation- and lightning-related failures, suitable for utility deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Weather-sensitive failure mechanisms require asset-level, spatially explicit risk models that are maintainable and adaptable to changing data availability, asset-management priorities, and climate-driven hazard conditions.

Method: An asset-level, extensible architecture with a harmonised geospatial ML pipeline. Integrates multi-source predictors (SRTM elevation, MODIS NDVI vegetation condition, LIS VHRMC lightning climatology, OpenStreetMap proximity features) and utility operational records; designed to scale to new PoF types without reworking the core pipeline.

Result: The framework is computationally efficient, operationally extensible, and suitable for utility-scale deployment. It yields actionable asset-level risk stratification to guide inspection prioritisation, vegetation management, asset hardening, and resilience planning, enabling earlier intervention and climate-resilient network operations.

Conclusion: The proposed framework provides an explainable, scalable approach to asset-level PoF modelling in weather-affected power networks, with modular data inputs and PoF types that support maintenance-friendly adaptation to evolving data and hazard conditions.

Abstract: Electric power networks are increasingly exposed to weather-sensitive failure mechanisms that require asset-level, spatially explicit risk modelling for effective intervention planning. This study contributes a modular, robust, and explainable probability-of-failure (PoF) modelling framework for utility asset management. The central contribution is an asset-level architecture that can be scaled to new environmental data sources and additional PoF types without reworking the underlying pipeline. This is particularly relevant for industry settings, where risk models must remain operationally maintainable while adapting to changing data availability, asset-management priorities, and climate-driven hazard conditions. We demonstrate the framework for vegetation-related and lightning-related failure modes using a harmonised geospatial machine-learning pipeline. The implementation integrates multi-source predictors, including topography (SRTM), vegetation condition (MODIS Normalised Difference Vegetation Index - NDVI), lightning climatology (LIS VHRMC), OpenStreetMap-derived proximity features, and utility operational records. The resulting architecture is computationally efficient, operationally extensible, and suitable for utility-scale deployment. It provides actionable asset-level risk stratification for inspection prioritisation, vegetation management, asset hardening, and resilience planning, supporting earlier intervention and more climate-resilient network operations.

</details>


### [153] [Coordination on a Budget: Federated Active Learning with Few Labels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18634)
*Liam Mohr,Daphna Weinshall*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Heterogeneity reversal in low-budget cross-silo Federated Active Learning (FAL): IID data needs more coordination for query selection than heterogeneous data; propose federated representation learning to align client data for globally coordinated active selection on obfuscated embeddings; achieves state-of-the-art results even with smaller annotation budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address privacy-preserving label-efficient learning across multiple organizations (cross-silo FAL) in the challenging low-budget regime, where lack of a global data view complicates query selection.

Method: Train a federated representation learning system to align client data into a shared embedding space. The server performs globally coordinated active selection on obfuscated embeddings, while annotations remain local to each client.

Result: The proposed framework achieves superior performance compared to existing FAL methods, even when those baselines receive substantially larger annotation budgets, demonstrating the value of centralized coordination under privacy constraints.

Conclusion: Centralized coordination via shared embeddings can substantially improve FAL under privacy constraints in the low-budget regime; IID data can be more challenging for query selection than heterogeneous data in this setting, reversing the usual FL narrative as budget constraints intensify.

Abstract: Federated Active Learning (FAL) addresses the dual challenges of data privacy and label scarcity, where the absence of a global data view introduces additional hurdles for coordinated query selection. We study cross-silo FAL in the low-budget regime, where annotation decisions are most critical. We characterize, both theoretically and empirically, a heterogeneity reversal: in low-budget settings, homogeneous (IID) data requires stronger coordination to avoid redundant queries, whereas heterogeneous data naturally promotes diversity; this trend reverses at higher budgets. Thus, in contrast to the standard federated learning (FL) narrative where heterogeneity is a primary challenge, we show that IID settings are more challenging for query selection in FAL.
  Motivated by these findings, we propose a new FAL framework that utilizes federated representation learning to align client data in a shared embedding space. This enables the server to perform globally coordinated active selection over optionally obfuscated client embeddings, while annotation remains local to each client. Although our framework operates in the more challenging low-budget regime, it achieves performance that surpasses existing FAL methods even when they are given substantially larger annotation budgets, demonstrating the value of centralized coordination under privacy constraints.

</details>


### [154] [ProxyGuard: Direct Reliability Inference for Randomized Data Release Mechanisms with Shared Targets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18643)
*Dipesh Tharu Mahato,Pramod Dhungana*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ProxyGuard offers two modes to bound errors in proxy-data evaluation across multiple releases, with finite-sample guarantees and notable power gains in direct-release mode; applicability shown via studies and audits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the risk that proxy-release search can falsely validate a generator's reliability by benefiting from multiplicity; aims to provide bounded risk evaluation with a sealed target set and mode-based guarantees.

Method: Introduce ProxyGuard with prespecified bounded risks and a sealed target set. Named-release mode corrects for multiplicity and certifies specific releases. Direct shared-target mode evaluates independent mechanism draws on a common target, lower-bounds their favorable-score rate, and subtracts a bound on favorable scores contributed by invalid releases. Conditional on the target, release scores are independent, yielding a finite-sample mechanism-reliability guarantee without independent target batches or assumptions on release-level p-value dependence. Derivations include that the mean-only penalty is sharp and a smooth-score certificate with additive target concentration. A registered three-requirement study reports power gains (5.6% to 64.2% at reliability 0.95); the named mode is stronger under high-signal evidence. Prospective audits span full-pipeline Rice–TVAE retraining on every draw and a non-tabular text mechanism.

Result: Direct mode yields substantial power gains (from 5.6% to 64.2% at reliability 0.95); named-release mode maintains superiority when signal is strong; overall, ProxyGuard provides finite-sample mechanism-reliability guarantees and practical validation through prospective audits across varied pipelines.

Conclusion: ProxyGuard enables principled, finite-sample reliability guarantees for evaluating proxy releases, with mode choice guided by signal strength. The approach is validated by theoretical guarantees and empirical audits, indicating practical applicability in diverse data-generation pipelines.

Abstract: Researchers often choose a proxy dataset from many releases, transformations, or seeds. Search can make an invalid release appear adequate, while one adequate release does not establish that its generator is reliable. ProxyGuard controls both errors using prespecified bounded risks and a sealed target set. Named-release mode corrects for multiplicity and certifies specific releases. Direct shared-target mode evaluates independent mechanism draws on a common target, lower-bounds their favorable-score rate, and subtracts a bound on favorable scores contributed by invalid releases. Conditional on the target, release scores are independent, yielding a finite-sample mechanism-reliability guarantee without independent target batches or assumptions on release-level $p$-value dependence. We show that the mean-only penalty is sharp and derive a smooth-score certificate with additive target concentration. In a registered three-requirement study, direct mode raises power from 5.6\% to 64.2\% at reliability 0.95, while named mode remains stronger under high-signal evidence. Prospective audits span full-pipeline Rice--TVAE, which retrains on every draw, and a non-tabular text mechanism.

</details>


### [155] [FlashAttention for Scalable Vector Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18656)
*Sonia Rani Gupta,Nikela Papadopoulou,Miquel Pericàs*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Blocked FlashAttention-V: a vector-architecture aware optimization of attention for CPUs that folds across heads and packs data to improve throughput on short and long vectors; shows large speedups over scalar FlashAttention but reveals quantization bottlenecks for long-vector scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Attention is the primary bottleneck in transformer inference on CPUs due to high memory bandwidth; scalable vector architectures (RVV, Arm SVE) offer a path to accelerate SLM inference, especially for long sequences.

Method: Develop FlashAttention-V, a blocked, head-parallel, inter-head packed attention implementation that improves vector register utilization and data locality. Integrate into ggml (llama.cpp) and evaluate on TinyLlama, Llama 3.2, Qwen2.5, Pythia-410M using gem5 and Banana Pi BPI-F3. Apply loop reordering/unrolling across attention heads for short and long vectors. Analyze prefill vs decode throughput and quantify bottlenecks in Q8_0 quantized layers across RVV and Arm SVE.

Result: FlashAttention-V achieves 22x–42x speedup over scalar FlashAttention at 512-bit VL in prefill; gains scale by 2x–2.5x when moving to 64 lanes and 4096-bit VL. During decoding, 8x–11x speedup at 512-bit vectors with diminishing sensitivity to vector width. Simulation identifies Q8_0 quantized linear layers as a structural bottleneck that limits arithmetic amortization for long vectors, a limitation observed across RVV and Arm SVE.

Conclusion: Long-vector scalability on CPUs remains constrained by quantization formats (e.g., Q8_0) despite effective inter-head packing and loop optimizations. FlashAttention-V demonstrates strong scaling from short to long vectors and across models, but further progress requires improved quantization schemes to unlock full long-vector potential.

Abstract: Inference with transformer models on CPUs is increasingly important, especially for Small Language Models (SLMs), where vector architectures are emerging as a promising execution substrate. The attention module is a major bottleneck due to high memory bandwidth requirements; FlashAttention mitigates this by fusing operations to improve data locality and reduce intermediate memory traffic. In this paper, we present FlashAttention-V, a blocked FlashAttention for scalable vector architectures that adapts efficiently from short to very long vectors by exploiting parallelism across attention heads, inter-head packing to enable efficient utilization of vector lengths beyond the head dimension, and improving vector register utilization and memory access locality. We integrate FlashAttention-V into ggml within llama.cpp and evaluate it on TinyLlama, Llama 3.2, Qwen2.5, and Pythia-410M using gem5 and a Banana Pi BPI-F3. On the Banana Pi BPI-F3, we confirm that loop reordering and loop unrolling across attention heads are effective optimization principles, scaling performance gains with larger models and most pronounced with short contexts and during decoding. Simulation-based analysis shows that FlashAttention-V achieves 22x-42x speedup over scalar FlashAttention at 512-bit VL in prefill, with an additional 2x-2.5x gain scaling to 64 lanes and 4096-bit VL. During decode, FlashAttention-V achieves 8x-11x speedup using 512-bit vector lengths over scalar FlashAttention, with performance showing diminishing sensitivity to vector width and lane count due to single-token, memory-bound execution. We further identify structural bottlenecks in Q8_0 quantized linear layers that limit arithmetic amortization under long-vector execution, consistent across RVV and Arm SVE, indicating that current quantization formats pose a fundamental challenge to long-vector scalability.

</details>


### [156] [Computational Measurement of Team-Process Phase Dynamics in Collaborative Virtual Reality](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18660)
*Qing Huang,Jianing Zhang,Pooja Pol*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A computational framework detects and interprets dynamic team-process phases from timestamped dialogue in collaborative VR, using late context-aware chunking, penalized Gaussian-kernel change-point detection, TF-IDF, NMF, LLM-based interpretation, and alignment with action logs to provide interpretable, traceable phase structures and phase-linked behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional transcript analyses in collaborative VR collapse temporal dynamics, obscuring phase transitions and coordination changes. There is a need for time-resolved, interpretable, and transferable methods to map dialogue to evolving team processes.

Method: 1) Late chunking to create context-aware transcript representations; 2) Aggregate representations into temporal chunks; 3) Apply penalized Gaussian-kernel change-point detection to identify semantic transitions; 4) Use TF-IDF, non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF), and representative transcripts for phase interpretation; 5) Run a locally deployed LLM with in-context learning for initial interpretations; 6) Human review of interpretations; 7) Align independently recorded interaction logs with detected phases to examine task-action patterns; 8) Evaluate representations, pooling, segmentation, parameters, and phase-aligned behavior.

Result: The framework yields coherent, interpretable phase structures that are traceable to transcript evidence. Phase interpretations align with observed interaction patterns, with robustness across representations, pooling strategies, segmentation methods, and parameter settings.

Conclusion: The approach provides a transparent, transferable method for studying temporal changes in teamwork from timestamped transcripts across collaborative task settings, and supports linking discourse phases to concrete action patterns.

Abstract: Collaborative virtual reality (VR) environments make team communication observable as it unfolds, but conventional transcript analyses often summarize entire trials or divide them into fixed temporal windows. Such approaches can obscure changes in team communication and coordination over time. This article presents a computational framework for detecting and interpreting dynamic team-process phases from timestamped dialogue in a collaborative VR game. The framework uses late chunking to generate context-aware transcript representations, aggregates them into temporal chunks, and applies penalized Gaussian-kernel change-point detection to identify semantic transitions in team communication. After boundary detection, term frequency--inverse document frequency (TF-IDF), non-negative matrix factorization (NMF), and representative transcript segments provide structured evidence for phase interpretation. A locally deployed large language model (LLM) uses in-context learning to generate initial interpretations that are subsequently reviewed by humans. Independently recorded interaction logs are then aligned with the detected phases to examine corresponding task-action patterns. The evaluation compares representations, pooling strategies, segmentation methods, parameter settings, reviewed phase interpretations, and phase-aligned interaction profiles. The results show that the framework identifies coherent and interpretable phase structures while preserving traceability to the underlying transcript evidence. The correspondence between transcript-derived phases and interaction behavior further supports their relevance for analyzing collaborative activity. The framework therefore offers a transparent and transferable approach for studying temporal changes in teamwork from timestamped transcripts across collaborative task settings.

</details>


### [157] [Reinforced Planning with Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18669)
*Armin Sommer,Jannik Schilling*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: RP1 learns to reinforce planning by training a critic to evaluate imagined outcomes and an offline optimizer to improve multi-step plans, achieving superior planning with far fewer world-model rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current planners are hand-designed, distilled from hand-designed optimizers, or only learn to inform policies rather than revise plans; a fully learned planner could improve planning efficiency and effectiveness.

Method: RP1 trains two components offline on imagined rollouts from a latent world model: (1) a critic that evaluates imagined outcomes, and (2) an optimizer that improves multi-step plans; it can be attached to any pretrained latent world model and then used to guide search.

Result: Across visual navigation, arm reaching, and robotic manipulation on two world-model backbones, RP1 outperforms hand-designed search, achieving near-perfect success in several settings, using 1000x fewer rollouts, and up to 67x faster than the strongest alternative during planner inference.

Conclusion: RP1 is the first method to fully learn how to improve multi-step plans and is compatible with pretrained latent world models, yielding substantial gains in planning efficiency and performance across diverse tasks.

Abstract: Humans solve complex problems by constructing plans and mentally simulating their outcomes with an internal model of the world. Machine learning has produced world models that similarly predict the outcomes of action sequences, but the improvement of candidate plans still isn't fully learned. Current planners are either hand-designed, distilled from a hand-designed optimizer, or learned only to inform an amortized policy rather than to revise the plan itself. We introduce the Reinforced Planning, a method based on the idea that search can be learned by reinforcing good search rules into a neural planner. Our implementation RP1 learns both how to evaluate imagined outcomes through a critic, as well as how to improve multi-step plans through an optimizer trained fully offline from imagined world-model roll-outs. To our knowledge, RP1 is the first method to fully learn how to improve multi-step plans. Furthermore, it can be trained independently of and attached to any pretrained latent world model. Across visual navigation, arm reaching, and robotic manipulation on two world-model backbones, RP1 substantially outperforms hand-designed search algorithms, reaching near-perfect success in several settings while using $1,000 \times$ less world-model rollouts and being up to $67 \times$ faster than the strongest alternative under concurrent planner inference.

</details>


### [158] [An Empirical Benchmark of Deep Time-Series Models for Smart Meter Energy Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18675)
*Behnaz Kavoosighafi,Maria Eidenskog,Wiktoria Glad,Katerina Vrotsou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Nine modern DL models outperform baselines on two smart-meter datasets; performance improves with more history up to a point; horizon degrades accuracy; lightweight models offer favorable trade-offs; architecture differences matter mainly for long horizons and heterogeneous data; model choice has limited impact across most population segments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a systematic, real-world benchmark of diverse deep learning architectures for time-series forecasting on smart-meter data, addressing gaps in comparative performance under realistic data conditions.

Method: Empirically evaluate nine modern DL models (linear, MLP-based, CNN, Transformer) across two public smart-meter datasets. Systematically vary historical input length and forecast horizon; compare with classical baselines; assess computational cost, statistical significance; analyze heterogeneity (geodemographic/household groups).

Result: DL models consistently outperform classical baselines. Lightweight architectures achieve comparable accuracy with substantially lower cost. Architectural differences become pronounced primarily at longer horizons and on more heterogeneous datasets. Extending history improves accuracy up to a saturation point. Prediction accuracy declines with longer horizons. Subgroup analysis shows limited impact of model choice for most population segments.

Conclusion: For smart-meter forecasting, select lightweight DL models to balance accuracy and cost, use sufficiently long history up to saturation, and consider horizon length and dataset heterogeneity when choosing architectures. Model choice matters mainly for challenging, long-horizon or highly diverse data; otherwise, impact is modest across population segments.

Abstract: Accurate forecasting of energy consumption is important for the efficient operation of power systems, with direct implications for operational costs, energy management, and system maintenance. Due to the availability of extensive high-resolution consumption data from smart meters, data-driven methods have been used for short-term and long-term forecasting. However, their comparative performance on real-world smart meter data is still not well studied. In this paper, we present an empirical benchmark of nine modern deep learning models for time-series forecasting, including linear, MLP-based, convolutional, and Transformer architectures. We evaluate these models on two publicly available smart meter datasets. Our analysis focuses on three factors that strongly affect forecasting performance: the length of historical input, the prediction horizon, and the choice of model architecture. We show that extending the historical context improves accuracy, but only up to a saturation point, after which additional input provides limited benefit. In contrast, accuracy decreases as the prediction horizon increases. We also investigate the trade-off between prediction accuracy and computational complexity, and assess the statistical significance and practical magnitude of performance differences across models. Our results show that deep learning models consistently outperform classical baselines, while lightweight architectures achieve relatively similar performance at significantly lower computational cost. Additionally, architectural differences only become meaningful at longer forecasting horizons and on more heterogeneous datasets. Finally, a subgroup analysis across geodemographic and household categories shows that model choice has limited impact for most population segments.

</details>


### [159] [Transforming Heart Disease Prediction with Advanced Machine Learning Techniques](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18687)
*Sami Ullah,Muhammad Mohsin Khan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comparative ML study on heart-disease prediction using two public datasets; SVM excels on UCI, Simple Cart on Kaggle; multiple classifiers tested; metrics include MAE, RAE, accuracy, precision, recall, F-measure; conclusion: ML can aid early diagnosis; future work includes hybrids and newer datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine which ML classifier yields the highest accuracy and lowest error for heart disease prediction across two public datasets.

Method: Train and evaluate nine classifiers (J48, Naive Bayes, Logistic Regression, Simple Cart, Bagging, Decision Stump, AdaBoost, Artificial Neural Networks, SVM) on UCI and Kaggle heart datasets (14 attributes each), using MAE, RAE, accuracy, precision, recall, F-measure for performance; apply proper tuning and validation.

Result: On the UCI dataset, SVM achieved the highest performance; on the Kaggle dataset, Simple Cart achieved the best accuracy and lowest error rates; overall results indicate ML models can provide strong predictive performance when properly tuned and validated.

Conclusion: ML models, with appropriate tuning and validation, can aid early heart disease diagnosis; future work could explore hybrid approaches and incorporate newer datasets to further improve accuracy.

Abstract: Heart disease remains the leading cause of mortality globally, necessitating early and accurate detection to improve patient outcomes. This research focuses on the predictive analysis of heart disease using machine learning (ML) techniques, comparing the performance of multiple classifiers to identify the most accurate and least error-prone method. Two datasets from UCI and Kaggle repositories were utilized, each containing 14 attributes related to heart health indicators. Techniques including J48, Naive Bayes, Logistic Regression, Simple Cart, Bagging, Decision Stump, AdaBoost, Artificial Neural Networks, and Support Vector Machine (SVM) were applied. Evaluation metrics such as Mean Absolute Error (MAE), Relative Absolute Error (RAE), accuracy, precision, recall, and F-measure were used for performance comparison. Results revealed that SVM achieved the highest performance on the UCI dataset, while Simple Cart performed best on the Kaggle dataset, offering the highest accuracy and lowest error rates. The research work concludes that ML models, when properly tuned and validated, can significantly assist in the early diagnosis of heart disease, offering critical support for clinical decision-making. Future work may involve hybrid approaches and the use of more recent datasets to further improve prediction accuracy.

</details>


### [160] [Europe's Climate Ambition Under Scrutiny: Evidence from Deep Learning Emission Projections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18690)
*Jacopo Ghirri,Carlos Rodriguez-Pardo,Lara Aleluia Reis,Massimo Tavoni*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: EU27 emissions are projected to miss the 2030 target by about 35% (≈620 Mt CO2 shortfall) under current momentum, with the Power sector on track driven by renewables but Mobility showing little progress and becoming a major contributor to emissions; only a minority of countries align with commitments. Substantial policy action and better energy information in Europe are required.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether the EU's 2030 ambition is compatible with observed trends and quantify sectoral contributions to the gap, informing policy urgency.

Method: Deep learning applied to high-resolution socioeconomic and sectoral data for EU27 up to 2023, projecting sectoral CO2 trajectories under current momentum without assuming changes in policy pace or effectiveness beyond historical data.

Result: Total emissions exceed the 2030 target by ~35% (620 Mt CO2). A small minority of member states follow trajectories consistent with commitments. Power sector reduces emissions due to renewable energy transition, while Mobility shows minimal progress and is projected to account for over a third of total emissions by 2030, indicating structural inertia across member states rather than geographic lag.

Conclusion: An explicit call for substantial additional intervention to close Europe’s ambition-implementation gap, and for establishing up-to-date energy information in Europe to support policy action.

Abstract: The European Union has committed to reducing greenhouse gas emissions 55% below 1990 levels by 2030, but whether current trends are compatible with this ambition remains uncertain. We apply deep learning to high-resolution socioeconomic and sectoral data across EU27 member states till 2023 to project sectoral CO$_2$ trajectories under current trends, extrapolating observed sectoral momentum without assuming changes in the pace or effectiveness of the policy environment beyond what is already reflected in historical data. We project that EU27 emissions will exceed the 2030 target by 35% (620 Mt CO$_2$ shortfall), with only a small minority of countries on trajectories consistent with the bloc's commitments. While the Power sector achieves target-consistent reductions driven by the renewable transition, Mobility shows minimal progress and accounts for over a third of total emissions by 2030, reflecting a structural inertia across member states rather than geographically concentrated lag. Our findings indicate that substantial additional intervention is required to close Europe's ambition-implementation gap, and call for establishing up-to-date energy information in Europe.

</details>


### [161] [Multi-Class Electrical and Mechanical Fault Classification Using Random Convolutional Kernels](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18716)
*Mouhamadou Mansour Lo,Mouad Talbaoui,Gildas Morvan,Mathieu Rossi,Fabrice Morganti,David Mercier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SelF-Rocket evaluated for multi-class rotating machinery fault diagnosis; introduces a multivariate extension; achieves the best accuracy–latency trade-off on MaFaulDa and is competitive on ITSC-UDG.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address reliable, efficient fault diagnosis in rotating machinery using time-series classification, leveraging ROCKET-based methods. The work also fills a gap by providing a multivariate extension of SelF-Rocket and benchmarking against state-of-the-art ROCKET-based methods.

Method: Empirical evaluation of SelF-Rocket for multi-class fault diagnosis on two public datasets (MaFaulDa and ITSC-UDG) under univariate and multivariate settings, and a comparison with leading ROCKET-based methods; introduction of a multivariate extension of SelF-Rocket as a new contribution.

Result: SelF-Rocket achieves the best overall accuracy–latency trade-off among evaluated methods; records the highest classification performance on MaFaulDa and remains highly competitive on the ITSC-UDG dataset.

Conclusion: SelF-Rocket is effective for multi-class fault diagnosis in rotating machinery; the multivariate extension broadens applicability to multivariate time series and complex datasets; the approach offers favorable accuracy–latency performance, particularly on mechanical fault data, with competitive results on electrical fault datasets.

Abstract: Diagnosing faults in rotating machinery is essential for ensuring the reliability of industrial processes. Random convolutional kernel-based Time Series Classification (TSC) methods, such as ROCKET and its variants, provide an attractive trade-off between predictive performance and computational efficiency. In this work, we evaluate SelF-Rocket for the multi-class diagnosis of both mechanical and electrical faults and introduce, as a new contribution, a multivariate extension of the original method. The proposed approach is compared with leading ROCKET-based methods on two public benchmark datasets, MaFaulDa (mechanical faults) and ITSC-UDG (stator inter-turn short circuits), under both univariate and multivariate settings. Experimental results show that SelF-Rocket achieves the best overall accuracy-latency trade-off among the evaluated methods, obtaining the highest classification performance on MaFaulDa while remaining highly competitive on the more challenging ITSC-UDG dataset.

</details>


### [162] [Visual-Aware Representation of Web Pages for Machine Learning Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18727)
*Radek Burget,Radek Hranický*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A platform for visual-aware ML on web pages using FitLayout, exporting RDF-annotated rendered pages via REST/SPARQL, enabling reproducible ML datasets and GNN-based content element recognition.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interpreting HTML with resources requires rendering to capture visual and layout properties; ML on web content is underexplored; reproducibility and dataset sharing are lacking.

Method: Render pages with FitLayout to capture visual/structural properties into an RDF representation; persist rendered documents; control processing via REST API; use SPARQL to extract structured data for ML; provide Python client; demonstrate by converting rendered pages into graph representations and training graph neural networks to recognize key content elements.

Result: Demonstrates transformation of rendered pages into graph-based representations and training of GNNs to identify key content elements; highlights applicability and reproducibility of results.

Conclusion: Platform enables end-to-end dataset preparation for web-page ML, supports reproducible experiments, and facilitates dataset sharing and integration with standard ML workflows.

Abstract: Applying machine learning to web pages is challenging due to the need to interpret HTML together with associated resources and perform rendering to obtain a meaningful visual and layout-aware representation. As a result, machine learning over web content remains comparatively underexplored. In this paper, we present a platform for visual-aware representation and machine learning over web pages based on the open-source rendering tool FitLayout. The platform provides a server capable of rendering web pages, explicitly capturing their visual and structural properties in an RDF-based representation, and persisting the rendered documents in an integrated storage. The processing pipeline is controlled via a REST API, while SPARQL queries are used to retrieve structured data suitable as input for machine learning algorithms. By explicitly modeling rendered web pages, including fine-grained layout details, the platform enables dataset sharing and supports the reproducibility of experimental results. The architecture supports the complete dataset preparation workflow, from web page collection and rendering through preprocessing and annotation of content elements to downstream learning tasks. We further provide a Python client library that integrates the platform with standard machine learning workflows. As a demonstration, we show how rendered web pages can be transformed into graph-based representations and used to train graph neural networks for recognizing key content elements, illustrating both the applicability of the approach and the reproducibility of the results.

</details>


### [163] [FedLNS: Leverage LayerNorm Signature Modeling to Mitigate Adversarial Manipulation in Federated LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18736)
*Kai Li,Jong-Ik Park,Carlee Joe-Wong,Wei Ni,Falko Dressler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FedLNS is a server-side filtering method for federated language-model training that detects malicious client updates by encoding updates as changes in normalization-layer parameters, enabling history-aware screening without extra data exchange. After filtering, standard FL aggregation proceeds; it improves perplexity under target manipulation across GPT-, BERT-, and Llama-style models with 200 clients in IID and non-IID settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevent degradation of federated LM training caused by malicious clients who train on corrupted targets or introduce incorrect context-token associations, which can lead to unreliable/hallucinatory generations.

Method: Represent each client's update via changes in trainable normalization-layer parameters (signatures). The server builds a robust, history-aware cross-client reference and flags suspicious updates. Only the retained, screened updates are aggregated using standard FL rules; no extra client data, server datasets, or detectors are required. Signatures are extracted server-side from returned local models, avoiding additional client-server exchange.

Result: Empirical evaluation on GPT-style, BERT-style, and LLaMA-style models trained from scratch with 200 clients shows FedLNS achieves lower test perplexity than the strongest baseline across all three architectures under both IID and non-IID partitions, even when up to 40% of clients are manipulating targets.

Conclusion: FedLNS provides a lightweight, data-free server-side defense against malicious FL updates for language-model training, enabling effective filtering and robust aggregation without new data or detectors.

Abstract: Federated training enables language models to learn from distributed private text, but the server cannot directly verify the local supervision or optimization process that produces each client update. A malicious client can therefore train on corrupted targets, introduce incorrect context-token associations, and degrade the global model through repeated aggregation. Such degradation can also increase the risk of unreliable or hallucinatory generation. We propose Federated Learning with Normalization Signatures (FedLNS), a server-side framework for lightweight malicious-update screening. FedLNS represents each client update through changes in trainable normalization-layer parameters and screens suspicious updates against a robust, history-aware cross-client reference. Because the signatures are extracted at the server from the returned local models, FedLNS requires no additional client-to-server parameter or metadata exchange compared to standard federated learning (FL) methods. After screening, the retained full-model updates can be aggregated using standard FL or another compatible aggregation rule. FedLNS requires no raw client data, trusted server dataset, labeled attack examples, or separately trained detector. Experiments on GPT-style, BERT-style, and LLaMA-style models trained from scratch with 200 clients show that, under 40% population-level target manipulation, FedLNS achieves lower test perplexity than the strongest of six baselines for all three architectures under both IID (independently and identically distributed) and non-IID data partitions.

</details>


### [164] [Decision-Metric Alignment in Latent World Models: Diagnostics and Action-Conditioned Objectives for MPC Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18746)
*Jiawei Wang,Ke Rui,Yushen Zuo,Yichun Feng,Minglei Li*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces decision-metric alignment between latent Euclidean costs and real task performance in JEPA-style models; proposes Plan-Real Spearman and CEM-stage Spearman to quantify latent-real rank agreement; analyzes when latent distance preserves real-cost rankings; proposes DA-LeWM with inverse-dynamics and demonstration-conditioned heads; empirical results show faster convergence and higher online success than LeWM, with unchanged probe scores; action-conditioned objectives improve latent geometry for MPC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To ensure that the distance-based cost used in latent MPC aligns with actual task progress, addressing misalignment between latent representations and real-world objectives.

Method: Define two rank-agreement metrics (Plan-Real Spearman and CEM-stage Spearman); derive sufficient conditions for latent distances to preserve real-cost rankings (encoder distortion, terminal rollout error, candidate margins); augment LeWM with inverse-dynamics and demonstration-conditioned heads to form DA-LeWM; evaluate on tasks comparing DA-LeWM to LeWM across convergence speed, online success, and probe scores.

Result: DA-LeWM accelerates convergence and achieves higher online success than LeWM while maintaining similar probe scores; results indicate action-conditioned objectives improve the geometry used by Euclidean-cost in latent MPC.

Conclusion: Action-conditioned objectives reshape latent geometry to better reflect real task progress, improving decision-metric alignment and enabling more reliable latent-MPC with Euclidean costs and CEM-based search.

Abstract: JEPA-style latent world models can use Euclidean distance to a goal latent as the cost for model-predictive control (MPC). Strong decoding of task variables, however, does not guarantee that this particular cost ranks candidate action sequences by real task progress. We call the latter property \emph{decision-metric alignment}. We introduce Plan-Real Spearman, which measures latent--real rank agreement on random plans, and CEM-stage Spearman, which measures the same agreement as cross-entropy-method (CEM) search concentrates its proposal. We analyze sufficient conditions under which latent distance preserves real-cost rankings, identifying encoder distortion, terminal rollout error, and candidate margins as the controlling quantities. Guided by the observed empirical alignment gap, DA-LeWM augments LeWM with inverse-dynamics and demonstration-conditioned goal-action heads. Across all our experiments, DA-LeWM accelerates convergence and achieves higher online success than LeWM, while probe scores remain similar. These results show that action-conditioned objectives improve the geometry used by Euclidean-cost, CEM-based latent MPC.

</details>


### [165] [Geometric Data Perturbation with Noisy-Anchor Alignment for Privacy-Preserving Collaborative Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18749)
*Keiyu Nosaka,Yamato Suetake,Yuichi Takano,Yukihiko Okada,Akiko Yoshise*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Noisy-anchor perturbation for GDP mitigates collusion-based data leakage while preserving utility, outperforming private-data-noise approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address privacy risks in Geometric Data Perturbation under analyst-participant collusion; shared anchors enable recovery; need methods that preserve compatibility without leaking private data.

Method: Participants perturb private data and a shared anchor matrix; they upload noisy anchor representations and private-data representations; analyst aligns with Generalized Orthogonal Procrustes; analyze alignment errors, provide convergence condition; evaluate three recovery attacks; experiments on MNIST and CelebA.

Result: Anchor-noise improves learning accuracy vs private-data-noise for similar leakage; maintains better privacy-utility trade-off; robust against attacks under various deployment settings.

Conclusion: Introducing noise into anchor representations is a viable privacy-preserving enhancement for GDP; improves robustness to collusion with acceptable utility loss; supported by theoretical analysis and empirical results.

Abstract: Geometric Data Perturbation (GDP) enables one-shot, privacy-preserving collaborative learning: each participant applies a distance-preserving transformation to its private data and uploads only the resulting representation to a central analyst. We study GDP under analyst-participant collusion, in which the analyst combines all uploaded representations with the private data and transformations disclosed by colluding participants to recover a non-colluding participant's private data. Participant-specific independent transformations resist this attack but map participants' data into incompatible representation spaces, degrading downstream model performance. Shared-anchor alignment from Data Collaboration (DC) analysis restores compatibility and improves utility, but we show that disclosing the DC anchor matrix enables exact recovery of non-colluding participants' private data even in the presence of collusion. Adding noise directly to the private-data representations mitigates this vulnerability but substantially reduces utility. We propose adding noise to the anchor representations instead. Each participant independently transforms its private data and the shared anchor matrix, perturbs only the resulting anchor representation, and uploads both representations in a single round. Using the noisy anchor representations, the analyst aligns the private-data representations by solving a Generalized Orthogonal Procrustes Problem. We characterize alignment and recovery errors, specialize a conservative sufficient condition for convergence of the alignment to our setting, and analyze three recovery attacks. Experiments on MNIST and CelebA show that, across the evaluated attacks and deployment settings, anchor noise achieves higher learning accuracy than private-data noise at comparable measured leakage, yielding a more favorable privacy-utility trade-off under the specified collusion model.

</details>


### [166] [Beyond Predictive Fairness: Quantifying Attribution Consistency Across Demographic Groups in Diabetic Retinopathy Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18759)
*Kerol Djoumessi,Philipp Berens*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Explanation Consistency Score (ECS) to measure cross-group consistency of attribution maps using Jensen-Shannon divergence; evaluates fairness in medical imaging, specifically diabetic retinopathy screening, across ethnic groups and disease severity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether models rely on consistent visual evidence across demographic groups and to complement predictive fairness metrics with explanation-based fairness assessment.

Method: Propose ECS, a JS-divergence-based metric applied to attribution maps across subgroups. Case study in diabetic retinopathy screening. Evaluate global and severity-stratified consistency, alongside predictive performance, and assess association between performance disparities and ECS.

Result: Predictive performance varies by ethnicity, but ECS remains relatively high and shows no significant link to performance disparities, suggesting that predictive fairness and explanation consistency capture different facets of model behavior.

Conclusion: Prediction fairness and explanation consistency are complementary. Fairness evaluations should include explanation-based metrics (like ECS) in addition to traditional performance metrics to obtain a fuller understanding of model behavior in medical imaging.

Abstract: Fairness in medical imaging is commonly evaluated through subgroup performance metrics, yet it remains unclear whether models rely on consistent visual evidence across demographic groups. This work introduces the Explanation Consistency Score (ECS), a fairness-aware metric based on Jensen-Shannon divergence that quantifies the similarity of attribution maps across subgroups. Using diabetic retinopathy screening as a case study, ECS is evaluated globally and within disease severity. Experiments reveal that while predictive performance differs across ethnic groups, explanation consistency remains relatively high and shows no significant association with performance disparities. These findings suggest that predictive fairness and explanation consistency capture complementary dimensions of model behavior, motivating fairness evaluations that extend beyond predictive performance.

</details>


### [167] [Enhancing Distance-Based Graph Autoencoders with Structural Penalties for Dynamic Graph Embedding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18762)
*Aleksandar Tomčić,Miloš Savić,Miloš Radovanović*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NC-LID-based regularization enhances distance-based graph autoencoders on dynamic graphs, outperforming baseline and hub-based regularization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GAEs often ignore structural heterogeneity across nodes; incorporating structural signals can improve embedding quality, particularly for structurally ambiguous nodes. NC-LID has been linked to embedding quality, motivating its use as a regularizer.

Method: Three distance-based GAE variants with a two-layer Graph ConvNet encoder and a Euclidean-distance decoder. Extend sparsity-corrected loss with (i) a hub penalty (degree centrality) and (ii) an NC-LID-based penalty to emphasize reconstruction errors for structurally ambiguous nodes.

Result: Across multiple dynamic graph datasets, NC-LID-based regularization consistently improves reconstruction performance over the baseline (no structural regularization) and hub-based regularization. NC-LID is identified as a useful structural signal for enhancing distance-based GAEs in dynamic settings.

Conclusion: NC-LID-based regularization is a valuable tool for incorporating structural heterogeneity into distance-based GAEs, yielding better reconstruction on dynamic graphs and suggesting broader applicability of NC-LID as a regularizer in graph representation learning.

Abstract: Graph autoencoders (GAEs) are widely used for learning representations of dynamic graphs. However, their optimisation objectives typically do not take structural heterogeneity across nodes into account. We propose three distance-based GAE variants that incorporate structural penalties into the reconstruction loss. All variants share a two-layer Graph Convolutional Network encoder and a Euclidean-distance decoder trained with distance-based reconstruction objectives. We extend sparsity-corrected loss with two node-level regularization terms: (i) a hub penalty based on degree centrality, and (ii) a penalty based on Natural Community Local Intrinsic Dimensionality (NC-LID). The paper is motivated by prior evidence linking high NC-LID to reduced embedding quality. The proposed methods are designed to emphasize reconstruction errors for structurally ambiguous nodes. Experiments on multiple dynamic graph data sets show that incorporating NC-LID-based regularization consistently improves reconstruction performance over the baseline without structural regularization and the method using hub-aware regularization. These findings highlight NC-LID as a useful structural signal for enhancing distance-based graph autoencoders in dynamic settings.

</details>


### [168] [GraphK: Variable-Size Graph Generation with Efficient Edge Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18777)
*Resul Tugay,Eren Oluğ,Elif Ak,Sule Gunduz Oguducu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GraphK is an encoder-sampler-decoder graph generator that enables upscaling and downscaling, using permutation-invariant latent representations and KDTree-based edge prediction for scalable, flexible, and accurate graph generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current graph generation methods struggle with scalability, flexibility across varying graph sizes, and capturing underlying graph structure. Autoregressive approaches are limited by node vocabulary sizes and computational cost; there is a need for models that generalize across graph sizes and structures.

Method: GraphK adopts an encoder-sampler-decoder architecture with permutation-invariant latent representations. It samples new node embeddings via maximum likelihood estimation to enable size-generalization (up/down-scaling). Edge generation uses KDTree-based top-k neighbor search in latent space to predict edges efficiently. The approach relies on the manifold smoothness assumption to capture graph properties.

Result: Empirical results show GraphK outperforms existing methods on both synthetic and real-world datasets, accurately learning graph structures and generating synthetic graphs without explicit definitions.

Conclusion: GraphK provides a scalable, flexible framework for graph generation that can upscale or downscale graphs, generalizes across sizes and structures, and reduces computational costs through latent-space KDTree edge prediction.

Abstract: Graph generation models have advanced significantly with deep learning, yet they remain limited in scalability, flexibility, and ability to model underlying structures. We present GraphK, a novel encoder-sampler-decoder framework for graph generation that overcomes these challenges through structural flexibility and computational efficiency. Unlike autoregressive approaches constrained by vocabulary size (i.e. number of nodes in graph generation), GraphK allows for both upscaling (generating graphs with more nodes than the input) and downscaling, providing a flexible control over output graph size. By learning permutation-invariant latent representations and sampling new node embeddings via maximum likelihood estimation, GraphK generalizes across graph sizes and structures. For edge generation, we employ edge prediction with a KDTree-based top-k neighbor search in the latent space, reducing computational cost. Based on the manifold smoothness assumption, our method effectively captures graph properties. Experiments on synthetic and real-world datasets show that GraphK outperforms existing methods, accurately learns graph structures, and generates synthetic graphs without explicit definitions.

</details>


### [169] [A Real-Time Tsetlin Machine-based Non-intrusive Load Monitoring System on MCUs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18780)
*Tianhang Tan,Han Wu,Tousif Rahman,Shengyu Duan,Alex Yakovlev,Rishad Shafik*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TM-based NILM for edge devices; real-time, privacy-preserving on MCUs; compact model with solid accuracy across 2–4 appliances; fast inference on ESP32.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable privacy-preserving real-time NILM on resource-constrained devices by moving from offline optimization to on-device learning with a Tsetlin Machine.

Method: Formulate NILM as a classification problem; train/use a Tsetlin Machine; deploy on MCU (ESP32); model occupies 18 KB of flash; inference latency 0.43 ms.

Result: Average precision/recall: 2-appliance: 90%/96%; 4-appliance: 77%/80% on REDD dataset; model size 18 KB; latency 0.43 ms.

Conclusion: TM-based NILM on MCUs is feasible for embedded privacy-preserving edge deployment with real-time performance.

Abstract: Non-Intrusive Load Monitoring (NILM) systems estimate individual appliance energy consumption from a single aggregate meter, without requiring separate sensors for each device. By installing a single meter that measures a building's total electricity consumption, NILM algorithms can determine the active status of each appliance. However, traditional NILM systems use computationally intensive optimization algorithms to process offline data, limiting their capability for on-device deployment, where sensitive household data must be processed locally. This paper proposes a Tsetlin Machine (TM)-based NILM framework, targeting real-time applications on resource-constrained microcontrollers (MCUs), enabling privacy-preserving edge deployment. The problem is reformulated as a classification task, and the proposed approach achieves an average precision of 90% and recall of 96% for two-appliance classification, and 77% precision and 80% recall for four appliances on the REDD dataset. The trained model occupies only 18 KB of flash memory and achieves an inference latency of 0.43 ms on an ESP32, demonstrating its suitability for embedded NILM applications on MCUs.

</details>


### [170] [Forgetting, plasticity, and co-observation: a third facet of continual learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18803)
*Timm Hess,Abhishek Jha,Gido M. van de Ven,Tinne Tuytelaars*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: This work demonstrates that data co-observation—the joint access to training data—provides benefits for continual learning beyond avoiding forgetting or maintaining plasticity, improving generalization in both supervised and self-supervised settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing explanations for continual learning failures (catastrophic forgetting and reduced plasticity) do not fully account for the gap between naive sequential and offline joint training; data co-observation is proposed as a distinct contributing factor.

Method: Systematically decouple data access from stability/plasticity, compare joint vs. separate training in generic data-incremental chunking scenarios across supervised and self-supervised paradigms, and relate findings to classic CL mechanisms (distillation, memory replay).

Result: Across paradigms, joint training consistently outperforms separate training; co-observation improves generalization beyond mere retention; the advantage does not rely on a continual distribution shift; memory replay reintroduces co-observation benefits beyond forgetting, while distillation mainly acts as memory retention.

Conclusion: Data co-observation is a distinct and impactful factor in continual learning, reshaping how we understand CL gains and the role of replay mechanisms in learning representations.

Abstract: Efficient continual learning remains a fundamental challenge for deep neural networks. While catastrophic forgetting and loss of plasticity are widely considered the primary obstacles to overcome, we show that these two issues cannot fully explain the performance gap between naive sequential training and offline joint training. In this paper, we highlight data co-observation as a distinct factor influencing continual learning performance. By decoupling the constraints of separate data access from stability and plasticity, we systematically investigate the representational benefits gained by observing training data together. Empirically, we demonstrate a consistent performance difference between joint and separate training across both supervised and self-supervised paradigms in generic data-incremental "chunking" scenarios, whilst mitigating forgetting and controlling for plasticity. Our findings indicate that simultaneous observation of training data (co-observation) yields benefits to the learner's generalization that extend well beyond mere knowledge retention, and that this effect does not require a specific continual distribution shift. Furthermore, we contextualize prominent continual learning mechanisms through this lens: while distillation-based approaches act only as effective knowledge retention mechanisms, our results suggest that the empirical success of memory replay goes beyond the mitigation of forgetting, actively reintroducing the benefits of data co-observation into the learning process.

</details>


### [171] [Tensor Field Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18808)
*Alexander Strunk,Roland Assam*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TFMs define a realization-level operator mapping admissible component-sections to time-dependent tangent fields on a Generative State Manifold, trained with Flow Matching; reusable condition representations enable amortized sampling and faster generation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To encode analytic/dynamical restrictions in a structured, component-separable operator framework, reducing design burden and enabling efficient generative sampling.

Method: Define Tensor Field Models (TFMs) with a Field Operator that combines distinct component representations, conditioned via c to reusable H_c, trained end-to-end using Flow Matching; extend to component-separable and Tensor Bundle TFMs as refinements.

Result: TFMs improve performance on evaluated tasks; amortized sampling via reusable conditions accelerates generation; Tensor Bundle TFMs provide structured refinements.

Conclusion: TFMs offer a principled, structured extension of common operator-based generative models, where reuse of condition representations enables efficiency gains and better performance.

Abstract: This paper introduces Tensor Field Models (TFMs), realization-level Mathematical Structures in which a learned Operator maps a product of admissible component-section families to a prescribed family of time-dependent tangent sections on a Generative State Manifold. Analytic and dynamical restrictions are encoded through the choice of admissible families rather than imposed by the root definition. Constructed, component-separable, and Tensor Bundle TFMs provide structured refinements of this common object. In the conditional realizations considered here, a structured condition $c=(c_1,\ldots,c_n)$ is mapped componentwise to a reusable collection $\mathbf H_c=(H_{c_1}^{(1)},\ldots,H_{c_n}^{(n)})$. In the architectures evaluated here, the component representations remain distinct and are combined only by the Field Operator to produce the generated Vector Field. All learned models are trained using Flow Matching. Experiments show that TFMs can improve performance and that amortized sampling enabled by reusable condition representations can accelerate generation.

</details>


### [172] [Many Optimizers But Only One Training Path: Repeated Resampling for Adaptive Optimizer Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18810)
*Ronald Richman,Mario V. Wüthrich*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: One-epoch Repeated Optimizer Resampling (ROR) dynamically swaps among candidate optimizers during training by short scouting intervals, achieving near-best fixed-optimizer performance at significantly lower cost across multiple tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hyperparameterizing the optimizer choice can boost performance, but exhaustive search over optimizers is costly and wastes partially trained runs. ROR aims to adapt the optimizer during training to changing training dynamics.

Method: At intervals b epochs, every candidate optimizer is evaluated by training from the current weights for s epochs. The best-performing scout continues for the remaining b−s epochs; if it improves the validation objective, it becomes the new incumbent. Two ROR variants are compared to nine fixed optimizers on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and two motor insurer claim-count models, using ten seeds.

Result: One-epoch ROR used only 24% to 35% of the aggregate training time needed to exhaustively identify the best fixed optimizer, and it remained close to that optimum across all four tasks.

Conclusion: Short, periodic scouting over optimizers is a practical approach to search over optimizers without fully training every candidate, enabling dynamic optimizer selection during training.

Abstract: An optimizer is usually chosen before training a deep neural network and then kept fixed. Treating optimizer choice as a hyperparameter could boost performance, but it requires several complete training runs and discards all but the winner. Repeated Optimizer Resampling (ROR) instead searches during one evolving run. Every $b$ epochs, each candidate optimizer scouts from the current model weights for $s$ epochs. The best scout continues for the remaining $b-s$ epochs, and that completed segment becomes the new incumbent if it improves the validation objective. This design allows the preferred optimizer to change as training progresses.
  We compare two variants of ROR on MNIST, Fashion-MNIST, and two motor insurance claim-count models. Nine fixed optimizers and both ROR variants are evaluated with the same ten seeds. One-epoch ROR uses 24\% to 35\% of the aggregate training needed to identify the best fixed optimizer exhaustively and remains close to that optimizer on all four tasks. These results support short scouting as a practical way to search over optimizers without completing every candidate run.

</details>


### [173] [A Unifying Relational Perspective on Expressive Lottery Tickets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18819)
*Lorenz Kummer,Samir Moustafa,Anatol Ehrlich,Franka Bause,Marco Nennstiel,Przemysław Andrzej Wałȩga,Nils Morten Kriege*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Sparse RGNNs can preserve 1-RWL expressivity via sparse subnetworks; a probabilistic bound on random pruning exists; TGNNs can inherit RGNN guarantees through reformulations; expressivity relates to optimization; experiments support the theory on temporal and molecular tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand how parameter sparsity impacts the expressivity of relational and temporal GNN variants, and whether sparse subnetworks can retain WL-like expressivity (1-RWL) in realistic multi-relational/temporal settings.

Method: Theoretically generalize the Strong Expressive Lottery Ticket Hypothesis to relational/temporal domains using relational WL (RWL); prove that sufficiently parameterized RGNNs contain sparse subnetworks maintaining 1-RWL expressivity; derive a lower bound on the probability that a random pruning yields such a subnetwork; show TGNNs and cross-graph message passing can be reformulated as RGNNs with the same guarantees; empirically instantiate the bound and relate pre-training expressivity to optimization and prediction metrics on temporal and molecular benchmarks.

Result: Proved existence of sparse RGNN subnetworks preserving 1-RWL expressivity with a lower bound on the success probability of random pruning; established reformulations of common TGNNs to RGNNs inheriting these guarantees; experimentally validated the bound and analyzed connections between pre-training expressivity, optimization dynamics, and predictive performance on temporal and molecular data.

Conclusion: Sparse subnetworks can retain the expressive power equivalent to relational WL in RGNNs, and these guarantees extend to TGNNs via reformulations. The work links expressivity to optimization behavior and pre-training, offering a principled basis for sparse, efficient relational- and temporal-GNN architectures.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are widely used, but how parameter sparsity affects the expressivity of relational (RGNNs) and temporal (TGNNs) variants is poorly understood. The Strong Expressive Lottery Ticket Hypothesis (SELTH) posits the existence of sparse GNNs that preserve Weisfeiler-Leman (WL) expressivity on static graphs. We generalize this existence result to a probabilistic statement for multi-relational and temporal domains via the relational WL (RWL). We prove that sufficiently parameterized RGNNs contain sparse subnetworks that maintain 1-RWL expressivity and derive a lower bound on the probability that a random pruning yields such a subnetwork. We show that common TGNNs and cross-graph message passing schemes admit RGNN reformulations such that they inherit these guarantees and, moreover, that the expressivity of a sparse RGNN is connected to its optimization behavior under common update regimes. Experiments instantiate the bound, compare it to empirical probabilities on synthetic data, and study how pre-training expressivity relates to optimization and prediction quality metrics on temporal and molecular benchmarks.

</details>


### [174] [MLREF: Efficient Module Reuse for Reward Design in Reinforcement Learning via Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18827)
*Chenglin Liu,Xun Wang,Ruishuo Chen,Zhuoran Li,Longbo Huang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A modular reward-evolution framework (MLREF) maintains a persistent pool of reusable reward components and constructs rewards as linear combinations from this pool, yielding improved performance and stability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reward design in reinforcement learning is a bottleneck; existing monolithic reward programs hinder reuse and cause unstable performance across iterations. MLREF enables component reuse and robust optimization through modular evolution.

Method: Maintain a module pool as the primary optimization target; evolve the pool by accumulating successful modules, refining underperforming ones, and reusing proven components; build reward functions as linear combinations of pool modules; employ reflection-based refinement, hybrid credit assignment, and a rollback-enabled merge strategy; evaluate on 17 tasks.

Result: MLREF outperforms strong baselines by 25.2% on locomotion and 6.6% on manipulation, with more stable optimization dynamics.

Conclusion: Module-level reward evolution with a persistent module pool improves reward design by enabling reuse of effective components, leading to more robust and scalable performance across tasks.

Abstract: Reward function design remains a bottleneck in reinforcement learning. While large language models (LLMs) have enabled automated reward generation, existing methods generate and revise reward functions as monolithic programs, making it difficult to reliably preserve and reuse effective components discovered in earlier iterations, leading to unstable performance across iterations. To address this, we propose Module Level Reward Evolution Framework (MLREF). At the core of MLREF is a module pool, a persistent repository of reusable reward components. MLREF treats the module pool as the primary optimization object: the pool evolves across iterations by accumulating successful modules, refining underperforming ones, and reusing proven components; while reward functions are constructed as linear combinations of modules drawn from this pool. To drive this evolution, MLREF integrates three mechanisms: reflection-based refinement, hybrid credit assignment, and a merge strategy with rollback, which together improve the effectiveness and robustness of reward optimization. Experiments on 17 tasks show that MLREF outperforms strong baselines by 25.2% in locomotion and 6.6% in manipulation, with more stable optimization dynamics.

</details>


### [175] [GEAR: Generative Expansion and Real Anchoring for Two-Stage Distillation of Tabular Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18849)
*Qi Qin,Jiajie Zhu,Dali Chen,Yuzhao Zhang,Jia-Xing Han,Yu Su,Peng Zhang,Ying Yan,Yifan Sun*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GEAR is a two-stage distillation framework that compresses tabular foundation models into lightweight predictors (MLPs or trees) for CPU deployment, using synthetic queries and real-label re-anchoring to expand coverage and reduce latency/memory while preserving or improving accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome latency and memory costs of context-dependent inference in TFMs when deployed at scale, enabling efficient CPU-based deployment without major accuracy loss.

Method: Stage 1: use synthetic covariates as teacher-query locations; train student on soft TFM targets to expand coverage beyond observed rows. Stage 2: re-anchor to real labels and out-of-fold teacher predictions to avoid leakage; derive a risk certificate relating query volume to generator fidelity.

Result: Two-stage MLPs outperform supervised MLPs by 1.81–2.00 AUC in binary tasks and 1.19–1.35 in multiclass; gains of 1.76–2.19 and 2.09–2.40 over real-data-only distillation; binary tasks gains transfer to LightGBM/XGBoost; CatBoost baseline underperforms relative to TFMs; GEAR yields large reductions in median inference time (57–2866x) and peak memory (1.9–3.3x) while maintaining higher AUC; ablations show stability benefits and diminishing returns with more queries.

Conclusion: GEAR provides a practical, broadly applicable distillation approach that substantially reduces runtime resources for tabular models while preserving or enhancing predictive performance, suitable for CPU deployment and capable of leveraging both MLP and tree-based student families.

Abstract: Tabular foundation models (TFMs) achieve strong performance through in-context learning, but context-dependent inference imposes substantial latency and memory costs, hindering large-scale deployment. We propose GEAR (\emph{Generative Expansion and Real Anchoring}), a modular two-stage framework that distills TFMs into lightweight MLP or tree-based predictors that can be deployed on commodity CPUs. Stage 1 uses synthetic covariates solely as teacher-query locations and trains the student on soft TFM targets, expanding coverage beyond observed rows.
