Stage 2 re-anchors the student to the target distribution using real labels and out-of-fold teacher predictions, whitch avoids self-labeling leakage. We further derive a risk certificate characterizing the trade-off between generated-query volume and generator fidelity. Experiments on TALENT and TabArena demonstrate the broad applicability of GEAR. Two-stage MLPs outperform supervised MLPs by 1.81--2.00 AUC points on binary tasks and 1.19--1.35 points on multiclass tasks, with additional gains over real-data-only distillation of 1.76--2.19 and 2.09--2.40 points, respectively. On binary tasks, the gains also transfer to LightGBM and XGBoost, and all three student families outperform CatBoost, the strongest non-TFM baseline, in mean AUC. Ablations show gains beyond longer training or alternative warm starts, greater stability from staged than mixed optimization, and generator-dependent diminishing returns as query volume increases. Finally, GEAR reduces median inference time by 57--2866 times and peak prediction memory by 1.9--3.3 times, while retaining higher AUC than matched supervised baselines.

</details>


### [176] [Multi-stage neural operator learning with application for convolutions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18851)
*Zhiping Mao,Zhenye Wen,Yong Zhang,Xiaofei Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Two multi-stage neural operator frameworks for convolutions: DCNO (supervised residual refinement) and DGNO (unsupervised weak-form PDE), achieving near machine-precision accuracy and efficiency gains, with multi-input extensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Convolution integrals are widespread in applications; standard one-shot operator learning often trades accuracy for speed. A multi-stage approach aims to progressively enrich the operator space for better accuracy and adaptability.

Method: DCNO: supervised, iteratively refines the operator by learning residuals from input-output data. DGNO: unsupervised, trains via the weak form of underlying PDE residuals when the target operator admits a PDE representation. Both build basis operators across multiple training stages and extend to multi-input scenarios (density and kernel variations).

Result: Theoretical analysis of approximation capabilities is provided. Numerical experiments show high accuracy approaching machine precision in single precision for convolution problems, with substantial efficiency gains for many queries or parametric variations compared to traditional solvers. Extensions to multi-input operator learning demonstrated.

Conclusion: The frameworks offer a powerful, general approach to learning convolution operators with high accuracy and efficiency, and extend to multi-input settings, broadening applicability to varied density/kernel configurations.

Abstract: Convolution integrals widely exist in applications, and to enable fast and accurate computations, this paper introduces two general multi-stage neural operator learning frameworks. The first, Deep Collocation Neural Operator (DCNO), is a supervised approach that iteratively refines the operator approximation by learning residuals from input-output data pairs. The second, Deep Galerkin Neural Operator (DGNO), is an unsupervised framework applicable when the target operator can be represented by a PDE, leveraging the weak form of the PDE residual for training. Both methods progressively construct basis operators through multiple training stages to enrich the approximation space, leading to significantly improved accuracy over standard one-shot operator learning. We provide theoretical analysis for their approximation capabilities and implement them for learning convolutions. Extensive numerical experiments demonstrate that both DCNO and DGNO achieve high accuracy, approaching machine precision under single float for convolution problems, and offer substantial efficiency gains for numerous queries or parametric variations compared to traditional solvers. We also extend these frameworks to handle multi-input operator learning scenarios involving variations in both the density and kernel of a convolution.

</details>


### [177] [Graph-Based Approaches to Learning Epileptogenic Zone Localization Using Stereo-EEG Recordings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18887)
*Daniel Wendelken,Brian Ervin,Ravindra Arya,Ali A. Minai*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A controlled comparison of graph-topology designs for EZ localization from resting-state sEEG in 40 patients shows Region-Bridge-c with ~30% edge retention gives best performance (PR-AUC ~0.371, ROC-AUC ~0.743) using ~69% fewer edges than dense graphs; topology choice and sparsification method impact results, with no universal best across patients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graph-based models of resting-state sEEG aim to localize the epileptogenic zone more efficiently and accurately, but their performance strongly depends on how the brain connectivity graph is constructed.

Method: A leave-one-patient-out evaluation across 40 patients comparing dense graphs, anatomy/geography-informed priors, budgeted sparsification, and learned sparsification, including Region-Bridge-c; edge budget is controlled by fixing the number of incoming edges per node and varying sparsity.

Result: Region-Bridge-c at ~30% edge retention achieves highest mean PR-AUC (0.371±0.015) and ROC-AUC (0.743±0.010) using ~69% fewer edges than Dense (PR-AUC 0.349±0.014); Spatial-k is competitive; random pruning requires near-dense retention; learned sparsification benefits from anatomical metadata but generally does not surpass the best fixed prior; the best topology varies by patient.

Conclusion: Graph construction is a critical hyperparameter for EZ localization from resting-state sEEG; explicit evaluation of topology is necessary, as no single graph design universally outperforms others across patients.

Abstract: The epileptogenic zone (EZ) is the brain region that generates seizures in an individual, and is the target of epilepsy surgery. Localizing the EZ from stereo-EEG (sEEG) recordings supports surgical planning, but manual interpretation is time-consuming and focuses on seizure recordings. Graphical learning models of resting-state functional connectivity among the recorded brain regions are an attractive alternative, but depend crucially on the network topology chosen for the model.
  We present a controlled study of graph-based models to explore how graph topology affects EZ localization from resting-state sEEG in 40 patients. Using the same simple learnable model and leave-one-patient-out evaluation, we compare dense graphs, anatomy- and geometry-informed priors, budgeted sparsification methods, and learned sparsification, including the proposed Region-Bridge-$c$ topology. To compare graph constructions fairly, we control the number of incoming edges per node and vary graph sparsity.
  At $\approx 30\%$ edge retention, Region-Bridge-$c$ achieves the highest observed mean PR-AUC ($0.371\pm0.015$; ROC-AUC $0.743\pm0.010$) while using $\approx 69\%$ fewer edges than Dense (PR-AUC $0.349\pm0.014$). Spatial-$k$ is competitive, whereas random pruning requires near-dense retention. Learned sparsification benefits from anatomical node metadata but, on average, does not surpass the best fixed prior. Across all topologies, the best choice varies by patient. These results suggest that graph construction should be evaluated explicitly rather than treated as fixed preprocessing.

</details>


### [178] [A FEM-Based Surrogate Modelling and Optimization Framework for Physics-Constrained Electromagnetic Coil Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18903)
*Yucheng Liu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Surrogate-assisted optimization for a seven-parameter coil–core benchmark shows optimizer performance depends on budget; no single method universally superior; EI-BO offers fast early progress, COBYLA early strength, and BOBYQA best terminal mean; results are conditional on an axisymmetric, physics-constrained setting and do not establish a fixed optimum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess the effectiveness of surrogate-assisted, physics-constrained optimization for coil–core design under geometry, manufacturing, and material-allocation constraints; compare optimization strategies (EI-BO, COBYLA, BOBYQA) under varying FEM-evaluation budgets and validate incumbents with FEM.

Method: Two-dimensional axisymmetric FEM model coupled to a Matern 5/2 GP surrogate via a Python–MPh–COMSOL workflow. Seven-parameter design subject to explicit physical, geometric, manufacturing, and material-allocation constraints. Bayesian optimization using expected improvement (EI) for candidate ranking; five paired runs with FEM verification of incumbents. A retrospective finite-pool study and a selected-design check at a common total current anchor conclusions.

Result: Optimizer ranking depends on FEM-evaluation budget: EI–BO improves rapidly at small budgets; COBYLA stronger at earliest checkpoint; BOBYQA attains the highest mean terminal response. Retrospective study finds no robust endpoint advantage of EI over posterior-mean ranking on the smooth surface. Broader conclusion: progress, terminal response, information use, and wall-clock cost can favor different methods in simulation-driven design. The selected-design check corroborates the observed ordering, but conclusions remain conditional on this axisymmetric benchmark and do not claim fixed-current optimum, fixed-power performance, or electrical-efficiency superiority.

Conclusion: In simulation-driven design under physics-constrained constraints, there is no universally superior optimization method. Algorithm performance trades off early progress, terminal performance, and resource costs; results are benchmark-specific and require broader validation across designs and operating points.

Abstract: This work evaluates surrogate-assisted optimization of a seven-parameter current-excited coil--core benchmark subject to geometric, manufacturing, and separate core and copper mass constraints. A Python--MPh--COMSOL workflow couples a two-dimensional axisymmetric finite-element method (FEM) model to a Matern 5/2 Gaussian-process (GP) probabilistic surrogate. Here, physics-constrained denotes a design problem evaluated by a governing-equation FEM model and restricted by explicit physical, geometric, manufacturing, and material-allocation constraints; it does not denote a physics-informed GP architecture. Sequential Bayesian optimization (BO) ranks candidates using expected improvement (EI), and every reported incumbent is verified by FEM. Five paired runs show that optimizer ranking depends on the available FEM-evaluation budget: EI--BO improves rapidly at small continuation budgets, COBYLA is stronger at the earliest checkpoint, and BOBYQA attains the highest mean terminal response. A retrospective finite-pool study further finds no robust endpoint advantage of EI over posterior-mean ranking on this smooth response surface. The broader result is that early progress, terminal response, information use, and wall-clock cost can favor different methods in simulation-driven design. A selected-design check at a common total current preserves the observed BOBYQA--COBYLA--EI-BO ordering. The conclusions nevertheless remain conditional on this axisymmetric benchmark and do not establish a fixed-current optimum, fixed-power performance, or electrical-efficiency superiority.

</details>


### [179] [On the Slow Convergence to Trivial Solutions of Algorithms for Hard Optimization Problems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18910)
*Ali Hussaini Umar,Jean Barbier,Matthieu Jonckheere,Manuel Sáenz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Finite-size effects slow convergence to asymptotic limits in hard combinatorial optimization; in intermediate/highly constrained regimes, local algorithms surpass high-density predictions, underscoring the continued importance of sophisticated algorithm design beyond asymptotic theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how real, finite-size instances behave relative to worst-case and asymptotic predictions, and to assess the practical performance of algorithms on random instances of MIS and Max-K-SAT across regimes.

Method: Combine rigorous large-graph asymptotics with numerical experiments on canonical problems (maximum independent set and maximum K-SAT), analyzing easy, intermediate, and hard regimes to compare finite-size performance with asymptotic bounds.

Result: Algorithms eventually approach theoretically predicted bounds but the convergence is slow; in the intermediate regime with high constraint density, local algorithms achieve substantially better solutions than the high-density asymptotic predictions; this creates a gap between finite-regime performance and asymptotic forecasts.

Conclusion: Finite-size effects are crucial for practical algorithm design. Asymptotic pessimism can be misleading, and sophisticated heuristics remain valuable even when theory suggests failure in the limit.

Abstract: Hard combinatorial optimization problems, many of which are NP-hard, present fundamental algorithmic challenges. Average-case analysis on random instances has emerged as a powerful framework for understanding typical algorithmic performance beyond worst-case guarantees. A substantial body of work has established negative results: for sufficiently hard instances (often controlled by the underlying graph connectivity/constraints density), no known polynomial-time algorithm can significantly outperform naive heuristics in the double asymptotic limit where both problem size and constraints density tend to infinity. We revisit this picture by studying the finite-size behavior of some optimization algorithms across easy, intermediate, and hard regimes. Through rigorous analysis of large-graph asymptotics combined with numerical experiments on canonical problems (maximum independent set and maximum $K$-SAT), we demonstrate that while algorithms do eventually converge to theoretically predicted bounds, this convergence can be remarkably slow. In the intermediate regime where instances are already highly constrained, local algorithms achieve solutions substantially better than their predicted performance in the high-constraint-density limit. This gap between finite-regime and asymptotic behavior has important practical implications: sophisticated algorithmic design remains crucial even when asymptotic theory predicts inevitable failure.

</details>


### [180] [Converting Expert Deliberation into Financial Signals Through A Context-Aware NLP Pipeline](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18911)
*Vivek Batra,Kristin Chen,Sanjiv Das,Samuel Judge,Harshad Khadilkar,Sukrit Mittal,Amir Nasrollahzadeh,Daniel Ostrov,Jacob Sisk*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A contextual NLP feature-engineering pipeline (CDSP) converts investment committee transcripts into predictive features for month-ahead asset performance, with best performance from combining sentence embeddings and engineered CDSP features (0.73 F1), though not statistically better than the baseline of always selecting stocks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether experts’ deliberations contain forward-looking information that can be extracted via context-aware NLP, and whether transcript-derived features improve financial predictions over baselines.

Method: Segment transcripts into topical chunks; assign asset-class context labels using a large language model (LLM); map financial keywords to a predefined taxonomy; construct features including sentiment polarity and mention frequency; evaluate raw text, sentence embeddings, and their combination on 48 monthly meetings to predict next-month relative performance of global equities vs global bonds.

Result: Prediction accuracy ranges from 62% to 73% across feature representations; the best model (sentence embeddings + engineered CDSP features) achieves 0.73 F1, not statistically significant vs the baseline of always choosing stocks which has 60.4% success; sentiment outperforms mention frequency for several taxonomy categories.

Conclusion: Context-aware NLP can extract forward-looking information from expert deliberations, and engineered features provide useful signals, though statistical significance against simple baselines may be limited; sentiment signals appear particularly informative for some taxonomy categories.

Abstract: We introduce the CDSP (context-conditional deliberation signal pipeline), converting an investment committee's meeting transcripts into structured predictive features. CDSP segments the meeting transcripts into topical chunks, assigns asset-class context labels using a large language model (LLM), maps financial keywords to a pre-determined taxonomy of labels, and constructs complementary features: sentiment polarity and mention frequency. This feature engineering framework is applied to a dataset spanning 48 monthly committee meetings to predict if global equities will perform better or worse than global bonds in the following month. In experiments with engineered features, raw transcript text, sentence embeddings, and combined representations, the prediction accuracy ranges from 62% to 73%, compared to always choosing stocks, which outperforms bonds 60.4% of the time. The best (73% accurate) model combines sentence embeddings with engineered CDSP features, achieving a 0.73 F1 score (although this is not statistically significant compared to always choosing stocks). Sentiment carries a stronger signal than mention frequency for several taxonomy categories. These findings suggest that experts' deliberations may contain forward-looking information that context-aware NLP can extract.

</details>


### [181] [Score the Algebra, Not the Span: Dimension Reduction for Transfer Operator Models of Dynamical Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18918)
*Mark Kozdoba,Shie Mannor*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Rank-based spectral reduction for Koopman operators fails on systems composed of several weakly interacting components: it either needs exponential modes or discards components. The authors propose an algebraic (σ-algebra) approach that uses generators to capture products/powers, with a χ²-divergence criterion guaranteeing that twice the intrinsic dimension suffices to embed the full spectral information. A variational estimator recovers VAMP in the bilinear limit. Empirical results show rank-based methods miss masked components even at large k, while a small set of algebra generators recovers them and enables labeling-based prediction where regressions fail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of rank-based, linear-kernel Koopman-based dimension reduction for multi-component dynamical systems, where interactions cause components to be masked or require many modes to represent their couplings.

Method: Replace coordinate-based (rank) models with an algebra-centered representation based on the σ-algebra generated by coordinates. Introduce a χ²-divergence between the embedded present and future as a criterion, with a budget guarantee: 2× the intrinsic dimension suffices to embed the operator’s spectrum. Use a variational formulation with estimators; restricting to bilinear class yields the VAMP score on the span.

Result: The algebraic approach yields a representation that captures the full spectrum with far fewer coordinates (e.g., 10 algebra coordinates recover masked components that rank-based methods miss for k<100). Demonstrates that masked components can be predicted from few labels, while high-dimensional regression or VAMP features fail.

Conclusion: Algebra-based coordinates offer a principled, structure-aware alternative to rank-based dimension reduction, with computationally tractable estimators, theoretical guarantees on embedding capacity, and empirical evidence of superiority in detecting and predicting components that traditional spectral methods miss.

Abstract: Dimension reduction for dynamical systems is standard practice, and the standard route is spectral: model the transfer (Koopman) operator by its leading modes. We show that on systems assembled from several weakly interacting components --- a structure common in physical and biological settings --- this may either require an exponential number of modes, or drop an entire component: the component is absent from the model rather than modeled coarsely, and no function of it can be predicted at any accuracy. We call this linear masking.
  The cause is that a rank-based model pays one coordinate per mode. We propose to score instead the $σ$-algebra the coordinates generate, so that products and powers come free and a component's cost is governed only by its generators rather than by all its interactions. The criterion is a $χ^2$-divergence between the embedded present and future, and it carries a budget guarantee: twice the intrinsic dimension of the dynamics is enough coordinates for an embedding whose algebra carries the operator's entire spectrum, with its full infinite rank.
  In variational form the criterion admits off-the-shelf estimators, and restricting its critic to the bilinear class returns the VAMP score on the span, so rank-based methods are one end of the same family. We demonstrate the proposed objective on a composite of published benchmark systems. We exhibit examples where the rank-based methods completely miss the masked components at all ranks $k<100$, while ten algebra coordinates recover all of them. In addition, the resulting algebra representation supports predicting the masked components from few labels, while direct regression from the high-dimensional observation or from the VAMP features fail.

</details>


### [182] [Lost in Aggregation: How Benchmarks Overlook Irreplaceable Model Strengths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18919)
*Andrej Tschalzev,Stefan Lüdtke,Heiner Stuckenschmidt,Christian Bartelt*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a data-centric peak performance frontier for benchmark evaluation to identify irreplaceable, sufficient, redundant, and fallible models, showing aggregation metrics miss dataset-specific strengths on TabArena.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond average performance metrics and capture dataset-level peak performance opportunities and model necessity for best results.

Method: Apply the frontier framework to TabArena benchmark; categorize models by their position relative to the frontier; analyze correlation between aggregation metrics and frontier-based notions.

Result: Aggregation metrics are highly correlated and reflect consistency and avoiding failures, but poorly align with dataset-level irreplaceability; many models never best yet score well on aggregations; frontier reveals potential for unique strengths to expand peak performance.

Conclusion: Benchmark progress should track whether new models expand the set of attainable peak performances on datasets, not just improve aggregation scores.

Abstract: Tabular machine learning benchmarks typically summarize performance by averaging scores, ranks, or pairwise wins across datasets. Such aggregates are useful for selecting robust default models, but they can obscure a different question: which models are necessary to attain peak performance on particular datasets? We argue that benchmark evaluation should also consider the data-centric peak performance frontier, defined by the best statistically supported performance achieved on each dataset. From this perspective, a model may be irreplaceable, sufficient, redundant, or fallible depending on where it lies on the frontier relative to other models. Applying this framework to the TabArena benchmark, we find that common aggregation metrics are highly correlated and largely measure consistency and avoiding failures, while being much less aligned with dataset-level irreplaceability. Consequently, models performing decently across datasets without ever being the best choice are rewarded while models with unique dataset-specific strengths appear mediocre under aggregation. Hence, benchmark progress should be measured not only by improvements on aggregation metrics but also by whether new models expand the set of attainable peak performances across datasets.

</details>


### [183] [Transportable Causal Effect Estimation across Networks under Interference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18932)
*Xiaojing Du,Jiuyong Li,Lin Liu,Debo Cheng,Jixue Liu,Thuc Duy Le*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes TranCE, a transportability framework for causal effects under network interference across different networks, using selection diagrams with covariate and structural network shifts, delivering doubly robust transport formulas and empirical validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interventions are often deployed in populations/networks differing from those used for training; topology, covariate composition, and spillover pathways vary, making direct transport of causal effects nontrivial.

Method: Extend selection diagrams to network settings to model covariate shift and structural network shift as separate selectors; derive transport formulas for direct, spillover, and total effects; implement TranCE with an interventional outcome model, domain density-ratio reweighting, and cross-fitted inference; rely on a doubly-robust combination.

Result: Validated on two semi-synthetic benchmarks from real networks and a real weather-insurance field experiment; transported effects concur with held-out randomized estimates, demonstrating effectiveness.

Conclusion: Transported causal effects across networks are feasible with the proposed framework, enabling better intervention strategies in networked systems such as social networks and public health.

Abstract: Estimating causal effects under network interference typically assumes that the network used for training and the network used for deployment coincide. In practice, an intervention is run on one population while the question of interest concerns a different population, and the two generally differ in topology, node-covariate composition, and spillover pathways. Transporting a causal effect across networks is therefore a data-fusion problem that no existing algorithm solves. We employ a selection diagram, extended to the network setting so that covariate shift and structural network shift enter as separate selectors, and derive from it a transport formula for the direct, spillover, and total effects in the deployment population. Each formula makes explicit which interventional mechanism is assumed invariant and which observational distribution must be reweighted. We then turn the formulas into TranCE (Transported Causal Effects), a doubly-robust algorithm combining an interventional outcome model, a domain density-ratio correction, and cross-fitted inference. Extensive experiments on two semi-synthetic benchmarks derived from real-world social networks and on a fully real weather-insurance field experiment, where the transported effects are checked against held-out randomized estimates, confirm the effectiveness of our approach. Our findings have the potential to improve intervention strategies in networked systems, particularly in social networks and public health.

</details>


### [184] [Graphical Design of Interpretable Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18936)
*Pietro Barbiero*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a graphical notation for designing interpretable AI architectures, adapted from Penrose tensor notation, that globally represents architectures and maps 1:1 to PyTorch einsum code; applied to interpretable constructs (concept bottlenecks, sparse probes, prototype networks, neural additive models, mixtures of linear models) and to Steerling-8B, yielding global architectural insights and a compact 33-line PyTorch implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing representations either lack a global view (symbolic equations) or omit concrete tensor manipulations (probabilistic graphical models/flowcharts). A pictorial, tensor-aware notation could unify design, interpretability, and reproducibility by providing a global perspective that directly translates to executable code.

Method: Develop a graphical notation inspired by Penrose tensor notation for representing tensor operations in neural architectures. Establish a one-to-one mapping to PyTorch einsum to ensure executable code can be generated. Apply the notation to interpretable-by-construction architectures (concept bottlenecks, sparse probes, prototype networks, neural additive models, mixtures of linear models) and to the architecture of Steerling-8B, an interpretable frontier language model. Use the diagrams to derive architectural insights (e.g., residual structure) and provide a direct translation to concise PyTorch code (reported as 33 lines).

Result: The notation yields a global, interpretable view of architectures and their component tensor operations, enabling immediate inference of properties (like Steerling being a residual model) and a ready-made translation to runnable PyTorch code (33 lines). It also supports analysis of multiple interpretable architectures within a unified diagrammatic framework.

Conclusion: A graphical, tensor-aware notation can bridge high-level architectural design and low-level implementation, enhancing interpretability, reproducibility, and comparative analysis across interpretable AI systems. Further work could validate scalability, ease of adoption, and breadth of applicability to more architectures.

Abstract: Designing, implementing, and comparing interpretable architectures requires a formal language to represent them. The most common representations fall short in one of two ways. Symbolic equations give no global view of an architecture at a glance. Probabilistic graphical models and flowcharts do not describe actual tensor manipulations, thus hiding key insights and limiting reproducibility. To close this gap, we introduce a graphical notation for designing interpretable AI architectures, adapted from Penrose tensor notation. This graphical notation gives a global view of an architecture and maps one to one onto PyTorch einsum code. We first use this notation to describe architectures that are interpretable by construction, including concept bottlenecks, sparse probes, prototype networks, neural additive models, and mixtures of linear models. We then diagram the key architectural components of Steerling-8B, a frontier interpretable language model. The diagram yields global insights into the architecture (e.g., showing that Steerling is a residual model), a geometric interpretation of each individual operation, and a direct translation into 33 lines of PyTorch code.

</details>


### [185] [Training Chemical Plausibility-Aware Large Language Models for Single-Step Retrosynthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18940)
*Bogdan Zagribelnyy,Ivan Ilin,Nikita Bondarev,Maksim Kuznetsov,Mathieu Reymond,Vladimir Aladinskiy,Alex Aliper,Alex Zhavoronkov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Top-K prompting for robust one-step retrosynthesis; ultra-large CREED-CCV-2+USPTO-XL data to train C3LM; fine-tuning with ChemCensor-based and novelty rewards; achieves SOTA on URSA-expert-2026; suggests ensemble systems due to complementary spaces.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the inherent one-to-many nature of retrosynthesis and the inadequacy of single-answer benchmarks; enable diverse, plausible reaction predictions through a uncertainty-aware training/inference paradigm.

Method: Propose Top-K prompting as training/inference paradigm; assemble CREED-CCV-2+USPTO-XL (~45.6M verified reactions) to train C3LM; fine-tune with ChemCensor-based constraints and novelty-oriented rewards; evaluate on OOD URSA-expert-2026 benchmark; analyze reaction uniqueness to motivate ensembles.

Result: Achieves state-of-the-art performance on the URSA-expert-2026 benchmark; reveals complementary exploration of reaction space between LLMs and conventional models; supports Top-K plausibility-aware training as a practical direction for robust LLM-based synthesis planning.

Conclusion: Top-K, plausibility-aware training offers a practical and effective approach for capturing diverse, plausible retrosynthetic routes and can inform ensemble synthesis planning; further work could explore integration strategies and broader benchmarks.

Abstract: Single-step retrosynthesis is a central component of computer-aided synthesis planning, yet its intrinsically one-to-many nature is poorly captured by single-answer evaluation and benchmarking protocols. To address this, we introduce Top-K prompting as a robust training and inference paradigm to better capture diverse, plausible reaction predictions. We compile CREED-CCV-2+USPTO-XL, an ultra-large-scale dataset of ~45.6 million verified reactions to train the C3LM (Chemistry Constraint-Consistent Language Model). By integrating fine-tuning with ChemCensor-based and novelty-oriented rewards, our model achieves state-of-the-art performance on the OOD URSA-expert-2026 benchmark. Further analysis of reaction uniqueness shows that LLMs and conventional models explore complementary reaction spaces, motivating ensemble-based retrosynthesis systems. Overall, our results establish Top-K, plausibility-aware training as a practical new direction for robust future LLM-based synthesis planning.

</details>


### [186] [Fuzzy Accuracy Compensates for Label Subjectivity in Classification of Skin Tone Using Wearable Photoplethysmography Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18969)
*Padmini Krishnadas,Urs Hackstein,Alen Bosnjakovic,Philip J. Aston*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Classification of Fitzpatrick six-class skin tone from PPG signals with a fuzzy accuracy metric that tolerates +/-1 class error; multiple ML approaches show high fuzzy accuracy, suggesting PPG carries detectable skin-tone information despite noisy labels.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Subjective skin-tone labeling (Fitzpatrick scale) is prone to small-scale inaccuracies. A 'fuzzy accuracy' metric is proposed to reflect observer tolerance and provide a more meaningful performance measure. The study assesses whether PPG signals contain skin-tone information.

Method: Three approaches: (1) deep learning or tree-based models on raw PPG signals (with a fuzzy cross-entropy loss in the first method); (2) deep learning on SPAR image representations of PPG; (3) ML on Hand-crafted PPG features. The fuzzy accuracy is defined as correct if the predicted class is within one of the labeled class.

Result: On raw PPG: up to 55% accuracy and up to 96% fuzzy accuracy. SPAR images: 44% accuracy and 85% fuzzy accuracy. Features-based ML: 42% accuracy and 87% fuzzy accuracy.

Conclusion: Skin tone can be predicted from PPG signals with high fuzzy accuracy, indicating PPG is influenced by skin tone in a measurable way. The fuzzy metric effectively mitigates label noise and supports the feasibility of using PPG for skin-tone assessment, though label noisiness and method-specific performance differences remain.

Abstract: We consider the problem of classification of skin tone using photoplethysmography (PPG) signals with labels of the ordinal six-class Fitzpatrick skin tones. A typical accuracy for this task is a poor 40-55 %. However, the labels are subjectively determined by comparing the skin with a colour chart, and hence contain widespread small-scale inaccuracies. By working with a "fuzzy accuracy", which deems a prediction of skin tone class to be correct if its difference from the labelled class is not greater than one, much higher accuracy is obtained which provides more convincing evidence that skin tone can be accurately predicted from PPG signals. Three machine learning approaches were used, namely deep learning or tree-based approaches on raw PPG signals, deep learning on image representations of the signals generated by the Symmetric Projection Attractor Reconstruction (SPAR) method, and machine learning on features extracted from the signals. The first method also employed a fuzzy version of the cross entropy loss function, which gave the best results. Tree-based models on raw signals give accuracies up to 55 % and higher fuzzy accuracies up to 96 %, while deep learning models on the SPAR images obtained lower results of 44 % accuracy and 85 % fuzzy accuracy. The machine learning on PPG features gave similar results to the SPAR method with accuracy of 42 % and fuzzy accuracy of 87 %. We have shown that classification of skin tone using PPG signals is possible with high fuzzy accuracy which implies that our modelling approach enables accurate prediction of skin tone class within at most one class of the observer's choice of class, from which we conclude that PPG signals are affected by skin tone in a discernible way.

</details>


### [187] [Monroe: A Molecular Foundation Model for In-Context Probabilistic Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18982)
*Blazej Banaszewski,Andrew W. Fitzgibbon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Monroe is a large-scale molecular foundation model that improves data-limited bioassay activity prediction and demonstrates effective PFN-based downstream adaptation, achieving state-of-the-art on activity cliffs and Polaris benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-limited bioassay prediction; need generalizable molecular representations and scalable pre-training; leverage PFN-based downstream predictors to improve transfer and discovery utility.

Method: Pre-train Monroe on 81M PM6 molecules; enhance stereochemistry graph representation; apply conformer denoising and embedding decorrelation losses; multi-task learning; use TabPFN as a prior-data-fitted downstream predictor; evaluate with principled pairwise comparisons on Polaris and activity cliff benchmarks; perform ablations and transfer to MiniMol/CheMeleon.

Result: Monroe matches or surpasses existing MFMs on Polaris; shows significant improvements on activity cliff benchmarks; PFN-based downstream predictors improve MiniMol and CheMeleon to create MiniMol_PFN and CheMeleon_PFN; code released at GitHub.

Conclusion: PFN-based downstream adaptation generalizes beyond Monroe, enabling new state-of-the-art variants and suggesting broad utility for molecular foundation models in data-limited discovery tasks.

Abstract: Bioassay activity prediction is often data-limited because drug-discovery datasets rely on time-consuming and expensive wet-lab experiments for data generation and evaluation. This challenge has inspired recent research into molecular foundation models (MFMs), which aim to encode general-purpose chemical knowledge into molecular representations that generalize well in data-constrained scenarios. This paper presents Monroe, a new MFM with several innovations over the existing state of the art: increased scale allowing pre-training on over 81 million molecules from the PM6 quantum chemistry dataset; improved graph representation of stereochemistry; improved training losses including conformer denoising and embedding decorrelation; improved multi-task learning; and the use of a prior-data-fitted model (TabPFN) for downstream in-context prediction. Our evaluations use a principled pairwise comparison framework that measures statistically significant performance differences. Across established Polaris benchmarks, Monroe matches or exceeds existing MFMs, while on activity cliff benchmarks, designed to assess utility for molecular discovery, it achieves significant improvements over prior methods. Finally, ablation and transfer experiments show that PFN-based downstream predictors also substantially improve two leading existing models, MiniMol and CheMeleon, yielding new state-of-the-art variants we call MiniMol_PFN and CheMeleon_PFN, suggesting that our downstream adaptation strategy generalizes beyond Monroe. Source code is at github.com/blazejba/monroe.

</details>


### [188] [Harness Continual Learning: Continual Adaptation Beyond Model Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19013)
*Borui Kang,Jinrui Gu,Junhan Lv,Wenbin Li,Lei Wang,Yang Gao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Harness Continual Learning (HCL) updates an agent's external harness (outside a frozen foundation model) rather than the model itself, to accumulate capabilities while controlling forgetting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model-centric continual learning ignores the fact that prompts, memories, tools, and routing policies jointly shape behavior; updating these harness components can cause forgetting of earlier capabilities. HCL defines harness-level forgetting and seeks to evolve the harness around a fixed model.

Method: Introduce four execution-facing components around a frozen foundation model: Task Interface, Experience Memory, Capability Map, Adaptive Router. Employ guarded harness evolution with a Continual Optimizer that proposes candidate harnesses post-execution, and a Continual Evaluator that commits a candidate harness only after validating improvement, retention, and validity. Evaluate via ablations and retention sweeps across textual reasoning, multimodal perception, and open-world interaction.

Result: Demonstrates capability accumulation and failure recovery; gains surpass 10% over baselines in multiple settings. Ablations quantify each component's contribution. Retention analyses reveal harness-level forgetting; the stability–plasticity trade-off is tunable.

Conclusion: HCL is a viable new continual learning paradigm where updating a harness around a frozen model yields measurable improvements while permitting explicit control over forgetting; guarded evolution enables safe harness updates and principled evaluation.

Abstract: Continual learning has largely been model-centric, treating model parameters as the state that changes with sequential experience. Modern agents can also adapt through a harness of prompts, memories, tools, skills, and routing rules. Because these contents jointly shape later execution, a harness update can disrupt previously reliable behavior even when the model is frozen. This raises a new question: how can an agent continually improve its state outside the model while retaining behavior acquired earlier? We formulate Harness Continual Learning (HCL), a new continual learning paradigm in which the harness evolves around a frozen foundation model, and define the resulting loss of earlier behavior as harness-level forgetting. We instantiate HCL with four execution-facing components: the Task Interface, Experience Memory, Capability Map, and Adaptive Router. We further introduce guarded harness evolution to separate update generation from state commitment. A Continual Optimizer proposes candidate harnesses from post-execution feedback, and a Continual Evaluator commits the resulting candidate harness only after checking current improvement, historical retention, and validity. Experiments on textual reasoning, multimodal perception, and open-world interaction demonstrate capability accumulation and failure recovery, with relative gains exceeding 10% over corresponding baselines in multiple settings. Component ablations assess the contribution of each harness component, while controlled retention sweeps reveal measurable harness-level forgetting and show that the stability--plasticity trade-off can be explicitly adjusted.

</details>


### [189] [Multi-Agent Off-Policy Deep Reinforcement Learning for Smart Campus Coverage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19049)
*Omar Rady,Mohamed Ayman,Ali Arafa,Mohamed Shalma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DRL-based framework for optimal millimeter-wave base station placement in a non-convex campus; multi-agent DDPG yields best performance, achieving full coverage and a Jain’s index of 0.94, with efficient convergence in dense scenarios (up to 400 users).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BS placement under max-min fairness in a realistic, non-convex campus is NP-hard due to non-convex, non-smooth objectives; DRL approaches can handle complex environment and real-time adaptation to achieve fair, full coverage.

Method: Formulate as a Markov decision process and benchmark four DRL schemes: (i) discrete single-agent DQN; (ii) spatially partitioned multi-agent DQN; (iii) continuous single-agent DDPG; (iv) geographically partitioned multi-agent DDPG; evaluate on realistic campus topology with dense user scenarios.

Result: Multi-agent DDPG substantially outperforms single-agent methods in dense scenarios, achieving full coverage and Jain’s fairness index of 0.94; demonstrates efficient convergence with 400 users.

Conclusion: Geographically partitioned multi-agent DDPG is an effective and scalable approach for NP-hard mmWave BS placement under fairness objectives in realistic campus networks.

Abstract: Deep reinforcement learning (DRL) has recently gained a great attention due to its real-time adaptation and effectiveness in complex optimization problems. This paper investigates the optimal deployment of millimeter-wave (mmWave) base stations (BSs) in a realistic, non-convex campus topology. The optimization problem is NP-hard, due to the non-convex, non-smooth nature of the max-min fairness objective. To overcome these constraints, we formulate the BS placement as a Markov Decision Process (MDP) and systematically benchmark four DRL schemes: a discrete single-agent Deep Q-Network (DQN), a spatially partitioned Multi-Agent DQN, a continuous single-agent Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DDPG), and a geographically partitioned multi-agent DDPG framework. Numerical evaluations reveal that the multi-agent DDPG approach substantially outperforms single-agent in dense scenarios. Additionally full coverage is achieved, and a fairness Jain's index of 0.94 is obtained. Finally, the multi-agent demonstrates highly efficient computational convergence of dense scenarios with $400$ users.

</details>


### [190] [Does Mapping Non-Maximal Probabilities to GMM Components Matter for S-JEPA Encoder Representations?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19084)
*Wenxuan He,Yunpeng Li,Shan Liang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Soft target probabilities alone are not sufficient; the mapping of non-top GMM probabilities to components matters for learned Encoder representations. REAL SOFT outperforms two controls (FIXED-RANDPERM, UNIFORM-TAIL) across three seeds, improving recovery of the original GMM tail and short-time spectral dynamics. Exposure experiments show gains as more frames retain the mapping; Phase 2 trajectory observed after online GMM switch. Overall, both probability values and component assignment influence learning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether the numerical values of soft GMM targets or the assignment of non-maximal probabilities to specific components drive the learned representations in an S-JEPA framework; evaluate two control strategies to isolate probability distribution effects from component-mapping effects.

Method: Compare REAL SOFT against two matched controls (FIXED-RANDPERM and UNIFORM-TAIL) across three seeds using two frozen Encoder readouts. Conduct two exposure experiments and descriptively follow one Phase 2 trajectory after switching to online GMM. Measure recovery of the GMM tail and accessibility of spectral dynamics under controlled current-frame distributions.

Result: REAL SOFT outperforms both controls on two frozen Encoder readouts, with better recovery of the original GMM tail and greater accessibility of spectral dynamics over short time scales. Exposure experiments show overall improvements as more frames retain the original mapping. A Phase 2 trajectory was descriptively followed after the switch to online GMM. Conclusion: the numerical probability structure alone does not fully determine the learned Encoder representation; the mapping of non-maximal probabilities to GMM components also matters.

Conclusion: The study demonstrates that both the soft-probability values and their mapping to GMM components influence representation learning; the component assignment of non-top probabilities has a non-trivial effect beyond the raw probabilities.

Abstract: S-JEPA uses soft Gaussian mixture model (GMM) posteriors instead of hard cluster labels to preserve uncertainty. It remains unclear whether the probability values alone are sufficient, or whether it also matters which GMM components receive the non-maximal probabilities. We test this with two matched controls. FIXED-RANDPERM keeps the top-1 component and probability together with the multiset of non-maximal probability values, but reassigns those non-maximal values using a mapping fixed for each physical frame. UNIFORM-TAIL keeps the top-1 component, its probability, and total non-maximal mass but distributes that mass uniformly. Across three independent seeds, REAL SOFT outperforms both controls on two frozen Encoder readouts. It provides better recovery of the original GMM tail and greater accessibility of spectral dynamics over short time scales after controlling for the complete spectrum of the current frame. In two exposure experiments, both readouts improved overall as more frames retained the original mapping. We also descriptively follow one Phase 2 trajectory after the switch to the online GMM. These results show that the numerical probability structure of the soft target does not fully determine the learned Encoder representation. The mapping of non-maximal probabilities to GMM components also matters.

</details>


### [191] [Open-MOPD: Diagnosing and Fixing Capability Imbalance in Multi-Teacher On-Policy Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19098)
*Huan-ang Gao,Haohan Chi,Yong Yan,Shiyuan Feng,Hanlin Wu,Zheng Jiang,Bingxiang He,Wei-Ying Ma,Ya-Qin Zhang,Hao Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Open-MOPD significantly improves cross-domain knowledge transfer in multi-teacher on-policy distillation by addressing token-budget misallocation; it bridges the integration gap from 35.6% to 83.4% headroom on a SmolLM3-3B-Base benchmark under oracle routing, and is released with open-source training and evaluation tools.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand why multi-teacher on-policy distillation (M-OPD) struggles to integrate capabilities across domains and to provide a reproducible, scalable recipe for robust generalist agents.

Method: 1) Create a controlled benchmark by using SmolLM3-3B-Base with oracle routing to isolate capability integration from routing ambiguity. 2) Diagnose the integration gap and identify three orthogonal factors causing token-level optimization budget misallocation: structural sequence-length disparities, dynamic convergence drift from non-uniform learning rates, and multi-step reward staleness due to asynchronous policy updates. 3) Propose Open-MOPD with three components: token-share balancing, gap-aware dynamic budget allocation, and student reward refresh. 4) Validate by showing headroom recovery from 35.6% to 83.4% in a deployable student; release end-to-end recipe and evaluation suite.

Result: Standard M-OPD captures only 35.6% of the available headroom relative to an oracle ensemble; with Open-MOPD, headroom rises to 83.4% on the same benchmark, indicating substantial improvement in cross-domain capability integration under the controlled setting.

Conclusion: Token-budget misallocation, driven by structure-induced sequence-length differences, non-uniform learning-rate-induced drift, and reward staleness, is the primary bottleneck in M-OPD. The proposed Open-MOPD framework effectively mitigates these issues, enabling substantially better domain integration and providing a reproducible, open-source path for generalist RL systems.

Abstract: Multi-teacher on-policy distillation (M-OPD) has emerged as a promising paradigm for consolidating domain-specialized reinforcement learning (RL) experts into a single generalist student via dense, token-level reward supervision. Despite its practical success, the optimization dynamics governing multi-teacher capability integration remain poorly understood, and open, rigorously reproducible recipes are conspicuously lacking. In this work, we establish a controlled M-OPD benchmark on SmolLM3-3B-Base with oracle routing, isolating capability integration from routing ambiguity. Our investigation reveals a pronounced capability integration gap: standard M-OPD captures only 35.6% of the available headroom relative to a domain-routed oracle ensemble, with concise tasks such as instruction following suffering severe degradation and premature stagnation. Crucially, we show that this failure stems not from gradient conflict, but from a severe misallocation of the token-level optimization budget. This pathology is driven by three orthogonal factors: structural sequence-length disparities across domains, dynamic convergence drift due to non-uniform learning rates, and multi-step reward staleness from asynchronous policy updates. To resolve these imbalances, we introduce Open-MOPD, a principled framework incorporating token-share balancing, gap-aware dynamic budget allocation, and student reward refresh. Together, these mechanisms systematically restore cross-domain balance, elevating headroom recovery from 35.6% to 83.4% in a single deployable student. We fully open-source our end-to-end post-training recipe, training trajectories, and evaluation suites on an academically accessible hardware budget.

</details>


### [192] [Pretraining Reusable Inference Across Views with Synthetic Task Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19115)
*Jielong Lu,Zhihao Wu,Jiajun Yu,Zhaoliang Chen,Haishuai Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes SIMPLE: a reusable, task-conditioned multi-view inference framework trained with a synthetic task prior; demonstrates transfer of multi-view reasoning across tasks with competitive results in frozen mode and leading results with adapters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixation on task-specific fusion functions in multi-view learning discards cross-task knowledge about view relevance, complementarity, reliability, and missingness. A reusable, task-conditioned inference procedure should transfer across tasks.

Method: SIMPLE: a prior-fitted multi-view in-context learner that conditions on a small labeled support set. Construct a controllable synthetic task prior in embedding space to generate diverse support-query episodes with varying class structures, shared/view-specific factors, geometries, cross-view dependencies, reliability, missingness, and distribution shifts. A hierarchical inference architecture performs reasoning within views, across views, and across support/query samples. Evaluates in frozen and adapter-calibrated variants.

Result: On multi-view and multi-omics benchmarks, the frozen SIMPLE achieves competitive performance without updating the inference backbone; lightweight adapter calibration yields leading performance on most datasets. The findings hold across frozen, one-shot, and missing-view settings.

Conclusion: The results support the central hypothesis that multi-view reasoning can be pretrained and reused, with lightweight adapters providing task-specific alignment when needed across diverse view configurations and domains.

Abstract: Modern pretrained encoders make representations from heterogeneous views increasingly reusable, but the procedure that determines view utility and combines evidence is still relearned for each downstream task. Consequently, knowledge about view relevance, complementarity, reliability, and missingness is repeatedly discarded rather than transferred across tasks. We therefore reformulate multi-view learning as learning a reusable, task-conditioned inference procedure rather than a fixed fusion function. Based on this perspective, we propose SIMPLE, a prior-fitted multi-view in-context learner that predicts query labels by conditioning on a small labeled support set. Since existing real-world datasets cover only a limited range of view configurations and task structures, we construct a controllable synthetic task prior in embedding space. It generates diverse support-query episodes with varying class structures, shared and view-specific factors, representation geometries, cross-view dependencies, reliability levels, missingness patterns, and distribution shifts. A hierarchical inference architecture then performs reasoning within views, across views, and across support and query samples. Experiments on multi-view and multi-omics benchmarks demonstrate that the frozen variant of SIMPLE achieves competitive performance without updating the inference backbone, while lightweight adapter calibration attains leading performance on most evaluated datasets. Together, the results under frozen, one-shot, and missing-view settings support the central hypothesis that multi-view reasoning itself can be pretrained and reused, while lightweight adapter calibration provides task-specific alignment when needed.

</details>


### [193] [Enhancing EBSD throughput of battery electrode materials using super-resolution generative adversarial networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19117)
*John Mangum,Andrew Glaws,Francois Usseglio-Viretta,Steven Spurgeon,Donal Finegan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A GAN-based super-resolution framework (SRGAN) increases EBSD throughput for Li-ion battery electrode materials, outperforming interpolation across 2x–12x upscaling and enabling 5x scaling with ~25x speed-up while maintaining acceptable microstructural metric accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: EBSD is inherently slow, limiting statistical representation of microstructure; high-throughput EBSD is desirable for Li-ion battery electrode materials (e.g., NMC cathodes) to support materials research and process development.

Method: Train a SRGAN on EBSD data of LiNixMnyCozO2 (NMC) cathode particles. Compare SRGAN upscaling to classical interpolation across upscaling factors 2x–12x. Evaluate via qualitative image metrics and quantitative microstructural metrics: grain area-equivalent diameter, grain maximum sphere-inscribed diameter, grain boundary length.

Result: SRGAN systematically outperforms interpolation, particularly preserving small grains and realistic grain boundaries. A 5x upscaling factor yields a 25x speed-up with acceptable accuracy: relative errors of +5.7% (grain area-equivalent diameter), +8.2% (maximum sphere-inscribed diameter), and -14.6% (grain boundary length).

Conclusion: The SRGAN framework significantly enhances EBSD throughput, enabling high-throughput, statistically robust microstructural characterization of battery electrodes and broader industrial applicability.

Abstract: Quantitative microstructural characterization of Li-ion battery electrode materials using electron backscatter diffraction (EBSD) has been proven as a critical method for optimizing cell performance. However, the inherently slow nature of EBSD can hinder the throughput of analyses needed for statistical representation of a material microstructure being developed. This work demonstrates a machine learning super-resolution framework using a generative adversarial network (SRGAN) to significantly increase EBSD throughput. The SRGAN model was trained on EBSD data of LiNixMnyCozO2 (NMC) cathode particles to computationally enhance low-resolution datasets and its performance is compared against classical interpolation methods across various upscaling factors (2x to 12x). Both qualitative image metrics and quantitative microstructural analysis verified that the SRGAN systematically outperformed classical methods, particularly in preserving small grains and maintaining realistic grain boundaries. We demonstrate that a 5x upscaling factor, corresponding to a 25x speed-up in acquisition time or a 25x larger field of view, is practical while maintaining acceptable accuracy in key metrics like grain size and shape. For instance, at 5x upscaling, relative errors were +5.7%, +8.2%, and -14.6% on grain area-equivalent diameter, grain maximum sphere-inscribed diameter, and grain boundary length, respectively. The SRGAN methodology developed in this work significantly enhances the efficiency of EBSD acquisition for more statistically robust microstructural dataset, enabling EBSD as a high-throughput characterization tool for materials research and industrial process development.

</details>


### [194] [Discretizing Continuous Time Series for Imputation with Masked Diffusion Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19119)
*Dongbin Kim,Seungyun Lee,Geonwoo Shin,Jaewook Lee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Masked Diffusion Time-series Imputation Model (MDTIM) uses a masked diffusion framework with a separate MASK token and a stochastic discretization to align representations and objectives with imputation, improving robustness and scalability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Imputation in time series is hampered by entangled missing/observed signals and diffusion models that predict noise rather than the original signal; there is a need for explicit structural separation and an ordinal-aware learning approach.

Method: Adopt a masked diffusion training paradigm where the MASK token is orthogonal to valid observations and the model directly predicts original values; introduce Stochastic Discretization to map continuous values to ordinal tokens while preserving continuous dynamics.

Result: Experiments on diverse benchmarks show MDTIM achieves superior robustness and scalability, outperforming state-of-the-art deterministic and generative baselines across various missing scenarios.

Conclusion: MDTIM provides a natural alignment of representation and learning objective for imputation; the combination of masked diffusion and stochastic discretization enables robust, scalable time-series imputation and offers a bridge between discrete diffusion processes and continuous-time data.

Abstract: Time series imputation is a crucial area for reliable time series analysis, yet it remains challenging due to the complex temporal dynamics and noise of real-world data. Existing approaches, however, exhibit two limitations: missing and observed values are embedded within the same representation space without explicit structural separation, and continuous diffusion-based methods are trained to predict added noise rather than the original signal. To address these, we propose the Masked Diffusion Time-series Imputation Model (MDTIM), which leverages the training paradigm of masked diffusion model for imputation tasks. The MASK token is structurally orthogonal to valid observations, and the model directly predicts the original values, naturally aligning both the representation and the learning objective with the imputation task. To bridge the gap between discrete masked diffusion and the continuous, ordinal nature of time series, we further introduce Stochastic Discretization, which maps continuous values to ordinal-aware tokens while preserving continuous dynamics. Our experiments on diverse benchmarks confirm that MDTIM achieves superior robustness and scalability, consistently outperforming state-of-the-art deterministic and generative baselines across various missing scenarios.

</details>


### [195] [PGFS++: Molecular Property Improvement under Synthesis and Diversity Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19121)
*Boqiao Zhang,Godbless James,Sai Krishna Gottipati,Andrew Fitzgibbon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PGFS+ and PGFS++ advance synthesis-aware reinforcement learning for molecular optimization. PGFS+ replaces indirect reactant embedding with trainable templates/embeddings and a stronger scoring/RL setup to improve properties. PGFS++ makes optimization input-specific by treating the input molecule as the starting point of a forward synthesis using in-stock building blocks, yielding improved molecules with explicit synthesis routes and preserved diversity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Molecule optimization in unconstrained chemical space often yields non-synthesizable results. Prior PGFS used reactant embedding prediction, which limited learning. There is also a reward-hacking risk when a powerful search maps inputs to a single high-reward product, collapsing diversity.

Method: PGFS+ uses trainable embedding lookup tables for reaction templates and second reactants, plus a refined scoring function and RL algorithm to enhance property optimization. PGFS++ casts the input as the starting point of a forward-synthesis trajectory, applies learned templates with compatible in-stock blocks, and outputs a molecule with better properties, an explicit synthesis route, and similarity to the input.

Result: PGFS+ significantly improves targeted properties over the baseline. PGFS++ achieves property improvements while maintaining high output diversity and providing explicit synthetic routes.

Conclusion: Synthesis-aware, input-specific RL frameworks (PGFS++, and to a lesser extent PGFS+) can produce practically valuable molecular designs by balancing property improvement with synthetic feasibility and diversity, addressing both learning effectiveness and reward hacking concerns.

Abstract: Improving molecular properties, such as drug-likeness or binding affinity, is a recurring task in early-stage drug discovery. However, molecules optimized in an unconstrained chemical space have limited practical value if they cannot be synthesized. Policy Gradient for Forward Synthesis (PGFS) is a synthesis-aware reinforcement learning method for molecular improvement, but its use of reactant embedding prediction makes reactant selection indirect, which, as we show, limits learning effectiveness. We first develop PGFS+, in which reaction templates and second reactants are represented by trainable embedding lookup tables. Combined with a more effective scoring function and RL algorithm, PGFS+ significantly improves the desired property. However, it exposes a reward-hacking failure mode: a powerful reactant search can map diverse input molecules to the same high-reward magnet molecule, improving the reward while collapsing the output diversity. We therefore introduce PGFS++, a synthesis-aware reinforcement learning framework for input-specific molecular improvement. Given an input molecule, PGFS++ treats it as the start of a forward-synthesis trajectory, applies learned reaction templates with compatible in-stock building blocks, and produces a molecule with improved target properties, an explicit synthesis route, and structural similarity to the input. Experiments on molecular improvement tasks show that PGFS++ improves target properties while preserving high output diversity.

</details>


### [196] [Leaf Values as Coordinates: Exact Contrastive Explanation for Gradient-Boosted Ensembles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19127)
*Emanuele Luzio*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Interprets gradient-boosted trees in leaf-value coordinates, enabling exact contrastive explanations and actionable recourse; shows auditability and favorable predictive-utility trade-offs on tabular data.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To achieve exact, verifiable explanations and practical recourse for tree ensembles without altering the model or features, by reinterpreting the model in a space where additivity is explicit.

Method: Represent the ensemble as a vector of leaf-values (one per leaf per tree). Exploit linearity in this leaf-space to produce exact contrastive explanations; construct a recourse method that traces recommendations to real splits; evaluate via repeated cross-validation on five tabular datasets, including credit data; compare Pareto-efficiency of recourse vs realism; test restricted, actionable changes.

Result: The recourse reconstruction error is 6.2e-15, enabling auditors to reproduce decisions; on credit datasets, the method is Pareto-non-dominated for effort vs realism; when restricting to actionable changes, retains 58% validity vs 41% baseline, highlighting evaluation sensitivity.

Conclusion: Viewing gradient-boosted ensembles in leaf-coordinate space yields exact, auditable explanations and practical, actionable recourse, with favorable evaluation outcomes and a stronger separation between feasible and infeasible recommendations than standard metrics.

Abstract: A gradient-boosted ensemble predicts by summing one leaf value per tree. Read
  those values as coordinates rather than as intermediate results, and every
  instance becomes a point in R^M on which the model acts linearly: the score is
  the sum of the coordinates.
  This small change of view makes contrastive explanation exact. The difference
  between two instances is a vector that is identically zero wherever they share
  a leaf, so the gap between a rejected applicant and an accepted one is carried
  by a handful of coordinates, each traceable to a real split in a real tree.
  Nothing is fitted, sampled, or assumed additive in features -- the additivity
  is already there, in the right space.
  We build a recourse method on this representation and evaluate it on five
  tabular datasets under repeated cross-validation. Its recommendation
  reconstructs the model's own decision to 6.2 x 10^-15, so an auditor can
  re-check the arithmetic without the model. On the credit datasets it is
  Pareto-non-dominated on effort against realism. And when recommendations are
  restricted to changes the subject could actually make -- not their age, not a
  settled delinquency -- it retains 58% of its validity where the strongest
  baseline retains 41%, a distinction the standard evaluation cannot see because
  it never asks whether a recommendation can be carried out.

</details>


### [197] [Beyond Trial Averaging: Anchoring Neural and Visual Representations for Few-Repetition Brain-to-Image Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19128)
*Zhenyao Cui,Siyuan Kan,Dingkun Liu,Dongrui Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Neural-anchor-based retrieval (NEAR) improves few-repetition brain-to-image retrieval by anchoring both neural queries and image representations to a high-repetition center, using a denoiser and a pseudo-anchor predictor to reduce data needs and improve accuracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Few repeats of neural stimuli yield noisy queries and unstable image galleries; existing averaging over many trials boosts performance but is impractical for real-time or user-friendly deployment. A method that maintains accuracy with limited data is needed.

Method: NEAR treats the high-repetition center as an anchor. A denoiser pulls the noisy query toward the anchor, while a small network predicts a candidate's pseudo-anchor from its image and pulls the image toward it. This dual both-sides anchoring addresses both query noise and gallery placement. Evaluated across EEG, MEG, and fMRI datasets (including THINGS-EEG2), focusing on 1–4 repetitions.

Result: NEAR consistently improves retrieval in the few-repetition regime across four datasets. On THINGS-EEG2, it yields 5.7 and 9.3 percentage-point gains in 200-way Top-1 accuracy for averaging one and four repetitions, respectively, demonstrating reduced reliance on repeated acquisitions.

Conclusion: Anchoring neural and visual representations enables robust brain-to-image retrieval with limited repetitions, moving neural decoding closer to real-world deployment.

Abstract: Decoding visual information from brain signals probes neural representations and enables neuro-rehabilitation and dream decoding. Recent brain-to-image retrieval approaches have achieved promising performance, typically by averaging many (up to 80) neural trials per image, requiring repeated stimulus presentation that increases latency, cost, and user burden. When only one or a few repetitions are available, the retrieval accuracy drops sharply. This drop is commonly attributed to query noise because averaging suppresses noise and increases signal stability. However, we find a non-transitive alignment pattern: the low-repetition query signal and the image representation each align with the high-repetition center, but not directly with each other. This pattern shows that query noise is only part of the problem and that gallery placement also affects retrieval. We therefore propose a neural-anchor-based retrieval (NEAR) framework that treats the high-repetition center as an anchor and approaches it from both sides: a denoiser pulls the noisy query toward the true anchor, and a small network predicts each candidate's pseudo anchor from its image and pulls the image toward it. Across four datasets spanning EEG, MEG and fMRI, NEAR consistently improved retrieval in the few-repetition regime. On THINGS-EEG2, it improved 200-way Top-1 accuracy by 5.7 and 9.3 percentage points respectively, when averaging one and four repetitions. By anchoring neural and visual representations, NEAR reduces reliance on repeated acquisition and brings neural retrieval closer to real-world deployment.

</details>


### [198] [SCORE: Subject Coordinate Recovery for Label-Free Cross-Subject EEG-to-Image Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19134)
*Zhenyao Cui,Siyuan Kan,Siyang Li,Ziwei Wang,Dongrui Wu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SCORE is a label-free cross-subject EEG-to-image decoding framework that aligns source EEG to a common image space and recovers unseen subject coordinates via an orthogonal transform, enabling robust zero-shot transfer without target labels; it achieves the top results on THINGS-EEG2 and Alljoined-1.6M.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cross-subject EEG-to-image retrieval lags behind within-subject decoding due to subject-specific coordinate representations; calibration-free deployment is desirable.

Method: Train source-aligned mapping to a common image space and simulate unseen-subject recovery with source-only episodes. At deployment, freeze encoders, use hubness-corrected landmark matching to select reliable EEG-image pairs, and estimate an orthogonal transformation to map target EEG coordinates without using source data or target labels.

Result: On 200-way retrieval tasks, SCORE outperforms the unadapted baseline for every target subject and achieves best overall accuracy. Top-1/Top-5: 53.23%/83.55% on THINGS-EEG2 and 12.01%/32.16% on Alljoined-1.6M, with margins of +17.45/+15.70 and +3.08/+4.62 over strongest baselines.

Conclusion: SCORE enables label-free cross-subject brain decoding with no target labels or encoder updates, bringing EEG-to-image retrieval closer to practical, low-latency deployment across users.

Abstract: Accurate visual decoding can reveal how the brain represents visual information and recover perceived content from neural signals such as electroencephalography (EEG), with potential for neural communication. However, current EEG-to-image retrieval methods perform far below their within-subject counterparts for new users without labeled calibration, limiting real-world deployment. To understand this gap, we analyze EEG features across subjects and find that different subjects preserve similar relationships among concepts but express them along different coordinate directions. We therefore propose Subject Coordinate Recovery (SCORE), a target label-free framework combining recovery-aware source training with coordinate alignment at deployment. During training, SCORE aligns source subject EEG with a common image space and simulates unseen-subject recovery through source-only episodes. At deployment, with both encoders frozen, SCORE selects reliable EEG-image landmarks through hubness-corrected matching and estimates an orthogonal transformation to recover target EEG coordinates without source data or target labels. In 200-way retrieval on two public benchmarks, SCORE outperforms the unadapted baseline for every target subject and achieves the best overall accuracy. It reaches 53.23%/83.55% and 12.01%/32.16% Top-1/Top-5 on THINGS-EEG2 and Alljoined-1.6M, respectively, surpassing the strongest baselines by 17.45/15.70 and 3.08/4.62 percentage points. Without target labels or encoder updates, SCORE brings brain-based visual decoding closer to robust, practical, low-latency deployment across users.

</details>


### [199] [Bridge Graphical Models: Coupling, Projection, and Current-Preserving Dynamics for Generative Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19144)
*Tiantian Zhang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Defines the Markovization gap, a pre-training diagnostic capturing the irreducible non-Markovian information bottleneck in endpoint-conditioned, continuous-time generative models. Proposes Bridge Graphical Models (BGMs) to separate design choices and shows a pre-training feature-gap proxy correlates with downstream performance across several model families, suggesting the gap as a design guide before training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Identify a fundamental bottleneck that prevents optimal decoding from endpoint-conditioned bridges, independent of learning, and provide a universal, architecture-agnostic diagnostic to compare bridge and coupling designs across diffusion-like and field-based generative models.

Method: Formalize the Markovization gap as the time-integrated conditional variance of bridge velocity given the Markov state; interpret it as the MMSE of predicting endpoint-conditioned motion from sampler-accessible information. Introduce Bridge Graphical Models (BGMs) that decouple endpoint coupling, bridge law, Markovian projection, and current-preserving dynamics. Extend the framework to Poisson and electrostatic field-line bridge kernels with a field-line Markovization gap. Empirically evaluate on CIFAR-10 and Fashion-MNIST across synthetic, latent, and pixel-space pilots, using a feature-space proxy gap computed before training.

Result: Across experiments, the pre-training feature-gap proxy ranks design choices in the same order as downstream training loss and FID when architecture, bridge, sampler, and compute are fixed. This correlation supports the Markovization gap as a practical pre-training diagnostic for bridge and coupling design.

Conclusion: The Markovization gap captures an irreducible bottleneck in bridge-to-decoder conversion and serves as a predictive pre-training diagnostic for choosing bridge and coupling designs. The BGMs provide a modular framework to reason about different design choices, and the framework extends to field-based models, offering a unifying lens for continuous-time generative modeling.

Abstract: Continuous-time generative models are often built from endpoint-conditioned bridges, but generation requires a different object: a non-anticipative Markov decoder that only observes the current state and time. We identify this bridge-to-decoder compression as a structural bottleneck shared by diffusion models, flow matching, rectified flow, Schrödinger bridges, and field-based generative models. We introduce the \emph{Markovization gap}, the time-integrated conditional variance of the bridge velocity given the Markov state. It is the MMSE of predicting endpoint-conditioned motion from the information available to a sampler, and it measures an irreducible loss incurred before any neural network is trained. To make this bottleneck comparable across model families, we define \emph{Bridge Graphical Models} (BGMs), which separate endpoint coupling, bridge law, Markovian projection, and current-preserving dynamics representation as independent design choices.
