The same formalism also represents Poisson and electrostatic models as field-line bridge kernels with a corresponding field-line Markovization gap. Across synthetic, latent, and pixel-space pilots on CIFAR-10 and Fashion-MNIST, a feature-space proxy gap estimated in minutes before training ranks design choices in the same direction as downstream training loss and FID under fixed architecture, bridge, sampler, and compute. These results support the Markovization gap as a pre-training diagnostic for bridge and coupling design.

</details>


### [200] [Continuous-Time Reinforcement Learning for Controlled Hawkes Jump-Diffusions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19151)
*Tomasz R. Bielecki,Thibaut Mastrolia,Haoze Yan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A finite-dimensional Markovianization of multivariate Hawkes processes via mixtures of exponential kernels enables tractable stochastic control and continuous-time reinforcement learning (Hawkes-CT DDPG). The authors prove convergence of the Markovianized model and objective value to the original non-Markovian system, and develop a model-free continuous-time policy gradient algorithm that operates with observed event times and decay filters, treating kernel coefficients as unknown. They compare performance across exponential, Erlang, and power-law kernels against discretetime RL baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Non-Markovian memory in Hawkes-driven stochastic control obstructs classical stochastic control methods. By approximating Hawkes kernels with finite mixtures of exponential kernels, the process becomes Markovian, enabling stochastic control and continuous-time RL approaches. This work aims to provide a rigorous foundation (convergence) and a practical algorithm (Hawkes-CT DDPG) for non-Markovian optimization.

Method: 1) Markovianization: approximate multivariate Hawkes processes with mixtures of exponential kernels, yielding a finite-dimensional Markovian representation. 2) Theoretical: prove convergence of the Markovianized Hawkes process, its intensity, and the value of the objective to the original non-Markovian processes and the primal value. 3) Algorithm: formulate continuous-time deterministic policy gradient (CT DDPG) on the Markovianized model (Hawkes-CT DDPG). 4) Model-free learning: propose an algorithm that uses only event times, the SDE solution, and a set of decay filters; kernel coefficients remain unknown. 5) Evaluation: compare Hawkes-CT DDPG with discrete-time RL across kernels (simple exponential, Erlang, power-law).

Result: Convergence guarantees establishing that the Markovianized approximation converges to the true non-Markovian Hawkes process, its intensity, and the optimal value. A new continuous-time policy-gradient RL framework (Hawkes-CT DDPG) is developed for the Markovianized problem, coupled with a model-free learning scheme that operates with partial observations and unknown kernel coefficients. Empirical comparison demonstrates how the approach performs under different kernel families, providing evidence for the viability of continuous-time RL in Hawkes-driven control scenarios.

Conclusion: Markovianizing Hawkes processes via mixtures of exponentials effectively enables stochastic control and continuous-time RL for non-Markovian Hawkes-driven systems. The Hawkes-CT DDPG framework offers a practical model-free learning method that leverages event-time data and decay filters, with convergence guarantees and kernel-type dependent performance observations when compared to discrete-time RL baselines.

Abstract: We study stochastic control of multivariate Hawkes-driven stochastic differential equations with machine learning algorithms in a non-Markovian setting. Due to the path dependence of the memory of the Hawkes intensity, this problem does not fall within classical stochastic control theory outside particular Markovian kernels. We first develop a finite-dimensional Markovianization procedure and algorithm to approximate multivariate Hawkes processes with mixtures of exponential kernels. We prove the convergence of the Markovianized approximation of the Hawkes process, its intensity, and the value of the problem to the original non-Markovian processes and the value of the primal problem. We then formulate continuous-time deterministic policy gradient learning on the Markovianized approximation of the problem, called Hawkes-CT DDPG. We propose a model-free algorithm to solve the non-Markovian Hawkes-driven optimization by observing only the event times of the process, the realization of the solution to the SDE, and a chosen set of decay filters, while the Hawkes kernel coefficients remain unknown. We compare our continuous time reinforcement learning Hawkes-CT DDPG method with discrete time reinforcement learning techniques under three different types of kernels: simple exponential, Erlang, and power-law kernels.

</details>


### [201] [Learned, Then Lost: A Measured Single-Example Counterfactual in Pre-training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19168)
*Zachary Speck,Asa Shepard*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A single training example can be injected into a GPT-2 model via a fixed 194-token passage, producing short-lived improvements in predicting that passage, but the effect decays and does not persist at the final checkpoint; the injection relocates the model within its loss basin without moving it out.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify the contribution of an individual training example to a final model by performing a counterfactual intervention and measuring how the injected information propagates, persists, or decays during training.

Method: Train 32 GPT-2 (124M) models from scratch on OpenWebText under four injection conditions and eight seeds. At early training (step 200 of 9,536) replace one row of a 256-row batch with a 194-token passage; compare three injection types (fluent prose with corpus-attested subject; fluent prose with a fabricated subject matched to the corpus and an accuracy delta; random keyboard characters) plus an uninjected twin. Measure cross-entropy on the passage, interpolation barrier, held-out cross-entropy, per-layer kernel alignment, and weight displacement across training; examine decay of the effect and the distance moved in parameter space.

Result: Across eight seeds, the injected passage reduces cross-entropy on the passage by 0.039–0.044 nats fifty steps after injection (p < 1e-4). At the final step, no detectable difference remains (p = 0.25 and 0.71 for the two injections vs baseline). Interpolation barrier shows no reliable effect (p = 0.509). Held-out cross-entropy change is negligible (p = 0.310). Kernel alignment does not detectable separation of conditions. Weight displacement reaches 44.1% of seed-to-seed distance; 92% of displacement occurs by mid-training; the barrier accounts for ~3.0% of seed-to-seed barrier. The injection relocates the model within its loss basin but does not push it outside.

Conclusion: A single example can imprint information transiently, moving the model within its loss basin and affecting short-term predictions, but the effect decays and does not establish a lasting, detectable change at convergence; estimation of single-example contribution yields a partial, short-lived signal rather than a durable imprint.

Abstract: A single training example's contribution to a finished model is normally estimated rather than measured, because measuring it takes two expensive full pre-training runs that differ in one row of one batch. We ran that counterfactual 24 times at a small scale. We trained 32 GPT-2 models at 124M parameters from scratch on OpenWebText, over four conditions and eight seeds. At step 200 of 9,536, at peak learning rate, we replaced one row of a 256-row batch with a fixed context injection carrying a 194-token passage. The three injected conditions are: 1. fluent prose with a corpus-attested subject, 2. fluent prose with a fabricated subject matched to it within 0.14% on full-batch gradient delta, and 3. random keyboard characters. The fourth condition is an uninjected twin. The passage is learned from one exposure and then decays. Fifty steps after injection, the arm that saw a passage predicts it better than the arm that did not by 0.039 and 0.044 nats of cross-entropy on the passage, at eight of eight seeds with p < $10^{-4}$. At the final step we do not detect that difference for either passage, at p = 0.25 and p = 0.71, against minimum detectable effects of 0.025 and 0.079 nats, nor between the two passages, at p=0.54. Every geometric measure we report is taken after that decay. Our pre-registered contrast on interpolation loss barrier is +0.0068 with p = 0.509, against a minimum detectable effect of 0.032 barrier units. Held-out cross-entropy is $-0.00044$ with p = 0.310. Per-layer centered kernel alignment does not detectably separate any condition at any layer. Weight displacement reaches 44.1% of the seed-to-seed Euclidean distance and is 92% settled by the midpoint of training, while the barrier reaches 3.0% of the seed-to-seed barrier. Those two figures sit roughly 15 times apart, and that is a lower bound. The injection relocates the model within its basin without moving it out.

</details>


### [202] [Lévy Attention: Single-Pass Predictive Uncertainty for Continuous-Time Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19171)
*Sotirios P. Chatzis,Loukas Papadoulas*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces Lévy Attention, a probabilistic cross-attention mechanism based on a Poisson random measure that yields an exact uncertainty estimate with no extra computation, improving trustworthiness on irregularly-sampled time series while maintaining performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses the lack of uncertainty quantification in attention over irregularly-sampled time series; preserves information (evidence) and disagreement in closed form via a Poisson construction, enabling trust estimates without extra cost.

Method: Defines cross-attention as a stochastic integral against an inhomogeneous Poisson random measure. Query-key compatibilities assemble an intensity over a continuous time×channel index space. The measure scatters atoms; the output averages an interpolated value field at those atoms. In expectation it reduces to a mollified cosine-kernel attention, enabling training with exact gradients. It yields closed-form evidence Λ_q and value spread tr Σ_V(q). The trust metric is hatσ(q)=sqrt(tr Σ_V(q) φ(Λ_q)).

Result: Empirical results show the operator leads to small performance loss (up to 5.6% accuracy drop on t-PatchGNN sparsity tests) with significant gains from the uncertainty signal; the disagreement improves on standard 20-pass MC dropout, and a one-pass method ranks 3,383 unseen patients by trust in 1.4 seconds; a split-conformal wrapper achieves nominal coverage.

Conclusion: Lévy Attention provides an uncertainty-aware cross-attention that preserves information lost by softmax, with exact gradients and scalable trust estimates, especially beneficial for irregularly-sampled or sparse data.

Abstract: Deep models for irregularly-sampled time series answer queries at arbitrary continuous timestamps, yet report nothing about how far each answer should be trusted. We show the attention layer itself can close that gap: with the right stochastic formulation, the pass that makes each prediction also reports, in closed form and at no extra cost, how far it should be trusted. We introduce Lévy Attention, a cross-attention operator whose output is a stochastic integral against an inhomogeneous Poisson random measure: query-key compatibilities assemble an intensity over a continuous (time x channel) index space, the measure scatters atoms under it, and the output averages an interpolated value field at those atoms. In expectation it reduces to a mollified cosine-kernel attention, so it replaces a softmax layer and trains with exact gradients.
  What softmax discards, the Poisson construction preserves in closed form: the evidence $Λ_q$ (total compatibility mass) and the disagreement $\mathrm{tr}\,Σ_V(q)$ (value spread). An exact variance identity makes their combination $\hatσ(q)=\sqrt{\mathrm{tr}\,Σ_V(q)\,\varphi(Λ_q)}$ the root-mean-square deviation of the sampled operator, emitted by the deterministic pass with no trained head.
  Empirically, disagreement carries the signal, while the evidence factor swings from uninformative on dense data to strongly informative on sparse. On t-PatchGNN the operator swap costs at most 5.6% accuracy against a matched control and nothing on the sparsest dataset. The free disagreement signal improves on 20-pass MC dropout across matched five-seed suites, and $\hatσ$ scales a calibrated Gaussian whose zero-sample CRPS beats a fifty-draw sampler; a split-conformal wrapper reaches nominal coverage at every level, and one pass ranks 3,383 unseen patients by trust in 1.4 seconds.

</details>


### [203] [Beyond Teacher Likelihood: Group-Calibrated On-Policy Distillation for Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19181)
*Zhu Zhang,Jixun Wang,Xiaoang Xu,Xiaorong Wang,Zihan Zhou,Zhiyuan Wang,Shuo Wang,Chaojun Xiao,Yuezhi Zhou*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GC-OPD calibrates on-policy distillation for long-context tasks by computing a signed residual from group-normalized verifier rewards and trajectory scores, then distributes this residual across tokens via RACA, improving performance while preserving dense token guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Token-level guidance from a strong teacher can miss global task constraints and evidence that is distributed across the long input; task-specific verifiers provide response-level rewards but may disagree with token-level signals. A method is needed to reconcile these signals.

Method: GC-OPD normalizes verifier rewards and trajectory-level OPD scores within each rollout group; computes a signed disagreement residual as their difference; applies RACA to allocate the residual across tokens proportionally to each token's OPD advantage while preserving the original OPD signal.

Result: On five long-context benchmarks, GC-OPD boosts official Qwen3-4B average from 29.08 to 40.47 and Qwen3-8B average from 35.12 to 44.65; vanilla OPD yields 39.31 and 43.56 under the same setup. Ablations show the signed residual outperforms an extra OPD term or group-normalized verifier reward alone, and RACA improves over uniform token allocation.

Conclusion: Group-relative residual calibration enables incorporating verifier outcomes without discarding dense token-level guidance, achieving substantial gains; code is released at the provided URL.

Abstract: On-policy distillation (OPD) trains a student on its own responses using dense token-level guidance from a stronger teacher. In long-context tasks, however, token-level teacher support can favor locally plausible responses that omit evidence distributed across the input or violate global task constraints. Task-specific verifiers, in contrast, evaluate task completion at the response level and may return graded rewards that reflect partial success. We diagnose this mismatch on fixed responses from two representative long-context evidence-aggregation tasks. Across longer input ranges, trajectory-level OPD scores become progressively less aligned with verifier rewards, indicating teacher-verifier disagreement. Motivated by this observation, we introduce Group-Calibrated On-Policy Distillation (GC-OPD). GC-OPD separately normalizes verifier rewards and trajectory-level OPD scores within each rollout group and uses their difference as a signed teacher-verifier disagreement residual. Relative-advantage-based credit assignment (RACA) distributes this trajectory-level residual across tokens according to their relative OPD advantages while preserving the original OPD signal. Across five long-context benchmarks, post-training with GC-OPD raises the five-benchmark averages of the official Qwen3-4B and Qwen3-8B checkpoints from 29.08 to 40.47 and from 35.12 to 44.65, respectively. Vanilla OPD reaches 39.31 and 43.56 under the same setup. Controlled ablations show that the signed residual is more effective than either an additional OPD-derived term or direct group-normalized verifier reward addition, while RACA further improves over uniform token allocation. Together, these results demonstrate that group-relative residual calibration can incorporate verifier outcomes without discarding dense token-level guidance. Code is available at https://github.com/SolereZhang/GC-OPD.

</details>


<div id='cs.AI'></div>

# cs.AI [[Back]](#toc)

### [204] [Position: Collusion Risks Among AI Reasoning Agents Justify Certification Requirements for Making Market Decisions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18078)
*Matthew Riemer,Tommaso Tosato,Amin Memarian,Maximilian Puelma Touzel,Glen Berseth,Irina Rish,Guillaume Dumas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AI agents with chain-of-thought reasoning may foster tacit collusion in market settings; a regulatory behavioral certification is proposed before deployment to prevent anti-competitive outcomes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prevent erosion of the legal distinction between competition and collusion in markets involving AI agents; ensure safe, stable deployment and preserve welfare by detecting and mitigating collusive tendencies.

Method: Empirical experiments with DeepSeek-R1 agents in a Bertrand oligopoly; analysis of reasoning traces; attempts to prompt agents not to collude; evaluation of manipulability of chain-of-thought to steer behavior; cross-LLM detection tests to assess semantic detectability of reasoning traces.

Result: Agents show a tendency to tacitly collude in pricing; chain-of-thought can be steered toward highly collusive or highly competitive behavior in ways not easily detectable by another LLM; collusion can arise without evidence of conspiracy or intent.

Conclusion: Recommend implementing behavioral certification based on observed agent behavior in representative situations; emphasize need for comprehensive, generalizable certification framework before real-world market deployment to ensure stability and efficiency and to guide steering toward efficient competitive equilibria.

Abstract: This position paper argues that AI agents with chain-of-thought reasoning capabilities are predisposed to exhibit collusive behavior and should be required to obtain behavioral certification before making decisions that affect economic markets. This is because integrating these agents into society could collapse the legal evidentiary distinction between competition and collusion among independent firms without eroding the economic harm distinction. Experiments with DeepSeek-R1 agents in the Bertrand oligopoly pricing domain reveal a tendency towards tacit collusion that persists even when humans prompt the agents not to collude. We further show that the chain-of-thought of these agents can be steered toward either extremely collusive or highly competitive behavior in a way that is not semantically detectable by another LLM analyzing the reasoning traces. As a result, deploying reasoning agents for market decisions leads to collusive economic outcomes without any evidence of conspiracy or intent. Thus, certification based on observed behavior in representative situations is necessary to prevent collusion. We provide preliminary evidence that such agents can be steered in a generalizable way toward efficient competitive equilibria. However, developing a comprehensive behavioral certification will be required before these models can be deployed in real-world markets while ensuring their stability and efficiency.

</details>


### [205] [Position: Profiling Game Worlds by Transition Complexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18079)
*Lele Cao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes Transition Complexity Profile (TCP) to quantify the difficulty of environment transition prediction in game world modeling (GWM) and reinforcement learning (RL). TCP uses a compact set of metrics: (i) intrinsic one-step branching, (ii) interaction-induced uncertainty and opponent influence when observable, and (iii) temporal/spatial dependency span via standardized probe curves. It includes explicit reference distribution, protocol stochasticity, and a versioned measurement budget (sampling/resampling and fixed probe compute) to enable cross-benchmark comparability. The authors advocate TCP as standard benchmark metadata and a required statistic in GWM and RL papers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge a gap where papers rarely quantify the underlying transition prediction difficulty at declared interfaces (pixels/tokens/latents with finite history). Provide a reproducible, comparable characterization of environments/datasets to improve interpretability, benchmarking, and fairness across GWM/RL work.

Method: Introduce a minimal, reproducible metric suite:
- intrinsic one-step branching: how many plausible next transitions exist from a state-action pair under the environment dynamics.
- interaction-induced uncertainty and opponent influence when observable: how much uncertainty arises due to other agents/players and interaction effects, given observability.
- temporal/spatial dependency span: reach of dependencies over time and space, captured via standardized probe curves.
Measurement protocol includes: explicit reference distribution, protocol stochasticity, and a versioned measurement budget (how sampling/resampling and fixed probe compute are allocated). Provides guidance for reporting and cross-benchmark comparability.

Result: Conceptual proposal and measurement framework for TCP; outlines how TCP can be applied across common game families and neural game engine domains; emphasizes standardization, reproducibility, and comparative benchmarking.

Conclusion: TCP should become standard benchmark metadata and a required statistic in GWM and RL papers to enable consistent, interpretable comparison of environment transition difficulties across benchmarks.

Abstract: Game world modeling (GWM) and reinforcement learning (RL) are often confounded because research papers rarely quantify how difficult the underlying transition prediction problem is at the declared interface (pixels/tokens/latents with finite history). We propose the Transition Complexity Profile (TCP): a small, reproducible set of metrics that characterizes an environment's (or gameplay dataset's) induced transition kernel by (i) intrinsic one-step branching, (ii) interaction-induced uncertainty and opponent influence when observable, and (iii) temporal/spatial dependency span via standardized probe curves. TCP is reported with an explicit reference distribution, protocol stochasticity, and a versioned measurement budget (sampling/resampling and fixed probe compute), enabling comparable numbers across benchmarks. We outline how common game families and modern "neural game engine" domains populate this landscape and call for TCP to become standard benchmark metadata and a required statistic in GWM and RL papers.

</details>


### [206] [Large Language Models in Mental Health: A Systematic Review of Applications, Innovations, and Ethical Challenges](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18080)
*Yisong Chen,Yifan Gao,Sijing Yu,Chuqing Zhao,Yang Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A comprehensive review of large language models (LLMs) in health with emphasis on mental health applications, spanning social media analysis, clinical conversational agents, therapy support tools, prompt engineering, multimodal learning, and ethical/regulatory considerations; highlights advancements in interpretability, domain adaptation, and multimodal fusion, while outlining challenges for safe deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Meet the growing need for scalable, accessible, and personalized mental health support; enable early detection of risk (depression, suicide), improve therapeutic assistance, and generate psychoeducational content, all while ensuring interpretability and clinical relevance amid ethical/regulatory constraints.

Method: Synthesis of interdisciplinary studies leveraging diverse data sources (social media posts, electronic medical records, multimodal inputs); evaluation of annotation strategies, LLM architectures, and prompt engineering; review of multimodal fusion techniques and ethical/regulatory discussions.

Result: Findings indicate that annotational and modeling strategies can enhance interpretability and clinical relevance; potential for early depression detection, suicide risk assessment, and personalized therapy support; multimodal fusion (text, speech, sensor data) shows promise for improved diagnosis and monitoring; prompt engineering is critical for effective domain adaptation; ongoing ethical, sociotechnical, and regulatory challenges; calls for safe, equitable deployment frameworks.

Conclusion: Advocate for robust governance, regulatory compliance, and validation of LLM-based mental health tools; emphasize privacy, bias mitigation, human-in-the-loop evaluation, and accountability; outline directions for future research to address safety, equity, and clinical applicability.

Abstract: We present a review on the applications of large language models (LLMs) in health, e.g., social media analysis, clinical conversational agents, therapy support tools, prompt engineering, multimodal learning, and ethical considerations. We integrate findings from interdisciplinary studies utilizing diverse data sources such as social media posts, electronic medical records, and multimodal inputs to enable early detection of depression, suicide risk assessment, personalized therapy support, and psychoeducational content generation. Our review highlights advancements in LLM models and annotation strategies that enhance interpretability and clinical relevance, while we also emphasize the critical role of prompt engineering for domain adaptation. We also discuss emerging multimodal fusion techniques integrating text, speech, and sensor data for improved mental health diagnosis and monitoring. Finally, we address ongoing ethical, sociotechnical, and regulatory challenges, and advocate frameworks to ensure safe, equitable, and accountable deployment of LLMs in real-world mental health care.

</details>


### [207] [Position: Behavioral Systems Require Behavioral Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18081)
*Manuel Cherep,Nikhil Singh,Pattie Maes*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Advocates a behavior-centric evaluation framework for AI agents, drawing on behavioral sciences to develop rigorous tests that recover decision strategies, isolate behavioral differences, and examine multi-agent dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AI evaluation emphasizes outcomes; to ensure robust, interpretable AI, we must study the underlying behavioral processes using methods from the behavioral sciences (observation, perturbation, interpretation).

Method: Proposes an explicit behavioral testing agenda: (1) recover decision strategies from action sequences, (2) construct environments that isolate behavioral differences, (3) probe emergent dynamics in multi-agent systems; adopt rigorous behavioral tests to study AI behavior.

Result: Conceptual roadmap and proposed methods; an integration of behavioral science principles into AI evaluation, aiming to establish a science of AI behavior.

Conclusion: Shifts evaluation toward systematic behavioral analysis, enabling better understanding, reliability, and interpretability of AI agents; provides concrete steps to build a dedicated science of AI behavior.

Abstract: Artificial agentic systems increasingly operate as behavioral systems by interacting with dynamic environments, pursuing goals, and adapting over time. Yet, current evaluation methods largely focus on performance outcomes, not the underlying behavioral processes that produce them. This paper argues that AI agents must be evaluated like other behavioral systems: through systematic observation, perturbation, and interpretation of their actions. We draw on lessons from the behavioral sciences to motivate this position, and propose a research agenda focused on developing rigorous behavioral tests. These include methods for recovering decision strategies from action sequences, constructing environments that isolate behavioral differences, and probing emergent dynamics in multi-agent systems. Taken together, these directions offer a roadmap for developing a science of AI behavior.

</details>


### [208] [Position: Current Model Cards Are Insufficient for Downstream Governance of Open-Weight Foundation Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18086)
*Sungwon Chae,Keonwoo Kim,Hoki Kim,Jaeyeon Ju,Sangchul Park*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a multi-layered governance framework for open-weight foundation models that integrates model cards, acceptable use policies, and licenses, based on an empirical analysis of 500 Hugging Face model cards to address safety gaps in current approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: OWFMs are proliferating, but current governance tools (model cards, regulatory approaches, heritage/ provenance) inadequately address safety challenges; there is a need to combine informational, normative, and legal artifacts to govern downstream use.

Method: Empirical analysis of 500 model cards hosted on Hugging Face to identify safety-critical information gaps; argumentation for integrating three components; outline of directions for evolving artifacts into a cohesive governance framework.

Result: Demonstrates that effective governance requires a three-layer approach (model cards, AUPs, licenses); finds that standard open-source licenses are ill-suited for OWFMs and may undermine AUP enforceability; calls for integrated artifacts that merge informational, normative, and legal dimensions.

Conclusion: Integrated safety artifacts—melding model cards, AUPs, and licenses—offer a more comprehensive governance framework for OWFMs, guiding safer deployment and use by aligning information, norms, and legal constraints.

Abstract: The growth of open-weight foundation models (OWFMs) has prompted the AI community to re-evaluate strategies for effective downstream governance. Although model cards have been widely adopted as transparency artifacts in model repositories, existing frameworks often fail to adequately inform downstream developers and users about the distinct safety challenges posed by OWFMs. This position paper analyzes 500 model cards hosted on Hugging Face and argues that effective governance of OWFMs requires a multi-layered approach integrating three complementary components: (i) model cards, (ii) acceptable use policies (AUPs), and (iii) licenses. To motivate this claim, we identify a safety gap left by existing regulatory approaches, including model heritage, alignment provenance, and empirically observed behaviors, through an analysis of model cards with safety-critical information. We further argue that standard open-source licenses (OSLs) are not well suited for OWFMs and may weaken the enforceability of AUPs. Building on these observations, we outline directions for evolving model cards, AUPs, and licenses into integrated safety artifacts to enable a more comprehensive governance framework that coherently integrates informational, normative, and legal dimensions.

</details>


### [209] [A Metamorphic Artificial Age Score Decision-Support Prototype for Flight-Log-Based Drone Propeller Health Monitoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18088)
*Seyma Yaman Kayadibi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Metamorphic AAS decision-support for drone propeller health using multi-indicator analysis from flight logs; retrospective evaluation shows distributed fault signals across channels and supports maintenance prioritization.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Propeller faults affect multiple flight-log channels; a unified, multi-indicator decision framework improves detection and prioritization for maintenance and autonomous oversight.

Method: Compute six indicators from raw MATLAB flight-log data, normalize to healthy baseline, evaluate candidate scoring policies, metamorphic adequacy relations, and redundancy-adjusted AAS; retrospective evaluation with one healthy and three defective propeller cases under the same flight profile.

Result: Fault effects manifest across channels; Severity 1-3 cases show varying burden and tracking errors; supports multi-indicator decision-support layer for post-flight maintenance prioritization and autonomous-system oversight.

Conclusion: AAS-based, metamorphic, multi-indicator framework can capture distributed propeller faults and enhance maintenance prioritization and system oversight.

Abstract: Drone propeller faults can create safety and reliability risks when their effects are distributed across multiple flight-log channels rather than appearing as a single diagnostic signal. This paper proposes a Metamorphic Artificial Age Score (AAS) decision-support prototype for flight-log-based drone propeller health monitoring. Using selected historical real flight logs from the 2024 DronePropA public dataset, the framework computes six health-related indicators from raw MATLAB matrices: trajectory tracking error, attitude instability, thrust-command burden, motor-command imbalance, ESC-command instability, and battery-level stress. These indicators are normalized relative to a healthy baseline and evaluated through candidate scoring policies, metamorphic adequacy relations, and a redundancy-adjusted AAS formulation. In this context, AAS is used as a structural policy-adequacy and burden measure rather than as a chronological age measure. A controlled retrospective evaluation was performed using one healthy baseline and three defective propeller cases under the same speed profile and trajectory. The healthy case was assigned to routine monitoring. The Severity 1 case was dominated by ESC-command instability and assigned to maintenance review. The Severity 2 case reached maximum motor-command and ESC-command burden, while the Severity 3 case reached maximum trajectory tracking error; both triggered mandatory inspection. The results show that propeller fault effects may appear through different operational channels, supporting the need for a multi-indicator decision-support layer for post-flight maintenance prioritization and autonomous-system oversight.

</details>


### [210] [Position: Multi-Agent Systems Should Prioritize Concurrency Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18092)
*Xin Yang,Letian Li,Zimo Ji,Terry Jingchen Zhang,Wenyuan Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM-based MAS failures are primarily concurrency-control problems due to concurrent reads/writes on shared state and long inference windows; the paper urges explicit CC mechanisms (conflict detection, isolation, structured resource access) as a first-class design concern.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliability declines when adding agents to MAS; failures are often attributed to coordination/communication but can be mapped to classical concurrency anomalies, especially with stale reads and lost updates caused by long LLM latency.

Method: Conceptual analysis in a position-paper format that maps MAS failure modes to classical concurrency anomalies and presents design principles for CC: conflict detection, isolation guarantees, and structured access to shared resources.

Result: A framework of design principles and a call to treat concurrency control as a core MAS design concern; no empirical evaluation is reported.

Conclusion: Concurrency control should be a foundational aspect of MAS frameworks to improve reliability and consistency in LLM-driven multi-agent collaboration.

Abstract: LLM-based multi-agent systems (MAS) promise scalable collaboration, yet adding agents often reduces reliability. This position paper argues that many MAS failures are fundamentally concurrency control problems: agents concurrently read and write shared state, and long LLM inference windows amplify the risk of stale reads, lost updates, and inconsistent outcomes. Failure modes commonly attributed to coordination or communication breakdowns can be mapped directly onto classical concurrency anomalies. We contend that MAS frameworks should address these failures through explicit concurrency control mechanisms: conflict detection, isolation guarantees, and structured access to shared resources. Concurrency control should be a first-class design concern, not an afterthought.

</details>


### [211] [FinSkillBench: Evaluating AI Agents and Domain Skills for Investment Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18099)
*Jermyn Zhen Yong Bek,Zhuang Qiang Bok,Zhongtian Sun*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FinSkillBench shows that access to curated procedural skills significantly boosts LM-agent performance in investment tasks; self-generated skills offer little benefit and are costly; benchmark and resources released for future research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate whether language-model agents can effectively use finance-domain skills to perform point-in-time financial tasks, and understand the relative value of curated vs self-generated procedural skills.

Method: FinSkillBench spans three domains (portfolio construction, risk management, fundamental analysis) with 12 subtasks and 2,603 task episodes. Episodes include point-in-time inputs, hidden ground truth, and verifiers. Compared conditions: no skill, curated skill packages (procedural docs + executable components), and self-generated skills (agents write/reuse their own procedures). Evaluated across 9 models; Hermes Agent replication with 8 models and 5,280 episodes.

Result: Curated skills raise mean performance from 0.366 to 0.528; largest gains in portfolio construction and risk management. Self-generated skills yield little benefit despite higher compute. Independent Hermes replication corroborates the directional pattern, with effects varying by subtask and harness.

Conclusion: Reliable procedural skills are as important as model choice in investment-management agents, while naive self-generation of skills is often ineffective. The authors provide the benchmark, tools, curated skills, and full trajectories to enable further research.

Abstract: Investment management is a high-stakes domain in which agentic AI systems must do more than generate plausible text. They must retrieve point-in-time data, assemble correct computational inputs, invoke specialized methods, and produce auditable structured outputs. We introduce FinSkillBench, an evaluation suite designed to measure whether language model agents can effectively use financial domain skills to solve investment management tasks. The benchmark spans three domains, portfolio construction, risk management, and fundamental analysis, and includes 12 subtasks with 2,603 task episodes.
  Each episode provides point-in-time inputs, hidden ground truth, and a task-specific verifier.We compare three conditions: no skill, curated skill packages consisting of procedural documents and executable components, and self-generated skills in which the agent writes and reuses its own procedures within an episode. Across 9 models and a large-scale evaluation, curated skills consistently improve performance, raising mean scores from 0.366 to 0.528, with the largest gains in portfolio construction and risk management.
  In contrast, self-generated skills provide little benefit despite higher computational cost. An independent evaluation using a separate agent framework (Hermes Agent, 8 models, 5,280 episodes total) reproduces the directional pattern across all three domains, with the magnitude of skill effects varying by subtask and harness.
  These results showthat in investment management agents, access to reliable procedural skills can be as important as model choice, while naive self-generation of skills is often ineffective. We release the benchmark, evaluation tools, curated skill packages, and full trajectories to support further research.

</details>


### [212] [Self-Evolving Agents as Dynamic Graph Transformation: A Survey and New Perspective](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18104)
*Yuanyuan Xu,Wenjie Zhang,Yin Chen,Xuemin Lin,Ying Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a dynamic-graph framework for self-evolving LLM-based agents, treating agent evolution as dynamic graph transformation. It offers a four-part taxonomy of graph evolution, maps nine dynamic-graph-learning subfields to agent capabilities, and outlines graph-aware evaluation and governance protocols to guide design and governance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current literature either treats graphs as static support for agent functions or focuses on agent-level mechanisms, with little attention to how evolving agent state couples with dynamic graph topology. A unified dynamic-graph perspective can unify design, learning, and governance of self-evolving agents.

Method: Model agent state as a dynamic, typed graph where memories, tools, skills, workflows, and inter-agent relations are represented as nodes, edges, and subgraphs updated via schema-constrained rewrites. Organize methods into four taxonomies: (i) node/feature evolution, (ii) edge/topology evolution, (iii) subgraph activation, (iv) cross-component co-evolution. Map nine dynamic-graph-learning subfields to agent-evolution capabilities and discuss adaptations and failure modes. Propose dynamic graph learning as reusable infrastructure, and describe five graph-aware evaluation and governance protocols.

Result: A compact, structured framework and taxonomy linking evolving agent states with dynamic graph topology, plus a proposed inspection/ governance layer and a mapping from existing dynamic-graph-learning subfields to agent-evolution tasks.

Conclusion: Dynamic graph learning provides a reusable infrastructure for designing and governing self-evolving agents, offering a structural lens to integrate memory, tools, skills, workflows, and inter-agent relations through dynamic graph transformations.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM)-based agents are increasingly becoming self-evolving systems that persist across interactions, maintain memories, use tools, acquire skills, refine workflows, and coordinate with other agents. These capabilities make agent states structural and dynamic: entities, relations, attributes, dependencies, and execution structures change with new evidence, feedback, and environmental conditions. Existing graph-agent surveys typically treat graphs as support structures for agent functions rather than as evolving substrates, while self-evolving-agent surveys focus on agent-level mechanisms and rarely discuss graph topology evolution. Thus, the coupling between evolving agent state and dynamic graph topology remains underexplored. This survey connects these two research lines by framing \textit{agent evolution as dynamic graph transformation}. We model agent state as a dynamic graph, where memories, tools, skills, workflows, and inter-agent relations are represented as typed nodes, edges, and subgraphs updated through schema-constrained rewrites. Based on this formulation, we organize existing dynamic-graph-based methods for self-evolving agents into four taxonomies: node/feature evolution, edge/topology evolution, subgraph activation, and cross-component co-evolution. Building on this taxonomy, we propose dynamic graph learning as reusable infrastructure for self-evolving agents and map nine dynamic-graph-learning subfields to agent-evolution capabilities, discussing their adaptations and possible failure modes. Finally, we discuss five types of graph-aware evaluation and governance protocols from a dynamic-graph perspective, which complement end-task evaluation. The goal is to provide a compact structural lens for designing and governing self-evolving agents.

</details>


### [213] [Emergence of Agentic AI: A Review on Evolution, Background, Working Principles, Applications, Adoption Factors, and Future Research Directions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18110)
*AKM Bahalul Haque,Al Amin Islam Ridoy,Mohammad Rayhan,Ivan Porres*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematic review of Agentic AI covering fundamentals, evolution, architecture, applications, challenges, and future directions; proposes a stakeholder-intention framework and system quality dimensions to guide adoption.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rapid advancements in Agentic AI necessitate a consolidated understanding of current state, gaps, and future research directions to guide researchers and practitioners.

Method: Systematic review of recent scholarly contributions across domains; synthesize fundamentals, history, architecture, functionalities, applications; identify challenges; propose framework for stakeholders’ intention to use/adopt Agentic AI using system quality dimensions.

Result: Provides a comprehensive overview of Agentic AI developments, highlights key research gaps, and outlines directions for future work; introduces a stakeholder-intention framework and QoS-like dimensions as a roadmap.

Conclusion: The field requires consolidated theoretical and practical guidance; this work offers a foundation for understanding Agentic AI and a roadmap for research and adoption.

Abstract: Agentic AI is gaining new insights and advancements in the field of Artificial Intelligence, fostering significant potential to enable rapid transformation across various domains.This rapid advancement and the potential to revolutionize various domains advocate the need for a deeper understanding and firm grasp of the technology. Moreover, an investigation into state of the art research directions in agentic AI needs to be conducted to comprehensively assess the potential scope for improvement and application.Therefore, to address these objectives, a comprehensive review can provide researchers and practitioners with valuable insights into the current state and future research scopes of agentic AI.Hence, this work considers the recently published scholarly contributions in agentic AI across various domains and discusses the fundamentals and working principles of Agentic AI, traces the historical and theoretical evolution of agency in artificial systems, explores and discusses Agentic AIs architecture, working principles, and functionalities, explores real-world applications of Agentic AI across various domains, analyzes the research findings, identifies current challenges, and discuss potential future research directions, and proposes a comprehensive framework of stakeholders intention to use and adopt Agentic AI with the help of proposed system quality dimensions.Therefore, this systematic review provides researchers and practitioners with a comprehensive understanding of Agentic AI, its current developments and applications, highlights key research gaps, and outlines future research directions.

</details>


### [214] [Solving Is Not Drawing: A Benchmark for Diagrammatic Reasoning in Olympiad Geometry](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18111)
*Hsien Xin Peng,Anthony Kim,Alvin Li,Calvin Supasanya,Shivank Garg,Kevin Zhu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces an open-source benchmark of 954 self-contained olympiad geometry problems, including a 297-problem hard subset, each with human-authored Asymptote diagrams, to measure diagrammatic reasoning in foundation models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current benchmarks (e.g., MathVista, MathVerse) assess problem-solving accuracy but not the ability to construct faithful geometric diagrams; progress in geometry AI requires both reasoning and accurate diagram construction.

Method: Assemble a dataset of 954 geometry problems with high-fidelity Asymptote diagrams and solutions, plus a suite of metrics across text, code, image, vision-language, and constraint-based analyses; evaluate state-of-the-art models on both solving and diagram fidelity.

Result: Found a pronounced gap between solving and drawing: models' diagrams are less faithful with an average diagram compile success rate of 36.14%; strong mathematical reasoning does not ensure accurate diagram construction.

Conclusion: A valuable benchmark and dataset to quantify and drive progress in diagrammatic reasoning for geometry, enabling targeted improvements in diagram generation alongside problem solving; the dataset is openly accessible.

Abstract: Foundation models such as GPT and Claude now solve olympiad-level mathematics with remarkable proficiency, so much so that geometry problem solving has become a standard proxy for their mathematical reasoning. Yet solving a geometry problem and drawing the figure it depends on are not the same skill: progress often hinges on a faithful diagram with the right auxiliary constructions and incidences, and it is unclear that a model which reasons its way to the answer can also produce one. A growing collection of benchmarks, including MathVista, and MathVerse, measures whether models reach the correct answer, but to our knowledge, none isolate the distinct ability to construct the diagram itself, leaving this capability unmeasured. We introduce an open-source benchmark that targets this gap: 954 self-contained olympiad geometry problems, with a 297-problem hard subset, each paired with its solution and a human-authored, high-fidelity diagram in renderable Asymptote code, together with a suite of text-, code-, image-, VLM-, and constraint-based metrics for what we term diagrammatic reasoning. Evaluating current foundation models reveals a pronounced gap between solving and drawing: their diagrams are markedly less faithful, with an average compile success rate of only 36.14\%. Strong mathematical reasoning, we find, does not imply the ability to construct accurate geometric diagrams. Our benchmark and dataset can be accessed at https://huggingface.co/datasets/max98765/hard_geometry_problems_with_diagrams.

</details>


### [215] [Position: AI Leaderboards Are Underserving the Global South: A Case Study from India](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18117)
*Sourav Banerjee,Saikat Saha*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Regional AI leaderboards with independent governance are necessary, as data is not the bottleneck; global benchmarks omit key regional benchmarks, and governance gaps sustain language- and region-specific gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address persistent gaps in AI evaluation for the Global South caused by misaligned incentives and lack of governance in global leaderboards; ensure inclusive coverage of languages and regions.

Method: Case study of India leveraging a consultation with 58 AI practitioners and analysis of existing regional benchmarks (IndicSUPERB, MILU, LAHAJA for India; IrokoBench for Africa; AlGhafa for Arabic) to argue for governance-focused reform.

Result: Consensus among practitioners favors formal governance and disclosure-based conflict management; identifies institutional design as the barrier; high-quality regional benchmarks exist but are excluded from global leaderboards; proposes regional leaderboards with independent governance from the start.

Conclusion: Data alone will not fix disparities; institutional design changes—specifically regional, independently governed leaderboards—are required to ensure inclusive, sustainable AI evaluation across languages and regions.

Abstract: This position paper argues that AI leaderboards are structurally ill-suited to serving the Global South because they lack independent governance, conflict-of-interest policies, and mechanisms for metric evolution. The barrier is not missing data; high-quality regional benchmarks already exist: IndicSUPERB, MILU, and LAHAJA for India; IrokoBench for Africa; AlGhafa for Arabic. The barrier is institutional design. Global leaderboards do not include these benchmarks, and no governance mechanism compels them to do so. Commercial pressure corrects leaderboard failures when paying customers in the Global North are affected. The Global South lacks equivalent leverage. Without governance, failures affecting Hindi, Swahili, or Arabic speakers persist indefinitely as documented but unaddressed gaps. Using India as a case study (1.4 billion people, 22 scheduled languages, high-quality benchmarks, but no trusted aggregation), we report findings from a consultation with 58 AI practitioners showing consistent preference for formal governance and disclosure-based conflict management. The solution is not more data but better institutions: regional leaderboards with independent governance from the start.

</details>


### [216] [Safety Alignment Illusion: The Cross-Lingual Safety Gap in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18131)
*Namya Bhatnagar*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multilingual evaluation benchmark (INCLUDE) probes Indian-centric biases in LLMs across English, Hindi, Bengali, Marathi, Tamil, and Hinglish; evaluated 10 LLMs with 2,604 prompts, yielding 14,988 bias scores, revealing Bengali as most biased in open-source models and English as least biased in open-source but most biased in closed-source models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address safety alignment gaps for non-English languages, especially in India, where voice assistants and dialogue systems risk propagating harmful bias due to English-centric safety filters.

Method: Create INCLUDE benchmark with prompts in six languages; evaluate 10 LLMs (open and closed-source); compute per-prompt bias scores across 14,988 instances; perform statistical analysis to compare bias across languages and model categories.

Result: Two key findings: (1) Bengali shows the highest average bias in open-source models; (2) English is least biased in open-source but most biased in closed-source models.

Conclusion: Cross-lingual safety gaps exist; a multilingual benchmark is essential. English-centric safety alignment may fail in non-English contexts, and model type (open vs closed) affects bias patterns. INCLUDE provides a tool to quantify Indian-centric biases and guide improvements.

Abstract: Current safety alignment training for Large Language Models (LLMs) are heavily English-centric. When such safety filters fail for non-English languages, the consequences are immediate and user-facing: voice assistants and spoken dialogue systems may produce stereotype-reinforcing outputs, bypassing the standard English-focused safety alignments and propagating harmful bias to non-English speaking communities. For spoken language technologies deployed across India's linguistically diverse population, this represents a critical failure mode. To address this cross-lingual gap, we introduce INCLUDE (Indian Cultural Lens for Understanding and Detecting Embedded Biases), a multilingual evaluation benchmark designed to quantify Indian-centric socio-cultural biases. INCLUDE consists of 2,604 prompts spanning six prompt languages: English, Hindi, Bengali, Marathi, Tamil, and Hinglish (Hindi-English code-mix). We evaluate ten open- and closed-source LLMs against this benchmark, analyzing 14,988 bias scores. Our statistical results reveal two key findings. First, Bengali yielded the highest average bias score in open-source models. Second, English demonstrated a notable reversal, producing the lowest bias in open-source models but the highest bias in closed-source models.

</details>


### [217] [Optimized Fuzzy Logic Approach with the IEEE Key Gas Method for Diagnosing Power Transformer Faults Using Dissolved Gas Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18133)
*Kim-Anh Nguyen,Huy Hoang Le,Ba Tu Phung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: An enhanced fuzzy-logic version of the IEEE Key Gas Method (FL-KGM) for transformer DGA markedly improves fault diagnosis accuracy (up to 98.6%), via refined membership functions, optimized fuzzy rules, and a CO/CO2 separation, using multidimensional gas ratios and adaptive classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: KGM suffers from ambiguous data and limited diagnostic accuracy in DGA for transformer faults; need for robust, accurate, and adaptive fault diagnosis to support predictive maintenance.

Method: Integrates fuzzy logic with KGM (FL-KGM). Key innovations: refined membership functions, optimized fuzzy rule sets, and a novel separation of CO and CO2; employs multidimensional gas ratio analysis and an adaptive classification framework to improve fault identification and classification.

Result: Experimental validation on real-world datasets shows FL-KGM attaining up to 98.6% accuracy, significantly outperforming standard KGM and other FL-based approaches.

Conclusion: FL-KGM demonstrates strong potential for intelligent transformer fault detection and predictive maintenance, advancing transformer monitoring in modern power systems.

Abstract: Reliable transformer fault diagnosis is essential for maintaining power system stability. The IEEE Key Gas Method (KGM), a widely utilized approach in Dissolved Gas Analysis (DGA), exhibits limitations in addressing ambiguous data and ensuring high diagnostic accuracy. This study presents An enhanced model combining Fuzzy Logic with the IEEE Key Gas Method (FL-KGM) that introduces refined membership functions, optimized fuzzy rule sets, and a novel separation of CO and CO2 to eliminate diagnostic inconsistencies. By leveraging multidimensional gas ratio analysis and an adaptive classification framework, FL-KGM delivers superior fault identification and classification. Experimental validation utilizing real-world datasets demonstrates that FL-KGM achieves up to 98.6% accuracy, significantly outperforming KGM and other FL-based approaches. These findings elucidate the potential of FL-KGM in advancing transformer monitoring, enabling intelligent fault detection, and enhancing predictive maintenance strategies in modern power systems.

</details>


### [218] [Improving Rural Medication Safety with AI: A Scoping Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18135)
*Jeong-ah Kim,Muhammad Ashad Kabir,Daniel Terry,Maryam Rouhi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A scoping review (2012-2025) on AI use to reduce medication errors in rural health settings; AI spans prescribing to monitoring, with ML surveillance reducing errors by 34–80%; barriers include governance, cost, and clinician resistance; overall potential for enhanced safety and workflow unless governance/integration challenges persist.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medication errors threaten patient safety, especially in rural areas with limited resources. This review synthesizes how AI is applied to mitigate errors across the medication process and assesses its effectiveness and rural-specific challenges.

Method: Scoping review of literature from 2012 to 2025 across EBSCohost, Emcare (Ovid), MEDLINE, and ProQuest Consumer Health Database. Twelve primary studies from nine countries were included. Data were analyzed thematically to extract AI interventions across the medication process.

Result: AI integrated across all stages of medication management (prescribing, dispensing, administration, post-administration monitoring). Four themes emerged: (1) AI types (Clinical Decision Support Systems, Machine Learning, Natural Language Processing, smart pumps); (2) affected medication process phases; (3) effectiveness in reducing errors and enhancing workflow safety; (4) rural-specific challenges (infrastructure, training, system integration, alert fatigue). ML-based surveillance improved incident detection and reduced prescribing/transcription errors by 34%–80%. Barriers include governance gaps, financial constraints, and clinician resistance.

Conclusion: AI technologies hold strong potential to enhance pharmaceutical safety in rural healthcare through data-driven monitoring, process automation, and decision support, provided governance, cost, and integration challenges are addressed.

Abstract: Introduction: Medication errors (MEs) represent a significant threat to global healthcare systems, contributing to patient harm. Introducing artificial intelligence (AI) in rural healthcare enhances patient safety. The aim is to explore the applications and effectiveness of AI technologies in enhancing patient safety and reducing medication errors in rural health settings.
  Methods: A scoping review was conducted through a systematic literature search spanning 2012 to 2025 across multiple databases, including EBSCohost, Emcare (Ovid), MEDLINE, and the ProQuest Consumer Health Database. Twelve primary studies from nine different nations were examined. Data were analysed thematically to obtain insights on AI interventions across the medication process.
  Results: AI technologies have been integrated into every stage of medication management, right from prescribing and dispensing to administration and post-administration monitoring. Four key themes came to light: (1) the various types of AI being utilised (like Clinical Decision Support Systems, Machine Learning, Natural Language Processing, and smart pumps); (2) the phases of the medication process that are affected; (3) how effective these technologies are in minimising errors and boosting workflow safety; and (4) rural-specific challenges including infrastructure, staff training, system integration, and alert fatigue. Several studies have demonstrated that machine learning-based surveillance improves incident detection and reduces prescribing and transcription errors by an impressive 34% to 80%. Barriers included lack of governance frameworks, financial limitations, and clinician resistance, which still present major obstacles.
  Conclusion: In rural healthcare, AI technologies hold great potential for enhancing pharmaceutical safety. They can allow data-driven monitoring, automate processes, and offer clinical decision assistance.

</details>


### [219] [FraudBench: Stress-Testing Policy-Grounded Banking Agents Against Adaptive Fraud](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18136)
*Dheeraj Mohandas Pai,Lu Xian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FraudBench: an executable, policy-grounded banking-agent benchmark to test safety under conversational manipulation, with dual-control actors and a 698-document internal policy corpus.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To evaluate how end-to-end banking agents safely act when a caller can manipulate identity, authorization, and trust through dialogue, addressing a gap in existing static transaction and generic safety benchmarks.

Method: An executable testbed based on the τ^2-bench dual-control framework and the τ-Knowledge banking environment where agent and caller exchange actions via tools over shared account state. The agent may grant tool access; policy retrieval from a 698-document corpus. FraudBench includes 150 authored scenarios, with a frozen 107-scenario graded set (90 fraud mechanisms plus 17 chained adaptive attacks) and 43 chained attacks held out. Each scenario is annotated with observable evidence, prohibited actions, safe dispositions, and intervention points. Safety is history-dependent and adaptive attacks exploit earlier probes or failed attempts. A four-agent, single-trial evaluation on the 107 graded tasks.

Result: FraudBench yields attack-security between 49% and 65% across four agents on the graded tasks; money-mule and first-party fraud are the most common cross-model weaknesses.

Conclusion: FraudBench provides a realistic, history-aware benchmark for evaluating policy-grounded banking agents and reveals substantial safety gaps under adaptive social-engineering-like attacks, motivating improvements in policy-grounded reasoning, access control, and monitoring.

Abstract: Conversational agents now act for end users through tools while holding access to customer databases and internal policy documents that a caller can reach through dialogue alone. Banking is the clearest case: the same agent that answers a question can also change contact details, reset a PIN, or move money, so ordinary customer service is inseparable from authorization, fraud detection, and policy compliance. Existing financial-fraud benchmarks classify static transactions or messages, and general agent-safety benchmarks target prompt injection or generic harmful use; none test whether a policy-grounded banking agent safely acts when a caller manipulates identity, authorization, and trust over a conversation. We introduce FraudBench, an executable benchmark built on the $τ^2$-bench dual-control framework and the $τ$-Knowledge banking environment. Both the agent and the simulated caller act through tools over shared, mutable account state, and the agent may grant the caller access to selected tools; the environment exposes a 698-document internal policy corpus that the agent must retrieve from. FraudBench contains 150 authored adversarial scenarios; a frozen public set of 107 (90 across ten fraud mechanisms plus 17 chained adaptive attacks) is used for all reported runs, with 43 further chained attacks held out. Safety is history-dependent: single-control tasks satisfy every precondition but one, and adaptive attacks make a later, locally valid request unsafe because of an earlier probe, admission, or failed attempt. Each scenario is annotated with observable evidence, prohibited actions, safe dispositions, and intervention points. A preliminary single-trial evaluation of four agents on the 107 graded tasks yields attack-security between 49\% and 65\%, with money-mule and first-party fraud the most common cross-model weaknesses.

</details>


### [220] [Efficient Adaptation of LLMs for Hate Speech Detection in Low-Resource Languages: A Comparative Study on Roman Urdu](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18142)
*Toneema Zubair,Muhammad Junaid Asif,Faisal Kamiran,Hafiz Hassan Saeed,Rana Fayyaz Ahmad*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LoRA-based PEFT enables effective hate-speech detection in Roman Urdu with large language models; zero-shot baselines are weak (~F1 0.56) but PEFT achieves F1 > 0.93 on PURUTT (72k comments).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address scarcity of annotated data and linguistic variability in Low Resource Languages (LRLs), exemplified by Roman Urdu, by leveraging PEFT with LoRA to adapt large language models for hate speech detection.

Method: Benchmark zero-shot LLMs (Mistral, LLaMA, Falcon, mBERT) against LoRA-based PEFT on the PURUTT dataset (~72k annotated comments) for hate speech detection in Roman Urdu.

Result: Zero-shot F1 ≈ 0.56; LoRA-PEFT fine-tuning yields substantial gains, achieving F1 > 0.93 with efficient (few-parameter) updates.

Conclusion: PEFT with LoRA is highly effective and computationally efficient for hate speech detection in a low-resource language setting, indicating strong potential for deploying LLMs in LRL HSD tasks.

Abstract: It is challenging to detect hate speech in Low Resource Languages (LRLs) because of the absence of annotated data, the informality of its language structure, and the lack of standardized grammar. A good example of such a challenge is Roman Urdu which is broadly used by South Asians on social media and has a high variation while lacking contextually consistent spellings. The objective of this paper is to conduct a comprehensive assessment of Large Language Models (LLMs) for Hate Speech Detection (HSD) in Roman Urdu script and fine-tune these models using the Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) method called Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). To evaluate zero-shot inference, we benchmarked it against PEFT on different transformer models, including Mistral, LLaMA, Falcon, and multilingual BERT. Experiments are conducted on the PURUTT (Parallel Urdu and Roman Urdu Corpus for Toxic Comments and Transliteration) dataset with over 72,000 annotated comments. The results suggest that zero shot models perform moderately (F1 = 0.56), but updating a small fraction of the model trainable parameters improves the classification performance significantly (F1 > 0.93). Our results have shown that PEFT delivers outstanding performance alongside excellent computational efficiency, making it highly suitable for low-resource language processing tasks.

</details>


### [221] [RDFdL: Integrating RDF with Differential Dynamic Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18165)
*Yuyang Li,Lukas Kubelka,Julia Butte,Tobias Käfer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes RDFdL: a framework that unifies RDF-based static knowledge with Differential Dynamic Logic to model and verify continuous dynamics in physical systems, linking dynamic verification results to RDF entailments via a translation to dL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RDF-based knowledge graphs handle static facts but lack expressive power for dynamic, continuous systems described by differential equations; integrating with differential dynamic logic enables formal verification of dynamic properties (safety, reachability) within an RDF/SHACL framework.

Method: Represent differential equations and state-space ranges syntactically in RDF/SHACL; provide semantic translation to differential dynamic logic (dL); connect RDF and dL via shared first-order logic, so dL verification results become RDF entailments (via SPARQL); implement pipeline with Apache Jena for RDF reasoning and KeYmaera X for dL proving; sketch applicability to manufacturing.

Result: A unified pipeline that allows static RDF data to be augmented with verified dynamic properties; safety and reachability results in dL can be queried as RDF entailments, enabling integrated reasoning over static and dynamic knowledge.

Conclusion: Integrating RDF with dL broadens the expressivity of RDF for cyber-physical systems, enabling formal verification feedback to RDF queries; practical realization with existing tools shows potential for manufacturing domains, though scalability and broader applicability warrant further work.

Abstract: Knowledge graphs modeled in RDF are powerful for describing static knowledge, but they cannot capture or reason about the dynamic behavior of physical systems, e.g., systems described by differential equations, which is a critical gap for AI-driven cyber-physical systems. To solve this, we propose RDFdL, a framework that integrates RDF with Differential Dynamic Logic (dL) to represent and reason about both static knowledge and the continuous dynamics of physical systems. For the dynamic part, we syntactically represent differential equations and ranges in the state space in RDF and SHACL and provide semantics using a translation to dL. Linking RDF and dL through their shared foundation in first-order logic achieves a unique integration: verification results for safety and reachability properties in the dynamic logic domain become available as entailment to SPARQL queries over RDF data. We implement the pipeline using Apache Jena for ontology-driven RDF reasoning and KeYmaera X, the theorem prover for dL, and sketch its applicability in manufacturing.

</details>


### [222] [Adversarial Review: Structured Disagreement for Grounded Agentic Code Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18167)
*Eric S. Qiu,Joyce Gill*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Adversarial Review (AR) shows that a minimal cooperative code-review setup with three agents (main coder, reviewer, critic) and structured disagreement can outperform larger multi-agent baselines, reducing overhead while preserving interaction benefits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between full multi-agent teams and subagent paradigms by enabling essential agent-to-agent critique with minimal communication, ensuring evidence-grounded disagreement in code reviews.

Method: Introduce AR protocol: reviewer evaluates code; critic audits the review via structured disagreement before the main agent edits; evaluate on LiveCodeBench, SWE-PRBench, SWE-bench Verified; compare to baselines including five-agent configurations; analyze failure modes like false consensus.

Result: AR achieves highest pass rate on LiveCodeBench among tested methods, beating five-agent baseline with only three agents; on SWE-PRBench, naive AR shows false-consensus, but adding a single prompt iteration to inject disagreement yields highest F1; on SWE-bench Verified, AR improves repository-level tasks vs baselines.

Conclusion: Cooperative code review benefits from minimal, structured, evidence-grounded disagreement; large agent counts and complex communication are unnecessary for effective code-review assistance.

Abstract: Early multi-agent LLM systems often used role-separated teams, yet scaling agent count yields diminishing returns on repository-level coding tasks. Recent alternatives treat agents as passive tools (subagents), yet this removes the benefits of agent interaction entirely. We study whether a subagent paradigm can support a middle ground: minimal agentic cooperation without the overhead of large multi-agent teams. We introduce Adversarial Review (AR), a minimal cooperative code-review protocol in which a main coding agent works with a reviewer and a critic agent. The reviewer evaluates code, while the critic audits the review through structured disagreement before the main agent edits. On LiveCodeBench, AR achieves the highest pass rate among tested methods, outperforming a five-agent baseline while using only three agents. On SWE-PRBench, naive AR exposes a false-consensus failure mode, where agents converge on agreement without sufficient evidence, but a single prompt iteration that adds disagreement explicitly achieves the highest F1 among tested methods. On SWE-bench Verified, AR also shows improvements over the baselines on repository-level coding tasks. Together, AR demonstrates that cooperative code review does not require many agents or complex communication structures: it requires that disagreement be minimal, structured, and evidence-grounded.

</details>


### [223] [Looped Language Models Improve Compositional Tool Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18171)
*Andrei Cristian Popescu,Haitz Sáez de Ocáriz Borde,Pietro Liò*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Looped language models improve multi-step, compositional tool use; recurrence depth boosts accuracy, with adaptive inference balancing compute and performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether looped LMs can handle agentic tool use requiring planning, state maintenance, and dependencies across API calls, and quantify effects of recurrence depth and training (native vs retrofitted) on multiple tool-use benchmarks.

Method: Evaluate native and retrofitted looped LMs on API-Bank, BFCL, and NESTful; compare looped vs non-looped models trained with matched supervised fine-tuning; vary recurrent depth at inference; perform controlled experiments and measure accuracy on multi-step tool use and API invocations; analyze adaptive inference behavior.

Result: Recurrent computation generally benefits compositional and dependency-aware tool use; smaller gains on isolated API invocations; multi-step accuracy improves with higher recurrent depth; adaptive inference yields better compute-performance trade-offs by expanding computation only when needed.

Conclusion: Looped LMs show promise as architectures for agentic systems requiring reliable planning, coordination, and execution of compositional tool-use workflows.

Abstract: Looped language models have shown promising results on reasoning benchmarks, yet their potential for agentic tool use remains largely unexplored. We study this question in compositional tool-calling settings, where models must coordinate multiple API calls, maintain intermediate state, and preserve dependencies across tool interactions. We evaluate native and retrofitted looped language models on API-Bank, BFCL, and NESTful, comparing looped and non-looped models trained under matched supervised fine-tuning recipes and varying recurrent depth at inference time. In controlled experiments, recurrent computation generally benefits compositional and dependency-aware tool use, while providing smaller and more model-dependent gains on isolated API invocation. Accuracy on multi-step tool use generally increases with recurrent depth; adaptive inference, however, achieves a more favorable compute-performance trade-off by allocating additional computation only when needed. Our results suggest that looped language models are a promising architecture for agentic systems that require reliable planning, coordination, and execution of compositional tool use workflows.

</details>


### [224] [On the Triangle Inequality for the Jaccard Distance in Arbitrary Lattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18194)
*Costin Bădică,Amelia Bădică*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Generalizes the Jaccard distance to lattices via valuations; triangle inequality shown under positivity, monotonicity, modularity (arbitrary lattices); for relatively complemented distributive lattices, holds under positivity, monotonicity, supermodularity, and log-submodularity; symmetric-difference variant for submodular valuations on sectionally complemented lattices; supermodularity is necessary for the standard generalized Jaccard to be a metric; discusses applications to quantum information, formal concept analysis, and machine learning, plus open problems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend metric properties of Jaccard distance beyond Boolean/distributive lattices to general lattices and sublattice structures; enable lattice-based metrics for applications in quantum information theory, formal concept analysis, and machine learning.

Method: Theoretical proofs establishing triangle inequality constraints for generalized Jaccard under specified valuation properties across lattice classes; adaptation of the symmetric-difference formulation to sectionally complemented lattices; analysis of necessity of supermodularity; and discussion of practical implications.

Result: Triangle inequality holds under identified conditions: for arbitrary lattices with positive, monotone, modular valuations; for relatively complemented distributive lattices with positive, monotone, supermodular, and log-submodular valuations; symmetric-difference Jaccard adapts to sectionally complemented distributive lattices; supermodularity is necessary for the standard generalized Jaccard to be a metric; connects to quantum information, formal concept analysis, and machine learning.

Conclusion: Extends the applicability of generalized Jaccard distances to broader lattice structures, clarifies necessary valuation properties, and highlights practical implications and open mathematical questions in computational fields.

Abstract: This paper presents new theoretical results on generalizing the Jaccard distance for lattices and real valuations. We demonstrate that when the valuation is strictly positive, monotone, and modular, the Jaccard distance satisfies the triangle inequality on arbitrary lattices, effectively generalizing earlier results that depended heavily on distributivity. Moving to relatively complemented distributive lattices (which safely drop the requirement for the global bounds found in Boolean algebras), we prove the triangle inequality holds as long as the valuation is positive, monotone, supermodular, and $\log$-submodular. Additionally, we adapt the symmetric-difference Jaccard formulation for submodular valuations to sectionally complemented distributive lattices.
