Shifting to necessary conditions, we prove that supermodularity is a strict requirement for the standard generalized Jaccard distance to operate as a valid metric. Finally, we map the practical value of relaxing these structural constraints to computational fields like quantum information theory, formal concept analysis, and machine learning, closing with a brief look at open mathematical problems.

</details>


### [225] [GenEx: A Graph-Based Representational Paradigm for SARS-CoV-2 Variant Detection via Codon Co-occurrence Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18238)
*Arefin Amin,Labiba Faiza Karim,M. Monir Uddin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GenEx introduces codon co-occurrence graphs and spectral features (MSCG, LAPCG) to improve SARS-CoV-2 variant classification, outperforming traditional alignment-based approaches using 23 ML models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional sequence analysis relies on alignment and distance matrices that treat sequences as linear strings, failing to capture contextual dependencies among codons. There is a need for contextualized, graph-based representations to improve inter-class separability and variant detection.

Method: Convert gene sequences into codon co-occurrence graphs via MSCG and LAPCG; treat codons as a structured vocabulary; apply spectral graph feature extraction using SVD with squared singular values (σ^2); feed features to 23 benchmarked ML models to classify SARS-CoV-2 variants.

Result: The approach achieved remarkable variant detection performance across all SARS-CoV-2 variants evaluated, with strong inter-class separability demonstrated by the spectral features and validated by 23 ML models.

Conclusion: Graph-based genomic representations with codon co-occurrence graphs and spectral features offer a significant advancement over classical alignment-based methods for variant detection and analysis, validating cross-disciplinary methods from computational linguistics in genomics.

Abstract: Genomic analysis on viruses such as SARS-CoV-2 variants: Beta, Gamma, Delta, and Omicron is heavily dominated by classical bioinformatics methods, including Sequence Alignment, Phylogenetic Analysis, and Mutation Frequency Statistics. These approaches use pairwise codon or nucleotide distance matrices to analyze gene sequences, treating them as linear strings rather than capturing their complex contextual interdependencies. We proposed GenEx, a pipeline that converts raw gene sequences into codon co-occurrence graphs and extracts more than 25 graph features. Our two most prominent techniques for graph generation and feature extraction are MSCG (Multi-Scale Codon Co-occurrence Graph) and LAPCG (Linear-time Adjacency PMI Codon Graph). Using these algorithms, we treated codon sequences as structured symbolic vocabularies interpretable to codon co-occurrence graph analysis, a representational paradigm borrowed from computational linguistics. Another major contribution includes implementing a spectral graph feature extraction using Singular Value Decomposition (SVD), using the squared singular value ($σ^2$) instead of the traditionally used eigenvalue, which helped us to amplify the separation between dominant and subdominant spectral components, thereby enhancing inter-class separability in downstream classification. And to further demonstrate that our method works, we trained 23 benchmarked ML models against the latest SARS-CoV-2 variants, achieving remarkable results in detecting all SARS-CoV-2 variants.

</details>


### [226] [Redakto - The Incognito Tab for LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18260)
*Saurav Kumar Saha,Tom Röhr,Felix Bießmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Redakto, an open-source tool for PII redaction and pseudonymization to enable privacy-preserving LLM usage, with local deployment and APIs; empirical evaluation shows maintained task utility in legal and medical text.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Growing privacy concerns and EU regulations hinder LLM adoption; there is a need for reliable preprocessing to remove or obfuscate PII before LLM input.

Method: Developed Redakto with redaction and pseudonymization capabilities, exposed via a web app, REST APIs, and MCP hooks; designed for local deployment with modest compute requirements; evaluated on legal and medical domain data.

Result: Anonymized texts retain utility scores comparable to original texts across evaluated tasks, demonstrating minimal negative impact on downstream LLM performance.

Conclusion: Redakto enables privacy-friendly LLM workflows, offering accessible end-user and developer interfaces, and can be deployed locally, aligning with regulatory privacy needs while preserving task performance.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are being increasingly used in everyday applications. A major challenge in the context of LLMs or Artificial Intelligence (AI) in general is to ensure privacy when using them, meaning that personally identifiable information (PII) is removed from any text that enters an LLM. These challenges have become more urgent with novel EU legislation. Uncertainty around LLM usage with respect to privacy concerns in EU countries can be a major blocker for the speed of innovation and transfer from research to applications. Here we present \textbf{Redakto}, a tool that can be used for anonymizing text prior to feeding it to an LLM or other downstream text processing. We provide state-of-the-art functionalities for both redaction of PII but also when used for pseudonymization. These functionalities are exposed such that they can easily be used by end-users, through the Redakto web application, and by developers and researchers, via REST APIs and model context protocol (MCP) hooks. The implementation is fully open source, requires modest compute resources, and can be readily deployed on local hardware. In contrast to prior work and in order to better assess the quality of the anonymized texts, we conduct extensive empirical evaluations on textual data from legal and medical domain with respect to both privacy and utility of the redacted texts. Our empirical results demonstrate that the texts anonymized with different redaction strategies achieve utility scores on par with the original texts, suggesting that anonymization with Redakto can be used for LLM tasks without substantial negative impact for the tasks we explored.

</details>


### [227] [Cacheable by Design? Training Mixture-of-Experts Routers for Locality Against the Edge Memory-Bandwidth Wall: A Pre-Registered Negative Result with a Systems Measurement Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18261)
*Shriniwas Ramesh Suram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: MoE inference on a single 8 GB GPU is bottlenecked by memory bandwidth rather than compute; edge storage and routing induce paging and caching challenges that persist with scale; training for cacheability under strict perplexity constraints fails, though cache-aware rerouting plus locality training reduces misses substantially; multi-domain evaluation reveals a real tax when scaling; code and traces released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation:  quantify memory-bandwidth bottlenecks in large MoE models on commodity hardware, and evaluate whether cacheability can be learned without harming perplexity; provide a rigorous, pre-registered multi-domain benchmark and edge-serving telemetry.

Method: Measure decode throughput and paging behavior on Qwen3-235B (134 GB) and Qwen3-30B; build llama-moe-trace to instrument routing; analyze adjacent-token reuse, expert coverage, and LRU cache efficacy; pre-register training of 137M MoE LMs with locality and router losses; assess miss rate versus perplexity; compare against StickyMoE; extend to 340M-rung; develop cache-aware rerouting with trained locality and test domain-primed prefetching; release code and traces.

Result: Decoding throughput is 0.44 tokens/s warm for Qwen3-235B with bandwidth-based pacing; batch 32 fails due to paging thrash; adjacent-token expert reuse ~2x; 95% of traffic uses 52.5% of experts; 13.4% LRU covers 66% of requests; misses cut by up to 60% with 99% static-hit but perplexity gate (≤1%) cannot be met; multi-domain tax observed at 137M; 340M rung tax persists; cache-aware rerouting with trained locality achieves ~80% miss reduction with ≤3.4% perplexity; domain-primed prefetching not beneficial; code, traces, and pre-registration released.

Conclusion: The bandwidth wall for MoE on consumer hardware is real and scale-related; learning cacheability under strict perplexity penalties is challenging, though practical gains come from cache-aware rerouting combined with locality; multi-domain evaluation captures the true cost of edge-serving; the work provides a valuable measurement suite and release for reproducibility.

Abstract: Serving a 235B-parameter Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) model on a single 8 GB GPU is bottlenecked not by compute but by memory bandwidth: decode must stream each token's active experts from whichever tier holds them, and on consumer hardware most experts sit on an SSD far slower than RAM. We quantify this bandwidth wall on Qwen3-235B (Q4_K_M, 134 GB): measured decode is 0.44 tok/s warm, matching a bytes-per-token / bandwidth model, while a batching scheme that should amortize one disk sweep instead collapses at batch 32 from paging thrash. We build llama-moe-trace, a zero-surgery router-telemetry tool, and measure routing on Qwen3-30B: adjacent-token expert reuse is 2.0x chance, 95% of traffic uses 52.5% of experts, and an LRU cache of 13.4% of experts serves 66% of requests. We then ask whether cacheability is trainable: we pre-register training of 137M MoE language models with auxiliary locality and domain router losses, under joint criteria on cache-miss reduction and perplexity. The mechanism works (misses down up to 60%; a 99% static-pin hit rate) but every configuration fails the pre-registered <=1% perplexity gate -- miss reduction and quality are tightly coupled. Concurrent StickyMoE reports the same loss as near-free on single-domain sub-25M models; on multi-domain 137M we find the tax real. Our contribution is this pre-registered, stricter-criterion, multi-domain evaluation plus edge-serving measurements. A 340M rung shows the tax does not shrink with scale (it rises slightly). We further show training-free cache-aware rerouting stacks with trained locality -- together ~80% miss reduction at <=3.4% perplexity at both sizes, far cheaper than either alone -- while domain-primed prefetching does not help. All code, traces, and the pre-registration are released.

</details>


### [228] [Evaluating Structured Information Extraction with Open Models in a High Risk Public Sector Application](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18289)
*Elias Schubert,Felix Bießmann*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Open-source OCR/LLM/VLM pipelines struggle on a high-risk, real-world multi-step task; only 4/35 configurations achieve F1>0.5, with VLMs generally better but unreliably zero-shot; input quality and OCR structure preservation are critical.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Systematic evaluation of open-source extraction pipelines on realistic, high-stakes public-sector tasks is scarce, yet essential for responsible deployment under high-risk regimes like the EU AI Act.

Method: Benchmark of open-source OCR engines, LLMs, and VLMs on end-to-end extraction of student applications, tested in zero-shot settings; varied model scales; evaluation primarily via F1; analysis of input quality and OCR structural preservation on performance.

Result: Only 4 of 35 configurations achieve F1>0.5; the best OCR+LLM pipeline can match top VLM performance, but most OCR+LLM combos perform poorly; ~75% of configurations score below 0.25; model scale has a non-linear effect; input/structural quality of OCR output is a critical determinant.

Conclusion: Open-source tools still struggle on complex real-world high-risk tasks in zero-shot settings; VLMs outperform OCR+LLM in general but improvements are needed; increasing model size alone is not sufficient; preserving OCR structure and input quality is crucial; more robust evaluation and technique development (e.g., few-shot or structured prompts) are needed for reliable deployment.

Abstract: The extraction of structured information from unstructured documents represents a critical component of digital transformations in all sectors. While proprietary solutions dominate commercial applications, a rapidly growing ecosystem of open-source Optical Character Recognition (OCR) engines, Large Language Models (LLMs), and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) offers accessible alternatives. However, systematic evaluations on realistic, multi-step extraction pipelines remain scarce. Responsible usage of such extraction tools require comprehensive evaluations on realistic tasks, especially as these solutions will be key components of applications in the public sector that the EU AI act categorizes as high risk. To address this gap we present a comprehensive benchmark assessing the end-to-end performance of open-source systems on a complex real-world document processing task classified as high risk: Student applications for an international study program. We conduct a comprehensive empirical evaluation with state-of-the-art OCR engines, LLMs and VLMs. Our results reveal that while VLMs generally outperform OCR+LLM pipelines, even state-of-the-art open-source models struggle to handle such tasks reliably in zero-shot settings. Only 4 of 35 configurations achieved F1 scores above 0.5, with the best OCR+LLM pipeline matching top VLM performance, though most OCR+LLM combinations performed substantially worse. Roughly 75\% of all configurations scored below 0.25. Model scale influences performance, yet the relationship is non-linear: substantially larger models do not guarantee proportionally better results. Input quality, particularly the structural preservation of OCR output, emerges as a critical factor independent of downstream model capability.

</details>


### [229] [The Lifecycle of LLM-as-a-Judge for Large-Scale Recommendation Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18300)
*Emma Yanyang Kong,JJ Tan,Ishan Gupta,Lars Olds,Claire Campbell,David Fagnan,Veli Balin,Rohan Gosain,Louis Garcia,Minsu Jang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A production-ready, lifecycle-based framework for LLM judges (Birth, Training with RART, Deployment for quality gating and reflective generation, Monitoring with human-in-the-loop) applied to Netflix recommendations explanations, showing positive user engagement effects in a five-week A/B test without quality takedowns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond static, one-off evaluation by LLM judges and address the end-to-end lifecycle of judges operating in production as data and tasks evolve, enabling scalable, reliable assessment of user-facing AI explanations.

Method: Define multi-criteria evaluation and curated human-labeled benchmarks (Birth); refine rubrics via Reasoning-Aligned Rubric Tuning using a meta-judge on reasoning outputs (Training); deploy a single judge serving quality gating and reflective generation in production (Deployment); implement continuous drift detection and a human-in-the-loop review gate to trigger re-tuning (Monitoring).

Result: Post-launch five-week A/B test across tens of millions of members showed judge-aligned explanations increased exposure to previously unwatched content and improved browse-to-play success compared to a no-explanation control, with no quality-related takedowns.

Conclusion: Lifecycle-oriented, continuously maintained LLM judges are practical and beneficial for production user-facing explanations, enabling adaptation to data shifts and improving engagement while maintaining quality.

Abstract: LLM-as-a-Judge, which leverages a large language model to evaluate natural language generated by another AI application or model, has become a standard, scalable approach for accelerating and extending costly human evaluation. However, most work treats a judge as a static artifact, evaluating it once at construction or against a fixed benchmark. In contrast, we argue that an LLM judge running in a production system is better understood as having a lifecycle: it must be built, trained, deployed, and continuously maintained as the surrounding data evolves, and each phase poses distinct technical and operational challenges.
  We present such a lifecycle for the LLM judges that evaluate user-facing recommendation explanations at Netflix, where our pipeline generates and the judges assess hundreds of thousands of distinct show-level explanations per week, served across the mobile experience to millions of members. Our framework has four phases: (I) Birth, defining multiple evaluation criteria and building curated benchmark datasets with human labels and rationales; (II) Training, refining the judges' rubrics via Reasoning-Aligned Rubric Tuning (RART), a rubric-tuning procedure that uses a meta-judge over reasoning output as the learning signal; (III) Deployment, in which one judge serves two production roles: quality gating and reflective generation; and (IV) Monitoring, a continuous Human-in-the-Loop alignment process that detects drift and triggers re-tuning behind a human review gate. We report post-launch results from a five-week A/B test over tens of millions of members, in which the judge-aligned explanations shifted member viewing toward novel content (previously unwatched) and increased successful browse-to-play sessions relative to a no-explanation control, with no quality-related takedowns.

</details>


### [230] [SESSE: Sketch, Expand, Sort, Summarize, Evaluate -- LLM-as-Judge Evaluation via Structured Decomposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18303)
*Dae Lee,Mihai Delgeanu,Adel Youssef*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SESSE trains no models; decomposes holistic LLM-judge judgments into sub-questions (Sketch, Expand, Sort, Summarize, Evaluate) mined from judge errors; achieves near parity with chain-of-thought and competitive with 92.7% baselines; enables per-criterion evidence for interpretability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Holistic LLM judgments obscure which quality dimensions drive preferences; existing methods rely on chain-of-thought or fine-tuned judges; need training-free, interpretable evaluation.

Method: Identify sub-questions from judge error cases; apply the SESSE steps to generate structured judgments without oracle responses or task-specific rubrics; aggregate per-criterion votes.

Result: On RewardBench with n=1000, near parity with CoT baseline; competitive with RISE-Judge-32B (92.7%), while remaining fully training-free.

Conclusion: Per-criterion vote evidence yields interpretable audit trails for diagnosing label ambiguity and judge failure modes; improves diagnostic capability beyond single holistic outputs.

Abstract: LLM-as-judge evaluation reduces response quality assessment to a single holistic A/B preference choice, providing no mechanism to isolate which quality dimensions drove the preference or distinguish model errors from genuine label ambiguity. We propose SESSE (Sketch, Expand, Sort, Summarize, Evaluate), a training-free framework that decomposes holistic judgment into structured sub-questions mined directly from the judge's own error cases; requiring no oracle responses, task-specific rubrics, or fine-tuning. On RewardBench (n=1,000), SESSE achieves near-parity with the chain-of-thought baseline and is competitive with RISE-Judge-32B (92.7%), a fine-tuned specialist, while remaining fully training-free. Per-criterion vote evidence provides an interpretable audit trail for diagnosing label ambiguity and judge failure modes unavailable from a single holistic output token.

</details>


### [231] [ComponentBench: Diagnosing Component-Level Failures in Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18307)
*Tianchen Guan,Xinlei Lin,Royce Cheng-Yue,Xiangjun Wang,Shuyan Zhou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ComponentBench provides a library-agnostic benchmark and diagnostic pipeline for component-level UI interactions, introducing 97 canonical UI components and 2,910 programmatically verified tasks across popular libraries, with cleaned human reference trajectories and a pipeline to audit structural difficulty and failure analyses. Evaluation across seven models shows that observation/action-space design greatly impacts performance; within a shared harness, changing only the observation and action space can shift success by over 30%, and even the fastest setup is 3.7x slower than humans, highlighting persistent challenges in spatial manipulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridges the gap between long-horizon workflow benchmarks and atomic GUI tests by targeting the mid-layer of realistic, component-centered interactions that are short enough to diagnose but rich enough to reveal modern UI burdens.

Method: Construct a library-agnostic ontology of 97 canonical UI components instantiated as 2,910 tasks across widely used component libraries; pair with cleaned human reference trajectories; evaluate task success and interaction efficiency; develop a scalable pipeline to audit realized structural difficulty and synthesize structured failure analyses across tasks and component families; compare seven models under four observation/action spaces in a shared harness.

Result: Changing observation/action spaces shifts task success by >30% for the same model (e.g., GPT-5 mini: 83.1% with accessibility-tree observations to 48.9% with coordinate-only Pixel control); the fastest configuration is 3.7x slower than the human reference; spatial manipulations that are trivial for humans remain challenging for current agents.

Conclusion: Observation-space and action-space choices critically influence performance; component-level benchmarks reveal strengths/weaknesses not captured by high-level benchmarks and enable systematic diagnosis and improvement of AI agents interacting with modern UIs.

Abstract: Current evaluation of computer-use agents is split between long-horizon workflow benchmarks and atomic GUI-grounding tests. This leaves an under-instrumented middle layer: realistic component-centered interactions (e.g., toggle a button set) that are short enough to diagnose and rich enough to capture the burdens of modern interfaces. We present ComponentBench, a benchmark and diagnostic pipeline for component-level evaluation of computer-use agents on modern web UIs. ComponentBench is organized around a library-agnostic ontology of 97 canonical UI components instantiated as 2,910 programmatically verified tasks across widely used component libraries, paired with cleaned human reference trajectories that enable evaluation of both task success and interaction efficiency. Beyond task collection, we introduce a scalable pipeline for auditing realized structural difficulty after implementation and synthesizing structured failure analyses across tasks and component families. Evaluating seven models -- GPT-5.4, Gemini 3 Flash, GPT-5.4 mini, GPT-5 mini, Gemini 3.1 Flash-Lite, Qwen3-VL-235B, and UI-TARS-1.5-7B -- across four observation and action spaces, we show that these design choices critically impact performance. Within a single shared harness, changing only the observation and action space shifts task success by more than 30% for the same model: GPT-5 mini falls from 83.1% with accessibility-tree observations to 48.9% with coordinate-only Pixel control. Moreover, even the fastest configuration takes 3.7x as long as the matched human reference, and spatial manipulations that are trivial for humans continue to challenge current agents.

</details>


### [232] [Governance Records as Supervision: Verifier-Selected Self-Training for Structured Workflow Repair](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18324)
*Jesus Salas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Verifier-selected governance records enable reliable one-shot execution for bounded models; experiments show substantial plan-acceptance gains and superiority over baselines across multiple setups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Test whether machine-verifiable governance logs (records linking task contract, model attempt, verifier decision, and outcome) can supervise bounded models and consolidate expensive capabilities into reliable one-shot execution.

Method: Empirical evaluation using plan-generation and verification loops: PlanBench replanning tasks with Qwen3-14B thinking generating plans; VAL verifier assessing acceptance; comparisons against schema-valid adapters, interface-gate, and model/self-selection baselines; ablations on target selection; experiments on 80 unopened cases and 160 new cases; a Phi stronger-teacher arm; statistical comparison (p-values) of gains; evaluation of latency relative to thinking.

Result: Initial: 24 VAL-admitted plans on fresh cases. 80 unopened cases: VAL-accepted plans rise 1→57; 56 paired gains; 0 regressions; thinking metric reaches 30; adapter latency ~1/56 of thinking. 160 new cases: base 1, schema-selected 55, model-self-selected 69, VAL-selected 102 accepted plans; VAL > self-selection by net +33 (p ≈ 1.96e-6). Ablations: matched conditions show consistent gains; Phi stronger-teacher arm: accepted plans 2→51 and outputs 35→80. Overall results show verifier-selected supervision yields robust, scalable improvements in bounded, machine-checkable planning.

Conclusion: Verifier-selected supervision can govern bounded, machine-checkable capabilities, enabling reliable one-shot execution while avoiding unbounded self-improvement or arbitrary planning; the approach demonstrates teachability and stopping boundaries, with implications for creating robust, verifiable AI governance mechanisms.

Abstract: Machine-verifiable workflows produce governance records linking a task contract, model attempt, verifier decision, accepted output, and target origin. We test whether these records can supervise bounded models, consolidating occasional or expensive capability into reliable one-shot execution.
  On fresh, structure-disjoint PlanBench replanning cases, Qwen3-14B thinking generated 24 plans admitted by the independently authored VAL verifier. Those plans trained the same checkpoint for non-thinking execution, without oracle targets or a stronger teacher. On 80 unopened cases, VAL-accepted plans increased from 1 to 57, with 56 paired gains and zero regressions; thinking reached 30. The adapter was schema-valid on all cases and used approximately 1/56 of thinking's mean latency. The separate paired interface-cure gate did not pass.
  A matched ablation fixed the source cases, 52-candidate pool, 24-target count, model, recipe, and seed while changing target selection. On 160 new cases, base, schema-selected, model-self-selected, and VAL-selected execution reached 1, 55, 69, and 102 accepted plans. VAL exceeded self-selection by paired net +33 (p=0.0000019647), with gains in both difficulty strata. Independent semantic selection is therefore load-bearing relative to matched alternatives within this band.
  A complementary Phi stronger-teacher arm raised base Phi-4 from 2 to 51 accepted plans and from 35 to 80 schema-valid outputs. Earlier synthetic experiments establish teachability, cumulative learning, construction robustness, and stopping boundaries. The results support verifier-selected supervision for bounded, machine-checkable capabilities, not arbitrary planning, enterprise validity, or unrestricted self-improvement.

</details>


### [233] [Measuring the Partial-Credit Gap: A Strict Benchmark on Vietnam's 2025 Convex Marking Scheme](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18336)
*Nguyen Quoc Hung,Nguyen Dang Minh,Le Nhu Quynh,Tran Khanh Linh,Nguyen Kieu Linh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Non-additive convex grading alters model evaluation; standard accuracy overestimates ability on exams with official rubrics. THPT-Ladder aligns LM assessment with real grading, revealing significant ranking shifts and dependency on answer structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to ensure machine-exam evaluations reflect official, non-additive scoring schemes used by education ministries, preventing overestimation of partial knowledge.

Method: Introduce THPT-Ladder: 632 items from 21 official Vietnam exams across 11 subjects; grade strictly by official convex rubric; benchmarks across eight LM families; compare LM scores to human cohort marks (over a million candidates).

Result: Official grading yields 0.020–0.159 fewer points per Part II item than proportional credit; this penalty changes model rankings (e.g., Qwen3.5-27B History drops from 90th to 77th percentile). Accuracy alone fails to predict penalties; different error distributions yield substantially different scores under the same accuracy level; official marks depend on grouping of correct statements rather than simple counts.

Conclusion: Accuracy-based benchmarks can misrepresent LM competence on exams with non-additive scoring. THPT-Ladder provides a realistic, ministry-aligned evaluation that can reveal ranking shifts and test robustness to error patterns; imperative for trustworthy deployment in educational contexts.

Abstract: When evaluating language models on human exams, benchmarks typically score each response as right or wrong and report the overall accuracy. This approach assumes that partial knowledge is worth proportional credit, an assumption that fails when an examination uses a non-additive grading scheme. The 2025 reform of Vietnam's National High School Graduation Examination demonstrates the cost of this substitution. In Part II of the exam, candidates evaluate four true/false statements per question. The grading is convex: the number of correct statements earns 0, 0.10, 0.25, 0.50, or 1.00 points. Identifying three statements correctly pays 0.50 points, not the 0.75 points that standard accuracy metrics would award. Because Part II accounts for 4.00 of the exam's 10.00 points, reporting accuracy inflates the score by rewarding partial knowledge that the state explicitly penalizes. We introduce THPT-Ladder, a benchmark of 632 items from 21 official exams across 11 subjects, graded exactly as the ministry grades its students. The ministry publishes the marks of over a million candidates, allowing us to place models directly into the human cohort. Across eight models, the official rubric pays 0.020 to 0.159 points less per Part II question than proportional credit. This shortfall changes a model's apparent competence. For Qwen3.5-27B on the 2025 History exam, a 0.042-point shortfall drops its standing from the 90th to the 77th percentile among 481,293 candidates. A model's accuracy does not predict this penalty. At Claude Sonnet 5's accuracy level, different distributions of errors yield scores varying from 0.869 to 0.932 points per question. Official marks depend on how correct statements are grouped, meaning standard benchmarks report a competence the institution would not certify.

</details>


### [234] [A Jagged Frontier: Evaluating Robustness of Code Agents to Semantics-Preserving Transformations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18389)
*Hasan Najib Mahmud,Shreya Gupta,Isha Chaudhary,Nathaniel Enis,Ravi Mangal,Gagandeep Singh,Corina Pasareanu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Semantics-preserving perturbations (SPTs) slightly degrade AI code agent reliability; robustness is uneven across scaffolds and models, with no universal ranking; simpler scaffolds tend to be more robust, but even top frontier models can fail under SPTs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate how semantically equivalent code rewrites affect the reliability of AI-driven code repair agents, addressing deployment risks in diverse real-world codebases.

Method: Introduce a random variant sampler applying semantics-preserving transformations (SPTs)—control-flow rewrites, dead-code injection, and identifier renaming—to produce perturbed variants. Evaluate two agentic scaffolds (mini-SWE and OpenCode) backed by four frontier models (Claude Opus 4.5, Kimi K2.5, MiniMax M2.5, Qwen 3.6-27B) on instances from SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro. For each instance, run agents multiple times on unperturbed and perturbed variants to obtain paired resolve-rate estimates that separate perturbation effects from stochasticity.

Result: Across configurations, perturbations caused small degradation: up to 6.7 percentage-point drop in mean resolve-rate in the most affected cases; 6 of 16 model/scaffold/dataset configurations showed statistically significant degradation. Model robustness rankings varied by scaffold; Qwen was relatively robust with mini-SWE on SWE-bench Verified but highly brittle with OpenCode. The simpler mini-SWE scaffold generally yielded greater robustness than the OpenCode scaffold.

Conclusion: Semantics-preserving perturbations can affect the reliability of AI code agents, with non-uniform effects across models and scaffolds. This raises deployment reliability concerns for AI-driven code repair across diverse codebases and emphasizes the need to account for perturbations in robustness evaluation.

Abstract: AI code agents are increasingly deployed to resolve real software issues, yet their reliability under superficial code variations remains poorly understood. We evaluate whether coding agents that repair repository-level issues remain reliable when the surrounding codebase is rewritten into a semantically equivalent form. We introduce a random variant sampler that applies common semantics-preserving transformations (SPTs) - spanning control-flow rewrites, dead-code injection, and identifier renaming - to produce perturbed variants. We evaluate two agentic scaffolds (mini-SWE agent and OpenCode) each backed by one of four frontier models (Claude Opus 4.5, Kimi K2.5, MiniMax M2.5, and Qwen 3.6-27B) across instances drawn from SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro. For each instance, the agent is run multiple times on the unperturbed and perturbed variants, yielding paired resolve-rate estimates that isolate the perturbation effect from intrinsic stochasticity. We find small degradation in most configurations: up to 6.7 percentage points mean resolve-rate drop in the most affected configurations with statistically significant degradations in 6 of 16 configurations of model, scaffold, and dataset. Crucially, no single model ranking by robustness holds across scaffolds - Qwen is among the most robust under mini-SWE agent on SWE-bench Verified yet the most brittle under OpenCode - revealing a jagged robustness frontier. The simpler scaffold (mini-SWE agent) is more robust to perturbation. Our results demonstrate that even top frontier models are susceptible to semantics-preserving perturbations although the effect is not uniform, raising concerns about the deployment reliability of AI code agents in diverse real-world codebases.

</details>


### [235] [When Clean Signals Are Not Enough: Detecting Structural Ambiguity for Safe Wearable Stress Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18397)
*Saba A. Farahani,Hung Cao,Amir M. Rahmani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ICCM is a lightweight pre-inference monitor that detects subject-specific cross-signal coupling divergence to defer/abstain on uncertain windows in wearable stress classification; it identifies individual-specific failure patterns but offers limited safety guarantees.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the problem that wearable stress classifiers can achieve high average accuracy yet fail completely for some individuals due to structural ambiguity in multi-signal channels; to provide interpretable monitoring of unsupported physiology without retraining the classifier.

Method: Introduce the Individual Conformal Coupling Monitor (ICCM), a lightweight pre-inference monitor that quantifies subject-specific coupling divergence and routes each window to classify, defer, or abstain without retraining the downstream classifier. Evaluate on WESAD (N=15) and Stress-Predict (N=35). Analyze the relationship between ambiguity and accuracy via Pearson correlations; perform robustness checks with rank correlations; report changes in false-positive counts and the handling of Subject 14’s stress windows.

Result: Full-cohort Pearson correlations between ambiguity (coupling divergence) and accuracy are negative: r = -0.607, p = 0.016 (WESAD) and r = -0.412, p = 0.014 (Stress-Predict). Robustness analyses show rank correlations not significant, and the WESAD association disappears when Subject 14 is removed. ICCM reduces false positives from 29→27 (WESAD) and 94→92 (Stress-Predict); neither reduction is statistically significant. ICCM withholds 3 of Subject 14’s 21 stress windows but does not repair the missed-stress failure.

Conclusion: ICCM provides an interpretable signal of unsupported physiology and individual failure rather than a stand-alone safety guarantee; it helps identify when a classifier may be unreliable for a given subject but does not fully resolve per-subject failures.

Abstract: Wearable stress classifiers can achieve strong average performance while failing completely for a particular individual. On WESAD, a Random Forest reaches 93.0% mean accuracy yet yields F1 = 0 for Subject 14, whose cross-signal coupling weakens near stress onset. We call this structural ambiguity: individually plausible physiological channels form an inter-signal pattern that is poorly supported by the person's non-stress reference. We introduce the Individual Conformal Coupling Monitor (ICCM), a lightweight and transparent pre-inference monitor that quantifies subject-specific coupling divergence and routes each window to classify, defer, or abstain without retraining the downstream classifier. Across WESAD (N = 15) and Stress-Predict (N = 35), full-cohort Pearson associations between ambiguity and accuracy are negative (r = -0.607, p = 0.016; r = -0.412, p = 0.014). Robustness analyses temper this finding: rank correlations are not significant, and the WESAD association disappears when Subject 14 is removed. ICCM changes false-positive counts from 29 to 27 and 94 to 92, although neither paired change is significant. It withholds 3 of Subject 14's 21 stress windows but does not repair the missed-stress failure. These results position ICCM as an interpretable signal of unsupported physiology and individual failure, rather than a stand-alone safety guarantee.

</details>


### [236] [Improving Natural-Language Combinatorial-Optimization Accuracy in Resource-Constrained Language Models via Formal Abstractions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18409)
*Shrenil Shaun Sharma,Avi Sharma*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SDDL is a neuro-symbolic framework that converts natural-language scheduling problems into compact solver-ready representations, enabling resource-constrained models to achieve higher feasibility by delegating search to a deterministic compiler and external solver.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Combinatorial scheduling is intractable for language models, and resource-constrained LMs struggle to preserve feasibility when solving from natural language.

Method: Translate natural-language scheduling problems into compact representations of tasks, resources, constraints, and objectives; delegate low-level modeling and search to a deterministic compiler and external solver; express problem structure rather than generating solutions.

Result: On a 300-instance multi-family subset, SDDL improves independently verified feasibility for resource-constrained models; top configurations reach 55.3% and 28.3% feasibility, vs direct-generation baselines of 23.7% and 1.3% and solver-code baselines of 21.7% and 7.0%; 0.0% median optimality gap among feasible schedules.

Conclusion: Expressing problem structure rather than generating solutions enables smaller models to approach the performance of larger frontier models and solver-backed configurations.

Abstract: Combinatorial scheduling poses a significant challenge for language models, requiring them to identify feasible solutions within exponentially large search spaces while satisfying complex constraints. This challenge is especially pronounced in resource-constrained settings, where larger language models are impractical and selection is limited to smaller models which often fail to preserve feasibility when scheduling directly from natural language. To address these limitations, we introduce SDDL, a neuro-symbolic framework that translates natural-language scheduling problems into compact, solver-aligned representations of tasks, resources, constraints, and objectives, while delegating low-level modeling and search to a deterministic compiler and external solver. On a 300-instance, multi-family subset of scheduling problems, SDDL improves independently verified feasibility for every resource-constrained model tested. The two strongest SDDL configurations reach 55.3% and 28.3%, up from direct-generation baselines of 23.7% and 1.3% and solver-code baselines of 21.7% and 7.0%, with a 0.0% median optimality gap among feasible schedules. By expressing problem structure rather than generating solutions or solver code, SDDL enables smaller models to approach the strongest evaluated direct- and solver-code configurations, including substantially larger frontier models.

</details>


### [237] [FM-Bench: A Benchmark for Long-Horizon Management with Competing Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18423)
*Tianyou Wang,Chongyang Gao,Kezhen Chen,Chen Dong,Yinghao He,Donghan Li,Wangcheng Xu,Hongjiu Zhang,Chi Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FM-Bench evaluates long-horizon LLM agent decision-making in a football-management sim; shows managerial behavior matters more than raw compute; Claude-fable-5 leads on average, but rankings are unstable across scenarios; best human-performance placement occurs late; code available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify sustained decision-making under dynamic environments and repeated actions, addressing the gap in measuring LLM agents beyond single-shot tasks.

Method: Two settings (solo and Arena) with 15 frontier models tested over a 20-year horizon in a deterministic football-management world. Each run involves ~340–400 decision points, 26 tools, and activities such as squad building, trades, contracts, facilities, youth, lineups, and board interactions. A scripted baseline and an anchor enable head-to-head comparisons. Performance is evaluated across three seeds on six behavioral capabilities, with no human judge.

Result: All models complete the horizon; scripted baselines often fail; Claude-fable-5 leads on mean score and in Arena. Model ranks are volatile across seeds and setups; scale/price/vendor do not predict order; final rankings emerge late in the horizon; best early-play human ranks near bottom. Improvements in performance correlate with managerial behavior: delaying slow-payoffs near end, keeping cash invested, and renewing ahead of deadlines. Token spend shows no predictive value; memory strategies (archive-only growth vs. replan-every-season) fail in opposite ways; models do not learn the market’s hidden prices from rejected bids.

Conclusion: Long-horizon LLM governance in dynamic environments is driven more by strategic/memory/planning behavior than raw computation power. FM-Bench provides a scalable framework to diagnose and compare long-horizon decision-making in LLM agents; results highlight the need for robust planning/memory mechanisms and emphasize practical managerial strategies (cash management, timely renewals) to sustain performance over extended horizons. Code is publicly available for replication.

Abstract: Language model agents now execute bounded tasks reliably. Whether they can sustain effective decision-making over long horizons, where actions have cumulative consequences and the environment responds to their choices, remains largely unmeasured. FM-Bench (Football Management Benchmark) measures this. An LLM agent runs a football club for 20 in-game years through 26 tools and roughly 340 to 400 decision stops. It drafts a squad on the same budget as every rival, trades players, negotiates contracts, invests in facilities and youth, sets lineups, and answers to a board that can fire it, while a deterministic engine accumulates every year into one final score with no LLM judge or human rater. The solo track plays each of 15 frontier models against a frozen scripted world, and the Arena places the same models plus a scripted anchor in one shared 20-year world; to our knowledge, the first head-to-head evaluation at this scale. We measure six behavioral capabilities behind the score. Across three seeds, all 15 models complete every horizon while the blind scripted baselines die out in most of theirs, and claude-fable-5 tops the solo board on mean score and the Arena, where the title nonetheless rotates among ten models. Neither scale, price, nor vendor predicts the order; the order settles only late in the horizon, and the best first-play human lands only at the bottom of the model board. What separates the models is managerial behavior rather than computation. Higher-scoring models reduce slow-payoff investment near the end, keep cash invested rather than idle, and open renewals well before the deadline, while token spend predicts nothing. No model learns the market's hidden prices from hundreds of rejected bids, and self-managed memory fails in two opposite modes: an archive that only grows or a plan rewritten every season. Code is available at https://github.com/Analogy-AI/fm-bench.

</details>


### [238] [UMER: Unifying Embedding and Ranking via Pair-Aware Discriminative Reasoning for Universal Multimodal Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18504)
*Libiao Chen,Xiyang Liu,Yanheng Wei,Tao Wang,Zhenyu Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: UMER introduces a Unified Multimodal Embedding and Ranking framework for universal multimodal retrieval that combines Pair-Aware Discriminative Reasoning with jointly learned contrastive embeddings and discriminative ranking, guided by mutual distillation, achieving state-of-the-art on MMEB-V2 with budget-adjustable inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses weaknesses of existing CoT-based embedding approaches that reason item-wise in isolation and lack explicit evidence to distinguish positives from hard negatives; also tackles the mismatch between global contrastive similarity and tasks requiring explicit pairwise relevance judgments such as verification and fine-grained reasoning.

Method: Proposes Pair-Aware Discriminative Reasoning that compares query–candidate pairs to extract instruction-relevant matching and discrepancy evidence; jointly learns contrastive embeddings for efficient global matching and discriminative ranking for explicit pairwise relevance within a single MLLM; employs a mutual distillation strategy to transfer reliable pairwise preferences between embedding and ranking components; supports budget-adjustable inference.

Result: On the MMEB-V2 benchmark, UMER achieves state-of-the-art performance under comparable experimental settings and supports budget-adjustable inference for efficient deployment.

Conclusion: UMER demonstrates that combining pair-aware reasoning with jointly trained contrastive embeddings and discriminative ranking within a unified MLLM, reinforced by mutual distillation, yields both strong global retrieval and precise pairwise relevance judgments for universal multimodal retrieval.

Abstract: Universal multimodal retrieval aims to support diverse instruction-aware retrieval tasks, demanding both efficient corpus-scale matching and fine-grained semantic reasoning. Recent MLLM-based embedding methods typically derive representations from hidden states, while Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning is emerging as a promising strategy for embedding enhancement by encoding intermediate semantic evidence into the representation space. However, existing CoT methods typically use item-wise reasoning over queries and candidates in isolation, providing no explicit evidence to distinguish a positive from a semantically confusable hard negative. Moreover, contrastive embeddings capture global similarity but struggle with meta-tasks requiring answer verification, category judgment or fine-grained reasoning. In this paper, we propose UMER, a Unified Multimodal Embedding and Ranking framework for universal multimodal retrieval. UMER replaces item-wise reflection with Pair-Aware Discriminative Reasoning, which compares query--candidate pairs to identify instruction-relevant matching and discrepancy evidence. UMER jointly learns contrastive embeddings for efficient global matching and discriminative ranking for explicit pairwise relevance judgment within a single MLLM. A complementary mutual distillation strategy further transfers reliable pairwise preferences between the embedding and ranking functions. On the MMEB-V2 benchmark, UMER achieves state-of-the-art performance under comparable experimental settings while supporting budget-adjustable inference.

</details>


### [239] [Which Negatives Matter? Ask Your Text Encoder: Adaptive Similarity Margins for Dense-Caption Retrieval](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18521)
*Haoyue Liu,Ye Chen,Zhichao Wang,Xiaoying Tang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HN-CLIP introduces per-negative adaptive margins derived from the text-text geometry of the caption encoder to address near-duplicate negatives in dense-caption retrieval, achieving consistent accuracy gains and faster training without extra data or parameters.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: InfoNCE-based contrastive fine-tuning saturates early on dense-caption benchmarks due to many near-duplicate negatives, limiting learning efficiency and performance.

Method: Compute a detached caption-similarity matrix from the text encoder and add it as per-negative margins to the negative logits during training. This avoids mining, synthesis, or resampling, uses a single matrix, and incurs masked logit additions with no extra data, parameters, offline preprocessing, or inference-time overhead.

Result: On four dense-caption retrieval benchmarks, HN-CLIP yields +2.4–+4.3 R@1 improvements, trains 2.4× faster than GOAL and 5.4× faster than StructXLIP, improves all six fine-tuning frameworks on in-domain data, and matches the strongest full-data baseline with only 20% of the training data.

Conclusion: A simple, data-efficient objective that leverages text-text geometry to reweight negatives improves effectiveness and efficiency across dense-caption retrieval settings without additional costs.

Abstract: Dense-caption retrieval has recently been improved by introducing segmentation, edge maps, LLM-filtered captions, and cross-modal modules into contrastive fine-tuning. However, these methods largely inherit the same InfoNCE objective, whose optimization can prematurely saturate under a strong pre-trained initialization: on dense captions, the loss falls below 10^{-3} on 80% of batches within the first epoch, while its gradient reaches exact zero in fp32 in 47% of measurements. We find that this behavior is closely related to the large number of near-duplicate captions in dense-caption benchmarks, where a few highly similar negatives remain unresolved after the easy majority has already been separated. As a remedy, we introduce HN-CLIP, which uses the text encoder's own text-text geometry to construct per-negative adaptive similarity margins. Specifically, a detached caption-similarity matrix is added to the negative logits, assigning larger margins to more similar captions without mining, synthesizing, or resampling negatives. The resulting objective requires only one caption-similarity matrix and a masked logit addition during training, with no auxiliary data, additional parameters, offline preprocessing, or inference-time overhead. Extensive experiments on four dense-caption retrieval benchmarks show that HN-CLIP improves over the strongest competitors by +2.4--+4.3 R@1 while training 2.4x faster than GOAL and 5.4x faster than StructXLIP. Moreover, the proposed objective improves all six tested fine-tuning frameworks on the in-domain benchmarks and reaches the strongest full-data baseline with only 20% of the training data.

</details>


### [240] [Pairwise Ranking Outperforms Single-Action RL for Offline Explanation Selection: A Practical Lesson](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18531)
*Tanay Chowdhury,Saeideh Shahrokh Esfahani*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two-stage explainable-recommendation: precompute explanations in a pooled pool; runtime selector on CPU chooses explanations to meet sub-100 ms latency and low cost; LambdaRank outperforms RL-based methods; KG-path selectors achieve perfect or near-perfect UX scores; end-to-end cost ~$15 on commodity hardware.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce LLM serving cost and latency in industrial explainable recommender systems by decoupling generation from selection and caching explanations.

Method: Generate explanations in advance using six prompt styles on two commodity LLMs to form a frozen candidate pool; at request time, a CPU-resident selector picks one explanation. Evaluate six offline-pool selectors (LambdaRank, PPO, GRPO, DPO, teacher-student distillation) and three KG-path selectors (random walks, edge-disjoint enumeration, MMR-reranked paths). Use BERTScore-F1 for offline pool evaluation aligned with XRec and G-Refer; USR for KG-path; two datasets (Google Local subset and MovieLens-1M) with a 5-seed evaluation; cross-dataset check with Claude versions. End-to-end cost reported on commodity hardware.

Result: LambdaRank yields F1=0.500 on Google Local and 0.329 on MovieLens-1M (seed variance <0.003). RL-based single-action policies underperform due to underutilized labels (K-1 labels). KG-path methods achieve USR=1.000 on Google Local and ~0.997–1.000 on MovieLens-1M, with per-request grounding yielding unique outputs and avoiding template-collapse. Generator-pool studies show minimal F1 shifts (0.001–0.006) across Claude 3 Haiku vs Haiku 4.5. End-to-end build cost ≈ $15 on commodity hardware; latency <100 ms without GPUs.

Conclusion: A decoupled two-stage design—precomputed explanation pools plus fast runtime selection—delivers fast, low-cost explainable recommendations while preserving competitive ranking performance and stable outputs. It enables independent evaluation of generator quality and selector strategies and shows strong robustness across seeds and prompts, with KG-grounded paths offering reliable per-query outputs.

Abstract: Industrial explainable-recommendation systems built on LLMs incur a substantial serving cost: each request triggers an LLM generation, with latency in the hundreds of milliseconds and cost that scales linearly with traffic. We separate generation from selection: explanations are produced ahead of time as a frozen candidate pool (six prompt styles, two commodity LLMs), and a small CPU-resident selector picks one at request time. The stack needs no GPU and returns in under 100 ms.
  Our primary benchmark is a 2,958-pair XRec Google Local subset, evaluating six offline-pool selectors (LambdaRank, PPO, GRPO, DPO, teacher-student distillation) and three KG-path selectors (random walks, edge-disjoint enumeration, MMR-reranked paths). A 300-pair MovieLens-1M split with Claude-Sonnet-4.5 references serves as an internal cross-dataset check, since no public benchmark exists for this setting. All variants use the same BERTScore-F1 protocol as XRec and G-Refer, averaged across five seeds.
  LambdaRank reaches F1 = 0.500 on Google Local, exceeding both G-Refer and XRec, and F1 = 0.329 on the MovieLens-1M check. With seed variance below 0.003 F1, the ordering is reliable: pairwise learning-to-rank outperforms single-action RL (PPO, GRPO, DPO), which use only one labelled candidate per rollout, leaving K-1 labels unused.
  The KG-path family targets a different objective: all three variants reach USR = 1.000 on Google Local and 0.997-1.000 on MovieLens-1M, since per-request path grounding yields a unique output per query, avoiding template-collapse failures affecting cached-LLM outputs.
  A generator-pool study comparing Claude 3 Haiku and Claude Haiku 4.5 shows small F1 shifts (0.001-0.006) while preserving selector ranking: selector and generator can be evaluated independently, though absolute F1 depends on the generator. End-to-end build cost is near $15 on commodity hardware.

</details>


### [241] [FinRCA-Bench: Benchmarking Evidence Retrieval and Reasoning for Financial AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18534)
*Pratik Ghawate*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FinRCA-Bench demonstrates that retrieval quality, not reasoning, largely determines LLM-based financial reconciliation performance; a robust evaluation must separate evidence retrieval from reasoning to avoid conflating the two.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To assess whether LLM-based diagnostic reasoning in finance truly reflects reasoning ability or is driven by access to the right evidence, given that financial reconciliation evidence is distributed across many relational sources and lacks textual similarity cues.

Method: Construct FinRCA-Bench, a deterministic synthetic benchmark with 2,250 accounts-payable-to-bank reconciliation cases over 14 operational tables, including 1,500 injected failures across 15 causal categories and 750 hard-negative cases. Hide root-cause labels and evidence contracts from the model to evaluate retrieval independently. Compare retrieval methods (Rules/SQL, classical ML, dense semantic retrieval, deterministic relational expansion, and TPGR) and measure retrieval impact on macro recall and exact 16-class accuracy, alongside error-type decomposition.

Result: Rules/SQL: 84.97% held-out exact accuracy; classical ML: 95.44% accuracy. By varying only retrieval while keeping reasoning prompt/gen settings fixed, macro recall rose from 0.83% to 77.70% and 16-class accuracy from 2.05% to 72.44%. Structural retrieval failures outnumbered reasoning failures by 95 to 15. 254 correct predictions occur despite incomplete retrieval; strict evidence-contract accuracy is only 5.72%.

Conclusion: Retrieval architecture is a dominant driver of AI-system performance in FinRCA-Bench; a correct root-cause label is a weak proxy for an auditable diagnosis, highlighting the need to separate and evaluate retrieval quality independently from reasoning in finance-focused LLM systems.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to support financial operations, but their apparent reasoning performance can depend on whether they receive the right evidence. In financial reconciliation, the evidence needed for diagnosis is distributed across invoices, purchase orders, approvals, allocations, payments, ledger entries, and bank activity, linked by transactional relationships rather than textual similarity. End-to-end accuracy can therefore conflate evidence access with reasoning quality. We introduce FinRCA-Bench, a deterministic synthetic benchmark of 2,250 accounts-payable-to-bank reconciliation cases spanning 14 operational tables, including 1,500 injected failures across 15 causal categories and 750 legitimate or hard-negative cases. Root-cause labels and record-level evidence contracts are hidden from the model, allowing retrieval to be evaluated independently of answer correctness. We compare Rules/SQL, classical machine learning, dense semantic retrieval, deterministic relational expansion, and Typed Provenance Graph Retrieval (TPGR), a typed traversal restricted to persisted transaction relationships. Rules/SQL reaches 84.97% held-out exact accuracy and classical ML reaches 95.44%. Holding the reasoning model, prompt, and generation settings fixed while changing only retrieval increases macro required-record recall from 0.83% to 77.70% and exact 16-class accuracy from 2.05% to 72.44%. Structural retrieval failures outnumber reasoning failures with sufficient retrieval by 95 to 15; 254 correct predictions occur despite incomplete retrieval, and strict returned-evidence contract accuracy is only 5.72%. On FinRCA-Bench, retrieval architecture strongly shapes observed AI-system performance, and a correct root-cause label is a weak proxy for an auditable diagnosis.

</details>


### [242] [Bridging Search and CRM: Productionizing AI Product Research Agents for Customer Re-Engagement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18543)
*Mandar Kulkarni,Pooja A.,Samir Shah*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A scalable production framework that bridges search and CRM via AI Product Research Agents to re-engage exploratory users on WhatsApp, showing CTR uplift and measurable GMV impact in a 23-day deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional e-commerce pipelines operate in silos (search, recommendations, CRM) and fail to proactively re-engage users with exploratory intents who may move research offline; there is a need for end-to-end, proactive customer journey optimization.

Method: Grounded multi-agent product research leveraging behavioral signals, external knowledge, and enterprise catalog data to identify users with exploratory intent and low engagement; generates personalized product recommendations and delivers them through WhatsApp within a production deployment.

Result: In a 23-day deployment (~15k WhatsApp notifications), the approach achieved substantial CTR improvements over traditional campaigns, observed secondary engagement via forwarding/sharing, and generated downstream purchases and GMV impact, validating practical effectiveness.

Conclusion: AI Product Research Agents can effectively bridge search and CRM to enable proactive customer re-engagement and end-to-end optimization of the customer journey in a scalable, production-ready system.

Abstract: Modern e-commerce platforms often operate search, recommendation, personalization, and CRM systems independently, limiting opportunities for proactive customer re-engagement. This is particularly challenging for exploratory intents such as best smartphones or latest 5G phones, where users may leave the platform for external research before purchasing. We present a scalable, production-deployed framework that bridges search and CRM workflows through AI-powered Product Research Agents. The system identifies users with exploratory purchase intent and low engagement, conducts grounded multi-agent product research using behavioral signals, external knowledge, and enterprise catalog data, and delivers personalized recommendations through WhatsApp. We evaluate the framework in a 23-day production deployment involving approximately 15K WhatsApp notifications for mobile product discovery. The campaign achieved substantial CTR improvements over traditional WhatsApp recommendation campaigns, with evidence of secondary engagement through message forwarding and sharing. The deployment also generated downstream purchases and GMV impact, demonstrating the practical effectiveness of AI Product Research Agents for proactive customer re-engagement and end-to-end customer journey optimization.

</details>


### [243] [FACET: Preserving Source Intent and Executable State in Terminal Task Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18580)
*Kou Shi,Zun Wang,Qisheng Su,Shiting Huang,Ziao Zhang,Zhen Fang,Qingnan Ren,Jin Liu,Yu Zeng,Yiming Zhao,Lin Chen,Zehui Chen,Feng Zhao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FACET enables scalable terminal-task synthesis by preserving source intent and grounding all artifacts in a shared executable state, addressing cross-artifact inconsistencies and preserving constraints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current terminal-task synthesis suffers when artifacts (instruction, environment, solution, verifier) are created under inconsistent assumptions or when multi-stage synthesis discards essential goals and constraints, leading to unsolvable tasks or mis-evaluations.

Method: Reconstruct related agent skills into information-rich, coherent scenarios; realize and repair the execution environment; generate final task artifacts with a shared container state that grounds instruction, solution, and verifier; use execution-based validation and targeted repair to fix artifact-specific failures without regenerating valid components.

Result: Produces complex terminal tasks with dense executable checks; trajectories from these tasks provide data-efficient supervision; multi-scale fine-tuning yields improvements on Terminal-Bench 2.1; analysis supports environment-grounded construction for task validity and solution-verifier alignment.

Conclusion: Source-intent preservation and shared executable-state grounding are key principles for scalable terminal-task synthesis.

Abstract: Training terminal agents requires scalable executable supervision, yet synthesizing high-quality terminal tasks remains challenging. Each task couples an instruction, an initialized environment, a reference solution, and an executable verifier; if these artifacts are generated from inconsistent assumptions, the resulting task may be unsolvable or incorrectly evaluated. Meanwhile, multi-stage synthesis can discard the goals, dependencies, state transitions, and procedural constraints encoded in the original sources. We present FACET (Fine-grained Agentic Construction of Executable Tasks), a framework that addresses both information preservation and cross-artifact consistency. FACET reconstructs related agent skills into coherent, information-rich scenarios, then realizes and repairs the execution environment before generating the final task artifacts. The resulting container state serves as shared grounding for the instruction, solution, and verifier, while execution-based validation and targeted repair correct artifact-specific failures without unnecessarily regenerating valid components. FACET produces complex terminal tasks with dense executable checks, and successful trajectories collected from these tasks provide effective, data-efficient supervision. Fine-tuning models across multiple scales consistently improves performance on Terminal-Bench 2.1, while analyses of alternative generation schemes support the importance of environment-grounded construction for task validity and solution-verifier alignment. These results establish source-intent preservation and shared executable-state grounding as key principles for scalable terminal-task synthesis.

</details>


### [244] [Can a Lightweight Multimodal Model Estimate LLM Reasoning Performance? A Study for Compute-Optimal Document Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18591)
*Zishan Ahmad,Vishal Vaddina*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BudgetDoc provides a multimodal benchmark and a dynamic budget allocator DRB to optimize inference budgets for document tasks, achieving strong predictive performance (0.753 weighted F1) and cost reductions across multiple models and datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Uniform budget allocation for LLM inference in document tasks is expensive and can over-allocate thinking; a principled estimator is needed to map budgets to expected performance and guide dynamic allocation.

Method: Introduce BudgetDoc as a multimodal benchmark with explicit supervision for budget-performance trade-offs. Train DRB (Document-Reasoning Balancer), a ~1B-parameter pre-flight estimator (SigLIP-2 + Qwen3-0.6B) predicting ordinal performance across budget levels. Evaluate dynamic budget allocation across five frontier models and three datasets, comparing to always-maximum-budget baselines.

Result: DRB achieves 0.753 weighted F1 on budget prediction. In 9 of 15 configurations, dynamic budgeting matches or improves F1 relative to always-maximum-budget baselines while substantially reducing cost. Preliminary evidence suggests DRB may generalize to cross-model selection.

Conclusion: DRB demonstrates that budget-aware inference can maintain or improve performance while reducing computational cost in multimodal document tasks, and shows promise for generalization to model selection across models.

Abstract: Uniformly allocating inference reasoning budgets to LLMs is expensive and prone to over-thinking penalties; especially in document tasks where visual layouts drive complexity. To address this, we introduce BudgetDoc, the first multimodal benchmark providing explicit supervision for model-budget-performance trade-offs across three document tasks. Using BudgetDoc, we train DRB (Document-Reasoning Balancer), an approx. 1B-parameter pre-flight estimator (SigLIP-2 + Qwen3-0.6B) that predicts ordinal model performance across budget levels, achieving a 0.753 weighted F1. When dynamically allocating reasoning budgets across five frontier models and three datasets, DRB matches or improves F1 scores compared to always-maximum-budget baselines in 9 of 15 configurations while drastically reducing cost. Finally, preliminary evaluations demonstrate DRB's potential to generalize to cross-model selection.

</details>


### [245] [CTIFoundry: An Agent-Native Corpus Scaffold for Cyber Threat Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18613)
*Yutong Cheng,Changze Li,Qian Cui,Wei Ding,Lingzhi Wang,Yan Chen,Peng Gao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CTIFoundry provides an agent-native corpus scaffold for cyber threat intelligence, structuring four knowledge bases into a typed ontology and grounding reports, enabling seven tools and three skills; it improves multi-model agent F1 on CTIConnect, with gains primarily from the structured ontology and skills, which combine super-additively.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CTI analytics for LLM agents is hampered by opaque, unstructured corpora; to enable reliable, tool-driven investigations, an agent-centric corpus with explicit structure and provenance is needed.

Method: At build time, CTIFoundry constructs a deterministic ontology graph over four KBs (CVE, CWE, CAPEC, ATT&CK) with typed cross-references as edges; adds a span-grounded report layer with canonical/alias-resolved cross-vendor entities that index provenance-carrying chunks; uses hybrid dense+lexical retrieval. At query time, exposes seven typed tools and three procedural skills on an open-source agent harness.

Result: On CTIConnect, swapping only the action surface lifts the identically-harnessed agent by +0.19 to +0.28 F1 across a four-model, two-provider panel; a small model on CTIFoundry surpasses a flagship on the flat substrate; gains occur with roughly half the tool calls for Claude models; ablations show typed structure accounts for the majority of gain, procedural skills translate structure into disciplined queries, and they combine super-additively.

Conclusion: Typed structure provides the majority of performance gains; procedural skills convert structure into discipline, and together they yield super-additive improvements due to their complementary coupling.

Abstract: Cyber threat intelligence (CTI) is increasingly consumed not by human analysts but by LLM agents that compose multi-step investigations at query time. The harness side of this shift has matured rapidly (planning loops, tool protocols, context management), but the corpus side has not: threat reports and vulnerability databases are still packaged for retrieval-augmented generation, as opaque chunks behind an embedding index. We argue that this substrate, not model capability, is the bottleneck on agentic CTI investigation, and present CTIFoundry, an agent-native corpus scaffold. At build time, CTIFoundry materializes the latent structure of a CTI corpus: a deterministic ontology graph over four authoritative knowledge bases (CVE, CWE, CAPEC, ATT&CK) whose official cross-references become typed, traversable edges; a span-grounded report layer whose canonical, alias-resolved cross-vendor entities index provenance-carrying chunks; and hybrid dense+lexical retrieval surfaces. At query time this structure is exposed through seven typed tools and three procedural skills mounted on a stock open-source agent harness. On the public CTIConnect benchmark, swapping only the action surface lifts the identically-harnessed agent by +0.19 to +0.28 overall F1 across a four-model, two-provider panel: a small model on CTIFoundry surpasses a flagship on the flat substrate, and the gain is not bought with search effort, since on both Claude models the scaffolded agent is more accurate at roughly half the tool calls. An ablation attributes it: typed structure carries the larger share, procedural skills convert structure into discipline, and the two compose super-additively, because skills bind only to structure that exists.

</details>


### [246] [Preference Reasoning under Indeterminacy in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18631)
*Hadi Hosseini,Samarth Khanna,Xiyuan Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Indeterminacy in preference reasoning is central; LLMs fail to distinguish determined from undetermined cases across tasks, revealing miscalibrated reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As LLMs evolve into decision-making agents, reasoning over preferences is essential for alignment, coordination, and collective intelligence. Real-world preference reasoning is inherently indeterminate due to incomplete information and potential nonexistence of solutions.

Method: Formalize two forms of indeterminacy: epistemic (incomplete/expressive preferences) and structural (nonexistence of solutions under standard social choice concepts). Evaluate across a hierarchy of tasks using state-of-the-art language models to assess whether they distinguish determined vs undetermined instances, including verification settings.

Result: Models systematically fail to distinguish determined from undetermined instances, showing miscalibrated reasoning even in verification tasks.

Conclusion: Indeterminacy should be central to AI reasoning and evaluation; beyond correctness, practitioners must account for epistemic and structural indeterminacy in model design, alignment, and benchmarks.

Abstract: As large language models evolve into decision-making agents, the ability to reason over preferences becomes fundamental to alignment, coordination, and collective intelligence. Yet, unlike standard benchmarks, real-world preference reasoning is inherently indeterminate: information may be incomplete, and valid solutions may not exist. We argue that indeterminacy, rather than correctness alone, is a central challenge for AI reasoning. We formalize this challenge along two axes, (i) epistemic indeterminacy, arising from incomplete, partial, or expressive preferences, and (ii) structural indeterminacy, arising from the non-existence of solutions under standard social choice concepts. Across a hierarchy of tasks, we show that state-of-the-art language models systematically fail to distinguish between determined and undetermined instances, exhibiting miscalibrated reasoning even in verification settings.

</details>


### [247] [Candidate-Fate Accounting for Transparent Sensor Diagnostic Pipeline Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18665)
*Haotao Xie,Yutian Chen,Yangqi Liu,Xiaoyu Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Candidate-fate accounting records every observed AutoML/AutoDL candidate in diagnostic search, enabling auditability of legality, budget, and completeness beyond fitted-trial reports.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current AutoML/AutoDL reporting omits many candidates (invalid, pruned, skipped, cached, unfitted), hindering review of constraints, budgets, and alternatives in industrial sensor diagnostics.

Method: Introduce a candidate-level audit framework that (1) hashes candidates to merge duplicates, (2) flags legality of candidates, (3) records allocation rationales for budget decisions, and (4) uses a closed fate ledger to assign a single terminal fate per candidate; applied to bearing-diagnostic datasets.

Result: The framework detects invalid candidates and reveals 30–41 candidates omitted by traditional fitted-trial-only reports; closed fate records enable complete accounting while maintaining competitive diagnostic performance.

Conclusion: Candidate-fate accounting provides transparent, auditable search traces for AutoML/AutoDL in diagnostics, enabling verification of constraints and resources without sacrificing performance; code is openly available.

Abstract: Industrial sensor diagnostics relies on preprocessing, representation, and classification pipelines, making automated pipeline search useful for reducing manual design cost. However, existing automated machine/deep learning (AutoML/AutoDL) reports typically retain only fitted trials, scores, and winners, omitting generated candidates that are invalid, pruned, skipped, cached, or unfitted. This omission limits reviewers' ability to check signal constraints, budget use, and unevaluated legal alternatives. To address this, we propose candidate-fate accounting, a candidate-level audit framework for diagnostic search traces. It records each observed candidate as auditable evidence: hashes merge repeated observations, legality checks flag invalid candidates, allocation rationales explain budget decisions, and a closed fate ledger assigns one terminal fate to each candidate. Experiments on three bearing-diagnostic datasets show that the framework detects invalid candidates and identifies 30--41 candidates omitted by fitted-trial-only reports, with closed fate records verifying complete candidate accounting while maintaining competitive diagnostic performance. The code is available at https://github.com/XXIE999/candidate-fate-accounting.

</details>


### [248] [Sanyu Studio: A Multi-Agent System for Art-Historical Narrative Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18677)
*Zhaoxi Wei,Hongye Yang,Shuyuan Tian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Sanyu Studio enables plural art-historical narratives via 321 painting agents; prompts and evidence organization yield divergent yet coherent interpretations; AI can amplify human agency with limited historical evidence.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether LLM-based multi-agent dialogue can support non-monolithic interpretations in art history and offer public-facing interpretive tools, addressing concerns that generative AI may standardize analysis.

Method: Implement Sanyu Studio with 321 painting agents each endowed with fact/interpretation/organization/memory-filtering modules; conduct a seven-day workshop with eight art university participants; analyze prompts, evidence organization, and cognitive biases to observe how divergent narratives emerge.

Result: User prompts and evidence organization shape multiple divergent yet coherent versions of digital Sanyu; limited historical evidence can be amplified by AI to broaden human agency and provide interactive access to art-historical interpretation.

Conclusion: LLM-based multi-agent systems can support plural, participatory art-historical interpretation, especially when historical evidence is sparse; design choices around prompts and evidence curation are key to fostering diversity and avoiding homogenization; future work on evaluation, scalability, and public-facing deployment.

Abstract: Amid concerns that generative AI may standardize art interpretation, this paper examines whether LLM-based interaction can support plural art-historical narrative construction.
