We present Sanyu Studio, a multi-agent dialogue system that models 321 Sanyu oil paintings as agents with fact, interpretation, organization, and memory-filtering mechanisms. Based on a seven-day workshop with eight art-university participants, the study shows that user prompts, evidence organization, and cognitive tendencies shaped divergent yet coherent versions of digital Sanyu. The findings suggest that, under conditions of limited historical evidence, AI can amplify human agency and offer public audiences an interactive entry point into art-historical interpretation.

</details>


### [249] [RTPO: Reverse-Turn Policy Optimization for Stabilizing Agentic RL Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18682)
*Yugu Li,Jimmy Cao,Jianglin Qiao,Siyi Hu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: RTPO (Reverse-Turn Policy Optimization) stabilizes multi-turn agentic RL by organizing rollouts as reverse-tree structures and updating policies in temporal reverse order, aligning each decision with downstream continuations. It addresses context mismatch, sparse credit assignment, and asynchronous policy drift, achieving improved stability and performance over baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training multi-turn agentic RL is notoriously unstable due to (i) rollout-training context mismatch, (ii) weak turn-level credit assignment under sparse terminal rewards, and (iii) asynchronous policy drift when short and long trajectories are optimized under different policy versions. These issues share a common origin in flattened trajectory optimization.

Method: Propose a unified reverse-turn formulation that treats multi-turn rollouts as sparse reverse trees. Perform turn-level policy updates in temporal reverse order (from downstream to upstream), ensuring causal credit assignment and on-policy continuation. The approach aims to align each decision with its downstream continuation, reducing context mismatch and drift while improving credit attribution.

Result: Theoretical guarantees show that RTPO eliminates context mismatch and asynchronous drift under the turn-level formulation, reduces credit bias, and converges to recursive optimality. Empirically, RTPO improves trajectory- and turn-level baselines by 21.50% and 10.76% on multi-turn agentic RL benchmarks, indicating more stable training for tool-using agents.

Conclusion: RTPO provides a theoretically grounded, unified framework for stable multi-turn RL with tool use, delivering notable performance gains and insights into turn-level credit assignment and policy consistency across time.

Abstract: Training multi-turn agentic workflows with reinforcement learning (RL) enables large language models to perform complex reasoning, use external tools, and conduct iterative search beyond single-turn settings. Yet multi-turn RL training remains highly unstable, often causing severe performance degradation as the number of turns increases. Through theoretical analysis, we identify three tightly coupled sources of instability: rollout-training context mismatch, weak turn-level credit assignment under sparse terminal rewards, and asynchronous policy drift when short and long trajectories are optimized under different policy versions. We show that these issues share a common structural origin in flattened trajectory optimization and address them through a unified reverse-turn formulation. We propose Reverse-Turn Policy Optimization (RTPO), which organizes multi-turn rollouts as sparse reverse trees and performs turn-level policy updates in temporal reverse order, aligning each decision with its downstream continuation. RTPO enables causally consistent turn-level credit assignment and on-policy continuation to control asynchronous drift. We provide theoretical guarantees showing that RTPO eliminates context mismatch and asynchronous drift under the proposed turn-level formulation, reduces credit bias, and converges to recursive optimality. Experiments on multi-turn agentic RL benchmarks show that RTPO improves upon trajectory- and turn-level baselines by 21.50% and 10.76%, respectively, highlighting its potential to support more stable training for tool-using agents.

</details>


### [250] [Competence, Not Accuracy: A Diagnostic for Reference-Free Judge Gates in Skill Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18719)
*Chenle Chen,Yangbo Wei,Chao Yao,Shaoqiang Lu,Junhong Qian,Chen Wu,Lei He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A theoretical pre-deployment diagnostic shows when an LLM-judge gate can meaningfully discriminate correct vs. incorrect answers, via a bound on ROC-AUC that depends on judge competence and answer space; reveals limitations such as item-difficulty confounding and benchmark accuracy misrepresenting true competence; validated by probes and closed-loop study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine if an LLM-based gating judge provides usable signal before deployment and to quantify when such a gate is informative for optimization workflows.

Method: Formally model a reference-free judge as a latent solver; derive a closed-form bound on discriminability (ROC-AUC) as a function of judge competence c and answer-space size k; establish a necessary condition c > 1/k; show marginal AUC is confounded by item difficulty while a within-question estimator is not; introduce a non-intervening probe to measure judge scores without affecting decisions; conduct a closed-loop study to assess gate behavior.

Result: The bound predicts discriminability outcomes: near-chance performance when competence is near the floor, usable discrimination above the floor; benchmark accuracy overstates the competence that matters; the screening signal forecasts which gating error type occurs; the probe provides a cheap diagnostic for gate deployment.

Conclusion: A practical, low-cost diagnostic tool to assess the viability of LLM-based judge gates before integrating them into optimization loops, with clear caveats about item difficulty effects and the difference between benchmark accuracy and operational discriminability.

Abstract: Text-space skill optimization adapts a frozen agent by evolving a natural-language skill document, accepting each candidate through a validation gate. Existing gates rely on verifiable rewards, confining these methods to tasks with an automatic verifier. Replacing the verifier with an LLM-judge gate would lift that restriction, but whether such a gate carries usable signal is untested. We ask a prior question: can we tell, before placing a judge in the loop, whether its scores separate correct from incorrect answers at all? We formalize a reference-free judge as a latent solver -- its verdict rests on agreement with whatever it would itself conclude, so its capacity to evaluate is bounded by its capacity to solve. The model yields a closed-form bound on discriminability (ROC-AUC) in the judge's competence $c$ and answer-space size $k$, a necessary condition $c > 1/k$, and the result that the marginal AUC is confounded by item difficulty while a within-question estimator is not. A non-intervening probe records judge scores on genuine optimization runs without altering any decision. We find discriminability at chance where competence sits near the floor and usable above it; that a judge's benchmark accuracy overstates the competence that matters; and, in a closed-loop study, that the screen predicts which kind of gating error occurs. The result is a cheap pre-deployment diagnostic for judge gates.

</details>


### [251] [A Multi-Agent Platform for Automated Enterprise Analytics and Insight Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18740)
*Manoj N M,Vijayakrishna S,Manjunath Srinivas,Rohit Pahan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Five-agent sequential framework for conversational business intelligence with MCP-driven visualizations, secure data isolation, and reusable dashboards; demonstrates high functional accuracy (95.3%), latency 24s, quality 4.52/5, and 93.0% hallucination-free, with cross-LLM generalizability; ablations indicate Data Analysis and Report Aggregation as primary drivers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable end-to-end conversational BI by orchestrating specialized agents to handle query processing, data retrieval/analysis, visualization, and reporting within a secure multi-tenant platform.

Method: Implemented on CrewAI; five specialized agents in a pipeline; uses Model Context Protocol for visuals; defense-in-depth security for data isolation; query parameterization to produce reusable dashboard components; evaluated on 300 end-to-end test cases across synthetic and production datasets; cross-LLM backends (four) and human validation; ablation study.

Result: Functional accuracy 95.3%; mean latency 24 seconds; quality score 4.52/5.0; hallucination-free rate 93.0%; 22.6 pp accuracy improvement and 20.2% quality gain over a single-agent baseline; generalizability across LLM backends and evaluator reliability; ablation shows Data Analysis and Report Aggregation as primary drivers.

Conclusion: Architecture generalizes across models and datasets; demonstrates practical viability of multi-agent conversational BI with secure multi-tenant data handling; Security and modularity enable reusable dashboard components and robust insights.

Abstract: This paper proposes a multi-agent framework built on CrewAI [1] for conversational business intelligence. Five specialized AI agents operate in a sequential pipeline to process natural language queries, retrieve and analyze data, generate visualizations via the Model Context Protocol (MCP) [2], and deliver actionable insights. The platform features a defense-in-depth security architecture for multi-tenant data isolation and a query parameterization mechanism for transforming conversational insights into reusable dashboard components. Evaluation across 300 end-to-end test cases spanning synthetic and production enterprise datasets demonstrates 95.3% functional accuracy, a mean response latency of 24 seconds, and a response quality score of 4.52/5.0 as assessed by an LLM-as-a-Judge framework, with a 93.0% hallucination-free rate, representing a 22.6 percentage point accuracy improvement and 20.2% quality gain over a single-agent baseline. Cross-model evaluation across four LLM backends and human expert validation confirm architectural generalizability and evaluator reliability. An ablation study confirms that the Data Analysis and Report Aggregation agents are the primary drivers of output quality.

</details>


### [252] [Metrics That Write Themselves: Evolving an Evaluator from Its Own Blind Spots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18744)
*Xing Zhang,Yanwei Cui,Guanghui Wang,Zhihao Lin,Peiyang He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A CEGAR-like method to automatically design a scoring metric for evaluating open-ended answers using a small pool of Python operators, achieving measurable gains on unseen tasks with low cost compared to LLM-based judges.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the difficulty of scoring open-ended tasks and the brittleness of hand-crafted metrics by learning a scalable, low-cost scoring system that can generalize beyond training data.

Method: Build a pool of small Python operators that flag candidate answers for named defects or abstain. Treat the pool as an abstraction and use EvalCEGAR to search for a collision (two answers scored identically by the operators) between a correct and an incorrect answer. The authoring request is that collision pair. If no collision can be found, widen what operators may read and continue. Evaluate on MBPP+ and HumanEval+ (with a sandbox providing ground-truth tests) to induce a 55-line operator that reduces the gap between flagging nothing and a perfect filter by 15.4% on 428 unseen tasks ( +0.0065, p=0.0010 ), at about a quarter the flags of the best hand-written operator. On the benchmark, the learned operator matches the effect of that operator on about one-third of the flags. Six of eight runs admit such an operator and all six improve out-of-sample performance; combining 15 hand-written operators as one filter underperforms. An LLM judge achieves a similar delta but incurs per-candidate model calls, whereas the operator incurs none.

Result: The approach yields a data-driven, low-cost metric that improves coverage on unseen tasks and can outperform hand-written operators in aggregate when combined, while avoiding the cost of LLM judgments and generalizing beyond the training distribution.

Conclusion: A promising framework for automatic metric design that leverages CEGAR-style search to synthesize compact operator-based scoring rules, enabling improved transfer to unseen tasks and reduced inference cost relative to LLM-based judges; future work could explore richer operator pools, more sophisticated collision discovery, and broader task domains.

Abstract: Agents improve quickly against a reliable automatic metric and stall without one, and the applications that need them most, report generation among them, are the ones nobody knows how to score. Can the metric write itself? Saying what makes an answer good is hard; pointing at something wrong with one is easier, so the metric we evolve is a pool of small Python operators that each flag a candidate for one named defect, or abstain, and vote. Asking a model for operators directly does not work: 183 candidates realise only 96 distinct behaviours, from one narrow region of an enormous space. EvalCEGAR instead borrows counterexample-guided abstraction refinement from program verification. It reads the pool as an abstraction and searches for a collision, two answers the operators score identically, one correct and one not. That pair, not a prompt, is the authoring request, and when a collision defeats every attempt the loop widens what an operator may read rather than resampling. On MBPP+ and HumanEval+, a sandbox whose hidden unit tests give exact ground truth, the loop writes a 55-line operator that closes 15.4% of the gap between flagging nothing and a perfect filter on 428 unseen tasks (+0.0065, p=0.0010) at a quarter of our best hand-written operator's flags. On the benchmark it never saw it matches that operator's effect exactly on a third of the flags. Six of eight runs admit such an operator and all six help out of sample; our 15 hand-written operators applied together as one filter lose accuracy. An LLM judge on the same information ties that delta on a nearly disjoint set of candidates, and charges a model call per candidate forever where the operator charges none.

</details>


### [253] [Pairwise Logical Selection of Enthymeme Completions under Semantic-Link Uncertainty](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18820)
*Xuyao Feng,Antonis Bikakis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PWAL extends a neuro-symbolic framework to missing-premise/claim selection by marginalizing logical resistance across semantic-link configurations, improving accuracy and transparency over Top-Link.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enthymemes omit premises/claims; existing NL methods don't expose the completion reasoning, while logic-based approaches assume access to required formulae and background knowledge. PWAL stabilizes and exposes reasoning by evaluating across plausible link configurations.

Method: Fix translated formulae; replace binary entailment with logical-resistance scores; move from Top-Link's single highest-confidence semantic link to Possible-World Atom-Link Formalization (PWAL) that marginalizes over cross-formula semantic-link configurations.

Result: Evaluated on five tasks: ARCT and a CDED-derived missing-premise task; iDebate- and AAE2-derived missing-claim tasks; alphaNLI abductive-hypothesis task. PWAL raises strict accuracy by 2.95–30.86 percentage points and reduces tie rates by 4.57–58.00 percentage points versus Top-Link; with half-credit ties, accuracy improves by 0.45–6.04 pp. PWAL also provides translated formulae, sampled link configurations, and resistance components for transparent traceability.

Conclusion: PWAL improves accuracy and interpretability by marginalizing over semantic-link configurations, enabling traceable, robust reasoning for missing-premise/claim and abductive hypothesis selection across diverse tasks.

Abstract: Arguments often omit premises or claims, forming enthymemes. We study pairwise logical selection between two candidates for the omitted component. Existing natural language methods can identify or generate candidates but often do not expose how the selected candidate completes the inference, while logic-based approaches usually assume that the required formulae and background knowledge are available. We extend a prior neuro-symbolic pipeline from missing-premise to missing-claim selection and replace binary entailment outcomes with logical-resistance scores. Top-Link uses weighted Partial MaxSAT under a single configuration of highest-confidence semantic links. We then introduce Possible-World Atom-Link Formalization (PWAL), which keeps translated formulae fixed and marginalizes logical resistance over alternative cross-formula semantic-link configurations. We evaluate PWAL on five tasks: ARCT and a CDED-derived task for missing-premise selection, iDebate- and AAE2-derived tasks for missing-claim selection, and alphaNLI for abductive hypothesis selection. Relative to Top-Link, PWAL raises strict accuracy by 2.95-30.86 percentage points and reduces tie rates by 4.57-58.00 percentage points on all five tasks. When ties receive half credit, accuracy still increases by 0.45-6.04 percentage points. PWAL also records the translated formulae, sampled link configurations, and resistance components for every comparison, providing a transparent trace of each score.

</details>


### [254] [Verifiable abstention makes AI leak diagnosis accountable in water distribution networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18836)
*Tianwei Mu,Yue Wang,Mingzhe Yuan,Manhong Huang,Wenhong Wang,Xuerui Yin,Qing Luo,Min Xiao,Hui Yang,Jun Li,Dan Xue*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Accountable autonomous leak localization via abstention using a dual-agent system (physics-grounded executor + supervisor with LLM auditor) and a code-verifiable contract; demonstrates high decision precision with a limited set of acted events.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Utilities lose treated water to leakage and distrust AI localizers due to lack of verifiable abstention; aim to enable defensible, verifiable automation in water-infrastructure operations.

Method: An executor agent tests hypotheses (leak, demand, sensor, valve) against a digital twin; an independent supervisor agent, with an LLM auditor, evaluates evidence against a code-verifiable contract and then certifies a dispatch, requests evidence, or abstains.

Result: With field-grade noise, decision precision on acted events rises from 32% (baseline) to 96%. On an independently generated benchmark, the system acts on only 4 of 33 leaks, all correct. A 194-event audited register yields five excavation dispatches, three correct, and 44% survey recovery at full district precision.

Conclusion: Accountable abstention provides a defensible pathway to autonomous operation of water infrastructure.

Abstract: Utilities lose a substantial share of treated water to leakage, yet rarely trust artificial-intelligence localizers to dispatch crews: guessing everywhere cannot justify excavation. The gap is accountability, not accuracy: no method proves when it should not act. Here we recast leak localization as decision-making under verifiable abstention. A physics-grounded executor agent falsifies hypotheses (leak, demand, sensor, valve) against a digital twin; an independent supervisor agent, with a large-language-model (LLM) auditor, checks evidence against a code-verifiable contract, then certifies a dispatch, requests evidence or abstains. Under field-grade noise, a 32% forced baseline becomes 96% decision precision on acted events. On an independently generated benchmark it acts on only 4 of 33 leaks, all correct. A 194-event register of audited real leak locations with twin-simulated pressures and flows yields five excavation dispatches, three correct, and 44% survey recovery at full district precision. Accountable abstention offers a defensible route to autonomous water-infrastructure operation.

</details>


### [255] [ORBITER: Conflict-Aware Decision-Making for Agentic Last-Mile Delivery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18846)
*Mingzhao Li,Chenxi Liu,Yan Zhao,Hao Miao*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ORBITER is an agentive framework for next-order decision-making in last-mile delivery that uses LLMs with an evidence-based, critique-assisted process to select the next order, achieving up to 9.2% average improvement across four cities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To bridge the gap between learning-based spatiotemporal models and reliable next-order decisions by making the decision process explicit and verifiable using language models with structured tool-assisted reasoning.

Method: Agentic architecture with decision points capturing courier spatiotemporal state and visible orders; fixed proposers ranking candidates; structured report highlighting disagreement; LLMs gather evidence via task-specific tools; independent critic validates decisions.

Result: Empirical evaluation on four cities showing ORBITER outperforms state-of-the-art baselines by up to 9.2% on average.

Conclusion: Combining explicit reasoning with evidence gathering and independent critique yields more reliable, effective next-order decisions in dynamic delivery settings, suggesting a viable template for LLM-augmented decision-making in logistics.

Abstract: Last-mile delivery aims to handle dynamically arriving orders with couriers while modeling complex spatial and temporal correlations. Recent learning-based methods model spatiotemporal dependencies among orders to predict courier service sequences, but leave next-order decision making unexplained. Describing the current delivery state in language allows LLMs to reason explicitly about the spatial, temporal, and behavioral cues behind an individual decision. As direct predictors, however, LLMs remain sensitive to task presentation and often produce unreliable decisions. To address these challenges, we introduce ORBITER, an agentic Order Arbiter for next-order decision-making in last-mile delivery. ORBITER models courier service through decision points, each containing the courier's spatiotemporal state and visible orders and exposing local trade-offs for modeling and verification. Fixed proposers rank the candidates, and a structured report identifies where their rankings disagree. The LLM uses task-specific tools to gather evidence on the leading alternatives, while an independent critic checks the resulting decision against that evidence. We conduct extensive evaluations on data in four cities, where ORBITER outperforms existing state-of-the-art baselines by up to 9.2% on average showing its effectiveness.

</details>


### [256] [SkillGate: Training In-Policy Skill Selection in Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18852)
*Qingyao Li,Wenxiang Jiao,Shuai Shao,Kangning Zhang,Yuan Lu,Yi Guo,Weiwen Liu,Weinan Zhang,Yong Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SkillGate fixes selector credit starvation by partitioning credits between outcome execution and skill-naming, enabling correct in-episode skill reads and outperforming outcome-only RL on 16-candidate skill slates.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In agent frameworks where procedural knowledge is accessed as on-demand skills, the policy must decide which skill to read mid-episode. Outcome-rewarded RL over the candidate slate inadequately trains this selector due to credit assignment problems that worsen with horizon.

Method: Introduce SkillGate, which splits credit into two disjoint channels: outcome credit (for execution tokens) and action-local advantage (for the skill-name tokens). The skill-naming token receives positive credit only when its corresponding read is the single correct one in the trajectory.

Result: On five agentic benchmarks with a 9B policy across a 16-candidate slate, SkillGate raises trial success from 40.8% to 53.2%, outperforms the same budget with outcome reward alone, reduces exposure to misleading candidates by ~66%, and reduces the number of skills read.

Conclusion: Partitioning credit signals to distinguish execution vs. skill-selection events resolves selector credit starvation, enabling more reliable mid-episode skill reads and improving efficiency and accuracy in large-slate skill selection tasks.

Abstract: Agent frameworks increasingly package procedural knowledge as skills: instruction files an agent reads on demand, while public libraries now hold thousands of them. Which skill to read has thus become a decision the policy itself makes in the middle of an episode, yet no existing signal trains it. We show that the default remedy, outcome-rewarded RL over the candidate slate, cannot teach it, for a structural reason we identify and name selector credit starvation: under a broadcast, sequence-level advantage, the few tokens that name the chosen skill carry a vanishing share of the loss, and the credit they inherit is increasingly wrong-signed as trajectories lengthen. A correct choice is punished whenever the execution after it fails, even though the choice itself is among the most valuable decisions in the trajectory. Auditing a completed run's own training artifacts confirms all three properties, each worsening monotonically with horizon. SkillGate removes the failure by construction: it partitions the token support into two disjoint credit channels, outcome credit reaching only execution tokens, and a separate action-local advantage reaching exactly the skill-naming tokens, positive only when a trajectory's single read is the correct one. On five agentic benchmarks under a 16-candidate slate, SkillGate lifts a 9B policy from 40.8% to 53.2% trial success, well ahead of the identical budget spent on outcome reward alone, while cutting exposure to misleading candidates by two thirds and reading fewer skills.

</details>


### [257] [DentAgent: Evidence-Centric Multi-Agent Coordination for Multimodal Dental Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18878)
*Zijie Meng,Xiwei Dai,Yixuan Tang,Jin Hao,Yang Feng,Fudong Zhu,Xiaoqiang Liu,Shaosheng Cao,Zuozhu Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DentAgent is an evidence-centric multi-agent framework for multimodal dental reasoning that unifies observations from domain knowledge, radiographs, intraoral photos, and 3D data via an evidence blackboard; it achieves state-of-the-art and interpretable results across four benchmarks, outperforming senior specialists on multi-label diagnosis by 17.3 percentage points.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome modality- or task-specific dental AI, reduce opaque end-to-end responses, and provide traceable, evidence-based reasoning across diverse dental data modalities.

Method: Orchestrator coordinates five specialized agents across modalities; each agent converts observations into structured evidence records using domain tools; an Evidence Blackboard manages coverage, gaps, and conflicts; standardized evidence representation enables a unified workflow.

Result: Leading performance across four benchmarks; 17.3-point improvement over senior specialists on multi-label diagnosis; demonstrates value of evidence-centric architecture for traceable multimodal dental reasoning.

Conclusion: DentAgent offers a broadly applicable, traceable foundation for multimodal dental assessment and has potential for population oral health management; future work may address scalability and generalization across datasets.

Abstract: Oral diseases affect billions of people worldwide, underscoring a pressing need for accurate and reliable dental assessment that integrates heterogeneous evidence from domain knowledge, radiographs, intraoral photographs, and 3D dental data. Most existing dental AI systems remain modality- or task-specific. Although recent vision-language models support flexible dental question answering, directly generated response leaves evidence implicit and untraceable. To address these limitations, we introduce DentAgent, an evidence-centric multi-agent framework, in which the Orchestrator coordinate five specialized agents spanning various modalities. Each specialist utilizes domain tools to convert observations into structured evidence records. The Evidence Blackboard manages these records as a shared evidence state, tracking coverage, gaps, and conflicts before response generation. This standardized evidence representation integrates isolated dental capabilities into a unified agentic workflow. Across four benchmarks, DentAgent demonstrates leading performance, even surpassing the senior specialists by 17.3 percentage points on multi-label diagnosis, which supports its value for broadly applicable and traceable multimodal dental reasoning, and highlights its potential as a technical foundation for population oral health assessment and management.

</details>


### [258] [Training-Free Inference-Time Self-Reflection and Cost-Bounded Early Stopping for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18884)
*Wei Yu,Suxing Liu,Minjie Yu,Jiahao Wang,Zhijian Zheng,Haocheng Deng,Bing Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: EvoResearcher is a training-free, inference-time protocol that adds cost-bounded self-reflection to a frozen LLM, using an iterative generate–self-critique–revise loop with an early CONFIRMED stop to cut compute while preserving accuracy on pure-reasoning benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reinforcement-learning training of reasoning LLMs is expensive and requires a controllable environment; a training-free, inference-time protocol is desired to enable cost-bounded reasoning without gradient updates.

Method: An iterative loop: generate -> self-critique -> revise, with a maximum depth D and a CONFIRMED sentinel for early stopping. Four meta-reward components (correctness, efficiency, reflection depth, tool-call diversity) guide prompt-level mechanisms without gradient updates. Evaluated on frozen backbones across BBH, GSM8K, MATH, with cross-model replication on Qwen2.5-72B; environment- and multi-agent extensions are future work.

Result: On Big-Bench Hard, no accuracy gain beyond the 95% Wilson interval; however, 82–88% of items are terminated early with equal accuracy, reducing to about 2.1 generations per question. Cross-domain tests show consistent behavior on GSM8K and MATH; tool-call diversity validated at the prompt level; cross-model replication confirms generality of the protocol.

Conclusion: A cost-bounded self-verification approach that requires no training and preserves accuracy on pure-reasoning tasks, offering compute savings by early stopping. Useful when resources are constrained; limited to inference-time improvements with current evaluation scope.

Abstract: Reinforcement-learning training of reasoning LLMs (e.g., GRPO) is expensive and requires a controllable environment, committing every contribution to a full training pipeline. We present EvoResearcher, a training-free, inference-time protocol that adds cost-bounded self-reflection to a single frozen LLM backbone. The protocol iterates generate -> self-critique -> revise until a maximum depth D is reached or the critique returns the CONFIRMED sentinel, an implicit early stop that lets the backbone self-verify its answer under a strict compute budget. Four self-reflective meta-reward components (correctness, efficiency, reflection depth, tool-call diversity) act as design principles instantiated as prompt-level mechanisms, so their benefits accrue with zero gradient updates. We validate the protocol on Big-Bench Hard (100 questions) and establish cross-domain behavior on GSM8K (500) and MATH (500) on the same frozen backbone, with cross-model replication on Qwen2.5-72B. All experiments use pure-reasoning benchmarks; the tool-call diversity component is validated in prompt-level form, and the environment-level and multi-agent extensions are design blueprints left to future work. On clean BBH the protocol does not raise accuracy beyond the 95% Wilson interval; its value is cost-bounded self-verification, with the CONFIRMED early stop terminating 82-88% of items at equal accuracy (about 2.1 generations per question).

</details>


### [259] [Syntactic Simplification of OWL Class Expressions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18899)
*Alkid Baci,N'Dah Jean Kouagou,Caglar Demir,Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A new approach, Class Expression Simplifier (CES), syntactically simplifies DL class expressions while preserving semantics; implemented in OWLAPY; evaluated on two medium ontologies; improves reasoning efficiency and readability; open-source.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: DL ontologies often yield complex class expressions that hinder interpretation and scalable reasoning; there is a need for semantics-preserving simplification to reduce redundancy and verbosity.

Method: CES applies a set of rewriting rules to DL class expressions to remove redundancies and identify simpler yet equivalent expressions; preserves entailments; produces compact, human-readable representations; implemented in Python OWLAPY.

Result: On expressions learned from two medium-sized ontologies, CES yields improved reasoning efficiency and reduced verbosity; maintains logical equivalence with originals.

Conclusion: CES contributes to accessibility, maintainability, and scalability of ontology-driven applications; has implications for knowledge graph construction, semantic search, and web-scale reasoning; released as open-source in OWLAPY.

Abstract: Class expression learning often produces complex OWL class expressions that are difficult to interpret and reason over. However, by following theoretically grounded simplification principles, this complexity can be reduced. In this paper, we propose Class Expression Simplifier (CES), a novel algorithm for the syntactic simplification of class expressions in Description Logics (DL). CES aims to preserve formal semantics while reducing representational complexity. It systematically applies rewriting rules to eliminate redundancies and identify simpler yet equivalent expressions, thereby producing more compact and human-readable representations without altering logical entailments. We evaluate the effectiveness of CES on class expressions learned from two medium-sized ontologies, demonstrating measurable improvements in reasoning efficiency and reductions in verbosity. This work contributes to the broader goal of making ontology-driven applications more accessible, maintainable, and scalable, with direct implications for knowledge graph construction, semantic search, and Web-scale reasoning. CES is implemented within the open-source Python framework OWLAPY and is publicly available.

</details>


### [260] [\textsc{TestifAI}: Tomography-Based Testing for Deep Learning Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18900)
*Arooj Arif,Tobias Hartung,Elena Botoeva,Alexandros Koliousis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TestifAI introduces a framework for estimating robustness under multiple perturbations by learning from low-order perturbations; achieves accurate high-order robustness predictions with substantially fewer inferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robustness testing for safety-critical AI faces combinatorial explosion in perturbation space; exhaustive testing is impractical, necessitating efficient estimation methods.

Method: Partial model tomography: train an auxiliary model on outcomes of tests with up to two perturbations to infer robustness for higher-order perturbations; supports structured semantic perturbations (e.g., blur, brightness, zoom) with discrete severity levels; enables querying any perturbation combination.

Result: High-order robustness (3–4 perturbations) predicted from 1–2 perturbation data with aggregate error <7%; reduces required inferences by 60–80% across five image and language tasks.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a scalable, efficient approach for multi-perturbation robustness estimation, enabling practical safety testing; effectiveness depends on perturbation coverage and auxiliary model accuracy.

Abstract: As AI systems are increasingly deployed in safety-critical application domains (e.g., autonomous driving), associated risks increase too. Deep learning models underlying modern AI systems, therefore, must undergo thorough testing to ensure their correct behaviour. A single robustness test involves thousands of inferences to empirically verify if a model's outputs remain stable under a bounded perturbation of its inputs. However, existing testing frameworks lack the means to systematically explore and summarise robustness across a combinatorial space of perturbations.
  We propose TestifAI, a deep learning testing framework for efficient and accurate estimation of robustness against combinations of perturbations. TestifAI enables users to specify operational conditions as structured spaces of semantic input perturbations (e.g., image blur, brightness and zoom) and discrete severity levels (e.g., low, medium and high). Users can query model robustness for any combination (e.g., "low blur, high brightness, and medium zoom"). To achieve efficiency and accuracy, TestifAI introduces partial model tomography, a novel approach to reconstructing model behaviour in a multi-perturbation space from tests that apply only a small number of perturbations (lower-order projections). To estimate robustness against at least three perturbations, TestifAI trains an auxiliary model on the results of tests involving up to two perturbations only, avoiding execution of an exponential number of tests. Our experiments on five image and language classification tasks show that TestifAI can predict higher-order (3 and 4 perturbations) test outcomes from low-order (1 and 2 perturbations) observations with an aggregate robustness estimation error of less than 7%, while reducing the number of inferences by 60-80%.

</details>


### [261] [Breaking the weakest link to evade vision language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18938)
*Ilan Zini,Boussad Addad,Katarzyna Kapusta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper studies the vulnerability of Vision Language Models (VLMs) to adversarial perturbations on visual inputs, presenting a gradient-based attack that optimizes only the vision encoder. It explores untargeted and targeted attacks, showing small, human-imperceptible perturbations can drastically alter the model's textual outputs across multiple open-source VLMs, highlighting robustness concerns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLMs' robustness against adversarial threats, especially in multimodal alignment, is underexplored despite deployment in real-world safety-critical applications; understanding vulnerabilities is essential for designing defenses.

Method: A gradient-based adversarial attack that optimizes perturbations on the vision encoder alone, enabling efficient generation of adversarial examples. The study considers two attack settings: untargeted (disrupt interpretation) and targeted (force a specific undesired description). Experiments are conducted on multiple open-source VLMs.

Result: Small, perceptible visual perturbations substantially change the textual descriptions produced by several VLMs, demonstrating a vulnerability in multimodal alignment and revealing a gap in current robustness.

Conclusion: Modern VLMs are vulnerable to adversarial manipulation via perturbations to the image input, underscoring the need for robust defenses and security measures in multimodal AI systems.

Abstract: Vision Language Models (VLMs) have recently emerged as a critical component of multimodal AI systems, enabling joint reasoning over visual and textual inputs in real-world and safety-critical applications. Despite their growing deployment, the robustness of VLMs against adversarial threats remains insufficiently explored, particularly in the context of evasion attacks targeting multimodal alignment. In this work, we investigate the vulnerability of VLMs to adversarial perturbations applied to visual inputs and study two attack settings: untargeted attacks, where the goal is to disrupt the model's interpretation of the original image, and targeted attacks, where the adversary aims to force the model to generate a specific semantic description unrelated to the original image. To efficiently generate adversarial examples, we propose a gradient-based attack method that performs optimization exclusively on the vision encoder of the VLM rather than on the entire multimodal architecture. This design significantly reduces the computational cost and resource requirements of the attack while maintaining strong effectiveness. We evaluate our approach on several open-source VLMs, including Qwen2.5-VL, Granite-Vision, FastVLM, and Phi-3.5-Vision, and show that small, human-imperceptible perturbations can substantially alter the textual interpretation produced by the models. Our findings highlight the vulnerability of modern VLMs to adversarial manipulation and emphasize the need for improved robustness and security mechanisms in multimodal AI systems.

</details>


### [262] [A Theory of Post-hoc Debate Judgement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19002)
*Xiang Yin,Adam Dejl,Antonio Rago,Lihu Chen,Francesca Toni*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Formalizes debate judgement with properties of reproducibility, robustness, groundedness, and explainability; compares LLM-based judges with formal argumentation semantics in claim verification; finds semantics offers formal guarantees with comparable accuracy, advocating argumentation semantics as principled judges for debate-driven AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Debates are used to boost agentic AI performance, explainability, and user engagement, but require principled judgment mechanisms to validate debate outputs; formal properties help ensure reliable, explainable results across settings.

Method: Proposes a theory of debate judgement; analyzes formal properties; conducts experiments in claim verification; compares two judging approaches: (1) LLM-based judges and (2) formal argumentation semantics from computational argumentation.

Result: Both judging methods achieve similar accuracy in mis-match settings, but LLM-based judges may lack formal guarantees that argumentation-semantics provide.

Conclusion: Argumentation semantics is an ideal candidate for principled judges in debate-driven AI due to its formal guarantees and comparable accuracy.

Abstract: Debates have recently emerged as a useful methodology for agentic AI to improve performance as well as to aid explainability and user engagement. For example, LLM-empowered agents may debate internally (with themselves) and/or externally (with other agents). In many settings where debates are used, debates' outcomes and resulting outputs are determined post-hoc by external judges, often LLMs. In this paper we develop and test a novel theory of debate judgement applicable to all settings where agents engage in debates by providing pros and cons for their opinions therein. Specifically, we identify a number of formal properties that debate judgement may be required to satisfy in general, as concerns reproducibility, robustness, groundedness and explainability. Then, we explore their satisfaction formally and/or experimentally, for claim verification settings, for two specific alternative debate judgement methods: variants of the LLMs as a judge idea and formal semantics drawn from computational argumentation. We show that the two methods give similar accuracy performances but the former may lack formal guarantees that the latter brings. Overall, our study indicates argumentation semantics as an ideal candidate for principled judges in debate-driven AI.

</details>


### [263] [Self-prompting and cross-model consensus enable reproducible data extraction from scientific literature with large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19025)
*Valentin Romanov,Monique Bax,Steven Niederer*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Frontier browser-based LLMs can extract contextualized data from research articles with four escalating workflows; expert prompts yield best results, self-prompting nears them, autonomous literature discovery is error-prone, and dataset creation aligns with human judges but needs human oversight, suggesting an auditable human–model division of labor for scalable data curation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluate the capabilities and limitations of frontier LLMs for contextualized data extraction from scientific articles and propose a practical, auditable workflow that scales curation while preserving expert oversight.

Method: Four escalating workflows were tested: (1) expert-curated prompts with articles; (2) LLMs producing their own prompts from simple instructions; (3) autonomous discovery of literature by agents; (4) creation of new datasets from published guidelines. Outcomes were compared to expert judgments and assessed for reliability, hallucinations, and need for human-in-the-loop.

Result: (1) With expert prompts, most frontier LLMs perform well at data extraction but struggle with scientific context and nuance; (2) self-generated prompts by LLMs were nearly as effective as expert prompts; (3) autonomous literature discovery was challenging, with agents missing references or hallucinating; (4) LLMs can generate datasets closely matching human judges but still require human oversight. These findings support an auditable division of labor among experts, model cross-checks, and human resolution of disputes to scale data curation while maintaining quality.

Conclusion: An auditable, human-in-the-loop workflow is practical for scaling scientific data curation: experts define evidence standards, models perform cross-checks, and researchers resolve disputes, enabling scalable extraction of contextualized data without sacrificing expert oversight.

Abstract: Accurately extracting nuanced, contextualized data from research articles is laborious and time intensive. Here, we investigate the performance of frontier, browser-based large language models (LLMs) to extract highly contextualized information. We demonstrate four escalating workflows, 1) given an expert curated prompt and research articles, most frontier LLMs perform well at data extraction, however can struggle with interpreting scientific context and nuance, 2) given simple instructions, LLMs can author their own prompts which were almost as eNective as expert-written prompts, 3) autonomous discovery of research literature was diNicult, agents either missed or hallucinated references, and 4) LLMs can create new datasets from published guidelines that closely match human-expert judges, but still require a human-in-the-loop. Together, these findings define an auditable division of labour in which experts specify the evidence standard, models cross-check repeated extractions and researchers resolve disputed cases, providing a practical route to scaling scientific data curation without relinquishing expert oversight.

</details>


### [264] [Adaptive Memory and Reflection Multi-Agent System for Medical Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19029)
*Pradeep Murugesan,Luoxiao Yang,Xueli Chen,Xinqi Fan*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent AMR (adaptive memory and reflection) system for medical QA improves performance by combining agent-specific memory, reflection-based feedback, and external retrieval, with complex workflow routing and ethical oversight; demonstrated on MedQA and MedMCQA with ablation showing strongest results when all components are combined; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current medical QA systems rely on single-agent architectures and static retrieval, lacking adaptive memory, structured decision-making, and oversight, which are crucial for nuanced medical reasoning and trustworthy outputs.

Method: A multi-agent framework with specialized agents, each equipped with dedicated memory and reflection-based feedback. The system retrieves relevant prior cases, routes questions through solo/collaborative/escalated workflows based on complexity, and uses consensus and an ethical overseer to consolidate reasoning and review outputs.

Result: Evaluated on MedQA and MedMCQA, achieving strong performance relative to several baselines. Ablation studies demonstrate that integrating agent-specific memory, reflection, and external retrieval yields the best performance.

Conclusion: Structured memory and feedback mechanisms can enhance the trustworthiness and effectiveness of medical QA agents, with public code available for reproduction.

Abstract: Accurate and responsible medical question answering (QA) is important in healthcare, where complex cases require factual knowledge and nuanced reasoning. Existing medical QA systems, typically based on single-agent architectures and static retrieval, often lack adaptability, persistent memory, and structured decision-making. This work introduces an adaptive memory and reflection (AMR) agentic system, a multi-agent framework in which specialized agents use dedicated memory and reflection-based feedback to retrieve relevant prior cases and improve subsequent reasoning. Complexity assessment routes questions through solo, collaborative, or escalated workflows, while consensus and ethical overseer modules support reasoning consolidation and output review. Evaluation on MedQA and MedMCQA demonstrates strong performance compared with several baselines. Ablation studies show that combining agent-specific memory, reflection, and external retrieval yields the strongest performance. These findings highlight the potential of structured memory and feedback for developing more trustworthy medical agents. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/mm-air/AMR-Agent.

</details>


### [265] [Eureka: Task-Conditioned Meta-Agent Orchestration for Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19047)
*Alizer Wong,Heng Cui,Yi Tan,Xiongchao Zhan,Liang Lin,Yuxiang Guo,Zhaorong Dai,Zixin Zeng,Wenyuan Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Eureka presents a task-conditioned Meta-Agent that compiles long-horizon tasks into dynamic obligation graphs, enabling Macro-Agents with specialized components and receding-horizon planning, backed by theoretical guarantees and strong empirical results; it also instantiates Theory-Discovery and Math/Conjecture Agents to study scientific reasoning, arguing that cognitive-structure–aligned architectures are key for scientific-agent capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable scalable, verifiable autonomous agents for long-horizon tasks and scientific reasoning by integrating planning, memory, verification, and adaptive architecture.

Method: Construct dynamic obligation graphs to compose Macro-Agents with specialized state, memory, operators, tools, verifiers, and local topology; employ receding-horizon planning, architecture promotion, and minimal-sufficient compilation; apply cost-benefit-gated evolution to update architecture; prove theoretical results on regret, planning invalidation, amortization, subtree interfaces, serializability, and verification; empirically validate on recursive tasks, certificate generation, context compression, recomputation reduction, and concurrent execution; instantiate a Theory-Discovery Agent and a Math/Conjecture Agent to explore theory formation and bottlenecks in mathematical research.

Result: Achieves 170/170 recursive tasks completed and 3,948 certificates with no false acceptances; active context compression reduces median input tokens from 9,490 to 4,005; incremental processing avoids 65.38% recomputation across 12,000 tasks; 16,000 concurrent executions serialize consistently; the Math/Conjecture Agent identifies bottlenecks in Riemann Hypothesis research and advances a positivity certificate for Suzuki’s localized Weil quadratic form to 0 < a <= 69/200 (0.345), reaching ~99.55% of (log 2)/2.

Conclusion: Scientific-agent capability stems from aligning architectural structure with the task’s cognitive demands, as demonstrated by Eureka’s scalable, verifiable meta-agent framework and its manifestations in theory discovery and conjecture exploration.

Abstract: We present Eureka, a task-conditioned Meta-Agent architecture that compiles long-horizon tasks into dynamic obligation graphs with explicit acceptance semantics. During execution, Eureka forms Macro-Agents with specialized state, memory, operators, tools, verifiers, and local topology via receding-horizon planning, architecture promotion, and minimal-sufficient compilation. When bottlenecks recur, cost-benefit-gated evolution updates the local architecture under constraints. Theoretically, we establish results on regret, planning invalidation, amortization, subtree interfaces, serializability, and verification. Experimentally, Eureka completes 170/170 recursive tasks and generates 3,948 certificates with no false acceptances. Active context compresses median input from 9,490 to 4,005 tokens; incremental processing avoids 65.38% recomputation across 12,000 tasks; 16,000 concurrent executions serialize consistently. The same Meta-Agent instantiates a Theory-Discovery Agent and a Math/Conjecture Agent. The former yields structural results in quantum-process and spacetime theory. The latter identifies bottlenecks in Riemann Hypothesis research and advances a positivity certificate for Suzuki's localized Weil quadratic form to 0 < a <= 69/200 = 0.345, reaching ~99.55% of (log 2)/2. These results suggest that scientific-agent capability depends not only on the base model but on whether an architecture can be formed to match the task's cognitive structure.

</details>


### [266] [What is Missing from AI Post-Training AI: An Empirical Analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19072)
*Joy Jia Yin Lim,Xin Huang,Hao Peng,Yaxi Lu,Xin Cong,Zhong Zhang,Maosong Sun,Yankai Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Post-training of LLM agents reveals a fixed initial strategy; execution improves with experience or guidance, but strategy remains static, and gains do not transfer to harder tasks. A spontaneous mechanism to reevaluate strategy during execution is needed.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle execution-level vs strategy-level capabilities in post-training LLM agents and understand why post-training does not naturally reallocate high-level strategy; empirically test leading hypotheses.

Method: Analyze a large corpus of publicly released post-training trajectories to identify how training budget is allocated between strategy and execution. Conduct controlled experiments simulating three explanatory interventions (experience-driven scaffolds, human guidance, and increased inference compute) across tasks, measuring execution performance and evidence of strategy adaptation.

Result: Findings show: (1) experience-driven scaffolds boost execution (e.g., +12.6 GSM8K, +40.8 HumanEval) but leave the strategy unchanged; (2) human guidance can redirect the initial strategy, yet agents revert to local adjustments during training; (3) more inference compute helps easier tasks but yields little to no gain on the hardest task.

Conclusion: The bottleneck is a lack of a mechanism for spontaneously reevaluating and updating the training strategy during execution, rather than simply lacking experience, guidance, or reasoning compute.

Abstract: Large language model (LLM) agents can now post-train an LLM end-to-end. They can write code, launch training, evaluate checkpoints, and improve downstream performance, raising the prospect of AI-for-AI. We argue that this picture conflates two distinct capabilities: execution-level capability, iterating within a selected training strategy; and strategy-level capability, revising the high-level judgment as experimental evidence accumulates. Analyzing a large corpus of publicly released post-training trajectories, we find that across different tasks, the agent's training strategy is locked in at the very beginning, and the entire remaining budget is spent on local adjustments within the selected strategy. We then examine three natural explanations--missing experience, missing guidance, and insufficient reasoning--with escalating interventions. Extensive experiments show that (1) an experience-driven scaffold improves execution across the board (+12.6 points on GSM8K and +40.8 on HumanEval) but leaves the strategy static; (2) human guidance effectively redirects the initial strategy, yet the agent falls back into local adjustment loops once training starts; and (3) additional inference compute pays off on easier tasks but yields almost no gain on the hardest one. In conclusion, what agents lack is neither experience, guidance, nor reasoning compute, but a mechanism for spontaneously reevaluating their strategy during execution.

</details>


### [267] [Robust Risk Under Evolving Uncertainty: A Wasserstein Counterpart of the Entropic Value-at-Risk](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19073)
*Deep Kumar Ganguly,Jan Křetínský*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Wasserstein entropic value-at-risk (WEVaR) to hedge against reachable catastrophes ignored by entropic risk; derives variational duals linking transport prices to inverse temperature, and yields a closed-form robust DP operator with provable safety guarantees, validated numerically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safely learning in uncertain environments requires risk measures that capture catastrophes reachable under model uncertainty. Entropic risk (relative-entropy balls) fails to cover some catastrophes; a Wasserstein (optimal-transport) framework offers a more robust hedge.

Method: Replace KL-based ambiguity sets with Wasserstein balls; analyze the resulting coherent risk measure (WEVaR); establish a variational dual analogous to the entropic formula; link transport radius to belief entropy; derive a closed-form robust dynamic programming operator; verify dualities numerically and demonstrate safety properties.

Result: The Wasserstein EVaR exhibits a coherent risk measure with dual representations and a transport-price interpretation; it accounts for catastrophes missed by entropic risk; numerical verification confirms the dualities; the derived DP operator provides a caution that contracts as belief sharpens, yielding a safety sandwich and a sharp safety switch.

Conclusion: WEVaR offers a principled, tractable framework for safe, robust decision-making under model uncertainty, bridging transport-based risk with entropic ideas and enabling explicit safety guarantees in dynamic programming.

Abstract: An agent still learning its environment should be cautious while ignorant and bold once confident. The entropic value-at-risk captures this through a robust-optimization identity---a confidence level fixes the radius of a relative-entropy ball of alternative models---but that ball cannot reach catastrophes the nominal deems impossible, precisely what a safe agent must hedge. We instead use an optimal-transport ball and study the coherent risk measure it induces, the Wasserstein entropic value-at-risk. It has a variational dual mirroring the entropic formula (an inverse temperature becomes a transport price), occupies a definite place in the risk hierarchy, and provably accounts for the reachable catastrophes the entropic measure ignores; we verify both dualities numerically. Driving the transport radius by belief entropy then yields a closed-form robust dynamic-programming operator whose caution contracts as the belief sharpens, with a certified safety sandwich and a sharp safety switch.

</details>


### [268] [Tuning the Stochastic Machine: A Systems Engineer's Operating Model for Human-AI Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19125)
*George Andrikopoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Correcting a language model's errors is an operations-and-governance problem, not a tooling gap; persistence of corrections needs versioning, provenance, recurrence monitoring, and retirement of stale rules. The author proposes a seven-principle operating discipline centered on an error loop, mapped from traditional systems to the LLM stack, with three practice-case illustrations, plus a measurement framework and proposed lab study.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current practice lets corrections die when sessions end, causing error recurrence. The work reframes corrections as persistent, governed configurations requiring provenance, retirement, and monitoring.

Method: Map the LLM stack (frozen silicon, firmware, loadable modules, persistent configuration, volatile memory) onto established systems practice, diagnose where mappings fail (stochastic generation, weak configuration binding, lack of default retirement/verification). Develop a seven-principle operating discipline with an error loop; illustrate with three cases from practitioner experience; outline a measurement framework and an empirical lab study to test the proposal.

Result: Identification of a practical governance framework: a seven-principle operating discipline with an error loop, plus a measurement framework and proposed lab study.

Conclusion: Operational governance and lifecycle management of corrections are essential for persistent, reliable error handling in LLM systems; implementing versioned provenance, recurrence monitoring, counter-metrics, and retirement of stale rules can prevent error recurrence.

Abstract: When an expert corrects an LLM assistant's error, the correction usually dies with the session, and the error class returns. I argue this is an operations problem, not a tooling problem: mechanisms for persisting corrections exist and are shipping, but the discipline for governing them -- versioning with provenance, recurrence monitoring, counter-metrics, retirement of stale rules -- does not. Writing as a systems engineer of thirty years, I map the LLM stack onto the machines my profession already operates (frozen silicon, firmware, loadable modules, persistent configuration, volatile memory), identify where the mapping fails (stochastic generation, configuration that binds only probabilistically, no general-purpose retirement (verification) stage by default), and derive from the failures a seven-principle operating discipline with an error loop at its core. Three cases from my own practice illustrate the mechanism, among them a control that silently became the exact harm it was built to prevent. I close with the measurement framework this view implies and the lab study required to test it.

</details>


### [269] [Grouping the Stochastic Machine: Precision, Not Capability, as the Frontier Metric for AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19140)
*George Andrikopoulos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The abstract argues that frontier LMs are distinguished by precision (output consistency) rather than capability (average accuracy). It proposes a cheap, deterministic measurement of per-task consistency across repeated trials, a defined grouping metric and harness, and demonstrates that measurement can guide decisions; a real run shows one rule fixed a gap, while rules authored by the system themselves yielded no additional value because frontier models already embody good practice.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmarking currently emphasizes central tendency (mean capability) and overlooks dispersion and reliability. The authors seek a cheap, non-circular method to quantify precision to guide deployment decisions and improve real-world performance.

Method: Run a fixed suite of deterministically scored tasks at fixed temperature across many repeated trials; compute per-task consistency of outcomes to derive a grouping metric; define a harness for evaluation; monitor a human-AI pair over time to detect compounding signal of precision improvements.

Result: Preliminary real run demonstrates the method and its limits: a performance gap closed completely by a single rule (0/5 -> 5/5); in contrast, a set of tasks designed from the rules themselves yielded no value because frontier models already exhibit good practice.

Conclusion: Shifting evaluation to precision/reliability reveals decision-relevant differences between systems. This measurement can distinguish between easily correctable, consistent failures and widespread, sampling-related failures, informing deployment strategies and future research. Real-world work should drive discipline value rather than outcomes derived from its own rulebook.

Abstract: Frontier language models are compared, marketed, and benchmarked on capability -- what their best or average output can achieve. I argue this measures the wrong axis. The models have saturated accuracy: their mean output lands on the target. What now separates one system from another in practice is precision: how tightly concentrated their outputs are around that target across repeated, identical requests. Borrowing the marksman's distinction, capability is where the average shot lands; reliability is the size of the group. I make three claims. First, precision, not capability, is the frontier differentiator between systems, and benchmark culture systematically fails to measure it, reporting central tendency rather than spread. Second, precision is measurable, cheaply and without circularity, by running a fixed suite of deterministically scored tasks many times at fixed temperature and computing the per-task consistency of outcomes -- no model-in-the-loop grader required. Third, the measurement is not merely descriptive but decision-guiding: it separates consistent failures (a tight group off-centre, correctable by the operating discipline of Paper 1 -- a sight adjustment) from scattered failures (a wide group, correctable only by changing the model or its sampling -- a rifle problem). I define a grouping metric, specify a harness, and show how tracking a human-AI pair's grouping over time yields the compounding signal that Paper 1's field study requires. A first real run, since replicated, illustrates both the method and its most important limit: one measured gap was closed completely by a single rule (0/5 -> 5/5), while a suite of tasks authored from the rules themselves found no value, because a frontier model already embodies explicit good practice -- establishing that a discipline's worth is found by measurement on real work, not constructed from its own rulebook.

</details>


### [270] [Beyond the Transcript: Detecting Covert Co ordination in Latent Multi-Agent Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19161)
*Ramneet Kaur,Pradyumna Chari,Ramesh Raskar,Jugad Singh,Sumit Kumar Jha,Anirban Roy*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Introduces Verifiable Latent Alignments (VLA) to monitor and steer hidden private latent-state channels in language-model agents, using a three-layer neutral monitor, a steerability framework, and evaluation in auction benchmarks showing strong detection (AUROC) and mitigation of collusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Private latent communications can enable covert harmful coordination in language-model agents. There is a need for verifiable, activation-aware monitoring and steering that links latent activity to public actions without excessive training data.

Method: A three-layer neutral monitor combining representation anomaly detection, counterfactual action-distribution influence, and sparse-autoencoder interpretation; a steerability framework that handles black-box instructions and white-box matched-neutral counterfactuals; evaluation on controlled multi-agent auction benchmarks with homogeneous and heterogeneous agents, including many-agent scalability and intervention effectiveness; a shared event identifier links latent state and channel status to public actions.

Result: Sequential monitor achieves AUROC of 0.993 for homogeneous agents and 0.854 for heterogeneous pairs (text- and latent-collusion positives). In Qwen3-0.6B auctions with 25-100 bidders, monitoring load is small; full white-box steering achieves 100% bid-distribution recovery and reduces collusive low-bid behavior by 47.3 percentage points. Exact recovery via replaying the matched neutral counterfactual serves as a sanity check. Private-channel attacks can be monitored without attack-specific training and mitigated when matched counterfactual access is available.

Conclusion: VLA enables verifiable monitoring and steering of private latent channels in language-model agents, with strong empirical support in controlled auctions. It can detect and mitigate covert coordination without needing attack-specific training, contingent on access to matched counterfactuals for effective steering.

Abstract: Language-model agents can communicate through continuous hidden states that are invisible in public transcripts, creating opportunities for covert harmful coordination. We introduce Verifiable Latent Alignments (VLA), an activation-aware framework for monitoring and steering these private communication channels. For every monitored decision, VLA links the private latent-state record and channel status to the resulting public action using a shared event identifier, enabling matched causal analysis. Our first contribution is a neutral-only three-layer monitor combining representation anomaly detection, counterfactual action-distribution influence, and sparse-autoencoder interpretation support. Our second contribution is a steerability framework spanning black-box behavioral instructions and white-box matched-neutral counterfactuals. Our third contribution is an evaluation on a controlled multi-agent auction benchmark covering homogeneous and heterogeneous model pairs, many-agent scalability, and intervention effectiveness. The sequential monitor achieves mean area under the receiver operating characteristic curve (AUROC) of 0.993 for homogeneous agents and 0.854 for heterogeneous pairs when text- and latent-collusion rows are pooled as positives. In Qwen3-0.6B auctions with 25-100 bidders, monitoring requires only a small normalized load relative to all possible directed pairs, while full white-box steering achieves 100% bid-distribution recovery and reduces collusive low-bid behavior by 47.3 percentage points. Because full white-box steering replays the matched neutral counterfactual, its exact recovery is a sanity check by construction. Overall, the controlled study shows that the evaluated private channel attacks can be monitored without training the primary monitor on attack examples and mitigated when matched counterfactual access is available.

</details>
